*Accounting for Survivability*

**Karen Pendergrass**

## AUTHOR’S NOTE

*This book makes one claim and walks the consequences of it through every food system worth evaluating.*

The claim is that the framework most often used to ask whether food production is sustainable — economic viability, social fairness, environmental fit — describes how a system performs under stable conditions. It does not describe how the system performs when the conditions change. That is a different question, and it has been left out of the framework we have been using to score food for forty years. I call the missing axis *survivability*. The rest of the book is the case for taking it seriously.

The argument is timely because the conditions are changing. Climate volatility now reaches a single growing season. Supply-chain shocks reach the grocery shelf within weeks. Soil base, irrigation supply, pollinator population, antibiotic ladder, fossil-fuel input — every variable food production rests on is moving, and most of them are moving in directions the current framework has no language for. A food system can be the very best system the present-day inputs can produce and still fail the survivability test. This book exists to make that test visible, applicable, and unavoidable.

I am writing it from inside the certification work, not outside it. Sixteen years of standards-writing for the Paleo Foundation taught me how to score food production in operation; the four-axis framework here is what I now believe we should also have been scoring all along.

The book ends in a hypothesis: that one of the food systems that scores best on all four axes is one we lost twelve and a half thousand years ago and could choose to bring back. The chapters arrive there in their own time.

*— K. P.*

## A NOTE ON WHERE I AM WRITING FROM

*This book could be read as a sixty-thousand-word advertorial for a certification ecosystem I have been part of for sixteen years. Reading it that way is not unfair, and I want to address it before it becomes the loudest thing in the room.*

Since 2010, I have been architecting standards for the Paleo Foundation. That work began with Paleo Approved, a farm and ranch certification that was a flop, and later expanded into Certified Paleo, Keto Certified, and most recently Heavy Metal Tested & Certified — which is where the heavy-metals chapter of this book sits with respect to my current professional work.

The Afterword names four sites I run alongside the certification work — wikibiome.com, microbiomemedicine.com, heavymetaltested.com, and heavymetalindex.com — and the heavy-metals chapter cites two of my own preprint papers as part of its evidence base.

At first glance these look like disparate interests: agriculture, diet, microbiome medicine, microbial metallomics, heavy metals. They are not. They are what comes of pulling on the same threads for sixteen years in different directions. They are intricately connected, and the book is the place where some of the connections finally come into view.

I am disclosing this here because the alternative —burying it in the Acknowledgments — would not change the conflict; it would only make finding it harder. The conflict is real. I want to be honest about what it is and what it isn’t.

*What it isn’t:* this book is not a sales document for HMTc certification. The certification work is a smaller proposition than the book is. HMTc tests for a specific set of contaminants in specific food categories under specific protocols. The book is an argument for a four-axis evaluative framework that applies to food systems whether or not anyone certifies anything. The framework was not built to backfill the certification work. The certification work was built because the framework, applied to the parts of the food system I happened to be looking at most closely, kept producing the same result: the heavy-metal load is the input nobody is measuring at the input level, and somebody had to start.

The heavy-metal load had consequences that could not be understood without connecting dots across agriculture, diet, the microbiome, human health and disease, and microbial metallomics. The patterns emerged, and AI will only make those patterns louder in coming years. But that’s beyond the scope of this book. Maybe in the next one.

*What it is:* sixteen years of standards-writing taught me, faster than any other route I can imagine, what it actually takes to score a food production system against a stated criterion. You write the criterion. You go look at the production. You discover what the criterion did not anticipate. You revise. You discover what the revised criterion did not anticipate. You revise again. After enough rounds, you start to see the structural shape of what the criterion was always missing, and the missing piece is usually the same piece across categories. The four-axis framework is what I think the missing piece, in the most general form I have been able to find, actually is. The reason the preprints are cited is not to send readers to my certification work; it is to show that I have been taking the question seriously enough to publish what I have found, in formats anyone can read, before asking anyone to trust the framework.

I am writing this book from inside that work, not outside it. The reader can decide what weight to give claims that come from inside that vantage. My obligation is to make the vantage visible.

*— K. P.*

# Prologue: One Hundred Camels

> *From Daniel H. Janzen & Paul S. Martin, Science (1982)*
>
> *“Neotropical Anachronisms: The Fruits the Gomphotheres Ate.”*
>
> *A continent’s flora carries the signature of the animals it evolved with. When those animals vanish inside a geological instant, the flora is left*
>
> *producing for mouths that no longer exist.*
>
> *— D. H. Janzen & P. S. Martin, 1982*

It is not lost on me that for three million years, millions of camels lumbered through the very place where I first sat down to write this book. A cafe in Santa Monica called 212. The cafe is gone now. The camels have been gone much longer.

The camel was *Camelops hesternus*, "yesterday's camel," the Western camel, native to North America for almost the entire existence of its genus. It stood roughly seven feet at the shoulder—somewhat larger than a modern dromedary—built for the shortgrass and sage of the late Pleistocene. Its bones turn up across the American West, from the La Brea tar pits in southern California to kill sites in the Great Basin to arroyos in the southern Plains. It ate mesquite and prickly pear. It browsed juniper. It moved in herds across a continent where horses, mammoths, ground sloths, American lions, and American cheetahs shared the same country. Then, about thirteen thousand years ago, it was gone.<sup>1</sup>

Most readers know this story, if they know it at all, in reverse. The camels Americans picture are Old World animals: dromedaries and Bactrians, the camels of the Silk Road and the Sahara, introduced to the Americas once, briefly, by the US Army Camel Corps in the 1850s, then failed out of service and absorbed into folklore. The ancestors of those Old World camels evolved on the North American continent and migrated out across the Bering land bridge millions of years ago. They diversified in Eurasia and Africa. They returned to their continent of origin only as imports. The camel's deep home is here. We have forgotten.

The forgetting is not personal. It is structural, and it is the reason I am writing this book.

The framework most of us have inherited for deciding what belongs on this continent begins in 1492. Animals present here before Columbus are "native." Animals introduced after Columbus are "non-native," "invasive," "exotic" — the vocabulary varies with the disposition of the speaker, but the baseline does not. The line is drawn at the date of European contact. What was here at that moment became the reference point against which all subsequent ecological change is measured.

It is an oddly narrow window. Camels had been absent from North America for roughly twelve and a half thousand years by the time Columbus made landfall. Horses had been absent for about the same span. Mammoths, mastodons, ground sloths, American cheetahs, American lions, giant beavers, dire wolves, and most of the continent's megafaunal assemblage were already gone. The continent the Spanish sailed into was not a pristine ecosystem frozen at its baseline—it was a depleted one, long depleted, more than half of its large-bodied fauna already missing.<sup>2</sup> The Columbian reference date captures the moment of European contact. It does not capture anything ecological about the land itself.

Paul S. Martin spent most of his career at the University of Arizona trying to put this observation into wider circulation. Martin was a paleoecologist who had worked on the Late Pleistocene extinctions for decades. By the time he published *Twilight of the Mammoths* in 2005, he had arrived at a position that is still, twenty years later, outside most popular conservation conversation. He proposed that the question "what belongs here" should be answered on a deeper timescale than 1492. He called the conservation paradigm that stops at Columbus "the Columbian Curtain." He proposed dropping it.<sup>3</sup>

On the other side of the Curtain is a different continent. Mammoths in the boreal north. Ground sloths the size of bears browsing the forest edges. American horses on the plains. American cheetahs in the canyons (which is why the pronghorn, the fastest land animal in North America, still runs at speeds no living predator requires; its legs are calibrated to a cat that no longer exists). And camels. *Camelops* across the basin and range. *Hemiauchenia*, a larger, stouter relative, through the woodlands. Smaller llamas along the southwestern edge. A continent arranged around a set of animal relationships that have, almost entirely, gone unreciprocated for a hundred and twenty centuries.

Martin was not sentimental about this. He was a scientist with the fossil record to work with. His argument was that ecological function does not care about the date on which a species disappeared. A prairie that evolved with proboscideans behaves differently in their absence than one that evolved without them. A predator-prey dynamic misses both parties when either one is removed. A fruit tree whose seeds evolved to be eaten by ground sloths is still waiting for the ground sloths. The ecosystem is a set of relationships, and most of the relationships on this continent in the late Pleistocene have been one-sided for a very long time.

If you grew up in the American Midwest or the mid-South, you have almost certainly met an Osage orange tree. *Maclura pomifera*. It is common along fence rows and in old hedgerow plantings — it was planted massively in the nineteenth century as a thorny living fence before barbed wire made it obsolete — and its fruit is the size and approximate color of a softball, grainy-skinned, slightly fragrant, faintly sticky with a white latex when cut. To any modern animal of the continent, that fruit is essentially inedible. Horses will nose one and move on. Cattle will not eat them. Squirrels pick through the seeds if they can reach them, which is rarely. The fruit falls, rots in place, and stains the ground. A tree with a bumper crop looks like it is producing for no one.

Because, in a real sense, it is. In 1982, Daniel Janzen and Paul Martin published a paper in *Science* titled "Neotropical Anachronisms: The Fruits the Gomphotheres Ate."<sup>4</sup> The argument was straightforward; once you have read it, you cannot un-see it. Many of the large, sugary, otherwise-puzzling fruits of the Americas (avocados, pawpaws, honey locust pods, Osage oranges, a long list of tropical species) carry the structural signatures of having co-evolved with large-bodied dispersers that are no longer there. A gomphothere, a mastodon-relative proboscidean that ranged widely across North and Central America until the end of the Pleistocene, would have taken an Osage orange whole, walked some miles, and deposited the seeds in a dung pile prepared for germination. Without the gomphothere, the tree continues to produce the fruit the tree evolved to produce. The fruit is a message to a mouth that no longer exists.

Janzen and Martin called these trees ecological anachronisms. The phrase stayed with me.

> **TRY THIS: The Osage Orange**
>
> Next time you see an Osage orange fruit on the ground, pick it up. Feel how heavy it is. Look at it for tool marks. There will not be any. No mouse has chewed it. No deer has browsed it. No squirrel has cracked a single seed out. The fruit is, for the present fauna, inert.
>
> Now picture the mouth the fruit was made for. A gomphothere stood roughly nine feet at the shoulder and weighed several tons. Its molars were the size of bricks. A fruit that looks large to you would have looked like a snack to it.
>
> If you want a small exercise: count the Osage oranges under one tree in late autumn. Note how many show any sign of being opened, by any animal, over the course of the winter. Most years, on most trees, the answer is zero. The tree produced a hundred-pound crop for a mouth that is not coming.
>
> The data are not earth-shaking. But they are yours, and they are specific to a tree you can walk to.

An anachronism, strictly, is something out of its time—a character in the wrong century, a gesture from the wrong era. The Osage orange is out of its time because its partner is out of its time. The tree is stranded in a present that has forgotten what it is for. Multiply this by every species with a large-fruit architecture, every grassland assemblage that evolved under the hooves and digestive tracts of animals weighing tons, every predator community whose prey has been gone for longer than there has been writing, and you have something like the actual ecological state of this continent. The landscape is not depleted in a vague or poetic way. It is missing specific animals. Their absence has specific consequences. The consequences have compounded across twelve thousand years of quiet rearrangement that most of us have not been taught to notice.

This is what the Columbian Curtain hides. Drop the baseline back to the end of the Pleistocene and the continent you are standing on is not recovering from European contact; it is recovering from something much older, and most of that recovery has not yet begun.

I should say that I have held various positions on this material over the years. I first read Martin in the late 2000s, and my initial reading left me convinced of a simpler story than I now think the evidence supports. For a long time I believed that the extinctions had been driven primarily by humans arriving across the Bering land bridge (the "overkill hypothesis," which Martin himself had done much to establish in his earlier career) and that the case for rewilding followed from a kind of collective human debt. Later work on rapid climate oscillation during the Younger Dryas, on the paleoclimate record more broadly, and on the difficulty of cleanly dating the extinctions against human arrival, has made me less confident. The question of cause is not settled, and I no longer think the rewilding argument depends on its resolution. Chapter 3 returns to this in the detail it deserves. The prologue does not need to.

What I want to put on the table first, before any framework or argument, is the shape of the absence itself. The animals are not here. The fruit is still being made. The grasslands still stand in the assemblages that evolved around grazers that are gone. The pronghorn still runs from a cheetah. The continent is a library that has lost most of its librarians, and it is somehow still trying to operate.

I would like, somewhere in my life, to see one hundred camels in an Osage orange orchard.

This is plainly said because there is no more elegant way to say it. I do not mean it as policy, or as provocation, or as a headline. I mean it as a wish—a specific one, with specific animals, in a specific arrangement. A hundred camels because that is enough for a small, real herd; large enough to be a population and small enough to manage honestly. An Osage orange orchard because I would like to know what those trees look like when their fruit has a mouth. Camels are not gomphotheres, but they are closer in body plan, gut size, and browsing habit than anything now available on the continent, and they are already here—feral in parts of the American West, stabled on hobby ranches elsewhere, just not in the ecological role they once had. I would like to know whether the fruit gets eaten. I would like to know whether the seeds, passed through a large mammalian gut and deposited with fertilizer, germinate at higher rates than the current state of abandonment allows. I would like to know whether the ground around the trees begins, even slightly, to resemble something other than rot.

I am not sure anyone would fund the experiment. I am not sure the permits would be approved, or the ecological risk assessments would come out favorably, or the neighbors would agree. I know what serious critics of Pleistocene rewilding would say about it. I have read their arguments, and the book takes them seriously in the chapters where they belong. The wish is not a program. It is the thing the program would be for.

This book is, for the most part, an analytical argument about how to evaluate food systems. It proposes a framework, applies it, and reaches conclusions I think are defensible. But the analytical case is not what drives it. What drives it is this: the orchard, the camels, the fruit with a mouth. The sense that we have been living for twelve thousand years on a continent whose ecological vocabulary has been spoken at half-volume, and that there is no reason to treat that half-silence as a permanent condition.

Paul Martin spent his career trying to make the silence legible. I am writing this book, more than anything else, to return his argument to a conversation that has mostly moved on without it.

The framework comes later. The camels come first.

# Chapter One: The Meat Counter

In September of 2009 I was standing at the meat counter of a major natural-foods retailer in Overland Park, Kansas, with my mother, scanning the selection and trying to make sense of what I was looking at.

*A note on naming. I am keeping the retailer anonymous throughout this account. The events are exactly as they happened. Naming the chain in print invites a category of legal dispute the book does not need, and the chain is recognizable enough from the geography and the era that anyone who wants to know which one will know. The choice to keep it anonymous here is to keep this book out of court.*

Overland Park is a suburb of Kansas City, the kind of sprawling Johnson County development where the grocery stores are large and clean and lit like operating theaters. The store was relatively new to the area. It occupied the spot in the local retail ecology that a retailer of that scale always occupies: the store where the prices are higher and the assumption, embedded in the brand, is that the higher prices are buying you something the other stores cannot guarantee. I was there because I believed that assumption. I had reason to.

I had been sick for the better part of a year. By September I was on the other side of the worst of it, eating carefully, paying attention to what I put in my body in a way I had never done before. The wrong food could set me back weeks. The right food (clean protein, known sourcing, no hidden grains) was not a preference but a medical necessity, and I was standing in the meat section of a store I trusted because the store's entire brand proposition was that it had already done the vetting for me. The grass-fed beef was what I had come for. The label said grass-fed. The price reflected grass-fed. The display case was arranged with the quiet confidence of a store that believed it was selling you exactly what you thought you were buying.

I picked up a package, turned it over, and asked the person behind the counter a question that would not have occurred to me six months earlier. "Is this a hundred percent grass-fed?"

He said yes.

I asked again — politely, but with the specificity that comes from having spent months learning that the wrong food could put me back in a condition I did not want to revisit. "A hundred percent? Grass the whole way through?"

He paused. "It's grass-fed," he said, "but it's grain-finished for the last three months."

My mother and I looked at each other. She said what I was thinking before I could say it: "If they're feeding them grain to fatten them up, how can you call it grass-fed?"

The man behind the counter was not being evasive. He was, as far as I could tell, repeating what he had been told. The beef was marketed as grass-fed. It had been raised on grass for most of its life. It had then been transitioned to grain for the final ninety days before slaughter (a practice called grain finishing, common in the beef industry, which puts weight on the animal quickly and produces the marbling American consumers have been trained to prefer). The man behind the counter was not the person who had decided to call this product grass-fed. He was the person standing between the decision and the customer, doing his job, and his job did not include explaining the regulatory architecture that made the label possible.

I did not buy the beef. I stood there for another minute, looking at the case — the chicken, the turkey, the pork, the lamb, all of it arranged under labels I had been reading as promises — and something shifted. Not dramatically. Not with any sense of revelation. Quietly, the way a crack appears in a wall you have been looking at for a long time without seeing it. The label said one thing. The practice was another. And if this label, on this product, in this store (the store whose entire value proposition rested on the reliability of its sourcing claims), could mean something other than what it appeared to mean, then what else in the case was I reading wrong?

The gap between the label and the practice was not an accident or an error. It was structural. I did not yet have that word for it. I only had the feeling, which was the feeling of having trusted a system that had not earned the trust, without knowing that the trust was unearned until I asked one specific question on one specific afternoon.

> **FIELD NOTE: What Actually Happened at the Meat Counter**
>
> The version of the scene above is the cleaned one. The actual scene was louder. This is the dirty version.
>
> I had been sick for over a year. By the end of 2009, at twenty-five, I had been diagnosed with insulin-resistant PCOS, PMDD, schizophrenia, grade-three anemia, cirrhosis, a kidney mass, GERD, peripheral neuropathy, optic neuritis, bacterial vaginosis, menopause, ascites with chronic edema, endometriosis, fibromyalgia, and finally, celiac disease. I had gained fifty pounds in three months. The anemia was severe enough that the physician treating me had already said I needed someone else to drive me around. Despite following the red-meat diet he suggested, I was still deteriorating.
>
> That day at the store, I had already been crying. I had been grocery shopping for over half an hour, and the only thing I had added to the basket was apples. They were the only thing I could eat at the time without adverse effects, and I avoided grain-finishing because it had previously caused digestive issues. That is why I was so intent on getting grass-finished beef. I needed it. This was a dire situation. I was emotionally exhausted.
>
> The man behind the counter had just told me that what I had been eating was grain-finished, which meant it had been making me sicker for months, which meant I had been paying premium prices to be poisoned more politely. There were expletives. There were a lot of them. Ten out of ten completely unhinged.

Security was called. My mother had to drag me out, and she did, and I have never loved her more. There were expletives all the way to the car. Then more crying.

I later found out that the incident traveled. It went up the chain to corporate headquarters, and there was an emergency meeting about what employees could and could not say about the grass-fed claim. For a while I was famous at that Overland Park store, in a way I did not want to be famous and have no reason to be quiet about now. That store is gone now, too.

I am not in the slightest bit embarrassed of 2009 Karen either. She was sick, she had finally figured out that the system she was paying premium prices to trust was not telling her the truth, and she said so, loudly, in the kind of place where loudly is not how you are supposed to say it.

> **FIELD NOTE: What I Did Next**
>
> I should say what I did next, because it is part of the same thing.
>
> The version of me who got dragged out of that store went home and started writing standards. Actual certification standards, the kind producers have to meet to put a label on a package. Paleo Approved came first, in 2010. It was an animal standard, and a strict one. It was also a flop. Ranchers did not yet understand the importance of it, and the Paleo community itself was still embryonic and not creating the demand. I was too early.
>
> Paleo as a popular movement did not really take off until 2013 or 2014, and by then we had abandoned ship to move toward where the demand actually was, which was in consumer packaged goods.
>
> What followed, under the Paleo Foundation, was Certified Paleo. Although I initially felt like a sellout for abandoning farm and ranch certifications, the most consequential ecological work I have done in my life is bound by non-disclosure agreements I signed willingly and intend to honor.
>
> In other words, the thing I am proudest of in that entire arc happened under the Certified Paleo label, not Paleo Approved.
>
> I am telling you this now because the Field Note above ends with me losing it at a meat counter. The version of me writing this book has a clearer view of the gap between what is on a label and what is in the package, in part because I have spent sixteen years writing the standards for the labels — and because there are things I wish I had done better.

## WHAT "GRASS-FED" MEANT

At the time I was standing in that store, the term "grass-fed" on beef packaging in the United States was governed by a voluntary marketing claim standard administered by the USDA's Agricultural Marketing Service. The standard, finalized in 2007, defined grass-fed as meaning that the animal's diet had been derived solely from forage (grass, legumes, hay, silage) with the exception of milk consumed prior to weaning.<sup>1</sup> Under this definition, grain finishing would have disqualified the beef from a grass-fed claim. The standard existed. On paper, it was clear.

In practice, the standard was voluntary, and its enforcement relied on a verification process that most producers did not undergo. The USDA did not inspect every operation making a grass-fed claim. It certified those that applied for certification and met the documentation requirements; those that did not apply were subject to the same labeling regulations that governed all meat products, which is to say, subject to review only if a complaint was filed or an audit was triggered. The gap between the standard's existence and its application was wide enough to drive a grain truck through. A retailer could sell beef labeled "grass-fed" that had been grain-finished, and unless someone with standing complained to the right agency, the label stood.<sup>2</sup>

The standard was later withdrawn entirely. In January 2016, the USDA's Agricultural Marketing Service revoked its grass-fed marketing claim standard, citing concerns about the verification process and the scope of the standard's applicability.<sup>3</sup> After the withdrawal, the term "grass-fed" on beef packaging became, in regulatory terms, essentially unregulated at the federal level: any producer could use it, under any definition they chose, provided they did not make a claim that was demonstrably false under the Federal Trade Commission's general deceptive-practices authority. The practical effect was that "grass-fed" became a term whose meaning was determined by whoever was printing the label.

I did not know any of this in 2009. I was a consumer standing in a store, reading a label, and trusting the label to mean what I thought it meant. What I thought it meant was that the animal had eaten grass. What the label actually certified — to the extent it certified anything — was something narrower, more hedged, and more dependent on the specific practices of a specific producer than the two words on the package could convey. The gap between my assumption and the reality was not a gap I had been invited to notice. It was a gap the label was designed to bridge without my having to look down.

## THE PATTERN

I might have treated the meat-counter moment as an isolated problem — a single misleading label on a single product in a single store — if the same pattern had not kept appearing.

Consider "free-range," as applied to poultry. The term appears on egg cartons and chicken packaging across the country, and its presence reliably commands a premium. Consumers pay more for free-range eggs than for conventional eggs, often significantly more, on the reasonable assumption that the word describes a meaningfully different life for the bird that produced them.

The USDA's Food Safety and Inspection Service defines free-range, for the purpose of poultry labeling, as meaning that the birds have been "allowed access to the outside."<sup>4</sup> The definition does not specify the size of the outdoor area. It does not specify the duration of access. It does not specify the quality of the outdoor space: whether it is grass or concrete or bare dirt. It does not specify whether the birds actually go outside, only that a door exists through which they could, if they chose to, if they could reach it, if they knew it was there. A producer can raise ten thousand birds in a single enclosed structure, open a small door at one end of the structure for some portion of the birds' lives, and label the resulting product free-range. The birds nearest the door may occasionally step through it. The birds at the far end of the structure — hundreds of feet from the opening, in a flock so dense that individual movement through the crowd is physically constrained — may never see daylight. The label does not distinguish between these birds. It certifies the existence of a door.

Most consumers, asked what "free-range" means, will describe something that looks like a farm: birds on grass, moving freely, doing the things birds do when they have room and sunlight and ground to scratch. The mental image is not irrational. It is the image the language invites. "Free-range" is a compound of two words that each carry spatial meaning: freedom and range, openness and distance. The phrase produces a picture. The regulatory definition behind the phrase produces a door.

I came to understand this only gradually, in the months after the meat counter, as I began reading labeling regulations with the attention I had previously reserved for ingredient lists. The gap between the picture and the door is not a failure of consumer education. It is not a case of consumers being too lazy or too credulous to read the fine print. There is, in most cases, no fine print to read. The label says "free-range." The regulation defines it. The consumer encounters only the label. The regulation sits in the *Federal Register*, available to anyone who knows where to look, written in the language regulations are written in, useful to anyone with the time and the training to parse it. The system is not hiding the definition. It is relying on the distance between the definition and the consumer to do the work that active deception would do if the system were less sophisticated.

"Pasture-raised" is a variation on the same theme. The term has no federal regulatory definition at all. It appears on packaging because producers choose to put it there, and its meaning is whatever the producer's own practices happen to be. Some operations using the "pasture-raised" label run birds on genuinely open pasture in mobile shelters, moved daily, with real access to grass and insects and sunlight. Others use it more loosely. The consumer cannot tell the difference from the package. Third-party certifications (Certified Humane, Animal Welfare Approved, and others) have stepped into the regulatory gap with their own standards for what "pasture-raised" means, and some of those standards are rigorous. But the label itself, the two words on the carton, certifies nothing in particular. It is a description without a definition, a promise without a guarantor.

Or consider "organic," which is the most comprehensively regulated of the major food labels and still produces a version of the same gap. The USDA's National Organic Program, established under the Organic Foods Production Act of 1990 and administered through a body of regulations codified at 7 CFR Part 205, defines organic production as a system that integrates cultural, biological, and mechanical practices to foster cycling of resources, promote ecological balance, and conserve biodiversity.<sup>5</sup> The standards are real, detailed, and enforced through a third-party certification process that is substantially more rigorous than anything governing "grass-fed" or "free-range." An organic label on a food product means something. It means the producer has been inspected, has met specific input and practice requirements, and has documentation to support the claim.

What the organic label does not mean, and has never meant, is what many consumers take it to mean. Organic certification does not certify that the food was produced locally. It does not certify that the animals were raised on pasture. It does not certify that the soil is being built rather than depleted, only that certain prohibited inputs (most synthetic fertilizers, most synthetic pesticides, most antibiotics, genetically engineered organisms) are absent from the production process. A large-scale organic operation can look, structurally, a great deal like a conventional industrial operation: confined animal housing, monoculture crop rotations, long-distance commodity distribution, significant fossil-fuel inputs for field work and transportation. The organic label certifies what is not being used. It does not certify how the system as a whole is functioning.

This is not a criticism of organic certification. The organic standard was designed to do what it does, and what it does is useful: it removes a specific set of chemical inputs from the production process and provides a verification mechanism for that removal. The problem is not that the standard fails at its own task. The problem is that the label, as encountered by a consumer in a grocery aisle, communicates something substantially broader than the standard certifies. The word "organic" carries associations (with naturalness, with ecological care, with a certain quality of attention to the land) that the certification process was not designed to guarantee and does not guarantee. The consumer's assumption is reasonable. The label's scope is narrower than the assumption. The gap is the same gap I encountered at the meat counter, wearing different clothes.

> **CASE: Three Labels, Three Definitions, One Gap**
>
> The three labels this chapter has walked through carry three different relationships to their definitions. "Grass-fed," under the 2007 USDA marketing-claim standard, had a precise definition that was voluntary and effectively unenforced; the standard was withdrawn entirely in 2016, after which any producer could use the term under any definition. "Free-range," under FSIS poultry labeling, requires only that birds have "access to the outside," which in practice is a door. "Organic," under the 1990 Organic Foods Production Act and its 2002 implementing regulations, carries the most rigorous definition of the three, verifiably prohibiting specific synthetic inputs through third-party certification.

Three different labels, three different definitional rigors, three different gaps between what the consumer assumes and what the label certifies. The gap is not uniform across labels. It is structural across the labeling system.

## THE ARCHITECTURE OF THE GAP

Standing at that meat counter that September, I did not have the vocabulary for what I was noticing. I had a feeling, which was the feeling of having been misled without anyone having lied to me, and I had a question, which was how a system this large and this well-funded could consistently produce labels that communicated less than consumers believed they were being told.

The answer, which I arrived at slowly over the years that followed, is that the labels are not failing. They are succeeding at a task different from the one consumers assume they are performing.

A food label in the United States is, in regulatory terms, a marketing claim. It exists at the intersection of the producer's desire to differentiate a product, the retailer's desire to command a price premium, and the regulatory apparatus's desire to prevent outright fraud without unduly burdening commerce. The label's job, within this intersection, is to communicate *enough* to support the marketing claim while remaining narrow enough to survive regulatory scrutiny. "Grass-fed" communicates pastoral images. "Free-range" communicates open space. "Organic" communicates naturalness. Each term does this reliably, for the majority of consumers, and each term is defensible under the regulatory definition that governs it. The system works. It works for the producer, who can differentiate a product. It works for the retailer, who can charge a premium. It works for the regulator, who can point to a definition. The only party for whom it does not quite work is the consumer who assumed the label was telling them something about the *system* their food came from, rather than about a single certified attribute of that system.

This is, I think, the structural observation the meat counter moment eventually led me to. The food labeling system is not broken. It is designed to communicate less than consumers want to know. Each label certifies one thing — one attribute, one absence, one practice verified at one point in the production chain. Consumers want to know many things — how the animal lived, what it ate, how the land was managed, what inputs were used, what the operation's relationship to its surrounding ecology looks like, what would happen to the system if one of its dependencies were removed. No single label, even a good one, can answer all of those questions. The labels were not designed to answer all of those questions. They were designed to answer one question each, narrowly, defensibly, in language that happens to evoke a broader picture than the narrow answer supports.

The gap between the narrow answer and the broad picture is not a conspiracy. It is an architecture. It is the predictable result of a system in which labeling is designed to facilitate market differentiation rather than to provide comprehensive transparency about production practices. Producers are not villains for using the labels as designed. Retailers are not villains for stocking labeled products. Regulators are not villains for writing narrow definitions. Consumers are not fools for reading the labels the way the labels invite themselves to be read. Everyone in the system is doing what the system was designed to let them do. The system itself is the problem, and the problem is not that it fails but that it succeeds at something less than what most of us need it to do.

The architecture has a further consequence worth naming, because it bears on everything the rest of this book discusses. A label that certifies a single attribute can, by its presence, create the impression that the system behind the product has been evaluated comprehensively. A package of chicken bearing an organic label and a free-range label and a "no antibiotics ever" label looks, to the consumer scanning the case, like a product that has been thoroughly vetted. Three labels. Three assurances. Surely the system has been looked at from multiple angles. In fact, what has happened is that three narrow attributes have each been verified independently, with no requirement that anyone has ever looked at the operation as a system: as a whole set of practices, dependencies, inputs, outputs, and vulnerabilities functioning together. The labels are point measurements. The consumer needs a map. The multiplication of labels can actually make the map harder to read, because it produces the feeling of comprehensiveness without the substance of it.

## WHAT I DID NOT KNOW YET

I want to be careful, here, about what I am not claiming. I am not claiming that I understood any of this at the meat counter. I understood almost none of it. I understood that the label had misled me, and I understood that I was angry, and I understood that the anger was not usefully directed at the person behind the counter, who was doing his job and had answered my question honestly when I pressed him on it. Beyond that, I was a sick person in a grocery store who had just learned that the food she trusted was not what she thought it was.

What happened next took years, not minutes. I went home. I started reading. I read about labeling standards. I read about certification processes. I read about USDA definitions and the regulatory comment process and the difference between a mandatory standard and a voluntary marketing claim. I read about organic certification's scope and limits. I read about the word "natural," which has no regulatory definition at all on most food products and functions as pure marketing language attached to nothing.<sup>6</sup> I read, eventually, about the deeper questions underneath the labeling problem: about food systems, about sustainability, about what it means to ask whether the way we produce food can continue to work under conditions different from the ones we are used to.

The reading took me to Paul Martin. It took me to Allan Savory and Joel Salatin and the regenerative agriculture literature. It took me to the paleontological record, to the Columbian Curtain, to the near-time extinctions, to the question of what this continent looked like before most of its large-bodied fauna disappeared. It took me to questions I could not have anticipated from a meat counter in suburban Kansas: questions about timescale, about ecological baselines, about what it means for a food system to survive under conditions different from the ones it was designed for.

It took me, eventually, to the framework this book proposes (a framework I did not have in 2009, did not begin to develop until several years later, and did not fully articulate until the middle of the last decade). The path between the meat counter and the framework was not a straight line. It included a number of positions I have since revised, a number of arguments I made with more confidence than the evidence warranted, and a number of assumptions I held longer than I should have. The authorial revisions that have shaped this book (about dietary universality, about the causes of the Pleistocene extinctions, about the tone in which disagreement should be conducted) are part of the trajectory. The meat counter was not the moment I found the answer. It was the moment the question became unavoidable. The distance between the question and whatever answer this book offers has been covered on foot, over sixteen years, with wrong turns.

> **FIELD NOTE: What I Wrote Down**
>
> I have my journals from 2009. I did not start writing seriously until later, but there is a page from that fall where I wrote, in capital letters, *labels are lies*. That was the first thing I wrote down.

It was wrong in the specific sense. The labels on the meat I was looking at were not legally false. But it captured what I had felt at the counter, which was the feeling of having been told something technically true and operationally misleading. I underlined it. I did not yet know that this was a decades-old problem in food policy, or that the regulatory architecture had been engineered to produce exactly that feeling without producing a prosecutable deception.

I was twenty-five. I thought I had discovered something. What I had discovered was a question a lot of other people had already asked. But it took sixteen years to get from the question to the framework, and I do not regret the underlined sentence. It was the beginning of where I am now.

I am noting this because the trajectory matters to the book's honesty. The temptation, in a chapter like this one, is to present the early moment of recognition as though it contained the later understanding, as though I stood at the meat counter and saw, fully formed, the structural inadequacy of the labeling system and the deeper questions it pointed toward. That is not what happened. What happened was small and specific: a label that did not mean what I thought it meant, a feeling of having been misled, and a question that did not resolve itself for years. The understanding came later, incrementally, through reading and experience and — as the revisions that have shaped this book will show — through being wrong about a number of things along the way. The meat counter was the beginning of a question, not the arrival at an answer.

## WHAT LABELS CANNOT TELL US

The labeling problem is, in the end, a problem about information: about what the consumer is given and what the consumer would need. The gap between the two is not a gap that better labels can fully close, because the questions a thoughtful consumer wants answered about a food system are not questions that fit on a package.

A label can tell you that an animal was fed grass. It cannot tell you whether the grassland the animal grazed on is building soil or losing it (whether the land is gaining organic matter year over year or slowly losing the biological capital that makes it productive). It cannot tell you whether the operation depends on purchased inputs from industrial commodity markets for parts of its production cycle, or whether it is genuinely self-sustaining on its own acreage. It cannot tell you whether the operation could survive the loss of a single input (a subsidy, a commodity feed source, a supply chain) or whether it would collapse. It cannot tell you what the land looked like before the operation was there, or what it will look like after. It cannot tell you whether the system the food comes from is one that could continue to function under conditions meaningfully different from today's, or whether it is riding on a set of assumptions about stable inputs, stable climate, and stable policy that may or may not hold.

These are not unreasonable questions. They are, I would argue, the questions that matter most: not just for the consumer deciding what to buy on a Tuesday evening, but for the society deciding what kinds of food production to support, to subsidize, to build infrastructure around, to invest in for the next century. The questions are about systems, not about attributes. They are about how the whole thing works, not about whether one variable meets one standard. And the labeling system, by design, answers attribute questions. It was built for attributes. It does attributes well. It does not do systems at all.

There is a reason the labeling system was built this way, and the reason is not sinister. Attributes are verifiable. You can inspect an operation and determine whether the birds have access to the outdoors, whether synthetic pesticides were applied, whether antibiotics were administered. These are binary or near-binary questions, and a regulatory system can answer binary questions through documentation and inspection. Systems-level questions — is this operation ecologically sustainable, is it resilient to disruption, could it continue to function under changed conditions — are not binary. They require frameworks of evaluation that the regulatory apparatus was not designed to perform and that the labeling system was never intended to communicate. Asking a food label to convey systems-level information is asking it to do something it was not built to do. The question is not why the labels fail at this. The question is what else we have.

This is where the meat counter moment, small as it was, connects to the larger argument the book is about to make. The label's inadequacy is not a labeling problem in the narrow sense. It is not a problem that can be solved by writing better definitions for "grass-fed" or tightening the enforcement of "free-range" or adding more labels to packages that are already covered in them. It is a problem about the kind of information we have access to when we make decisions about food, and the kind of information we would need. The gap between the two is where most of the interesting questions in food-systems thinking live. It is the gap the rest of this book is written to address.

I do not have a better label to offer. I want to be clear about that, because this chapter could easily be read as a setup for a pitch — here is what is wrong with existing labels, and here is the label that fixes it. That is not where this book goes. The problem is not that the right label has not been invented. The problem is that labels, as a category of communication, cannot carry the weight of what consumers need to know about the systems their food comes from. The solution, if there is one, is not a better label but a better framework: a way of evaluating food systems that addresses the questions labels were never designed to ask.

The prologue planted an image: camels in an Osage orange orchard, a continent missing its animals, a longing for restoration. This chapter has planted a different kind of image: a woman at a meat counter, reading a label, discovering that the label is not enough. Both images are starting points. The prologue's starting point is emotional — the feeling that something is missing from the landscape. This chapter's starting point is practical — the feeling that something is missing from the information. The two feelings converge on the same set of questions. If the labels cannot tell us what we need to know about our food systems, what can? If the dominant framework for evaluating those systems is measuring the wrong things, or measuring the right things too narrowly, what would a more adequate framework look like? And if such a framework exists, what does it reveal about the systems we rely on — and about the ones we have not yet tried?

The next chapter begins the work of answering those questions, by going back much further than a meat counter in Kansas, to the moment an entire continent's ecological vocabulary was quietly, catastrophically reduced.

I am going to give the answer this book ends in away here, because I would rather you read for the argument than for the suspense. One of the food systems that scores best on all four axes — including the one I will argue most twentieth-century work has been missing — is one we lost twelve and a half thousand years ago, and could choose to bring back. The chapters between here and there are the case for taking that claim seriously. The reader who finds it ridiculous now is the reader I am writing for; the reader who finds it ridiculous at the end is the reader I have failed to convince. I am willing to be told I have failed. I am not willing to write the book without making the case.

# Chapter Two: The Columbian Curtain

> *From Daniel Pauly, Trends in Ecology & Evolution (1995)*
>
> *Each generation of fisheries scientists tended to take the species composition and abundance of the oceans at the start of their own careers as the reference*
>
> *condition against which subsequent change was measured.*
>
> *The cumulative effect, across several generations, was that the “natural” state of the oceans slid progressively further from anything the oceans had*
>
> *been before industrial fishing began.*
>
> *— paraphrase of Pauly, “Anecdotes and the*
>
> *shifting baseline syndrome of fisheries”*

The story most Americans carry about the horse is this: horses came from Spain. The Spanish brought them in the early 1500s, and before that there were no horses on the continent. This is, as far as it goes, true. It is also among the most misleading true things in the country's ecological imagination, because the sentence after it almost always gets dropped.

The sentence after it is that horses evolved here.

The genus *Equus* originated in North America roughly four million years ago and diversified on this continent for most of its evolutionary history.<sup>1</sup> Horses walked out across the Bering land bridge, populated Eurasia and Africa, and continued to evolve there, eventually producing the animals that would be domesticated on the Eurasian steppe some six thousand years ago. Meanwhile, the horses that had stayed in their continent of origin went on being American horses until the end of the Pleistocene, when all of them disappeared in the same wave of extinctions that took the camels and the mammoths.<sup>2</sup>

> **DEFINITION: Equus and the deep equid record**
>
> The genus *Equus* — the lineage that includes every living horse, donkey, and zebra — is the crown of a much longer equid story. Before *Equus* the continent already had a succession of horse-family genera: *Dinohippus*, its likely direct ancestor, already essentially modern in body plan and single-toed; before that, the three-toed *Pliohippus*; before that, the grazing and browsing radiation of *Merychippus* and its contemporaries; and before those, the small, multi-toed browsers of the early Miocene. The equid family has been running on this continent for close to twenty million years. *Equus* is the last four million of that twenty. When paleontologists say horses evolved in North America, they are not saying one species showed up here, stayed a while, and left. They are saying the entire deep history of the family played out on this continent, and the Spanish ships of the sixteenth century were returning the crown of a tree whose roots had been American the whole time.

> **CASE: The American horses by name**
>
> The late-Pleistocene equid fauna of North America went by several specific names, and the specifics matter if only to show that this was not one horse but many. *Equus scotti* was large, stout-bodied, and widely distributed across the southern and central portions of the continent; its body mass places it comfortably within the range of modern domestic horses. *Equus conversidens* was smaller and more gracile, centered on what is now Mexico and the American Southwest. *Equus lambei*, the Yukon horse, persisted in the Alaskan and Yukon refugia; the Thistle Creek genome that confirmed the deep relationship between late-Pleistocene American horses and modern *Equus caballus* came from this population. Other named forms — *Equus francisci*, *Equus complicatus*, *Equus occidentalis* — fill out the record with varying degrees of preservation. The point of the list is not taxonomic tidiness. American horses were a diversified fauna occupying a range of habitats and body sizes, and the wave of terminal-Pleistocene extinctions took the whole set.

> **DEFINITION: Camelops and Its Kin**
>
> Camelidae is the family. Its living members include the dromedary and the Bactrian of Asia and Africa, *Camelus dromedarius* and *Camelus bactrianus*, and four South American species: the llama, the alpaca, the vicuña, and the guanaco. Llamas are camels.
>
> The American Pleistocene fauna held two named camelid genera that are no longer with us. *Camelops hesternus*, the dominant North American camel of the late Pleistocene, ranged from the Yukon to central Mexico and is the species the rewilding proposal is most directly about. *Hemiauchenia macrocephala*, the large-headed llama, occupied the same continental range and is preserved at La Brea, alongside *Camelops*, in the asphalt fossil record of southern California. Both were extinct by the terminal Pleistocene pulse around twelve thousand years ago. Recent paleogenomic work places *Camelops* as sister to the Old World dromedary and Bactrian lineage, not to the South American camelids as morphology had previously suggested.
>
> What is known about *Camelops* from the La Brea record and from skeletal material elsewhere on the continent: roughly seven feet at the shoulder, a foot taller than a modern dromedary, longer-necked, with leg proportions consistent with a sustained running speed in the neighborhood of forty miles per hour. Tooth wear and microwear analysis identifies it as a browser, feeding on shoots, leaves, and the woody growth its long neck would have placed within reach.
>
> Whether *Camelops* carried the fatty hump or humps of its living Old World relatives is unresolved. Soft tissue does not preserve in the fossil record, and the vertebral column shows no anchoring features that would force the answer either way.
>
> A pedantic but important note. *Camelops* itself is extinct. The rewilding hypothesis this book ends in does not propose its resurrection. What it proposes is the introduction of modern camelids — dromedaries, Bactrians, or, depending on the landscape, llamas, alpacas, vicuñas, or guanacos — into a continent that is the family’s ancestral home. Heintzman 2015 and adjacent paleogenomic work establish that *Camelops* sat within the Camelini tribe alongside the modern Old World camels, not within the South American camelid lineage. The animal that walked North America for most of the existence of the camelid family is gone. Its closest living relatives are the dromedary and the Bactrian. The proposal is to put a related camelid back into the niche, not to put *Camelops* back. The book’s argument requires the niche to be real and the family to belong here. It does not require resurrection biology.
>
> What is known, however, with certainty, is that *Camelops hesternus* is as American as apple pie.

Genetic work in the last two decades has tightened the story. In 2013 a team led by Ludovic Orlando reported a complete nuclear genome recovered from a horse bone frozen in permafrost at Thistle Creek in the Canadian Yukon. The bone is dated to the middle Pleistocene, around seven hundred thousand years old. The genome clustered within the diversity of modern *Equus*, confirming that the domestic horse and the Late Pleistocene American horse are close members of a single long-running lineage — the same animal, in a deep sense, with its genetic lights never quite going out.<sup>3</sup> Earlier mitochondrial work by Jaco Weinstock and colleagues had reached a similar conclusion from Alaskan and Yukon Pleistocene material: North American late-Pleistocene horses fell within, not outside, the modern *Equus caballus* / *Equus ferus* group.<sup>4</sup> The phylogeny does not particularly care which ships crossed which ocean. A horse on the plains in 1600 is the same kind of animal as a horse on the plains in 15,000 BP. The gap between the two dates is the question.

The same story holds, with different details, for *Camelops*. In 2015, Peter Heintzman and colleagues recovered paleogenomic data — mitochondrial genomes and partial nuclear genomes — from three western-camel specimens excavated from interglacial deposits along Hunker Creek in the Yukon, dated by paleoecological context to roughly seventy-five to one hundred thirty thousand years ago. The phylogenetic placement was unequivocal in both data sets: *Camelops* is sister to the Old World *Camelus* lineage, the dromedary and the Bactrian, and not to the South American camelids of *Lama* and *Vicugna* as morphology had previously suggested. Divergence between the *Camelops* and *Camelus* lineages dates to the Late Miocene, around seven and a half to six and a half million years ago.<sup>16</sup> What this means for the rewilding argument is direct. The dromedary and the Bactrian are not merely large camelids of convenience; they are the closest living relatives of the animal the American landscape lost.

The gap is also not, by any reasonable ecological measure, a long one. Twelve and a half thousand years sounds like a great deal in the scale of a human life. It is not very much in the scale over which grassland plant communities, ungulate digestive physiology, or soil microbial assemblages evolve. The grasses of the central plains spent tens of millions of years evolving in the presence of large-bodied grazers of the horse's general architecture. Their defenses, their growth habits, their response to compaction and disturbance are calibrated to those animals and to their relatives. Thirteen thousand years of relative quiet on that front is not enough time for those calibrations to disappear. When the Spanish horse arrives on the American plains, it does not enter an ecosystem naive to its body plan. It enters one that has been waiting.

That gap is what the standard story hides. Told as "the Spanish brought horses," the sentence puts the entire evolutionary record of the genus outside the frame. Told as "horses returned to their continent of origin with the Spanish, after an absence of roughly thirteen thousand years," the sentence includes the record, and the ecological meaning of the animals' arrival changes. They are not an introduction from elsewhere. They are a homecoming that took the scenic route.

This is the conceptual move the chapter is about. Nothing in the fossil record changes whether we call the horse native or non-native. What changes is our choice of when to start counting.

## THE BASELINE IS A CHOICE

The dominant framework of American conservation treats the year 1492 as the effective ecological baseline for the continent. Animals present here when Columbus made landfall are categorized as native. Animals not present are categorized as non-native, invasive, exotic, or feral, depending on the disposition of the speaker and the legal category in which the animal has been slotted. The vocabulary varies; the baseline does not. Under this framework, the horse that returns with the Spanish is non-native. The burro that escapes into the Grand Canyon watershed in the nineteenth century is feral. The cattle that replace bison across the western grasslands are livestock rather than wildlife, even where they live in effectively free-ranging herds. Each classification depends not on the animal's relationship to the land but on whether its species was on the continent on a particular October morning in the late fifteenth century.

Paul S. Martin, who spent most of his career as a paleoecologist at the University of Arizona, named this framing the Columbian Curtain. The prologue has introduced it. I want us to take the name more seriously here than the prologue had room to. A curtain is the right image. It is not a hard wall. It is not a border or a boundary in any physical sense. It is a visual instrument — a piece of fabric hung at a particular point in time, with everything behind it obscured from view. The observer standing in front of the curtain treats whatever is in front of it as the whole of the room. Whatever is behind it is out of sight and, therefore, out of the conversation.

Martin's argument was not that the curtain is wrong in the sense that 1492 is a fake date. European contact is a real event, and it does mark one of the largest biotic exchanges in the planet's recent history. His argument was that the curtain is arbitrary. There is nothing about the ecological state of North America in 1492 that makes it the correct reference point against which to measure restoration. The continent Columbus encountered had been losing large-bodied fauna for twelve and a half thousand years by the time he arrived.<sup>5</sup> More than forty species of large mammals were already absent from North America before a single Spanish sail appeared on the horizon. Calling that assemblage "the native condition" is a bit like calling a library "complete" on the strength of its remaining shelves, after most of its collection has been carted off.

Granted, every historical science has to choose a reference point somewhere. Paleontology works in sliding windows and does not pretend they have natural edges. The choice of 1492 as the baseline for "native" was not a deliberate scientific decision so much as a cultural inheritance. The framing came downstream from the history we were telling ourselves about the continent. The arrival of Europeans was the episode after which record-keeping, in the form that came to count, began. The animals present at that moment became the reference animals, and the ones that had been here earlier dropped out of the frame along with everything else the record did not carry forward. The baseline is a cultural artifact dressed as an ecological one.

Notice what the baseline does once it is fixed. A horse that shows up in the sixteenth century is, under this frame, an introduction. A horse that was here in the fourteenth millennium BP is not an ancestor to which the arriving animal is returning; it is an unrelated animal in an unrelated world. A burro roaming the Colorado Plateau is not occupying an ecological niche left behind by extinct American equids; it is a non-native species degrading a native landscape. The entire vocabulary of invasion biology, as applied to these animals, assumes the 1492 baseline. Change the baseline and you do not change whether the animal is doing what it is doing. You change what you call it.

The Bureau of Land Management currently manages roughly eighty-three thousand free-roaming horses and burros across ten western states, at levels its own appropriate-management-level estimates describe as well above the range at which the agency believes the rangeland can be sustainably grazed.<sup>6</sup> The resulting program involves gathers, long-term holding facilities, fertility-control trials, and a political conflict that has continued for decades without much resolution. I am not here to resolve the management dispute. I want only to point out that the argument, as currently constructed, rests on a baseline choice. A horse on BLM land is treated as a non-native animal whose population must be reduced to protect a native range. Push the baseline back eleven thousand years and the same horse is a partially returned native whose relationship with the range is something the range has not experienced in a long time. The animals are the same. The analytical frame is different. The management implications are different. And the frame is almost never examined.

This is the first thing the Columbian Curtain hides: that it is a frame at all, rather than a fact.

## THE VOCABULARY OF INVASION

The baseline does not stand alone. It is propped up by a vocabulary that carries the baseline's assumptions into every sentence in which the vocabulary appears. Once the vocabulary is in circulation, one can argue within it for decades without ever noticing that the argument has already been shaped by what the words assume.

"Invasive" is the clearest case. The term is doing real work in ecology and conservation biology — there are species introductions that cause measurable ecological harm, and a name for the category is useful. But the term is defined, operationally, against the 1492 baseline. A species is invasive, under the standard definitions deployed by state and federal agencies, if it has been introduced to an ecosystem in which it was not previously present and is causing or likely to cause harm.<sup>12</sup> "Previously" here, in nearly every operative framework, means "at the time of European contact." The definition is internally coherent, and it reflects a genuine ecological concern about the rate at which human commerce has moved species across biogeographic barriers in the last five centuries. What it cannot see, by construction, is any species whose "introduction" is in fact a reintroduction at a deeper timescale.

"Non-native" carries the same assumption in a softer form. "Exotic" adds a faint aesthetic flavor, the suggestion that the animal is elsewhere's animal visiting. "Feral" contains an additional implication about the animal's relationship to human management, and typically marks an animal as out of place. All of these words are useful within their proper scope. All of them encode the 1492 baseline as a hidden premise.

The vocabulary produces one further effect worth flagging. A well-adjusted ecological assemblage (one that, under a 1492 baseline, is categorized as intact) can be assumed to be self-sustaining unless shown otherwise. A species classified as invasive can be assumed to be harmful unless shown otherwise. The burden of proof is asymmetric, and the asymmetry follows from the baseline. Reverse the baseline and the burden shifts. A species whose lineage was present on the continent for most of the last four million years does not become categorically benign by virtue of its lineage — there can be real ecological reasons to manage specific populations at specific densities — but the question of its presence stops being foreclosed by vocabulary. It becomes a question to argue.

This is, I think, the quieter cost of the Columbian Curtain. The baseline is not sitting in conservation textbooks as a debated proposition. It is sitting in the verbs and adjectives used to describe every animal on the continent, and most practitioners are using it without noticing.

## NEAR TIME

A paleoecologist looking at the continent in 1492 does not see a native baseline. They see a system very recently stripped of most of its large-mammal biomass, in the middle of a long readjustment that has not yet stabilized. The frame in which this is visible is what Ross MacPhee, in the 1999 edited volume that gave the idea its current name, calls *near time* — the last fifty thousand years or so of Earth history, the window during which most of the world's large-mammal extinctions have occurred and the present biota has taken its current shape.<sup>7</sup>

Near time is not a sentimental concept. It is the timescale on which the actual cast of the current ecological play was assembled, because most of the changes that produced the present continent happened within it. Plate tectonics and long-term climate shifts operate on vastly deeper scales. The specific animals and plants in any given ecosystem today, and the gaps in those ecosystems where animals used to be, settled into their current arrangement inside the near-time window. If we want to know what a continent was before humans arranged it into the present, near time is the window to look through.

MacPhee's framing also sets the stakes. The volume assembles case studies from every inhabited continent and major island group, and the synthesis across cases is unambiguous: the world has lost an extraordinary proportion of its large-bodied fauna within this window. Island losses are in some cases total. Continental losses are proportional but massive. Martin's own summary of the North American figure —more than forty large-mammal species extinct within near time, and thirty-five or so of them gone in the terminal Pleistocene pulse around thirteen thousand years ago — sits within this larger pattern rather than outside it. South America lost more than fifty genera. Australia lost nearly all of its megafauna tens of thousands of years earlier. The near-time extinctions are the single largest event shaping the modern ecological assemblages we inherit.<sup>8</sup>

The comparison to what was there is sobering in a way statistics alone cannot convey. Using the late-Pleistocene fossil record, paleontologists estimate that the mean body mass of large mammals in North America during the period immediately before the terminal extinctions was roughly comparable to that of the modern East African savanna.<sup>9</sup> The Great Plains supported proboscidean biomass. The southwestern basins supported camelid and equid biomass. The eastern woodlands supported an assemblage of ground sloths, peccaries, and tapirs whose nearest living analogues are neotropical. The continent Europeans encountered was a structurally different place from the continent that had existed twelve thousand years earlier, and the difference was not an improvement or a maturation or a natural development. It was a subtraction.

Near time is the window in which that subtraction happened. To take the 1492 baseline as "native" is to take the post-subtraction assemblage as the reference. It is to treat the end result of a mass extinction as the default state. The near-time frame does something different. It asks what the continent was like across the whole window in which the present system came together, and it lets the observer see the losses as losses rather than as the background against which all subsequent change is measured.

This is not a rewilding argument yet. It is a framing argument. The near-time window is the analytically honest timescale for thinking about what any given continent is now, because it is the window in which what it is now was assembled. Using any shorter window — five hundred years, two hundred years, the span of European land-use records — is not wrong, exactly, but it is structurally unable to see the shape of the assemblage's losses. A shorter window can tell us how a given forest has changed since the timber surveys of the 1870s. It cannot tell us what the forest is missing on a scale that matters to how the forest functions.

The near-time argument has a close cousin in fisheries science, and the parallel is worth drawing because it arrived at a similar insight from a different direction. In 1995 the fisheries biologist Daniel Pauly published a short essay in *Trends in Ecology & Evolution* naming what he called "shifting baseline syndrome."<sup>13</sup> Pauly had noticed that each generation of fisheries scientists tended to take the species composition and abundance of the oceans at the start of their own careers as the reference condition against which subsequent change was measured. A scientist trained in the 1970s considered the 1970s ocean normal and was alarmed by the changes since then. A scientist trained in the 1990s considered the 1990s ocean normal and did not find the reduced abundance alarming, since it was what they had always known. Each generation ratcheted the reference condition down. The cumulative effect, across several generations, was that the "natural" state of the oceans slid progressively further from anything the oceans had been before industrial fishing began.

Pauly's essay was a disciplinary turning point within fisheries biology. It did what this chapter is trying to do for terrestrial ecology: it named the assumption hiding in the reference condition. The syndrome Pauly described is not quite identical to the one the Columbian Curtain produces — Pauly's diagnosis involves a rolling baseline that shifts with each observer, while the Columbian baseline is fixed at a particular date — but the underlying structure is the same. Both are cases where the reference condition is a recent artifact, shaped by the observer's position in time, mistaken for a deep property of the system. Both can be corrected only by stepping back to a longer frame.

In the fisheries case, the longer frame looks to historical records — ship logs, cannery data, colonial-era fish markets, archaeological fish-bone middens — to reconstruct what abundance was before the baseline slipped. In the terrestrial case, the longer frame is paleontological: the fossil record, the dung caves, the bone beds, the radiocarbon chronologies. The data are different. The corrective move is the same. Find the frame the observer has inherited, locate the assumption it is making about where to start counting, and then look further back.

I find the parallel reassuring in the sense that it was arrived at independently by people working on an unrelated problem. The 1492 baseline on land and the rolling baseline at sea are not identical pathologies, but the cognitive mechanism behind both is the same, and the correction in both cases is the same.

## WHAT A BASELINE IS FOR

A baseline does three kinds of work in conservation. It tells us what to count as native. It tells us what to count as damage. And it tells us what to count as restoration. Change the baseline and all three categories rearrange.

Consider the first. Under the 1492 baseline, the native vertebrates of Arizona are the ones present when Coronado's expedition passed through in the 1540s — a set that includes pronghorn, mule deer, bighorn sheep, elk, a handful of smaller ungulates, and the surviving predators. Under a near-time baseline, the native vertebrates of Arizona include those animals *and* the camelids, equids, and proboscideans whose fossils are distributed across the state's late-Pleistocene sediments, some of which are well enough preserved to appear in local natural-history museums within an hour's drive of many of the state's largest school districts. The animals are the same animals either way. Whether they count as native to the state is entirely a function of which window the question is being asked across.

Consider the second. Damage, in an ecological assessment, is usually measured against a baseline — the reference condition the system has diverged from. Under the 1492 baseline, damage to the American shortgrass prairie is the conversion of grassland to cropland, the loss of bison, the encroachment of mesquite, the overgrazing of exhausted cattle regimes. All of those are real and serious. Under a near-time baseline, damage to the same prairie includes all of those *and* the absence of the proboscidean biomass that likely contributed to the system's historical openness, the absence of the American cheetah that shaped the pronghorn's running speed, the absence of the ground sloths that may have played a role in seed dispersal for some of the legumes still standing there now. The losses the Columbian Curtain hides do not cease to be losses because the curtain is hiding them. They simply cease to be counted.

Consider the third. Restoration, under the 1492 baseline, means returning a degraded landscape to something resembling its state at the time of European contact. It is the frame against which Park Service management plans, Forest Service stewardship documents, and most published habitat-restoration studies are written. Under a near-time baseline, restoration can still include all of the 1492 targets, but the target list expands: it includes restoring, where possible, the large-bodied animals and ecological relationships that were present before the terminal Pleistocene losses. The two frames do not necessarily conflict in practice on short time horizons —returning bison to the plains scores well under both frames, and reforesting cleared eastern hardwoods scores well under both — but they begin to diverge on the margin. Reintroducing American burros to public land scores as damage under the 1492 frame and as partial restoration under the near-time frame. Considering camels as proxies for the extinct American camelids of the Southwest is, under the 1492 frame, not even a conversation worth having; under the near-time frame, it is a hypothesis worth testing.
