The point is not that one frame is correct and the other is mistaken. The point is that both frames are choices, with different consequences, and the choice is not intellectually neutral. Conservation biology has, on the whole, chosen the 1492 baseline without examining the choice closely. The result is that a great many restoration proposals that would be coherent under a near-time baseline are ruled out before they can be evaluated. They are non-starters because of the frame, not because of the evidence.

I want to be precise about what I am claiming. I am not claiming that any restoration proposal that invokes the Pleistocene baseline is automatically a good one. I am not claiming that species introductions should be attempted casually. I am not claiming that conservation biologists who work under the 1492 baseline are foolish, or that the 1492 baseline produces worse outcomes than alternative frames in every case. Many of the people who have shaped the 1492 baseline into its current form are serious ecologists whose work I have learned from. The dominant paradigm is not dominant by accident.

What I am claiming is that the baseline is a choice, that the choice shapes the outcome, and that serious conservation thinking requires naming the baseline rather than treating it as a given. Once the baseline is named, it can be argued about. Until it is named, the argument is smuggled into every premise.

## WHAT IS ON THE OTHER SIDE

The prologue made the point through camels and an Osage orange orchard. I will not revisit either here, except to say that they are instances of a pattern, and the pattern is worth naming.

On the other side of the Columbian Curtain, the North American fauna is a different assemblage from the one Americans grew up knowing. The flatlands held proboscideans. The broken country of the Southwest held equids and camelids in numbers that, by modern standards, seem implausible. Eastern hardwoods held ground sloths — at least two genera of them, *Megalonyx* and *Eremotherium*, the smaller of which was roughly the size of an ox and the larger of which approached the size of a modern elephant. The canyons of the West held cats whose closest living relatives are African. The boreal north held mammoths until approximately 10,500 years before present in some refugial populations.<sup>10</sup>

The list, stated all at once, has a faintly unreal quality for most readers. It reads like a natural-history exhibit rather than an inventory. But the animals were not mythical. They left bones in amounts that were once, and in some places still are, routinely exposed by arroyo cutting after heavy rains. They left dung in caves dry enough to preserve it for ten thousand years.<sup>11</sup> They left tracks turned to stone. They left, in some cases, well-characterized genetic material. They were as concrete as any animal on the continent today. The only thing odd about them, from a modern standpoint, is that they are gone.

The recovery record is not speculative. It is grounded in physical evidence that can be held, measured, dated, and in many cases sequenced. Ground sloth coprolites from dry caves on the Colorado Plateau have yielded preserved plant material detailed enough to reconstruct the animals' browse preferences across thousands of years of occupation.<sup>14</sup> Mammoth material recovered from permafrost has supported nuclear genome sequencing in the same rough ballpark as the Thistle Creek horse. Tar-seep deposits at Rancho La Brea in southern California have produced preserved animal bodies by the tens of thousands, from dire wolves to saber-toothed cats to the American lion *Panthera atrox*, with radiocarbon-dated material spanning roughly fifty thousand years.<sup>15</sup> The late Pleistocene is not prehistory in the sense of being inaccessibly remote. It is prehistory in the sense that it predates writing. Much of what we know about it, we know at a resolution comparable to the resolution at which we know the historical Roman road network — from the ground, from what was left behind, from dating the physical evidence.

I am not going to make the rewilding case here. That case belongs to later chapters — to the chapter on what proxy species might plausibly be considered, to the chapter on the specific restoration work already under way, to the chapter that takes the critics of Pleistocene rewilding seriously and answers the criticisms I think can be answered. Here I only want the reader to hold, briefly, the shape of the other side.

The continent on the other side of the Curtain is not exotic. It is the continent the land itself still remembers. Grasses on the plains evolved under the hooves and digestive tracts of animals whose modern descendants are managed as livestock or warehoused on reservations or gone altogether. Soil communities in the Southwest evolved under trampling patterns that have not been produced in twelve thousand years. Predator-prey dynamics in the high canyons evolved around hunters whose bodies are in museum drawers. The landscape is not recovering from a bad decade. It is not recovering from European contact. It is, on the whole, still in the long thaw after a much larger subtraction.

The Columbian Curtain is the frame that makes this hard to see. Drop the frame and it becomes legible.

## NATIVE TO WHEN

I opened this chapter with the horse because the horse is the cleanest case for the argument, and because it is a case the evidence has already essentially made on its own. The horse of the Plains is both a sixteenth-century immigrant and a thirteen-thousand-year returnee. Under a 1492 baseline, it is the former. Under a near-time baseline, it is the latter. The only question is which window we are using to look.

The question the chapter lands on is not whether horses are native, or whether camels would be native if reintroduced, or whether burros belong in the Grand Canyon. Each of those is a specific question that requires its own specific argument. The question the chapter lands on is the one upstream of all of them, the question the 1492 baseline has silently answered without asking: *native to when?*

Every conservation decision rests on an implicit answer to this question. The answer is rarely stated, and it is often buried under vocabulary that presents the baseline as natural rather than chosen. Once the question is stated in the open, every subsequent decision becomes a live argument rather than a settled one. We can argue about the right window. We can argue about what to do about particular species under particular windows. We can argue about the risks of different proposals. What we cannot do, once the question is in the open, is pretend that conservation has been doing anything other than drawing a time-line through the ecological past and calling one side of it native.

I want to note one thing about how the argument will develop from here, because it matters for the register in which the rest of the book is written. The point of naming the Columbian Curtain is not to discredit the practitioners who have worked within it. A great deal of serious conservation biology has been done under a 1492 frame, and a great deal of that work has produced real results. Wetland restoration in coastal North America, the recovery of the bald eagle and the American alligator, the return of the gray wolf to parts of its historic range, the ongoing expansion of bison onto grassland that has not seen significant bison presence in over a century — all of these operate within the 1492 baseline and all of them represent genuine conservation gains. None of this chapter's argument dismisses any of that. The argument is additive, not subtractive. The near-time frame does not replace the shorter-window frames used in existing conservation work; it makes visible a set of additional considerations that the shorter windows cannot see. The chapters that follow ask what those additional considerations are, what we should do with them, and where they lead.

The baseline is a choice. It has always been a choice. The work of the rest of the book, in large part, is to argue that the choice should be made deliberately rather than inherited without examination, and that when it is made deliberately, the near-time window is the one most consistent with what we actually know about the ecosystems we inhabit. A baseline chosen deliberately is still a baseline; it will have its own exclusions and its own assumptions, and it will need to be defended with the same honesty required to defend any other analytical frame. The difference is that a deliberate baseline can be argued against, refined, and corrected. The inherited one, by design, resists each of those moves.

But the argument begins here. With the horse returning to its continent of origin after a long absence, and a culture that mostly has not noticed the return. With a baseline dressed as a fact. With a continent whose memory is older than its paperwork. Native to when is the question. Near time is, I think, the defensible answer.

The next chapter asks what was lost in that window, and when, and what we know and do not know about why.

# Chapter Three: Near Time

From Paul S. Martin, *Twilight of the Mammoths* (2005)

“A climatic catastrophe forced extinctions in Australia around 46,000 years ago, and in North and South America 10,000 to 12,000 radiocarbon years ago.” If so, “neither event registered in New Zealand.”

“I will lay aside impartiality. I think the overkill theorists have the more convincing argument for what happened in America 10,000 years ago.”

— P. S. Martin, chs. 7–8

Paul Martin came to the extinction question in the 1950s, at roughly the moment the question first became answerable. He was a young paleoecologist at the Desert Laboratory in Tucson — Arizona's field station for the study of arid-land ecology, perched on Tumamoc Hill above the city — and he had been trained in the generation for which the fossil record was a stratigraphic problem. Animals disappeared from the record at some point in the strata. The strata told you roughly when. What the strata could not tell you, at the resolution then available, was whether one animal disappeared at the same moment as another, or centuries earlier, or centuries later, or whether any of them disappeared at the same moment as anything else: a climatic event, a vegetation shift, the arrival of a new predator. The granularity was not fine enough to see coincidence at the scale where coincidence becomes causation.

Willard Libby changed that. Libby's radiocarbon technique, published in the late 1940s and refined through the 1950s, allowed organic material up to about fifty thousand years old to be dated at a resolution of centuries rather than millennia.<sup>1</sup> Martin later described what this did for the field in plain terms: it took a question that had been stratigraphic and made it chronological. Instead of asking which layer a bone came from, you could ask what year (within a century or two) the bone had lain down in its layer. For the first time, the timing of specific extinctions could be matched against the timing of specific other events. What the dating began to show, across the 1950s and 1960s, was that the timing of the great North American extinctions sat uncomfortably close to one specific other event, and the coincidence bothered Martin for the next half century.

The event was the arrival of humans on the continent.

## WHAT WAS LOST

The chapter has to begin with the inventory, because without the inventory the rest of the argument does not land. The prologue named a few of the animals as emblems. Chapter 2 gestured at the catalog without unfolding it. Here the catalog unfolds.

North America north of Mexico lost at least thirty-five genera of large mammals in the terminal Pleistocene pulse, concentrated in the few centuries around thirteen thousand calendar years before present.<sup>2</sup> That is a genus-level figure, not a species count; counted at the species level the losses are higher. Martin's own tabulation in *Twilight of the Mammoths* ran to more than forty North American species above forty-five kilograms of body mass, or roughly a hundred pounds (the threshold biologists often use to separate large-bodied mammals from medium-sized ones).<sup>3</sup> The loss on the proboscidean front alone involved both of the continent's mammoth species, the Columbian mammoth *Mammuthus columbi* and the woolly mammoth *Mammuthus primigenius*, together with the American mastodon *Mammut americanum*, a separate lineage with a range that extended through the eastern hardwoods. Three proboscideans, all gone in the same window. The closest parallel anywhere on Earth today would be to imagine Africa losing both species of elephant in the course of a human lifetime.

The ground sloths went with them. Martin counted at least four North American genera: *Megalonyx jeffersonii*, Jefferson's ground sloth, widely distributed through the east; *Nothrotheriops shastensis*, the Shasta ground sloth, familiar from the dung-dense caves of the Colorado Plateau; *Paramylodon harlani*, Harlan's ground sloth, common enough at La Brea to be an exhibit standard; and *Eremotherium laurillardi*, a massive southern genus that ranged north into the Gulf Coast and occasionally farther. Two of these animals (*Megalonyx* and *Eremotherium*) reached sizes comparable to modern elephants. The others were bear-sized. None of them is present in any modern ecosystem. Their closest living relatives are the two- and three-toed tree sloths of Central and South American rainforests, which weigh a few kilograms and hang in canopies that the extinct North American sloths would have flattened.

The equids went with them. The genus *Equus* had diversified across the continent for the entirety of its evolutionary history, as the previous chapter described. By the terminal Pleistocene pulse several species of native American horse were still in place (*Equus scotti*, *Equus conversidens*, *Equus lambei*, and others whose taxonomy paleontologists continue to revise), and every one of them disappeared in the same window. <sup>4</sup> The camelids went the same way. Martin described three North American genera going out together: *Camelops*, the Western camel of the prologue's concern; *Hemiauchenia*, the larger of the two native llamas; and *Palaeolama*, the smaller. The bovids and cervids took losses too, though not total ones; the woodland musk ox *Bootherium*, the stag moose *Cervalces*, and several pronghorn relatives *Capromeryx*, *Stockoceros*, and *Tetrameryx* all went out in the pulse.

The predator assemblage that had evolved around these herbivores collapsed with them. The American lion *Panthera atrox*, larger than any living cat, disappeared from a range that had extended from Alaska to central Mexico. The saber-toothed cat *Smilodon fatalis* and the scimitar-toothed cat *Homotherium* both went. The American cheetah *Miracinonyx*, whose pursuit pressure had shaped the pronghorn's still-extant running speed, was gone. The dire wolf *Aenocyon dirus* was gone. The short-faced bear *Arctodus simus*, the largest carnivorous land mammal of the North American Pleistocene and in some reconstructions of its ecology the most aggressive scavenger the continent has ever hosted, was gone.<sup>5</sup> Beyond the predators, the bear-sized giant beaver *Castoroides*, the capybara-like *Neochoerus*, Jefferson's extinct peccary lineages *Platygonus* and *Mylohyus* — on and on, and then the list stops.

The list does not stop because the subtraction tapered gently. It stops because the continent had little more above forty-five kilograms of body mass to lose. Counting what remained in the Holocene — bison, elk, moose, bighorn, mountain goat, pronghorn, the surviving deer, and the surviving carnivores of wolf and grizzly and cougar — the North American large-mammal fauna at the beginning of written history was roughly half of what it had been, by genus, a few thousand years earlier.<sup>6</sup> The same pattern, at varying magnitudes, appeared on every inhabited continent in near time. South America lost more than fifty genera, a greater proportional loss than North America; the glyptodonts, the car-sized armored herbivores *Doedicurus* and *Glyptodon*, the massive ground sloth *Megatherium*, the native ungulates *Macrauchenia* and *Toxodon*, and a range of sabertooths and short-faced bears all exited the continent's fauna in the same terminal Pleistocene window. Australia's losses were earlier, roughly forty-six to fifty thousand years ago by current chronology, and were nearly total for the continent's megafaunal assemblage: the rhinoceros-sized diprotodontids, the marsupial lion *Thylacoleo*, giant kangaroos, and the flightless bird *Genyornis* all disappeared. Eurasia lost the woolly mammoth, the woolly rhinoceros *Coelodonta antiquitatis*, the steppe bison *Bison priscus*, the giant deer *Megaloceros giganteus*, the cave bear *Ursus spelaeus*, and the cave hyena, each on its own regional chronology distributed across the late Pleistocene and into the early Holocene. Madagascar's losses came more recently still, within the last two thousand years: the giant lemurs, the elephant birds, and a dwarf hippopotamus disappeared after Malagasy colonization. Each continent's loss profile differed in timing and in which specific animals were involved. What each continent shared was the shape: disproportionate culling of the largest-bodied terrestrial fauna, with little parallel effect on small mammals, plants, marine species, or insects.<sup>7</sup>

This is the subtraction the previous chapter framed as the reason the 1492 baseline is an inadequate reference point. The chapter has not yet said why it happened. The literature has been trying to say why it happened for the seventy years since Martin first began trying, and the literature has not, on the whole, settled.

## THREE HYPOTHESES

Three explanations have done most of the work in the scholarly debate. They are not strictly mutually exclusive, and the current synthesis involves combinations of them, but it is easier to see the shape of the combination if the three are taken one at a time.

The first is overkill: the hypothesis that the extinctions were caused, primarily or substantially, by predation from newly arrived human hunters. Its most forceful articulation is the one Martin himself gave it, refined over four decades and summarized in *Twilight of the Mammoths* in 2005.

The second is climatic forcing: the hypothesis that the late-Quaternary climate oscillations, in particular the abrupt cooling known as the Younger Dryas around 12,800 calendar years before present, pushed species past thresholds their populations could not recover from. This was the dominant view among most vertebrate paleontologists before Martin's formulation entered the field, and it remains a serious position today, though the version that prevails now is more chronologically precise than the one Martin engaged with in the 1960s.

The third is cosmic impact: the hypothesis, developed in the peer-reviewed literature over the last two decades, that the Younger Dryas cooling itself was initiated or amplified by an airburst or impact event near the Laurentide ice sheet at approximately 12,900 calendar years before present. This is the Younger Dryas Impact Hypothesis, and it interacts with both of the other two: if the hypothesis is correct, the climatic mechanism has a proximate trigger it otherwise lacks, and the timing of the extinction pulse with respect to human arrival becomes a triple rather than a double coincidence.

Each of these is worth walking through in its strongest form, because each has been argued in its strongest form by serious researchers whose work deserves that level of presentation.

> **REVISION: The Overkill Hypothesis**
>
> **2011:** I was confident the Late Pleistocene extinctions were caused by humans arriving across the Bering land bridge. The overkill hypothesis, as laid out in Martin's earlier work, was the version of the story I believed. Human arrival, large mammals gone within a few thousand years, clean narrative, rewilding justified as a kind of debt repayment.
>
> **2017:** I began reading the paleoclimate work on the Younger Dryas and the challenges to the timing of human arrival. The fossil record did not line up as cleanly with the human signal as the overkill story required. At least some of the extinctions ran ahead of the first firm dates for human presence.
>
> **2024:** I am no longer confident in overkill as the single cause. I think humans were involved. I think climate was involved. I think the mixture varies by species and by region, and I am not sure it is knowable from the evidence we currently have.
>
> This matters for the book only in that the case for rewilding used to depend, in my thinking, on human responsibility. It no longer does. The case now rests on ecological function. The landscape is operating below capacity because animals that shaped it are missing. Why they are missing is a separate question, and the rewilding case holds either way.
>
> I changed my mind. Because I changed my mind, I considered other ideas and arguments against overkill's premise more seriously. It sharpened the thinking, and narrowed the arguments. The book is better for it.

## OVERKILL, AS MARTIN MADE IT

Martin did not invent the overkill hypothesis. Richard Foster Flint at Yale had floated human involvement earlier. Darwin's contemporary Richard Owen had proposed something similar for New Zealand's moas in the nineteenth century. What Martin did (working through the 1960s, 1970s, and 1980s, often with collaborators in simulation work, comparative island ecology, and radiocarbon calibration) was make the case in a form rigorous enough that climate-centric alternatives had to argue against a specific alternative rather than merely assert their own.<sup>8</sup>

The case, as Martin stated it in *Twilight of the Mammoths*, rests on three observations about near-time extinctions that climate-centric explanations struggle to accommodate.

The first is time-transgressiveness. Near-time megafaunal extinctions did not happen at the same moment around the world. Australia's pulse is roughly forty-six thousand years ago. Continental North America's is around twelve to thirteen thousand years ago. New Zealand's moa extinctions occurred less than a thousand years ago. Madagascar's losses date to within the last two thousand years. The Caribbean island sloths persisted into the middle Holocene and disappeared only after human colonization of the islands. What does fit each of these extinction events closely is the arrival of humans on the landmass in question. Climatic shifts of comparable magnitude, if they are invoked as the primary mechanism, have to be invoked at different times in different places, without a unifying global pattern of climate that matches the extinction pattern. "A climatic catastrophe forced extinctions in Australia around 46,000 years ago, and in North and South America 10,000 to 12,000 radiocarbon years ago," Martin wrote. If so, "neither event registered in New Zealand."<sup>9</sup> Time-transgressiveness is the signature the overkill hypothesis predicts and the climate hypothesis does not.

> **DEFINITION: Time-transgressive Extinction**
>
> A time-transgressive extinction is one that occurs at different times in different places: the same pattern recurring across landmasses, offset from each other by thousands or tens of thousands of years.

The near-time megafaunal losses are the paradigmatic example. Australia's pulse dates to roughly 46,000 years before present. Continental North America's centers on 12,000 to 13,000 years. New Zealand's moa extinctions occur less than 1,000 years ago. Madagascar's sit within the last 2,000. The pattern that unifies these events is not a single global climate shift, which would affect all landmasses simultaneously. It is the arrival of human populations, which occurred at different times on different continents.

Time-transgressiveness is the diagnostic signature by which paleoecology distinguishes human-driven from climate-driven extinction events, and it is the single observation the climate hypothesis has the hardest time accommodating.

The second is selectivity. Near-time losses fell almost exclusively on large-bodied terrestrial mammals. Trees and shrubs show essentially no contemporaneous extinction in the North American fossil record. Small mammals show essentially none. Marine mammals show essentially none. Beetles, birds outside flightless insular lineages, and other less-hunted groups show essentially none. The earlier Quaternary had repeatedly imposed severe climatic switches on the same flora and fauna without similar losses. If climate was the primary mechanism, its selectivity for mammals above forty-five kilograms (the threshold at which human hunting returns become energetically attractive) would be an extraordinary coincidence. Human predation, by contrast, predicts exactly that size cutoff. Large animals reproduce slowly, track easily, and yield high energetic returns per successful hunt. They are the animals a novel predator would exhaust first.

The third is the uniqueness problem. Climatic changes as dramatic as the Allerød–Younger Dryas switch had occurred repeatedly during the Quaternary. Greenland ice cores record dozens of them. None of the earlier switches, over hundreds of thousands of years, are associated with comparable megafaunal die-offs. For a climatic explanation of the near-time losses to be adequate, the climate shift at the end of the Pleistocene had to be uniquely severe. The ice-core evidence does not obviously support that uniqueness.<sup>10</sup>

Martin paired the three criteria with a quantitative demonstration. In 1975, Martin and the statistician James Mosimann published a simulation showing that a founding human population of a few thousand, advancing at plausible rates through naive megafaunal prey, could in principle extinguish the continent's large-bodied mammal fauna in three to five hundred years. The simulation was sensitive to assumptions (population growth rate, hunting success, prey density, the rate of population spread), but its central finding was robust across a wide band of those assumptions. The claim was not that the simulation proved overkill had happened; it was that a critic could no longer reject overkill on the grounds that a small human population could not plausibly cause continental extinction. A small human population could. That was the point the simulation made, and its significance for the debate was that it shifted the burden of argument. Afterward, the climate camp had to explain why something that was computationally plausible had not, in fact, happened.<sup>8</sup> Martin's later articulation of a "blitzkrieg" model — a rapid wave of human advance through an ecologically unprepared fauna — is the popular form of the same argument, and it remains the most vivid framing of the overkill case.

Martin did not claim the overkill hypothesis was complete. He did claim —openly, and with the pugnacity the climate-change camp sometimes found tiresome — that it was the hypothesis most consistent with the global pattern, and that the climate-change camp had a harder time explaining the pattern than they usually admitted. "I will lay aside impartiality," he wrote in the opening of the chapter of *Twilight* that takes the debate head on. "I think the overkill theorists have the more convincing argument for what happened in America 10,000 years ago."<sup>11</sup>

It is important to hold the integrity of this argument in view. Martin was not a polemicist. He was a paleoecologist who had spent half a century on the evidence and had reached a considered conclusion. The hypothesis carried his name, fairly, because he had done more than any other researcher in his generation to put it on firm empirical footing. Anyone who wishes to retire the overkill hypothesis, as many working in the field now effectively have, owes Martin the courtesy of retiring it for reasons the evidence supplies, not by gesturing vaguely at discomfort with its implications.

## CLIMATE, RECONSIDERED

The climate-change hypothesis, as Martin encountered it in the 1950s, was the inherited default position of vertebrate paleontology. Most vertebrate extinctions in the Cenozoic fossil record had been accounted for by climate change, often in the absence of any more specific mechanism. Applying the same default to the near-time losses was, from inside the discipline, the conservative move.

Martin's three-criterion critique — time-transgressiveness, selectivity, uniqueness — landed hard on the default. The default response, in the decades since, has been to refine the climate mechanism rather than abandon it. Current climate-centric accounts do not usually claim that a single global cooling event extinguished the world's megafauna in one stroke. They claim, more modestly, that rapid regional shifts in temperature, precipitation, vegetation structure, and seasonality interacted with the life-history traits of particular species to push specific populations below recovery thresholds in specific places. Under this account, the climate mechanism is local rather than global, continuous rather than catastrophic, and cumulative rather than discrete.<sup>12</sup>

The Younger Dryas cooling (an abrupt return to near-glacial conditions in the northern hemisphere around 12,800 calendar years before present, lasting roughly 1,300 years) does receive particular weight in these accounts for continental North America. The timing of the Younger Dryas onset aligns closely with the terminal Pleistocene extinction pulse in North America. The severity of the climate shift was substantial, involving rapid drops in North Atlantic surface temperatures and significant reorganization of precipitation patterns across the continent. The leading proximate mechanism in the paleoclimate literature is a freshwater pulse from the catastrophic drainage of glacial Lake Agassiz (an enormous proglacial lake along the retreating margin of the Laurentide ice sheet) whose outflow into the North Atlantic is thought to have disrupted the thermohaline circulation and triggered the cooling. The details of that mechanism, including the precise routing of the meltwater, continue to be actively argued in the literature; what is not argued is that the cooling was abrupt and that its continental consequences were substantial.<sup>13</sup> Whether the shift was sharp enough to drive extinction of animals that had weathered dozens of comparable earlier shifts remains the unresolved point. Ice-core records from Greenland do show the Younger Dryas clearly; they also show earlier oscillations of comparable magnitude. The selectivity problem Martin identified — why this oscillation rather than the last twelve — continues to cut.

What the climate camp has credibly retrieved from the Martin critique is that *regional* climate dynamics matter. The loss of mammoth populations in the Siberian arctic, where some refugial mammoths persisted on Wrangel Island until roughly four thousand years ago and on St. Paul Island until roughly five thousand six hundred years ago, is difficult to explain by human pressure alone; the chronology shows that these populations outlasted terminal Pleistocene conditions by thousands of years, then disappeared under what appear to be largely climatic or habitat-fragmentation stresses.<sup>14</sup> What the climate camp has not credibly retrieved is the claim that a single, unique, catastrophic climatic event explains the synchronous disappearance of an entire large-bodied mammal fauna. That stronger claim runs into Martin's critique as soon as it is made globally.

## THE YOUNGER DRYAS IMPACT HYPOTHESIS

A third candidate entered the peer-reviewed literature in 2007. Richard Firestone and colleagues, in a paper in the *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences*, proposed that an extraterrestrial impact event at approximately 12,900 calendar years before present (a comet or fragmenting bolide, most likely an airburst over or near the retreating Laurentide ice sheet) had initiated the Younger Dryas cooling and contributed to the megafaunal extinctions. The authors identified a dark sedimentary layer, which they called the "black mat," at archaeological and paleontological sites across North America, dating to the Younger Dryas boundary. The black mat contained elevated concentrations of magnetic microspherules, carbon spherules, nanodiamonds, iridium, and other markers they interpreted as consistent with an impact signature.<sup>15</sup>

The paper was controversial on publication and has remained so. Critics have challenged the claimed peak concentrations, the interpretation of the spherule and nanodiamond markers, the statistical robustness of the chronology, and the plausibility of the proposed mechanism. Several rigorous critical reviews have argued that the evidence for the impact is insufficient to support the strong causal claim, and that alternative explanations for some of the proposed markers (cosmic dust background, wildfire signatures, biological spherules) have not been adequately ruled out.<sup>16</sup>

Two subsequent papers extended the physical case. In 2013, James Wittke and colleagues reported evidence for deposition of approximately ten million tonnes of impact spherules across roughly fifty million square kilometers on four continents, centered on the Younger Dryas boundary layer.<sup>17</sup> In 2015, James Kennett and colleagues applied Bayesian chronological analysis to the black-mat horizons at twenty-three sites across four continents and reported age ranges consistent with synchronous deposition at 12,835 to 12,735 calendar years before present.<sup>18</sup> The synchrony result does not by itself prove an impact — a rapid globally distributed sedimentary horizon can be produced by processes other than impact — but it is a feature of the record the hypothesis predicts and alternatives have to account for.

The hypothesis remains contested. It is not the consensus position in the paleoclimate or paleontological literature. It is, however, a peer-reviewed hypothesis that has accumulated a non-trivial evidentiary body over the last two decades, and it is the correct place for a serious discussion of catastrophe at the Younger Dryas boundary to take place. Any reader interested in the question of what happened at 12,900 BP should read Firestone 2007, Wittke 2013, and Kennett 2015 in the original, along with the major critiques of each, and form their own judgment.

I want to state clearly what this book's engagement with the hypothesis is and is not. This book engages the Younger Dryas Impact Hypothesis through the peer-reviewed literature only. It does not engage catastrophist popularizations of the hypothesis circulating in trade publishing or documentary media, which have tended to detach specific claims from the evidentiary context in which the peer-reviewed work has made them. The distinction matters. The peer-reviewed work is a serious scientific hypothesis, contested but defensible; the popular accounts are a separate cultural artifact and do not carry over.

## THE STRONGEST CURRENT CASE FOR HUMAN CAUSATION

While the impact hypothesis was accumulating evidence, a distinct body of work was re-examining the human-causation case at global scale with tools Martin did not have. In 2014, Christopher Sandom, Søren Faurby, Brody Sandel, and Jens-Christian Svenning published a paper in *Proceedings of the Royal Society B* that applied spatially explicit global analysis to the late-Quaternary extinctions, testing the relative contributions of climate change and human arrival as predictors of extinction severity. The analysis used extinction data for roughly 178 mammal species across all ice-free continents, paired against reconstructed climate change metrics for the period 132,000 to 1,000 years before present and against hominin colonization chronology for each region.

The result was unambiguous. Extinction severity was strongly and consistently predicted by hominin arrival; it was not consistently predicted by climate change. The authors wrote that their findings "provide empirical support for prehistoric humans being the main driver of the Late Quaternary Megafauna Extinction."<sup>19</sup>

The Sandom paper is, in my reading, the strongest current empirical case for human causation as the primary driver. It is not Martin's argument — Martin had made the comparable point at regional scale with the tools available to him in 2005 — but it is Martin's argument extended with global data and statistical methods that did not exist when Martin was writing. Anyone attached to the overkill hypothesis has a stronger set of references to cite in 2026 than they did in 2006. The hypothesis has aged, but it has also sharpened.

What Sandom and colleagues did not claim is that climate played no role. Their result was about the *relative* contributions. Humans appeared to be the dominant driver; climate appeared to modulate the outcome in specific regions and for specific species. That is a multifactorial finding dressed as a human-cause headline, and it is best read that way.

## TOWARD A MULTIFACTORIAL SYNTHESIS

Anthony Stuart, in a 2015 synthesis in *Geological Journal*, drew the continent-by-continent picture the last decade of work had assembled.<sup>20</sup> Stuart's synthesis does not crown a winner. It argues, across Eurasia, North America, South America, and Australia, that the near-time extinction pattern is best explained by the interaction of climate change and human pressure, with the relative weight of each varying by region, by species, and by the phase of the late Quaternary under examination.

In some regions, the climate contribution appears to have been substantial. The mammoths that persisted on Siberian refugia well past the terminal Pleistocene pulse and then disappeared in the middle Holocene were not, as far as the archaeological record shows, subjected to sustained human hunting pressure in a way that would explain their final extinction; habitat fragmentation and climate-driven vegetation change are the more plausible proximate causes. In other regions, the human contribution appears to have been decisive. The New Zealand moa extinctions within a few centuries of Polynesian arrival cannot be accounted for by climate change; there was no substantial climate change to account for. Most of the world's near-time extinctions sit between these cases, with both factors present and the relative contributions unresolved.

Stuart's synthesis is not a retreat into vagueness. It is a recognition that the question "what caused the near-time megafaunal extinctions" is itself a shorthand for a family of more specific questions, and the specific answers differ. What caused the loss of *Mammuthus columbi* in Arizona is not necessarily what caused the loss of *Mammuthus primigenius* on Wrangel Island five thousand years later. The synthesis allows the specific answers to be specific.

Notice what this does to the larger interpretive framework. If the causation is multifactorial, with climate and human pressure interacting differently in different places and for different species, the clean story of either a single human culprit or a single climatic catastrophe is replaced by a more layered account. The layered account is less satisfying as a headline. It is probably closer to what actually happened.

## WHERE I NOW STAND

I have held more than one position on this material. I read *Twilight of the Mammoths* in 2012, shortly after finishing the first version of this book, and was persuaded quickly. For a long stretch afterward, into the essays I published in the middle of the 2010s, I wrote as though the overkill hypothesis was effectively settled, and as though the rewilding case carried an ethical weight drawn from collective human responsibility for the extinctions. My 2015 and 2016 writing, which I have since retired, states the human-causation position flatly. I now think that framing was too confident.

What I missed at the time was exactly the material the present chapter has walked through: the peer-reviewed work on the Younger Dryas oscillation and its mechanisms, the Firestone and Wittke and Kennett papers on the impact hypothesis, the Stuart synthesis, and the global statistical work (including Sandom 2014) that has made the human-causation case more defensible but also more bounded than Martin's original formulation was. Reading across the whole corpus, I am now not confident that the extinctions had a single dominant cause. I am confident they were heavily human-involved in most regions. I am also confident that climate oscillation, potentially triggered or amplified by the impact event the Firestone hypothesis describes, contributed materially in at least some regions and at least for some species.

This revision is not a repudiation of Martin. Martin's critique of mid-century climate-centric accounts was correct on the terms in which those accounts were being made. The subsequent refinement of the climate side of the debate has partly answered Martin's critique, and the subsequent accumulation of impact-hypothesis evidence has supplied a mechanism Martin did not have available to consider. The overkill hypothesis in its strong 2005 form is not quite where the field now stands. The overkill hypothesis in a weaker, multifactor-inflected form is, I think, the best reading of the current evidence.

I want to be explicit about what changes when one moves from a strong-overkill view to a multifactorial view, because the change is not merely scholarly. It alters the moral inflection of the rewilding case. The strong-overkill framing carries with it, often implicitly, an accounting of debt: humans caused the extinctions, humans ought to consider repair. The multifactorial framing does not carry that accounting. Humans may or may not have caused the extinctions — in any given region, in any given case, with any given confidence — and the case for restoration stands or falls on something other than collective human guilt. I think the case stands. I think it stands more cleanly when it is not entangled with an attribution question the evidence does not cleanly resolve.

It is worth naming, plainly, that I arrived at the strong-overkill position by reading Martin first and reading the challenges to Martin much later. That is a reasonably common trajectory for readers drawn to the rewilding argument, and I am not the only author to have walked it. The corrective, when it came, came from reading the Quaternary climate literature carefully, from taking the impact hypothesis seriously as a peer-reviewed proposition rather than dismissing it because of what had been made of it downstream, and from the simple act of sitting with Stuart's synthesis long enough to notice that it was not a split-the-difference move but a closer reading of the actual evidence than either pole had offered. A revised position of this kind is not a loss of confidence in the subject. It is a gain of confidence in the shape of what is known and what is not.

## WHAT THE REWILDING CASE ACTUALLY DEPENDS ON

The argument the rest of the book is going to make rests on an ecological observation, not a historical one.

The observation is that an ecosystem missing its keystone large-bodied animals functions differently than one in which those animals are present, and functions worse by a number of measurable metrics: seed dispersal, nutrient cycling, vegetation structure, predator-prey regulation, fire regime, soil compaction and turnover. The observation is independent of why the animals are absent. The continent is without mammoths, ground sloths, and native equids for whatever reason. The reason does not change the ecological consequence. A grassland that evolved under proboscidean herbivory is poorer in structure and function without proboscidean herbivory, whether the proboscideans were hunted to extinction, cooled to extinction, or died in some combination of the two. A fruit evolved for a gomphothere is abandoned by its disperser regardless of the cause of the gomphothere's disappearance. A predator-prey calibration between cheetah and pronghorn is one-sided whether the cheetah was speared by a Clovis hunter or starved in a Younger Dryas winter.

This is the chapter's load-bearing point, and it is where the chapter lands. Near time is the window in which the present ecological assemblage was stripped of much of its large-bodied biomass. The stripping was probably multifactorial. Apportioning blame is a worthy scientific question that the literature will continue to refine. The decision of whether to attempt restoration does not depend on the blame question's resolution. It depends on whether we take the observation seriously that the stripped system is still, twelve thousand years later, functioning as a stripped system —incompletely, with unfilled niches, with species waiting on partners that are not coming back on their own.

The continents that lost their megafauna have not recovered the function those animals performed. No continent has. The fossil fruits still fall, the grasslands still miss their grazers, the pronghorn still runs from nothing. Whatever the cause of the missing animals was, the absences are still here, and they are still absences. The next part of this book asks what, knowing that, we should do.

# Chapter Four: The Fourth Axis

> *From Tendall et al., Global Food Security (2015)*
>
> *Food system resilience: “the capacity over time of a food system and its units at multiple levels, to provide sufficient, appropriate and accessible food to all,*
>
> *in the face of various and even unforeseen disturbances.”*
>
> *The concept, they note, remains underspecified in most applied assessment*
>
> *frameworks.*
>
> *— Tendall, Joerin, Kopainsky, et al.*

Picture a poultry operation in the Piedmont of Virginia: five hundred acres, rotationally grazed, solar panels on the barn roof, organic certification on the feed. The birds live outdoors in mobile shelters moved daily across perennial pasture. The soil organic matter has climbed two percentage points in a decade. The operation employs eight people from the surrounding county, pays above the local median, and sells direct to consumers within a hundred-mile radius. A journalist doing a sustainability profile would find everything in order. The economic numbers work at the operator's scale. The social indicators — employment, community retention, wage levels — are positive. The environmental metrics are strong: no synthetic pesticide runoff, measurable soil carbon gains, arthropod and avian biodiversity substantially above the row-crop fields across the road. By any standard assessment of sustainability, this is a model operation. It would score well on every axis the standard framework knows how to measure.

Now remove one thing. Not a catastrophe. Just one input. Suppose the commodity corn supply that feeds the birds becomes unavailable. Not forever. For one season. A drought in the Midwest reduces the corn harvest. Grain futures spike. The regional feed mill that supplies the operation cannot fill its orders at a price the farm can absorb without selling birds at a loss. The operation has no on-farm grain production. Its acreage is in perennial pasture, which is why the soil numbers are so good. The birds cannot eat grass. They are monogastrics. They require concentrated energy and protein, and the concentrated energy and protein come from somewhere else, and the somewhere else has, for one season, failed to deliver.

The operation does not adapt. It does not pivot to an alternative feed source, because at the volumes required there is no alternative feed source available at a survivable price on the timeline the birds' metabolisms demand. It does not switch to a different species, because its infrastructure, its processing relationships, its customer base, and its brand are built around poultry. It does what an operation in this position does: it reduces the flock, absorbs the financial loss, and waits for the commodity market to restabilize. If the disruption lasts one season, the operation survives diminished. If it lasts two, the operation is in financial distress. If it lasts three, the operation that scored so well on every other measure of sustainability is gone.

This is not a hypothetical constructed for rhetorical convenience. Versions of it have played out across the pastured-poultry sector in every year the corn market has spiked significantly: in 2012, when Midwest drought drove corn futures above eight dollars a bushel for the first time; in 2022, when the disruption of Ukrainian grain exports compounded existing supply-chain stress to push US feed costs to levels that several direct-market poultry operations, in interviews and trade-press accounts, described as existential.<sup>1</sup> The operations that survived those spikes survived them by drawing on reserves, raising prices past what some of their customers would pay, or reducing production. They did not survive them by being sustainable in the sense the word is usually used. They survived them by having enough financial margin to absorb a shock the system had not been designed to withstand.

The gap the example is meant to illustrate is not a gap in the operation. It is a gap in the framework we use to evaluate the operation. The three-axis sustainability model — economic, social, environmental — measured the operation and found it sound. What the model did not measure, because the model was not built to measure it, is what happens to the operation when one of its underlying conditions changes. The model assessed the system's performance under current conditions. It did not ask what the system requires to keep performing, or what happens when those requirements are not met. That question — the question of what the system is riding on and whether the ride continues — is the question this chapter exists to introduce.

> **FIELD NOTE: The Zombie Apocalypse Litmus Test**
>
> The question this chapter is built around — what does a food system require to keep functioning, and what happens when those requirements stop being met —is older than any framework I have built around it. The first time I asked it was while watching an episode of *The Walking Dead*.
>
> The show was set in a world where the supply chains had collapsed. The episode involved a farm. Hershel — the older farmer character with the beard — had a flock of chickens, and the show had taken the trouble to mention what he was feeding them. Corn and soy. Industrial poultry feed. It dawned on me: farmer Hershel is in trouble. There was no commodity grain in his world anymore. No gasoline-powered transport. His chickens were on a clock the show had not yet noticed.
>
> I went home and started thinking about what a sustainable food system would have to look like if you removed the assumption that the inputs would keep arriving. Most of the systems I had been calling sustainable did not survive that test. They were *fragile*.
>
> I gave the test a name. In the previous version of this manuscript I called it the zombie apocalypse litmus test of sustainability.

I have a different name for it now — survivability — and a more careful (and serious) framework around it. But the question is the same question. I just asked it for the first time on a couch while watching a fictional farmer feed his chickens industrial feed.

Imagine being this invested in a plot hole in a TV show. Yet, here we are.

> **DEFINITION: Fragile, Robust, Antifragile**
>
> Nassim Taleb's 2012 taxonomy names three distinct responses a system can have to volatility.

A *fragile* system performs well under stable conditions and breaks under stress: concentrated supply chains, high-debt financial instruments, monocultures. A *robust* system withstands stress without breaking but does not improve from it: a well-engineered bridge, a conservative portfolio. An *antifragile* system gains from disorder, strengthened rather than merely preserved by exposure to shock: evolutionary populations under selection pressure, decentralized networks that route around failures, certain biological immune systems.

Most industrial food systems are fragile in Taleb's sense, optimized for efficiency under stable conditions and brittle under the conditions we can now see coming. The survivability axis measures this property directly, and it is the dimension most twentieth-century food-system assessment simply omitted.

## THE THREE AXES AND THEIR ASSUMPTIONS

The framework that most sustainability assessments operate within was not handed down as a finished schema. It accumulated. Its current shape — the familiar triad of economic viability, social equity, and environmental integrity — crystallized over roughly two decades of international development and environmental policy conversation, from the Brundtland Commission's 1987 report through the 1992 Rio Earth Summit and into the early-2000s proliferation of corporate sustainability reporting, triple-bottom-line accounting, and the institutional architecture that now supports the field.<sup>2</sup>

The Brundtland Commission's formulation is worth returning to, because the formulation contains the assumption. The commission defined sustainable development as "development that meets the needs of the present without compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own needs."<sup>3</sup> The definition is elegant, widely cited, and quietly dependent on a premise it does not examine: that the conditions under which "needs" are being met remain roughly stable over time. The future generations in the Brundtland formulation are imagined as living under conditions recognizably continuous with the present, different in degree, perhaps, but not in kind. The framework asks whether a given system can keep going. It does not ask what happens if the ground the system stands on shifts.

This is not a criticism of the Brundtland Commission or of the researchers and policymakers who built the three-axis framework on its foundation. The framework was built during a period when the premise of continuity was reasonable, or at least more reasonable than it has since become. The late twentieth century was, by historical standards, a period of unusual stability in the global systems that food production depends on: fossil energy was cheap and reliably available, international commodity trade was expanding under relatively stable geopolitical arrangements, the Green Revolution's yield gains were still compounding, and climate variability, while present, had not yet produced the frequency and severity of disruption that the early twenty-first century has delivered. The framework was built for the world it was built in, and in that world its omission was less visible.

The three axes, as they came to be operationalized in sustainability assessment, each do real work. The economic axis asks whether the system generates sufficient returns to persist as an economic entity: whether the farm stays solvent, the business model holds, the value chain from producer to consumer functions at each link. The social axis asks whether the system's benefits and costs are distributed in ways that are equitable or at least defensible: whether workers are treated fairly, whether communities bear disproportionate externalities, whether access to the system's outputs is broadly available. The environmental axis asks whether the system's ecological footprint is within bounds that the surrounding ecology can absorb: whether soil is being built or depleted, whether water is being contaminated or conserved, whether biodiversity is being maintained or eroded, whether greenhouse gas emissions are being managed.

Each of these axes captures something real about a food system's relationship to the world it operates in. The scholarship behind each axis is extensive, the metrics have been refined over decades, and the institutional infrastructure for measuring them (from life-cycle assessment protocols to social-impact auditing to the environmental management standards codified in the ISO 14000 series) is among the most developed bodies of applied assessment methodology in existence.<sup>4</sup> The framework is not casual. It is not superficial. It is, within its scope, serious and well built.

Consider what the framework produces in practice. Imagine a mid-sized organic dairy cooperative commissioning a sustainability assessment under the Global Reporting Initiative standards, one of the most widely adopted three-axis reporting frameworks in use.<sup>5</sup> A competent assessment of this kind will document performance across all three axes: the cooperative's member farms are economically viable under the prevailing organic price structure, the social metrics show fair labor practices and positive community economic impact, and the environmental indicators — soil organic matter trends, water quality in adjacent watersheds, biodiversity surveys on member farms — are favorable. A reader encountering the report would conclude, reasonably, that the operation is sustainable. What the report does not document (because the framework it is built on does not ask) is that the cooperative's economic viability depends entirely on the organic price premium, which depends in turn on consumer willingness to pay a substantial markup over conventional milk, which depends in turn on household disposable income remaining stable. The organic dairy sector's recurring price-premium compressions (documented in USDA Economic Research Service reporting on organic milk markets across multiple years of the past two decades) have repeatedly produced the pattern in which farms that scored well on three-axis assessments exited production when the premium narrowed.<sup>6</sup> The three-axis assessment finds the system sustainable. The system's dependency on a single economic condition (the premium) is not part of the assessment, because the framework does not measure dependencies. It measures performance.

The scope, however, has a boundary, and the boundary is the one the Brundtland premise draws. All three axes describe a system's performance under a given set of operating conditions. The economic axis asks whether the system is profitable *given current input costs, current market structures, current policy arrangements.* The social axis asks whether the system distributes its effects equitably *given current labor markets, current community structures, current regulatory environments.* The environmental axis asks whether the system's ecological footprint is sustainable *given current climate patterns, current hydrological regimes, current soil conditions.* Each axis takes the operating conditions as given and evaluates the system's behavior within them. None of the three asks what happens when the operating conditions themselves change.

This is not an esoteric distinction. It is the distinction between asking "is this building structurally sound?" and asking "is this building structurally sound in an earthquake?" The first question is answerable with one kind of engineering assessment. The second requires a different kind, because it asks about behavior under conditions the building was not specifically designed for. Both questions matter. The first tells you whether the building will stand on a calm day. The second tells you something about what the building is made of: what it depends on, where its tolerances are, what breaks first. A sustainability framework that answers only the first question tells you about calm days. It does not tell you about earthquakes. And the 2020s have been, for food systems, a decade of earthquakes.

## THE DECADE THAT MADE THE GAP VISIBLE

The assumption of stable operating conditions did not break all at once. It frayed across a series of events that, taken individually, could each be absorbed as an anomaly, and taken together, constituted a pattern that the three-axis framework had no native vocabulary to describe.

The COVID-19 pandemic, beginning in early 2020, exposed the fragility of just-in-time supply chains across the US food system in a matter of weeks. Meat processing — concentrated into a small number of very large facilities, each handling volumes that made the facility a single point of failure for its region — shut down or dramatically reduced throughput as infection spread through workforces.<sup>7</sup> The result was not a gradual adjustment. It was a simultaneous surplus of live animals on farms and shortage of meat in retail outlets, a condition that the supply chain's efficiency had made possible by eliminating the redundancy that would have buffered the shock. At the same time, direct-to-consumer vegetable operations that had built their distribution around restaurant sales and farmers' market attendance lost both channels simultaneously and had to rebuild their customer-facing infrastructure on the fly. The pandemic did not discriminate between conventional and alternative food systems. It discriminated between systems with slack and systems without it, and the decades-long optimization of the US food system for efficiency over redundancy meant that most systems, of whatever kind, had very little slack.

The Texas winter storm of February 2021 added a different lesson. Winter Storm Uri dropped temperatures across the Texas grid to levels that the state's energy infrastructure had not been designed for, producing cascading power failures that lasted days in some areas. Agricultural impacts were immediate and severe: an estimated $600 million in crop losses across the Rio Grande Valley citrus industry, widespread livestock losses in operations without backup power for water and heating systems, and disruption to the cold-chain infrastructure on which the state's export-oriented produce industry depends.<sup>8</sup> The event was not a food-system crisis in the classic sense. It was an energy-system crisis that became a food-system crisis, because the food system's dependence on continuous energy had not been tested against a disruption of this magnitude. The three-axis framework, applied to the Texas agricultural sector before the storm, would have found operations scoring well on economic, social, and environmental metrics. The storm did not change any of those scores. It revealed that the scores were contingent on a condition (reliable grid power) that the scoring had taken as given.

The disruption of Ukrainian grain exports following Russia's invasion in February 2022 made the dependency structure of global commodity agriculture visible at a scale that domestic disruptions had not. Ukraine and Russia together accounted for roughly twenty-eight percent of global wheat exports and a significant share of corn and barley before the invasion. The blockade of Black Sea ports and the destruction of Ukrainian agricultural infrastructure removed a substantial fraction of global grain supply from the market in a matter of months.<sup>9</sup> The price effects rippled through every system that purchased commodity grain as an input. In the United States, feed costs for livestock operations (including the pastured-poultry operations whose vulnerability the opening example described) rose sharply. In North Africa and the Middle East, where dependence on Ukrainian and Russian wheat imports was highest, the price spikes contributed to food-security crises that the affected governments had limited capacity to buffer. The event did not cause a global famine. It demonstrated that the global commodity system on which most food production depends is a single interconnected substrate, and that a disruption at one node propagates to every node that draws on the same supply.

Climate volatility has compounded the pattern. The 2012 US drought — still, at the time of writing, among the most severe and extensive single-year droughts in the modern record of the US Corn Belt — reduced the national corn yield by twenty-seven percent and pushed commodity prices to levels that imposed real financial stress on every operation in the country that purchased corn as a feed input.<sup>10</sup> The drought was not unprecedented in a climatic sense; comparable events appear in the paleoclimate record and in the instrumental record of the twentieth century. What was new was the degree to which the US food system had been optimized around the assumption of a reliable corn harvest. The system had removed the buffers — the stored reserves, the regional crop diversity, the on-farm feed production — that would have absorbed the shock in an earlier era. The three-axis framework, evaluating the system before the drought, would not have flagged this removal as a sustainability concern. The removal improved efficiency. Efficiency, under stable conditions, looks like a strength. Under unstable conditions, it is the mechanism of transmission.

I do not want to make the 2020s sound unique in kind, because disruptions to food systems are not new. What is new — or newly visible — is the frequency and the compounding. Droughts, energy failures, pandemics, and geopolitical disruptions have always occurred. What has changed is that the food system's optimization for efficiency under stable conditions has simultaneously increased its vulnerability to disruption and reduced its capacity to absorb disruption when it occurs. The system has become better at performing under the conditions it was designed for and worse at surviving conditions it was not designed for. The three-axis framework captures the first quality. It does not capture the second.

The compounding is worth pausing on, because it is the feature that separates the current moment from earlier disruption episodes. In 2022, a pastured-poultry operator in the mid-Atlantic faced not one of these disruptions but several simultaneously: feed costs elevated by the Ukrainian grain-export disruption, diesel costs elevated by the same geopolitical event, labor costs elevated by the lingering effects of pandemic-era workforce contraction, and a customer base whose own purchasing power had been reduced by the inflationary environment all of these inputs were feeding. No single disruption was necessarily fatal. The combination was, for some operations, unmanageable. The system's dependency profile was not a single thread that could snap; it was a web of threads all under tension at the same time, and the three-axis framework had no mechanism for registering the difference between a system with one vulnerability and a system with five vulnerabilities compounding simultaneously.

This is the gap the opening example was designed to make tangible, and it is the gap this chapter proposes to name.

## THE FOURTH AXIS: SURVIVABILITY

The framework I want to introduce adds a fourth axis to the three the sustainability literature already uses. I call it survivability, because the word captures precisely what the axis measures: not whether a system functions under current conditions, but whether the system survives when conditions change.

The definition is worth stating with some care, because the precision matters for everything the framework is used for in the chapters that follow.

Survivability, as I use the term, asks two linked questions about any food system under evaluation. First: what does this system require to keep functioning? The requirements include inputs (energy, water, feed, fertilizer, seed, labor, capital, infrastructure), market conditions (commodity prices, consumer demand, distribution networks), policy conditions (subsidies, regulations, trade agreements), and ecological conditions (climate stability, soil fertility, hydrological patterns, pollinator populations). Every food system has a dependency profile: a set of things it needs from outside itself in order to continue operating. The first question maps that profile.

Second: what happens when one or more of those requirements is not met? This is the stress question, and it is the question the three-axis framework does not ask. A system whose dependency profile is narrow (one that requires only conditions the system itself produces or that are reliably available independent of any single external supply chain) scores high on survivability. A system whose dependency profile is broad — one that requires continuous diesel, continuous commodity feed, continuous federal subsidies, continuous just-in-time logistics, continuous stable climate — scores low on survivability, regardless of how well it performs on the other three axes when all of those requirements are being met.

The axis is not binary. It is a continuum, and the continuum has a direction. A system that requires fewer external inputs and can tolerate the loss of any one input without collapsing is more survivable than a system that requires many external inputs and collapses when any one of them is removed. A system that degrades gracefully under stress (that reduces output without failing catastrophically) is more survivable than one that operates at full capacity until a threshold is crossed and then fails entirely. A system whose dependency profile is composed of conditions that are themselves stable and likely to persist is more survivable than one whose dependency profile is composed of conditions that are volatile, contested, or historically contingent.

The concept is not, in itself, novel. Ecologists have been studying the behavior of systems under stress for decades. C. S. Holling's foundational 1973 paper on ecological resilience drew the distinction between stability (a system's tendency to return to an equilibrium state after a small perturbation) and resilience (a system's capacity to absorb disturbance without shifting to a qualitatively different state).<sup>11</sup> Holling's insight was that the two qualities are not the same, and that a system can be stable (returning quickly to equilibrium after small shocks) while being fragile (collapsing entirely when the shock exceeds a threshold). The distinction has been enormously productive in ecology, and versions of it have been imported into food-systems thinking. Tendall and colleagues, in a 2015 review in *Global Food Security*, defined food system resilience as "the capacity over time of a food system and its units at multiple levels, to provide sufficient, appropriate and accessible food to all, in the face of various and even unforeseen disturbances," and noted that the concept remained underspecified in the applied literature, that the food-systems field had adopted the vocabulary of resilience without having fully operationalized it into assessment frameworks.<sup>12</sup>

Nassim Nicholas Taleb's work on antifragility (the property of systems that gain from disorder rather than merely surviving it) has added a further conceptual layer. Taleb's 2012 *Antifragile* argues that the categories of fragile, robust, and antifragile describe a spectrum of system responses to volatility, and that most modern institutions and supply chains are fragile precisely because they have been optimized for efficiency under stable conditions at the expense of robustness under unstable ones.<sup>13</sup> The argument applies to food systems with obvious force. A concentrated industrial supply chain is the paradigmatic fragile system in Taleb's vocabulary: it performs superbly under the conditions it was designed for and breaks catastrophically under conditions it was not.

Survivability, as I use it, draws on these intellectual predecessors without duplicating any of them. It is narrower than Holling's resilience, which is a property of ecological systems at scales ranging from a pond to a biome. It is narrower than Taleb's antifragility, which is a property of systems and institutions across domains. It is specific to food systems, and it is designed to function as an axis: a dimension of evaluation that can be applied alongside the existing three, producing a four-axis assessment that captures both a system's current performance and its behavior under changed conditions.

The four axes, stated together, are:

**Economic viability:** does the system generate sufficient returns to persist as an economic entity under current conditions?

**Social equity:** does the system distribute its benefits and costs in ways that are equitable or defensible under current conditions?

**Environmental integrity:** does the system operate within ecological bounds that the surrounding environment can sustain under current conditions?

**Survivability:** what does the system require to keep functioning, and what happens when those requirements are not met?

Notice the structural difference. The first three axes share a temporal orientation: they describe performance under *current* conditions. The fourth axis asks about performance under *changed* conditions. The first three axes tell you how the system is doing. The fourth axis tells you what the system is made of: what it is standing on, how deep the foundations go, and what happens when the ground moves.

A system can score well on three axes and poorly on the fourth. This is, in fact, the most common configuration in the contemporary US food system. A well-managed industrial grain operation can be economically profitable, can provide employment and community economic activity, and, under best-management practices with cover cropping, reduced tillage, and precision nutrient application, can score reasonably on environmental metrics. It can do all of this while being deeply dependent on continuous fossil energy, continuous synthetic fertilizer derived from natural gas, continuous federal commodity-support payments, and continuous favorable climate for the specific cultivars it has planted. The three axes find the system sustainable. The fourth axis finds it piggybacking on a set of conditions that are historically contingent, geopolitically vulnerable, and climatically uncertain. Both findings are accurate. They are measuring different things.

A system can also score moderately on the first three axes and well on the fourth. A household-scale subsistence polyculture, growing diverse crops and raising a few animals on its own feed with minimal purchased inputs, may not score impressively on economic viability (the returns are low by commercial standards), may have limited social reach (it feeds a family, not a community), and may have an environmental footprint that is positive but modest in scale. On survivability, however, it scores high: it requires little from outside itself, it can tolerate the loss of any single input without collapsing, and it can continue to function under a wide range of conditions because it was never optimized for any single set of conditions in the first place. The three-axis framework would rank it below the well-managed industrial operation on most metrics. The four-axis framework reveals that the two systems have fundamentally different relationships to the future.

## WHAT THE AXIS DOES NOT DO

I want to be precise about the limits of the framework, because precision about limits is what distinguishes a tool from a sales pitch.

The survivability axis does not measure moral worth. A system that scores poorly on survivability is not evil; it is structurally vulnerable. The industrial grain system that feeds most of the planet scores poorly on survivability for reasons that are structural and historical, not because anyone set out to build a fragile system. The fragility is a side effect of the optimization for efficiency, and the optimization for efficiency was, in its historical context, a rational response to the problems it was designed to solve: feeding a rapidly growing global population at declining real cost. The system succeeded at what it was built to do. Its survivability deficit is a feature of its design, not evidence of malice.

The survivability axis does not predict specific failures. It identifies dependency profiles and maps vulnerability, but it does not tell you which specific disruption will occur or when. A system that depends on continuous diesel, continuous commodity corn, continuous federal subsidies, and continuous stable climate has four distinct vulnerability surfaces. The axis can identify all four. It cannot tell you whether the disruption will come from diesel, corn, subsidies, or climate, or whether it will come from something the analysis did not anticipate. The axis is a diagnostic tool, not a crystal ball. It tells you where the fault lines are. It does not tell you when the earthquake comes.

The survivability axis does not produce a single numerical score. Attempts to reduce complex systems to single scores are, in my experience, more useful for marketing than for understanding. The axis produces a profile: a description of what the system depends on, how deep each dependency runs, and how the system behaves when each dependency is stressed. The profile can be compared across systems, and the comparison is informative, but the comparison is qualitative and contextual rather than a simple ranking. A system that depends on diesel is vulnerable in a different way than a system that depends on federal subsidies, and collapsing both vulnerabilities into a single number would obscure more than it reveals. The framework is a tool for thinking, not a scoreboard.

The framework is also not personally branded. I developed the core of this argument in a 2016 essay, where I proposed that sustainability assessments should include survivability as a dimension and sketched the evaluative logic that the current formulation extends. The essay named the framework with my own name, which I no longer think was the right choice. The framework is a tool. Tools are described by what they do, not by who made them. What matters is whether the four-axis assessment produces useful distinctions that the three-axis assessment does not. I believe it does, and the next two chapters are the test of that belief.

> **FIELD NOTE: Testing the Score Against What I Liked**
>
> I tested the framework on the systems I liked, and that part is not in the chapter body.
>
> The week I finished the 2016 essay, I scored monocultures, and the result is the one the REVISION sidebar in the next chapter records. Then, deliberately, I turned the framework on the cases I had been writing about with admiration. I scored Allan Savory’s holistic-management work. I scored rewilding, in the form the literature was using at the time. I needed to know whether the framework was a piece of analysis or a way of dressing up disdain in quantitative clothing. I needed to know whether it would punish the systems I disliked while flattering the ones I admired. If it was the latter, it was not worth keeping. It cut both ways. Some of what I admired held up. Some did not. The cases that did not are part of why this book exists in the form it does: the conversation with Savory that Chapter 7 records, the conflicting views about Polyface that close out Chapter 6. The framework had to be willing to find against my favorites for me to trust it. It did. I am still using it.

## WHAT THE FRAMEWORK REVEALS

The value of a framework is in the distinctions it makes visible.
