The three-axis framework makes visible whether a food system is currently functioning well: whether it is profitable, equitable, and ecologically responsible under the conditions it is operating in. These are real and important distinctions. The four-axis framework makes visible an additional distinction: the difference between a system that functions well under current conditions because it is well designed and a system that functions well under current conditions because the conditions happen to be favorable.

This distinction matters because conditions change. They have always changed —climate has oscillated, trade routes have opened and closed, energy regimes have shifted, empires have risen and collapsed and taken their agricultural systems with them. What is different now is not that conditions change but that the food systems we have built are more sensitive to change than most of their predecessors, because they have been more thoroughly optimized for a specific set of conditions than most of their predecessors. The global commodity system is the most efficient food-production apparatus in human history. It is also, by the metric of survivability, among the most dependent on the persistence of its specific operating conditions. The efficiency and the dependency are the same feature, viewed from different angles.

The chapters that follow apply the framework. Chapter 5 takes the two dominant industrial food systems — industrial feedlot animal production and industrial grain monoculture — and runs them through the four-axis assessment in detail, with the survivability axis producing findings that the three-axis assessment alone would not. Chapter 6 has already done the same for regenerative agriculture, and the finding there is instructive in a different way: the system scores substantially better than industrial production on the first three axes and meaningfully better on the fourth, but the survivability score is still constrained by the structural ceiling the chapter identified: the dependency on industrial commodity feed for monogastric animal production, which is a survivability deficit embedded in the system's relationship to its inputs rather than in its own management practices. The framework does not declare winners and losers. It maps the dependency profiles and shows where the vulnerabilities live. What the reader does with the map is the reader's decision.

The work of Part II of this book, which this chapter opens, is to use the framework and see what it shows. The survivability axis is the analytical addition the book proposes. It is not the book's conclusion. The conclusion lives in Part III, where the framework is brought to bear on a set of food-system possibilities that the current sustainability conversation has mostly overlooked: possibilities that score well on all four axes, including the fourth, because their dependency profiles are structured differently from anything the dominant food system currently offers.

There is one further point worth making before the framework is put to use, because it bears on how the rest of the book's argument is constructed. The survivability axis does not exist to condemn any existing food system. It exists to ask a question that the existing assessment framework does not ask, and to make visible a set of distinctions that the existing framework cannot see. An industrial operation that scores poorly on survivability is not thereby a failure. It may be, under current conditions, the most productive, most affordable, most efficient food-production system available. The survivability axis does not erase those achievements. It adds a dimension to the assessment: a dimension that asks whether the current conditions on which those achievements depend are conditions we can count on persisting. If they are, the three-axis assessment is sufficient and the fourth axis adds nothing of practical consequence. If they are not — if diesel prices are volatile, if commodity supply chains are geopolitically exposed, if climate patterns are shifting in ways that affect crop yields, if the policy environment that subsidizes current production is politically contestable — then the fourth axis is measuring something the first three cannot, and the measurement matters.

> **REVISION: On Discovering Resilience Thinking**
>
> Sometime after I had written the four-axis framework as it appears in this chapter, a reader pointed me at C. S. Holling’s 1973 paper on ecosystem resilience, then at Walker and Salt’s *Resilience Thinking* (2006), then at the broader literature developed by Folke, Berkes, Ostrom, and others over fifty years. I had not arrived at the framework through that field. I arrived at it through certification work, and through asking, repeatedly, which of the food systems I had been writing standards for would still be food systems the day after their inputs got harder to count on.
>
> It would have been a better book had I known about the resilience-thinking field when I started. I did not. The framework here was developed independently, and that fact does not shield me from the criticism that I am reinventing wheels with my own labels.
>
> What I will say is this. When independent thinkers, working in different fields, with different prompts, arrive at the same structural observation — that systems described well at one moment in time can be described badly across changes in their conditions — the convergence itself is evidence. Evidence that the observation is not a quirk of one camp’s framing. Evidence that something is missing in the framework most of us have been using. The reader should pause at the convergence.
>
> I owe the resilience-thinking field a careful read. I will not pretend to have done that read in time for this book. Where my framework and theirs say the same thing, I want to be clear that they were there first. Where they say different things, I want to be clear about what the difference is, and I do not yet know enough to do that work in this volume. It is the next thing I owe, and I will write it.

The framework is a lens. The next chapters are what the lens is pointed at.

# Chapter Five: Scoring the Industrial System

The previous chapter introduced a framework. This chapter puts it to work.

The four-axis assessment was designed to do something the three-axis model cannot: to distinguish between a food system that functions well under current conditions and a food system that functions well under current conditions because those conditions happen to be favorable. The distinction matters only when the framework is applied to specific systems and the application produces specific findings. Abstractions do not feed anyone. The question is what the framework shows when it is pointed at the systems that actually feed most of the people reading this book.

Two systems dominate the American food landscape, and between them they account for the overwhelming majority of calories and protein consumed in the United States. The first is industrial feedlot animal production, the confined animal feeding operation (CAFO), which finishes roughly ninety-nine percent of the meat animals sold in US retail markets on concentrated grain rations in confined facilities.<sup>1</sup> The second is industrial grain monoculture: the corn, soy, and wheat production system that supplies both the feed for the first system and the caloric base for most processed food consumed in the country. They are structurally linked. The feedlot depends on the grain monoculture for its feed inputs. The grain monoculture depends in turn on the feedlot, on the export market, and on the ethanol mandate for its demand. Neither system, in its current form, would exist without the other. Scoring them separately is an analytical convenience; in practice they are two faces of a single industrial substrate.

The chapter takes each system through all four axes in turn. The work is analytical, and I want to be clear about what that means. The findings are structural, not moral. A system that scores poorly on a given axis is not thereby condemned. It is described: its dependency profile mapped, its vulnerabilities identified, its genuine achievements registered alongside its genuine costs. The framework is a diagnostic instrument. It is not a verdict.

> **REVERSAL: The Strongest Case for the Industrial System**
>
> The strongest version of the case for the industrial system goes like this.
>
> Nine billion people, by mid-century. A carrying capacity question that preindustrial agriculture has never met. Norman Borlaug's Green Revolution averted famine at a scale no other twentieth-century intervention can claim. Industrial grain yields per acre are several times what the same land produced a century ago.
>
> The system, whatever its externalities, has fed more people per unit of land and per unit of labor than any system in human history. To propose replacing it with anything less productive is to propose, by arithmetic, that some fraction of those nine billion does not eat.
>
> This is not a straw man. It is the argument the FAO has made in its food-security publications. It is the argument made in peer-reviewed journals by economists at land-grant universities whose work I respect. I had to take it seriously before I could write a book that proposes the system has to change.
>
> My answer is in the chapters that follow. But the argument deserves to be heard at full strength before the rebuttal, because the consequence of being wrong in the other direction is famine, and that is not a small matter.

> **REVISION: On Monocultures**
>
> 2012: I treated monocultures as a moral fact. They were destructive. They were unsustainable. There was no version of the case for them I considered worth taking seriously. After all, there was no such thing as a closed-loop agricultural system. 2016: I scored monocultures of animals and grains on the four-axis framework of survivability. I came up with the concept that year, when I still felt I had something to prove, and called it the *Pendergrass Score of Survivability*. They scored abysmally. My disdain for monocultures continued. The name did not survive. The framework did.
>
> 2017: I read Matt Ridley’s *The Rational Optimist*. Ridley took seriously a case I had been refusing to take seriously: that yields per acre are not incidental, that what is required to feed a population at industrial scale is a real constraint and not a propaganda line, that the people working on technological improvement of agricultural systems are not, mostly, villains. I had been arguing against a caricature. And, most importantly, over time these systems will inevitably improve. That is the benefit of time × human ingenuity.
>
> 2018: Monocultures have serious and structural sustainability problems — the four-axis framework lays them out. But they also have meaningful potential for improvement under cover-cropping, reduced-tillage, perennial-integration, precision-input, and microbial-soil-management regimes. The case I had to take seriously was not “monocultures are fine.” It was “the strongest argument for monocultures is not the one I was attacking.”
>
> This evolution of thought matters for the chapter that follows because the scoring is structural, not moral. Even though I softened my stance against monocultures, industrial grain monoculture scores poorly for specific reasons that the four-axis framework makes legible. That is a different argument from “monoculture is evil.”
>
> The binary position of 2012 is a reflection of where I sat on the Dunning-Kruger curve at the time. And, while holding space for two conflicting ideas at the same time, I recognize that this chapter still owes a great deal to ideas from more than a decade ago that held strong.

## INDUSTRIAL FEEDLOT ANIMAL PRODUCTION

> ***Economic Viability***

At the point of sale, the industrial feedlot system is an economic success by the metric most consumers encounter: price. Inflation-adjusted retail prices for beef, pork, and chicken in the United States have declined substantially over the last half century. The USDA's Economic Research Service tracks this in real terms: between 1960 and 2020, the inflation-adjusted retail price of chicken fell by roughly forty percent, and beef and pork prices have remained lower in real terms than they were at mid-century peaks, despite periodic spikes driven by drought, disease, or commodity-market disruption.<sup>2</sup> For a consumer standing in a grocery store, the system delivers protein at a price point that most household budgets can absorb. This is not a trivial achievement. Cheap animal protein has been one of the defining features of the American diet for two generations, and the system that produces it has been remarkably effective at the narrow task of minimizing per-unit cost at the point of purchase.

The point-of-purchase price, however, is not the full economic accounting, and the gap between the two is where the system's economic picture changes. The Pew Commission on Industrial Farm Animal Production, an independent panel convened by the Pew Charitable Trusts and the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, published its comprehensive assessment in 2008, and its finding on this point was direct: the retail price of industrial animal products does not reflect the external costs the system imposes on public health, environmental quality, and rural economies.<sup>3</sup> The externalities are real, they are large, and they are borne by parties other than the producer or the consumer at the point of sale.

The antibiotic-resistance externality is among the most economically consequential. A 2019 review published by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention estimated that antibiotic-resistant infections cause more than 2.8 million illnesses and more than 35,000 deaths annually in the United States, with associated healthcare costs and productivity losses running into the tens of billions of dollars.<sup>4</sup> The connection to agricultural antibiotic use is not casual. The FDA reported that in 2021, approximately sixty-six percent of medically important antibiotics sold in the United States were sold for use in food-producing animals, administered not primarily to treat clinical disease but to promote growth and prevent illness in the high-density conditions that confined operations require.<sup>5</sup> The mechanism by which sub-therapeutic antibiotic use in livestock selects for resistant bacteria that subsequently affect human health has been documented across multiple peer-reviewed studies and was identified as a significant public health concern by the World Health Organization as early as 2001.<sup>6</sup> The cost of that resistance is externalized. It does not appear on the price tag of the chicken breast or the ground beef, but it appears in the healthcare system, in the insurance premiums, and in the reduced efficacy of antibiotics that were once reliable clinical tools.

Pollution remediation adds a second layer of externalized cost. The EPA estimated in 2003 that animal feeding operations were contributing to water quality impairment in more than twenty thousand miles of rivers and streams and had contaminated groundwater in seventeen states.<sup>7</sup> The cleanup costs for that contamination are borne by municipalities, by taxpayers, and by downstream agricultural and recreational operations rather than by the CAFOs whose waste produced the contamination. Property values in communities adjacent to large-scale confined operations decline measurably — studies in North Carolina, Iowa, and Minnesota have documented decreases ranging from six to eleven percent in residential property values within a three-mile radius of a CAFO, with effects persisting for years after the operation's establishment.<sup>8</sup>

Federal subsidies add a further economic distortion. The commodity support system, administered through the farm bill and its various iterations since the 1970s, has kept the price of corn and soybeans below the full cost of production for most of the last four decades, and those below-cost commodity grains are the primary input for confined animal feeding. Researchers at Tufts University calculated that between 1997 and 2005, the industrial livestock sector saved an estimated $35 billion through below-cost feed made possible by federal commodity support payments.<sup>9</sup> That saving is not an efficiency gain in any meaningful economic sense. It is a transfer from the taxpayer to the industrial system, and it renders the retail price of industrial meat artificially low relative to what the system actually costs to operate.

The economic axis, then, produces a split finding. At the point of sale, the system scores well: it delivers affordable protein to consumers. When the externalities are accounted for — healthcare costs from antibiotic resistance, pollution remediation, property value losses, and taxpayer-funded commodity subsidies — the economic picture reverses. The system's apparent efficiency is substantially subsidized by costs it does not pay.

> ***Social Equity***

The social axis asks whether the system distributes its benefits and costs equitably or defensibly. For industrial feedlot production, the distribution is sharply asymmetric.

Worker conditions in meat processing have been documented extensively. The Government Accountability Office reported in 2005 that meatpacking workers experienced injury and illness rates substantially above the national average for manufacturing, with the Bureau of Labor Statistics recording injury rates roughly two and a half times the average across all private industry.<sup>10</sup> The COVID-19 pandemic made the workforce conditions newly visible: the CDC documented infection clusters at 115 meat and poultry processing facilities across 19 states in the first two months of the pandemic alone, with facility-level attack rates that reflected the combination of close working proximity, high line speeds, and inadequate protective measures.<sup>11</sup> The workers in these facilities are disproportionately immigrant, disproportionately non-English-speaking, and disproportionately employed under conditions where reporting injuries or unsafe practices carries economic and sometimes legal risk. The social cost of industrial meat processing is concentrated in the workforce that makes the system operate.

Community health impacts extend beyond the processing facilities. Residents of communities adjacent to large-scale confined operations report elevated rates of respiratory symptoms, headache, nausea, and psychological distress in epidemiological studies conducted across North Carolina, Iowa, and other states with high CAFO concentrations.<sup>12</sup> The communities bearing these health effects are disproportionately rural, disproportionately low-income, and disproportionately communities of color, a pattern that has led environmental justice researchers to identify CAFO siting as a significant environmental equity concern.<sup>13</sup>

The displacement of family farmers is a structural feature of the system's economic logic. Vertical integration in the poultry and pork industries has consolidated production into contract-grower arrangements in which the processor owns the animals, the feed formula, and the processing relationship, while the grower owns the capital infrastructure: the houses, the land, the debt. Congressional Research Service analyses have documented that the number of independent hog farms in the United States declined from more than 660,000 in 1980 to fewer than 67,000 by 2012, while total hog production increased over the same period.<sup>14</sup> The consolidation was not the elimination of pork production. It was the elimination of the people who had been doing it, replaced by a smaller number of larger-scale contract operations producing more animals at lower margins under tighter corporate control. The social cost of that consolidation has been documented by rural sociologists for decades, and the industry itself does not seriously contest it. It shows up in rural depopulation, in the loss of small-town economic activity, and in the civic infrastructure that contracts when the people who supported it leave.

The social axis scores net negative: the system's benefits flow primarily to consumers in the form of cheap protein and to the corporate entities that control the processing and distribution chain; its costs are concentrated in workers, in adjacent communities, and in the rural regions whose agricultural economies the system has restructured.

> ***Environmental Integrity***

The environmental axis is where the industrial feedlot system's costs are most extensively documented and least disputed.

Waste management is the entry point. The EPA's regulatory definition of a large CAFO, an animal feeding operation confining more than 1,000 animal units at 1,000 pounds each, means that a single large cattle feedlot can produce waste volumes equivalent to a small city. The USDA estimated in 2009 that confined animal operations in the United States produce approximately 500 million tons of manure annually, roughly three times the volume of human sanitary waste produced by the entire US population.<sup>15</sup> That waste is typically stored in anaerobic lagoons or applied to surrounding cropland at rates that frequently exceed the land's absorptive capacity for nitrogen and phosphorus. When the application exceeds the capacity, the surplus runs off into surface water.

The water quality consequences are measurable at continental scale. Nutrient runoff from agricultural operations, of which confined animal waste is a leading component, is the primary driver of the hypoxic zone in the Gulf of Mexico, a seasonal dead zone that covered approximately 6,334 square miles in 2024 according to NOAA survey data, making it one of the largest areas of oxygen-depleted water in the world.<sup>16</sup> The Chesapeake Bay, closer to the concentrated poultry production of the Delmarva Peninsula, has experienced a parallel pattern of nutrient-driven hypoxia that has degraded its fisheries for decades.

The numbers are real. What they describe is also visible. Louisiana shrimpers working the waters off the Atchafalaya Delta have watched the dead zone's expansion reshape their industry over three decades. In peak hypoxia years — midsummer, after the spring flush of nitrogen from the Mississippi watershed reaches the Gulf —trawlers that once worked nearshore waters are pushed miles further out, burning more diesel to reach the oxygenated water where the shrimp still congregate. The catch does not disappear. It migrates, and the boats follow it, and the economics tighten. In 2023, the dock-side price for Gulf brown shrimp hit its lowest point in over a decade, driven in part by import competition but compounded by the operational costs of fishing around a dead zone the size of Connecticut. The hypoxic zone is not an abstraction measured only by NOAA research vessels. It is a feature of the working landscape that the people who fish the Gulf navigate around every season, and its growth tracks the nutrient load from the agricultural system upstream with a fidelity that the science has been documenting since the early 1990s.

Greenhouse gas emissions from confined animal production are significant in the global accounting. The FAO's widely cited 2013 assessment estimated that global livestock supply chains contribute approximately 14.5 percent of all anthropogenic greenhouse gas emissions, with the confined production segment contributing disproportionately through enteric methane from cattle, nitrous oxide from manure management, and carbon dioxide from feed crop production and transportation.<sup>17</sup> The feedlot system, specifically, contributes on the production side through methane from enteric fermentation and manure storage, and on the input side through the full emissions profile of the commodity grain system that feeds it: the diesel for planting, harvesting, and transporting the corn, the natural gas for synthesizing the nitrogen fertilizer the corn requires, and the emissions from the land-use practices under which the corn is grown.

The system sequesters no carbon. Feedlot pens are bare dirt or concrete. The land on which the animals stand contributes nothing to soil carbon accumulation. The land from which the feed is grown, under annual row-crop cultivation with conventional tillage, is losing soil carbon rather than gaining it, as the next section documents.

The environmental axis scores net negative across every metric the framework measures: water pollution, greenhouse gas emissions, soil degradation via feed production, and zero contribution to carbon sequestration.

> ***Survivability***

The survivability axis asks what the system requires to keep functioning and what happens when those requirements are not met. For industrial feedlot production, the dependency profile is broad and deep.

The system requires continuous commodity grain inputs. A single large cattle feedlot finishing 30,000 head per year requires, at roughly six to eight pounds of grain per pound of weight gain over the finishing period, millions of pounds of corn annually.<sup>18</sup> That corn must be available, at a price the feedlot can absorb, delivered on a schedule that matches the metabolic demands of the animals currently on feed. A disruption to the corn supply — drought, export-market competition, ethanol-mandate pressure on available supply — translates directly and immediately into financial stress on the feedlot.

The system requires continuous federal subsidies for its feed inputs. As the economic section documented, below-cost commodity grain has been the structural condition of the feedlot's profitability for decades. Without the commodity-support system, feed prices would reflect the full cost of production, and the feedlot's margins, already thin in most years, would narrow further or invert. The policy structure is the floor the system stands on.

The system requires continuous diesel for transportation. Commodity grain moves from the Corn Belt to feedlots across the country by truck, rail, and barge. Finished animals move from feedlots to a small number of large processing facilities. Processed meat moves from those facilities to distribution centers and retail outlets nationwide. Each link in this chain requires petroleum-derived fuel, and the chain is long. A sustained increase in diesel prices propagates through every link.

The system requires continuous antibiotics for animal health at the stocking densities the system operates at. Confining thousands of animals in close quarters at the stress levels inherent to the feedlot environment creates disease pressure that sub-therapeutic antibiotic administration manages. Remove the antibiotics and the disease pressure reasserts itself at densities that make clinical treatment of individual animals economically unfeasible.

Remove any one of these requirements — grain supply, federal subsidies, diesel, antibiotics — and the system cannot continue to function at its current scale and cost structure. This is not a prediction of imminent failure. It is a description of the system's dependency profile. The system operates successfully because all four of these conditions are currently being met. Its survivability score reflects what happens when any one of them is not.

The survivability axis scores very low, not because the system is poorly managed, but because the system's design requires continuous external inputs that are historically contingent, geopolitically exposed, and, in the case of climate-dependent grain supply, increasingly volatile.

## INDUSTRIAL GRAIN MONOCULTURE

The second system is, in a structural sense, the first system's substrate. Industrial grain monoculture — the large-scale, chemically intensive production of corn, soybeans, and wheat that dominates the American agricultural landscape — supplies the feed for the feedlot system, the caloric base for most processed food, and the raw material for the ethanol industry. It covers roughly 250 million acres of US cropland, and the three crops together account for the majority of planted acreage in the country.<sup>19</sup>

> ***Economic Viability***

The economic picture for industrial grain monoculture mirrors the feedlot's in structure: positive at the point of sale, substantially different when externalities are accounted for.

For centralized producers — the large-scale operations with enough acreage to capture economies of scale in equipment, input purchasing, and commodity marketing — the system is profitable in most years, though the margins are sensitive to commodity prices, input costs, and the federal support structure. The farm bill's commodity support programs — direct payments, counter-cyclical payments, crop insurance subsidies, and their successive reformulations — have provided a floor under commodity grain profitability for decades, smoothing the volatility that would otherwise make large-scale monoculture economically unsustainable in low-price years.<sup>20</sup>

For family-scale operators and local economies, the picture is different. The consolidation of US grain production has followed the same trajectory as livestock production: fewer, larger operations producing more output. The number of farms in the United States has declined from approximately 6.8 million in 1935 to roughly 2 million today, while average farm size has more than tripled, and the majority of production value is now concentrated in a small fraction of operations.<sup>21</sup> The economic benefits of the system flow disproportionately to the largest operators and to the input suppliers, processors, and commodity traders that the system depends on. Rural communities in the Corn Belt and the wheat-growing regions of the Plains have experienced the same pattern of depopulation, retail contraction, and civic thinning that the livestock consolidation produced in poultry- and pork-producing regions.

The system delivers cheap calories to urban consumers. The calories come from corn syrup, soybean oil, and refined wheat flour, and their prices have kept food expenditure as a share of household income at historically low levels. This is a real benefit to household budgets. It is also a benefit whose costs appear elsewhere: in the healthcare system, where diet-related chronic disease driven in part by the overconsumption of cheap refined carbohydrates and seed oils imposes costs that the National Institutes of Health has estimated at hundreds of billions of dollars annually; in the environmental costs documented below; and in the rural economic hollowing the consolidation has produced.<sup>22</sup>

> ***Social Equity***

The social axis for grain monoculture is structurally similar to the feedlot case: the system's benefits are distributed broadly to consumers in the form of cheap food, and its costs are concentrated in the communities where the production occurs.

Rural communities in intensive monoculture regions bear the environmental health burden: pesticide exposure, nitrate contamination of drinking water, respiratory effects from dust and chemical drift. They bear it without capturing a proportional share of the economic value the production generates. The value is captured at the processing and distribution stages, which are physically and economically distant from the fields. Farm labor in large-scale grain operations has shifted from a human workforce to a mechanical one; the tractor, the combine, and the chemical application system have replaced the hands, and the people those hands belonged to have, for the most part, moved elsewhere.

The displacement narrative for grain monoculture is quieter than for livestock. There are no CAFO siting controversies, no concentrated processing facilities with high-visibility labor conditions. The underlying structural change is the same. Fewer people are needed to produce more grain, and the communities that were organized around the people are contracting as the people leave.

> ***Environmental Integrity***

The environmental costs of industrial grain monoculture are extensive, well documented, and, in some dimensions, cumulative in ways that compound across decades.

Topsoil loss is the foundational concern. David Pimentel, whose career at Cornell established much of the empirical basis for energy-input analysis in agriculture, estimated that US cropland loses topsoil at an average rate of roughly ten times the natural soil-formation rate, with annual erosion rates on conventionally tilled cropland averaging approximately 1.8 billion tons across the country.<sup>23</sup> The Natural Resources Conservation Service's more recent modeling using the Revised Universal Soil Loss Equation places the figure at roughly four to five tons per acre per year on average for conventionally managed cropland, against a natural formation rate of roughly half a ton per acre per year.<sup>24</sup> The loss is quiet. It does not produce dramatic events in most years. It produces a slow, cumulative thinning of the soil horizon that reduces the land's productive capacity over generational timescales. The soil that took centuries to build is being removed in decades.

Biodiversity reduction is a direct consequence of the monoculture model. A cornfield is, by design, one species of plant over a very large area. The organisms that would normally occupy the ecological niches of a diverse plant community — the insects, the birds, the soil microbiota, the mycorrhizal fungi — are either absent or suppressed by the chemical regime required to maintain the monoculture. Tilman and colleagues' landmark 2002 paper in *Nature* documented that intensification of agricultural production practices was the single largest driver of biodiversity loss in terrestrial ecosystems globally, operating through habitat conversion, chemical inputs, and the simplification of plant communities from diverse assemblages to single-species stands.<sup>25</sup>

Groundwater contamination from nitrogen fertilizer and pesticide leaching is a chronic and geographically extensive problem. Nitrate contamination of drinking water from agricultural sources affects millions of Americans, particularly in rural areas overlying the shallow aquifers of the Corn Belt and the Great Plains. The USGS National Water-Quality Assessment has documented nitrate concentrations exceeding the EPA's maximum contaminant level in a significant fraction of shallow groundwater wells in agricultural areas, with the highest concentrations correlated with the most intensive corn and soybean production.<sup>26</sup>

Mycorrhizal damage from fungicide application is a less visible but ecologically significant consequence. Mycorrhizal fungi, the symbiotic networks that connect plant root systems to soil nutrient pools and to each other, are suppressed by the fungicide applications that modern grain production relies on for disease management. The disruption of mycorrhizal networks degrades the soil's capacity to cycle nutrients, retain water, and support the biological activity that builds soil organic matter over time. The consequence is a system that becomes progressively more dependent on synthetic inputs to replace the biological functions it has disabled, a dependency spiral that the environmental literature has documented across multiple cropping systems.<sup>27</sup>

Nutrient runoff from grain monoculture is the leading cause of non-point source water pollution in the United States. Synthetic fertilizer is applied at rates calibrated to maximize yield in an average year, which means applied in excess of what the crop uses in any below-average year. The nitrogen and phosphorus that the crops do not absorb move through the soil profile into groundwater and across the surface into streams, rivers, and eventually into the coastal waters where they drive the eutrophication and hypoxia described in the feedlot section. Foley and colleagues, in their 2011 *Nature* paper surveying the environmental footprint of global agriculture, identified nutrient management in intensive grain systems as one of the highest-priority leverage points for reducing agriculture's environmental impact.<sup>28</sup>

Heavy-metal contamination is a quieter dimension of the same inventory, and it is worth slowing down for. The reader does not have to be a toxicologist to follow the argument; the test the framework cares about is whether each metal accumulates faster than the system can clear it. Most of them do.

Phosphate rock carries cadmium as a co-occurring trace element, and the soils that have been receiving phosphate fertilizer for half a century carry a legacy cadmium load that moves into the food chain through plant uptake. Arsenic entered US cropland through approved organoarsenical poultry-feed additives whose drug approvals the FDA withdrew after 2011, with decades of accumulated soil arsenic still in place. Lead persists in the upper soil horizons of much of the Corn Belt as legacy load from the leaded-gasoline era. Nickel cycles into agricultural soils through phosphate fertilizers, manure, and atmospheric deposition. The metals do not move through the system on the same timescales as nitrogen does. They accumulate. The soil that is being thinned by erosion is also, where it remains, slightly more contaminated with each crop.<sup>29</sup>

Nickel deserves a specific note. Nearly every pathogen on the WHO’s antimicrobial-resistance priority list — *Helicobacter pylori,* virulent *Escherichia coli, Klebsiella pneumoniae,* and others — uses nickel-dependent virulence factors to establish and maintain infection. Nickel itself has no established essential role in human nutrition. The asymmetry — essential for pathogens, nonessential for hosts — is what makes agricultural nickel accumulation a microbial-system question rather than a contamination question alone, and it is one of the inputs the survivability axis is measuring that the three-axis framework is not.<sup>30</sup>

The connection to the mycorrhizal disruption noted above is not incidental. The soil microbial communities that mediate metal speciation and bioavailability — the communities that determine whether a given soil cadmium load reaches a wheat kernel or stays bound — are the same communities the fungicide regime suppresses. The chapter has space here only to name the connection and to score the axis. The full pathway-by-metal exposition, with the primary literature, is in the Technical Appendix at the back of the book; readers who want to follow the chemistry into the soil microbiology should turn there.<sup>31</sup>

The environmental axis scores net negative: topsoil loss, biodiversity reduction, groundwater contamination, mycorrhizal disruption, heavy-metal accumulation, and nutrient runoff as the nation's leading non-point source water pollutant.

> **FIELD NOTE: The Rabbit Hole**
>
> What started as one question — what did my chronic conditions have in common — has not stopped asking the next question for sixteen years.
>
> The first question opened onto food. What I was eating, and what I had been told I was eating. The second question opened onto agriculture. What was being fed to the animals I was eating, and what that feed was grown in. The third opened onto soil. What the soil was, and what the agricultural systems had done to it. The fourth opened onto microbes. The soil microbiome and the human gut microbiome are not, on close inspection, the distinct and unrelated systems I had assumed they were. They are parts of the same continuum, exchanging metabolites, signals, and loads through every meal.
>
> The fifth opened onto heavy metals. The same agricultural systems that simplified the soil microbial communities had been concentrating cadmium, arsenic, lead, and nickel in those soils for decades. The metals do not just sit there. They are mediated, sequestered, transported, and sometimes mobilized by the soil microbial communities the fungicide regime has been disrupting for the same decades. They reach plants. Plants reach plates. The same gut microbiomes the agricultural regime has been simplifying are also the ones doing the most consequential work on those metals once the metals arrive.
>
> This is where my interest currently sits, and it has pulled me harder than I expected. The certification work the Field Note in Chapter One named — Paleo Approved, then Certified Paleo — is now organized around heavy-metal testing under a label called HMTc. The Afterword names where the rest of that work has gone.

The book in your hands is built on the first four questions. The fifth has its own work, in progress.

> ***Survivability***

The survivability axis for grain monoculture reveals a dependency profile that is structurally parallel to the feedlot's.

The system requires continuous chemical inputs. Synthetic nitrogen fertilizer, produced from natural gas through the Haber-Bosch process, is the single largest energy input in grain production. Pimentel and Pimentel's analysis in *Food, Energy, and Society* documented that roughly forty percent of the total energy input in corn production is accounted for by nitrogen fertilizer alone.<sup>32</sup> Without it, corn yields fall to a fraction of their current levels, because the monoculture regime has depleted the biological nitrogen-fixation capacity that diverse systems maintain through legume rotation and mycorrhizal function. The system has substituted a chemical input for a biological process, and the substitution is not reversible on a short timeline.

The system requires continuous fossil fuel for field operations and irrigation. Every pass of the tractor — planting, cultivating, applying chemicals, harvesting —requires diesel. In the western Corn Belt and the Great Plains, where rainfall is insufficient for reliable yields, irrigation from the Ogallala Aquifer and other groundwater sources requires energy for pumping, and the Ogallala itself is being drawn down at rates that exceed recharge in much of its extent, making the water supply a diminishing resource rather than a renewable one.<sup>33</sup>

The Ogallala drawdown is worth pausing on, because it is the survivability axis made physically legible. The aquifer underlies approximately 174,000 square miles across eight states from South Dakota to the Texas Panhandle, and it accumulated its water over millions of years, recharged slowly by precipitation filtering through the High Plains soils above it. In southwestern Kansas — Grant, Stanton, and Haskell counties, the heart of the irrigated corn and feedlot corridor — the saturated thickness of the aquifer has declined by more than sixty percent since large-scale pumping began in the 1950s. The Kansas Geological Survey has documented wells that produced reliably for decades going dry or dropping below the threshold at which irrigation is economically viable, and the projection for much of the southwestern Kansas portion of the aquifer is functional depletion within a generation.[^30a] This is not a speculative scenario. It is a measurement. Farmers in the region can tell you the rate their water table is dropping; many of them track it year to year. The arithmetic is not ambiguous. The system that depends on this water is drawing down its supply faster than geology can replace it, and when the water is gone, the irrigated agriculture it supports goes with it. The dependency the survivability axis measures is, in this case, a physical quantity with a known depletion rate and a calculable endpoint.

The system requires continuous federal subsidies. Commodity grain production in the United States has operated under federal price support, crop insurance subsidy, and conservation-compliance payment structures for decades. These programs smooth the volatility of commodity markets and keep production profitable in years when market prices fall below the cost of production. Without the subsidy structure, the marginal operations, those farming the least productive land at the highest per-acre cost, would exit production, and the system's total output would contract.

The system requires continuous favorable climate for predictable yields. The cultivars planted across the Corn Belt are selected for performance under a specific range of temperature, moisture, and growing-season conditions. Deviations from the expected range produce yield losses that propagate through the entire commodity system. The 2012 drought reduced the national corn yield by roughly twenty-seven percent in a single season.<sup>34</sup> Climate projections for the central United States indicate increasing frequency and severity of growing-season heat stress and drought stress over the coming decades, which means the climate condition the system was optimized for is itself becoming less reliable.<sup>35</sup>

Remove any one of these — chemical inputs, fossil fuel, federal subsidies, favorable climate — and the system cannot continue to produce at its current scale and cost structure. As with the feedlot system, this is not a prediction of when the disruption occurs. It is a mapping of the dependency profile. The system's survivability score reflects the breadth and depth of its dependencies, each of which is contingent on conditions that are volatile, contested, or trending in directions unfavorable to the system's continuation in its current form.

## THE IMPROVEMENT ACKNOWLEDGMENT

I want to register something that the axis-by-axis scoring, taken alone, might obscure.

Industrial grain monoculture is not a fixed technology. It is a set of practices, and the practices are changing. Cover cropping means planting a secondary crop between cash-crop seasons to hold soil, fix nitrogen, and maintain mycorrhizal networks. The practice has expanded substantially in the United States over the last decade, and the evidence for its benefits across multiple dimensions is strong and growing. The USDA's Census of Agriculture reported that cover crop acreage in the US increased from roughly 10 million acres in 2012 to over 18 million acres by 2022, a pace of adoption that suggests the practice is moving from the margins of the system toward something closer to standard practice in progressive operations.<sup>36</sup> Reduced tillage and no-till systems, which leave crop residue on the soil surface rather than turning it under, reduce erosion rates dramatically and improve soil biological activity. Integration of perennial vegetation into row-crop systems — buffer strips, alley cropping, silvopasture adjacent to grain fields — can restore some of the habitat complexity and biological function that pure monoculture eliminates. Precision agriculture, variable-rate application of inputs guided by GPS mapping and soil sensing, can reduce fertilizer and pesticide use per unit of output by applying inputs where the crop needs them rather than uniformly across the field.

These improvements are real, and they are meaningful. Gabe Brown's operation near Bismarck, North Dakota, is the case that cover-crop advocates cite most often, and the case is worth citing because the numbers are specific. Brown transitioned a 5,000-acre grain and livestock operation from conventional tillage and continuous monoculture to a no-till, diverse cover-crop, integrated-grazing system over roughly two decades. His soil organic matter measurements, documented in NRCS technical visits and in Brown's own published accounting,<sup>37</sup> rose from less than two percent to over six percent on fields that had been under the system longest. His input costs dropped substantially: no synthetic fertilizer purchases, no fungicide, no insecticide, herbicide use reduced to a fraction of the county average. His water infiltration rates, a direct measure of soil biological health, increased severalfold, meaning his fields absorb rainfall that would run off a conventionally managed field carrying topsoil and nitrogen with it. The operation is profitable, and the profitability does not depend on commodity support payments in the way his neighbors' operations do, because his input costs are low enough that the margin holds without the subsidy floor.<sup>38</sup>

Brown's case is not typical. It is a best-case demonstration of what the improving trajectory produces when a skilled operator commits to the transition fully. But it establishes that a grain monoculture operated with winter cover cropping, no-till practices, precision nutrient application, and integrated perennial buffer strips would score materially better on the environmental axis than the same acreage operated under conventional practices. It would build soil rather than lose it, support biological activity rather than suppress it, and reduce nutrient runoff rather than contribute to it. The practices exist, they work, and their adoption is expanding.

The book does not present monocultures as categorically destructive. It presents the current dominant industrial implementation, the version of monoculture that operates across most of the 250 million acres of US cropland without these improvements, as scoring poorly on the four-axis framework for specific, structural reasons. The distinction matters because the structural analysis and the technological trajectory are both true at the same time. The dominant implementation has the vulnerabilities the framework identifies. The improving implementation has the potential the evidence supports. Both findings are accurate. They describe different populations of practice on the same landscape.

What the improvement trajectory does not resolve, however, is the survivability question. A cover-cropped, no-till, precision-managed corn operation is still a corn operation. It still requires seed, fertilizer, and diesel — in reduced quantities, but still required — and a climate regime compatible with corn production. It still sells into a commodity market whose price is shaped by global supply and demand and by federal policy. The improvements narrow the dependency profile. They reduce the system's dependence on any single input by making the system more biologically self-sustaining. But they do not eliminate the profile. A system that depends on fewer external inputs is more survivable than one that depends on more, and the improving monoculture is more survivable than the conventional one. It is not, by the framework's measure, as survivable as a system that depends on very few external inputs and can tolerate the loss of any one of them without collapsing. The improvement is real. The structural constraint remains.

## WHAT THE SCORING SHOWS

Two systems, eight axis scores, one pattern.

Industrial feedlot animal production and industrial grain monoculture both score positively on the economic axis at the point of sale, and both score negatively when externalized costs are accounted for. Both score net negative on the social axis, with benefits flowing to consumers and corporate entities and costs concentrated in workers, adjacent communities, and the rural regions whose economies the systems have restructured. Both score net negative on the environmental axis across essentially every metric. Both score very low on the survivability axis, with dependency profiles that are broad, deep, and contingent on conditions that are volatile.

The pattern is structural rather than incidental. The two systems were not designed by malevolent architects. They evolved through seventy years of economic incentive, agricultural policy, technological development, and consumer demand, each reinforcing the others, producing a food system that is extraordinarily efficient at converting fossil energy and federal dollars into cheap calories under stable conditions. That efficiency is real. It feeds people. The framework does not deny this. What the framework adds is the observation that the efficiency is built on a specific set of conditions, and that the conditions are not guaranteed.

The question the scoring raises is whether an alternative exists that escapes these structural constraints: a food system that scores well on the first three axes without depending on the specific set of external conditions that make the industrial system's performance possible. The next chapter takes up that question, and the answer it finds is instructive in a way I did not expect when I began this work. The leading alternative — regenerative agriculture, holistic management, the pasture-based model — scores substantially better than industrial production on every axis. It also scores better on the survivability axis, which is the axis where the industrial system is most vulnerable. But it does not score as well on survivability as its advocates might hope, and the reason it does not is a structural one that the framework makes visible in a way that three-axis assessment cannot.

The industrial system's survivability deficit is the floor. The question is what can be built above it.

# Chapter Six: Pasture, Honestly

Joel Salatin's Polyface Farm sits in the Shenandoah Valley of Virginia, on about five hundred and fifty acres of rolling pasture, woodlot, and pond that have been in his family since the early 1960s. The operation is the most publicly described small farm in the United States. A steady stream of agricultural interns, journalists, documentary crews, and Polyface's own mail-order customers have made the farm into the de facto reference site for what has come to be called, interchangeably, regenerative agriculture, holistic management, or pasture-based farming. Salatin has earned that attention. By his own published accounting, the soil organic matter on Polyface rose from roughly one and a half percent when his parents bought the land in the early 1960s to around eight percent by the time he was writing *You Can Farm* at the end of that century, against a continental cropland average closer to one to three percent.<sup>1</sup> That is not a trivial gain. An inch of topsoil takes a century or more to form under conventional agriculture. Polyface has built measurable inches within a human career.

Nothing in this chapter should be read as denying that it is working. The previous chapter applied the four-axis framework, economic, social, environmental, survivability, to the two dominant industrial systems and watched them score poorly on each axis, for reasons that were structural rather than incidental. The obvious next question is whether the leading alternative scores better. It does. By a substantial margin, on the axes where it is easiest to measure, it does. The harder question, which is what this chapter is actually about, is whether scoring better is the same thing as escaping the structure. I have come to think it is not, and I have come to think this in a way that takes Salatin seriously rather than the other way.

## WHAT THE WORK ACTUALLY ACHIEVES

The case for regenerative agriculture, as a practice rather than a brand, is strongest when it is presented specifically. I want to take the time to present it specifically before anything else.

Start with soil. The conventional row-crop regime in the United States has been losing topsoil for a century. USDA Natural Resources Conservation Service modeling using the Revised Universal Soil Loss Equation places average annual erosion rates across conventionally managed cropland at roughly four to five tons per acre, against natural soil-formation rates of a half ton or so. The result, on aggregate, is the loss of something on the order of ninety centimeters of topsoil across the major US cropping regions since the start of European farming on the continent.<sup>2</sup> Rotationally grazed pasture, by contrast, does not erode at anything like that rate. Machner and colleagues, reviewing long-term grazing trials across the southern and central United States, reported that well-managed perennial pasture under adaptive multi-paddock grazing can build soil organic carbon at rates on the order of 0.3 to 1 ton of carbon per hectare per year in the upper soil horizon over multi-decadal time frames, in a system from which the ruminants are simultaneously producing meat.<sup>3</sup> The soil carbon and the meat are not trading against each other. They are being produced together.

The animal-welfare comparison is not close. A broiler chicken in a conventional confined house lives thirty-five to forty-two days in a structure whose stocking density is regulated, in the United States, by the industry's own standards rather than by federal law.<sup>4</sup> The bird typically cannot turn around without stepping on another bird and cannot exhibit most of its species-typical behaviors at all. A broiler on pasture lives in daylight on grass, in a covered mobile structure moved once a day, with access to insects, seeds, and whatever small animals it can catch. The life is shorter than wild chickens' lives and is still ended by slaughter, but within the window of the bird's life the quality of that life is, by any reasonable behavioral measure, incomparable. The same comparison holds for laying hens, for pastured pigs, and for cattle, though with different gradients. Confined animal feeding operations have imposed a kind of welfare compression that has no real historical precedent in Western agriculture, and pasture-based systems do not operate at that compression. On the welfare axis, the difference is not a matter of degree but of kind.

The operation's internal design deserves credit on its own terms as well. Polyface's signature move, and the move most imitated across the pastured-livestock literature, is the integration of species in rotation across the same ground. Cattle graze first. The pasture is then rested for a set interval, during which the cow manure ages and fly larvae develop in the pats. The broilers follow in mobile field shelters, eating the larvae and other insects mobilized by the cattle, adding their own manure to the surface, and continuing to be moved forward. Laying hens, in a separate mobile structure Salatin calls the "eggmobile," circulate through the same ground in a slightly different phase of the rotation, completing the fly-cycle disruption and contributing a different manure-deposition pattern. Pigs are used elsewhere on the operation for targeted tillage and forest-floor disturbance. The design moves biological work: pest control, manure distribution, vegetation management, nutrient cycling, from purchased inputs and mechanical operations into the animals themselves. It is a serious piece of agroecological engineering — and it has earned the attention it has received. Any fair assessment of the operation starts by acknowledging that the integration works and works well within its own perimeter.

The rural economic picture is similar in structure. A typical industrial broiler contract puts the grower in capital debt to a vertically integrated processor, with most of the margin on the bird captured at processing and distribution.<sup>5</sup> The grower absorbs the capital cost, the environmental externalities, and the labor; the processor absorbs most of the profit. A pasture-based operation at Polyface's scale, by contrast, captures significantly more of the value of the bird on the farm itself. Salatin has argued for decades that rural economic renewal runs through the direct-marketed farm rather than through the contract-grower arrangement, and he is right about this as a matter of basic accounting. Where pasture-based operations exist at scale in a region, they support local processing infrastructure, farm-to-consumer distribution networks, agritourism, and a retained agricultural workforce that the contract system has hollowed out. That hollowing has been measurable for decades. The rural sociology literature, some of the longest-running sociological work on American agriculture, has documented across fifty years of comparative studies beginning with Walter Goldschmidt's original comparisons of Arvin and Dinuba in the 1940s that the consolidation of US livestock and commodity production has coincided with measurable declines in rural population, retail employment, small-town median income, and civic participation across regions where the transition from diversified to contract production has been most complete.<sup>6</sup> The pasture-based operation is working against that curve. The people Polyface employs live in the Shenandoah Valley. The money Polyface earns is spent in the Shenandoah Valley. The operation is not extracting value from its region toward a distant headquarters — which is what the contract model does structurally.

The carbon case for well-managed grazing, finally, is genuine. This is not the same thing as saying it is sufficient. Richard Teague and colleagues argued in a 2016 paper in the *Journal of Soil and Water Conservation* that adaptive multi-paddock grazing, if scaled across existing North American grasslands, could plausibly offset a meaningful fraction of North American agriculture's greenhouse gas footprint through soil carbon accumulation and reduced reliance on synthetic nitrogen fertilizer.<sup>7</sup> The Teague paper is the most forceful peer-reviewed articulation of the climate case for regenerative grazing, and I take it seriously. I will come back to its limits later in this chapter — because the limits are real — but the positive case is not fabricated. Well-managed grazing genuinely pulls carbon into soil — and it does so while producing food — and the effect is not a rounding error.

Put all of that together and you have a system that is, by any fair accounting, substantially better across several important axes than what it is trying to replace. It builds soil. It produces food. It restores something close to normal animal lives. It supports rural economies. It sequesters carbon. None of those achievements is imaginary — and none of them should be held at arm's length in what follows.

## APPLYING THE FRAMEWORK

Run the four-axis framework across this version of the operation — and the scoring changes noticeably from the industrial comparison in the previous chapter.

On the environmental axis, pasture-based operations score well. Soil carbon accumulates rather than erodes. Chemical inputs — synthetic fertilizer, herbicide, insecticide, fungicide, antibiotic — are absent or dramatically reduced, meaning the downstream externalities of runoff, resistance, and non-target toxicity are absent or dramatically reduced. Biodiversity on and around the operation is higher: perennial pastures host arthropod, avian, and small-mammal assemblages that row-crop fields cannot support.<sup>8</sup> Water infiltration rates into rotationally grazed soil are higher than into tilled soil by substantial multiples, with consequent reductions in flood peaks and downstream sedimentation.<sup>9</sup> The environmental axis, in a pasture-based system run competently, moves from the deep negative register of the industrial score into a positive register that is not marginal.

On the social axis, the picture is similarly improved. Worker conditions on a pasture-based operation are not the worker conditions of an industrial slaughterhouse. The operation sits inside the community it feeds rather than at the end of a commodity chain that terminates at a processor two states away. Rural community indicators — employment, retention, median income for agricultural families — track with the presence of diversified direct-marketed agriculture in ways that have been documented for a generation now.<sup>6</sup> The social axis improves.

On the economic axis, the scoring is conditional and depends on scale. At the scale of a Polyface or a comparable operation, several hundred acres, owner-operated, direct-marketed, the economics work: the farm is profitable, the family is supported, the next generation has a ladder onto the operation if one of them wants it. The work is not easy and the margins are not wide, but the operation is economically viable in a way that much of conventional small-scale commodity agriculture is not. At the scale of the continental food supply, the economic axis becomes much harder to score because the system as currently practiced has never operated at that scale. Scaling a direct-marketed pastured-poultry model from one operation in the Shenandoah Valley to, say, the broiler demand of a metropolitan Atlantic seaboard would require land, processing infrastructure, labor supply, and concentrated feed in volumes that the existing direct-market architecture has not produced and the existing commodity architecture cannot produce without compromising the ecological distinctions that make the pasture-based operation worth doing in the first place. This is not a case against the operation at its own scale. It is a case for treating the operation's achievements as operation-scale achievements, which is how Salatin himself has generally framed them. The claim that what Polyface does could be extended to feed the country is a claim that has rarely been made by Polyface itself. It has more often been made by journalists and advocates around the operation, and the operation is not responsible for the claims made on its behalf.

On the survivability axis, pasture-based operations score substantially better than industrial ones, which is the axis the industrial system scored worst on. The operation does not depend on continuous diesel for its own field work in the same way a row-crop operation does, because most of the field work is being done by the animals themselves. It does not depend on continuous antibiotic administration, because the stocking densities and the rotation pattern handle the disease pressure that makes antibiotics necessary in confined systems. It does not depend on continuous federal subsidies for its own outputs, because those outputs are priced into a direct market rather than a commodity one. The system is more survivable than what it replaced.

But the survivability score is not the maximum possible score, and the reason the score is not maximum is the subject of the rest of this chapter. The operation, as currently practiced, still depends on something the industrial system supplies — that dependency is the structural ceiling.

## THE STRUCTURAL CEILING

Regenerative agriculture, in its current operative form in the United States, is structurally tethered to the industrial commodity system it opposes. This is not a statement about any particular operator's motives or honesty — it is a statement about how the economics and the supply chain of the current US agricultural system constrain even the operations explicitly trying to operate outside it.

The ceiling shows up in different places at different operations. For operations that raise ruminants on pasture — grass-finished beef, pastured lamb, grass-fed dairy — the ceiling is lower and the tether is thinner, because ruminants can live most or all of their lives on forage produced on the farm itself and require relatively little from outside the operation. For operations that raise monogastric animals on pasture —broilers, layers, pigs — the ceiling is higher and the tether is thicker, because monogastrics cannot live on forage alone and must be supplemented with concentrated protein and energy that, under current conditions, comes from somewhere else.

Polyface is the worked example because it is the operation whose numbers are most completely in the public record. The numbers are worth walking through carefully.

## THE POLYFACE MATH

Polyface's broiler operation, at the scale Michael Pollan described in *The Omnivore's Dilemma*, processes approximately twelve thousand broiler chickens per year in mobile field shelters moved daily across pasture.<sup>10</sup> Salatin's own *Pastured Poultry Profit$* gives the feed conversion ratio for the birds as roughly three pounds of feed for each pound of finished body weight — with an average finished weight in the neighborhood of four pounds per bird.<sup>11</sup> The arithmetic from those numbers is direct. Twelve thousand birds, at four pounds of body weight each, at three pounds of feed per pound of body weight, comes to approximately one hundred and forty-four thousand pounds of feed per year for the broiler flock alone.

The composition of that feed is also in the public record. Polyface has published its layer and broiler ration specifications; independent writing on the farm, including longer profiles and follow-up interviews over the years, has confirmed the proportions. The ration is built around roughly forty-nine to fifty percent corn and roughly thirty to thirty-one percent soy, with the remainder made up of mineral premix, fish meal, kelp, and a few other supplements.<sup>12</sup> Applied to the total feed figure, that means roughly seventy-one thousand five hundred pounds of corn and roughly forty-four thousand five hundred pounds of soybean meal per year, for the broiler flock alone. The layer flock, smaller but year-round, adds to these totals. So do the pigs that spend part of their finishing period on concentrate.

The corn and soy do not come from Polyface's own acres. The farm's perennial pasture system is not arranged for row cropping, and the Shenandoah Valley's climate and soils are not particularly suited to corn on the scale required. The feed comes from off-farm sources — grain sold by neighboring farmers and feed mills in the broader region of the valley and adjacent states. Salatin has written openly about this sourcing, and has emphasized that the farm works to buy from local non-GMO producers rather than from anonymous commodity channels.<sup>13</sup> The sourcing preference is real. The underlying point is that even with the most scrupulous regional sourcing, the corn and soy feeding the Polyface birds are grown under the conditions that most corn and soy in the United States are grown under: annual row crop, tillage or herbicide-based residue management, synthetic nitrogen inputs, petroleum-powered field operations, and — directly or indirectly — the federal commodity support system that keeps the price of corn and soy low enough for a pastured-poultry operation to purchase them profitably. The industrial substrate is the substrate.

I want to be careful about how this finding is registered, because the framing matters.

It is not a gotcha. Salatin publishes these numbers himself, in his own books, for farmers who want to replicate his system. The feed proportions are printed in *Pastured Poultry Profit$*; the scale is in *The Omnivore's Dilemma* and subsequent profiles. There is no concealment to expose. Anyone who has read the source material with any attention has encountered the math. The math was already public, in that sense, when any of us started having this conversation.

It is also not a personal failure. The operator is working inside the structural conditions of US agriculture as those conditions currently exist. Grass-finishing a broiler in any meaningful volume is not, under present conditions, economically possible at a price point that sustains a family farm. The price of corn and soy has been held down by half a century of agricultural policy; the cost of land, labor, and processing has not. An operator deciding, in 1995 or 2005 or 2025, whether to build a pastured broiler enterprise has faced a narrow set of real choices, and at every point in that window the economically survivable choice has involved purchasing grain from someone else. The alternative — growing the grain on the farm under perennial-compatible practice — is not absent from the regenerative literature, but it has not, to my knowledge, been implemented at any scale that makes broiler operations like Polyface's replaceable.

The point is worth sharpening, because the sharpening matters for everything that comes after. An operation like Polyface is doing the most that is currently doable inside the existing system. The ceiling on what it can do sits not at the gate of the farm but at the edge of the commodity market the farm buys from. Raising the ceiling would require changing the commodity market, which is a different kind of problem than changing how a farm is managed. An individual farmer can change how the farm is managed; an individual farmer cannot change the structure of US corn and soy production. The work Salatin and operators like him have done on the manageable side is the work they could do. The work on the substrate side is the work the rest of the food system has not done.

What the finding is, then, is a structural observation. Even the best version of regenerative agriculture as currently practiced, the operation with the most documented soil gains, the most direct-marketed income, the most visible animal welfare improvements, the most sustained rural economic impact, still has its monogastric-animal enterprises running on carbohydrate and protein inputs produced by the industrial commodity system it opposes. The system it is offering as the alternative is, in its concentrate-dependent component, riding on the substrate it wants to replace.

This is the structural ceiling. It is not the end of the argument — it is the thing the argument has to take seriously before it continues.

## THE MCWILLIAMS-SALATIN EXCHANGE

The substrate question became public in a way most non-specialists would remember when James McWilliams published "The Myth of Sustainable Meat" in the *New York Times* in April of 2012.<sup>14</sup> McWilliams, a historian at Texas State, had been arguing for several years that the environmental advantages of pasture-based animal production were overstated. The op-ed compressed the argument. It made several claims, some of which were wrong, one of which was right, and the combination produced a public exchange that illustrated the limits of the conversation as then framed more clearly than either side would likely have wanted.

McWilliams's central environmental claim, that pasture-raised beef produces substantially more methane per unit of beef than feedlot beef because grass-finished animals live longer and eat more lignocellulose, was overstated as applied. The methane-per-unit-beef numbers are genuinely higher on pasture, but the comparison is incomplete without the soil carbon side of the ledger, which can offset a meaningful fraction of the methane difference in well-managed systems. The Teague analysis and the subsequent literature have laid this out in more detail than the op-ed allowed for.<sup>7</sup> The land-use claim, that pasture requires more acres per pound of finished animal than feedlot, was true on its own terms but also incomplete. The relevant question is not acres-per-pound but which kind of land is being used, for what, under what set of effects on downstream ecosystems. A great deal of pastureland is land that cannot productively support row crops.

The claim McWilliams made that landed, and landed in a way neither he nor his critics particularly wanted to admit, was the feed-sourcing claim. He pointed out that pasture-based operations raising monogastric animals still relied on purchased grain produced in the conventional industrial system, and that this reliance made the sustainability advantages of the pasture component narrower than their advocates claimed. On the feed-sourcing point, the math is the math, and it was already public in Salatin's own books.

Salatin's public response came through Polyface Farm's own channels and through subsequent essays and interviews.<sup>15</sup> The response was vigorous and, in places, sincere in a way the op-ed had not been about Salatin's work. Several of his counterpoints were substantive: the methane comparison was being handled more simplistically than the science warranted; the land-use comparison was ignoring land-use distinctions; Polyface's local non-GMO sourcing was real and deserved to be distinguished from anonymous commodity sourcing. One argument in particular deserves engagement on its own terms, because it is the most serious thing said in the exchange and because it gets close to the structural question without quite reaching it.

Salatin argued, in effect, that the movement of grain from grain-growing regions into grazing regions via animal feed is not a pathology of the industrial system. It is an ancient pattern. Historically, lowland grain-growing regions and upland grazing regions have exchanged nutrients through exactly this kind of trade: grain flowed to the uplands as animal feed, and animal products flowed back to the lowlands as food. The pattern long predates petroleum agriculture, commodity subsidies, and the US Department of Agriculture. A pastured-poultry operation buying corn from a farmer fifty miles away is, under this framing, participating in a trade relationship as old as agriculture itself.

There is real history in that framing. The highland-lowland nutrient exchange is genuine, and it is defensible as a way of organizing agricultural landscapes. What the framing does not quite reach, in my reading of Salatin's reply, is the point underneath McWilliams's otherwise flawed argument. The historical highland-lowland exchange was regional and operated at a scale that did not require a continental commodity system. The modern version of the exchange operates at a scale that does. When Polyface sources non-GMO corn from within the Shenandoah region, it is still sourcing from farms whose own inputs, seed, fertilizer, herbicide, field equipment, transportation to market, run through the continental industrial substrate that corn and soy have been embedded in since roughly the Nixon administration's rewrite of the farm bill.<sup>16</sup> The regional-sourcing move reduces the transportation footprint and the genetic-engineering exposure. It does not detach the feed from the underlying substrate.

Salatin's response, read carefully, is best understood as a defense of the farming model's integrity at the farm-gate scale, and at that scale it is largely correct. It is not a response to the structural observation that the farm, as a node in a larger agricultural system, cannot fully escape that system as currently organized. McWilliams had framed the feed-sourcing point as an accusation, which it did not need to be and which made the response defensive. The point was better made as a structural observation. This is how I am trying to make it now.

What the 2012 exchange illustrated, in retrospect, was the limit of the conversation as then conducted. McWilliams wanted to indict the pasture model. Salatin wanted to defend it. Between the two positions there was not much room for the observation that the pasture model was genuinely doing most of what it claimed to do and was still riding on the industrial substrate to do it. Both of those are true. Neither side's framing in the 2012 exchange made that doubled picture easy to see. The conversation has moved somewhat since then. Versions of the structural observation now appear in places where they did not appear in 2012: in *Civil Eats* coverage, in the peer-reviewed agroecological literature, and in the internal conversations of regenerative-agriculture advocates who have been working on perennial-grain and on-farm feed alternatives precisely because the ceiling is a ceiling they can see from inside the movement.

> **FIELD NOTE: The Things Worth Doing**
>
> The first version of this chapter was easily the best chapter in the book. Everyone said so, from Robb Wolf to the publisher. They also did not want me to publish it.
>
