> It was scathing. *The math was scathing.* The chapter was passed on to another individual, someone close to the system the math was taking apart. Later that year, she approached me at a dinner in Austin and said, “If you publish this chapter, it would be the end of Organic farming as we know it.” I leaned in ominously and said, “If little ol’ Karen Pendergrass could say something that crushed that system, then it deserves to crumble. And it deserves to be built up on a better foundation.”
>
> That scathing chapter still lives in my head.
>
> The same person was a guest speaker at Expo West in Anaheim in 2015. She said something to me there I have never forgotten: “The things worth doing — are worth doing poorly at first.” I have repeated it more times than I can count, in conversations she is not in. Those words *softened* me. She was right, and I was wrong.
>
> She has, on other occasions, eye-rolled the whole idea of camels — *will people seriously eat camel?* — and I have eye-rolled back. Who knows. There is, for instance, a real demand for camel milk for autistic children. The question is worth answering nonetheless.
>
> Today I hold two conflicting views about Polyface. Both are true. But one, I believe, is truer.
>
> The things worth doing — are worth doing poorly at first.

## GRAZED AND CONFUSED

The climate side of the ledger deserves the same honest treatment. The case for well-managed grazing as a substantial climate mitigation lever is real but bounded. It is bounded in ways the most enthusiastic popular framing has not always absorbed.

Tara Garnett and colleagues at the Food Climate Research Network at the University of Oxford published *Grazed and Confused?* in 2017, which remains the most rigorous systematic assessment of grazing's climate claims.<sup>17</sup> The report reviewed the accumulated peer-reviewed evidence for soil carbon sequestration under improved grazing management and concluded that the sequestration effect is genuine, measurable, and at a global scale able to offset somewhere on the order of twenty to sixty percent of annual ruminant greenhouse gas emissions under optimistic assumptions. The report also concluded that the effect saturates after several decades. A grassland accumulating carbon under improved management does not continue accumulating at the initial rate indefinitely. The upper end of the estimated sequestration potential requires management conditions that are difficult to achieve and maintain at scale.

Teague and colleagues have pushed back on parts of the *Grazed and Confused?* analysis, arguing that the report underestimates the sequestration potential of adaptive multi-paddock grazing specifically and that it aggregates the grazing-management literature in a way that flattens differences between management regimes that are in fact ecologically distinct.<sup>18</sup> Their case is not implausible. The specific management regime matters: a continuous grazing system and an AMP system are not interchangeable from the soil's perspective, and the literature has not always treated them as distinct.

The saturation mechanism in the Garnett analysis deserves a moment of its own, because its implications are easy to miss in popular framings. Soil carbon accumulation under improved grazing is fastest in the early years after management change, when the soil is most below its equilibrium carbon level for the new management regime. As the soil approaches the new equilibrium, a process that takes in temperate grasslands somewhere on the order of twenty to forty years, the annual sequestration rate declines toward zero. The soil is not permanently scrubbing carbon out of the atmosphere the way an unsaturated chemical sink would. It is moving from one equilibrium to a higher equilibrium, and the movement is bounded. The total amount of carbon ultimately stored is meaningful; the rate of annual accumulation has an arc. Any long-run climate accounting for grazing has to incorporate this shape, and the popular framings often do not. This is not a criticism of Polyface or of any particular operator; it is a criticism of the rhetorical compression that has sometimes attended the climate argument for regenerative agriculture in the broader public conversation.

Read against each other, Garnett and Teague produce an honest calibration. Well-managed grazing is genuinely carbon-positive at the operation scale. It is not climate-neutral for ruminant production overall, even in the most optimistic scenarios. It is a meaningful contribution to the climate side of the agricultural ledger, substantially better than poorly managed grazing or feedlot, and not by itself a climate solution for ruminant agriculture. The claim that pastured livestock operations are climate-positive, stated carefully, is about the operation's own carbon balance rather than about the emissions the operation is also producing. Stated carefully, the claim is defensible. Stated as a blanket net-negative framing for ruminant agriculture as a sector, it is not.

None of this undoes the soil, welfare, or rural-economy case. All of those stand on their own. It does mean that the climate claim specifically needs to be stated with care. This care is compatible with the operation being ecologically serious, which it is.

## WHAT THE CEILING MEANS

The chapter has, I hope, been doing two things at once. It has been taking regenerative agriculture seriously as the most developed alternative to the industrial system on offer — and it has been looking at what the most developed alternative can and cannot do as currently practiced. Both of those are honest descriptions. The operation does what it does, and it does not do what it does not do, and the honest account requires holding both.

What the structural ceiling means, stated as plainly as I can state it, is this. Regenerative agriculture as currently organized in the United States is significantly better than what it is trying to replace. It is also still riding on the substrate it is trying to replace. Its monogastric-animal enterprises import tens of thousands of pounds of corn and soy per year from the industrial commodity system. Its ruminant enterprises depend less directly on that system but still operate inside the broader infrastructure: the diesel for equipment, the steel for fencing, the mineral supplements, the veterinary care, that the industrial substrate supplies. An operation like Polyface is an achievement inside a system whose boundaries the operation cannot fully escape.

This is not a defeat. It is a limit. Limits can be mapped and worked against. A substantial fraction of the regenerative-agriculture literature over the last decade has been exactly this work: perennial-grain breeding programs trying to make cereal production compatible with perennial root systems,<sup>19</sup> silvopastoral systems integrating timber, browse, and ruminant production on the same acreage, and multi-species rotation schemes that reduce the dependence on external concentrated feed. Every one of these is a real response to the structural ceiling. None of them has, as of this writing, produced the fully untethered alternative the framework I have been using would score highest.

The Land Institute's intermediate wheatgrass program, marketed under the trade name Kernza, is the most commercially advanced of these responses. After decades of breeding work in Salina, Kansas, Kernza now has commercial acreage in the low thousands of acres across the upper Midwest, a published grain market, and a small but real downstream supply chain in the craft-brewing and specialty-baking sectors. It is not yet a corn replacement at any scale that would matter to a Polyface feed order. It is not even a credible partial replacement at that scale. What it is is existence-proof that a deep-rooted perennial can be bred toward commercial grain production, and that a seed company and a farmer can, between them, make the numbers work at the margin. Silvopasture research in the southeastern United States and the Mediterranean dehesa literature offer similar existence proofs on the animal side: systems in which ruminants, browse, mast, and tree crops coexist on the same acreage under management regimes that resemble pre-industrial agroforestry more than they resemble modern pasture-based operations. The point is not that any of these is ready to replace the current substrate. The point is that the work of designing an alternative substrate is actively under way, even if the finished substrate is not yet here.

The question that closes this chapter is the question Part III of the book exists to take up. If the best version of the currently-available alternative is structurally tethered to what it opposes, even at the scale of a farm as studied and careful as Polyface, what would actually escape the substrate? What would an operation look like whose feed comes from the landscape rather than from the commodity system, whose animals are selected for the landscape rather than for the commodity market, whose relationship to the land the continent sits on runs through the land's deeper history rather than through the last two centuries of row-crop engineering?

I think the answer lives in the direction Paul Martin has been pointing us since the 1970s. The landscape this continent is, considered on the scale the continent has actually existed on, is not a landscape that evolved under row-crop corn and feedlot cattle. It is a landscape that evolved under a suite of large-bodied animals most of which have not been present for twelve thousand years. The animals we use to feed ourselves now are, in the main, animals whose ecological partnership is with somewhere else. The animals whose ecological partnership is with this continent are, in the main, not being used to feed us at all. That mismatch is the hinge of what follows.

Regenerative agriculture has taken the food system as far as it has been able to take it while working within the cast of species it inherited and the commodity substrate that cast depends on. The distance it has covered is real and should not be given back. What comes next is a different question: whether the cast of species can be reopened, and whether, reopened, it changes what sustainability looks like on this continent. The next chapter starts there.

# Chapter Seven: The Wrong Proxy in the Right Place

> *From Paul S. Martin, Twilight of the Mammoths (2005)*
>
> *“From fossils we know that the grasses, forbs, shrubs, and trees of the Americas coevolved with a much greater variety of large herbivores than*
>
> *exists today.”*
>
> *The thorns are not decorative. They are anti‑herbivore defenses calibrated to*
>
> *animals the continent has not carried for twelve thousand years.*
>
> *— P. S. Martin, p. 185*

The first time I saw Allan Savory speak in person was at his very first conference. There were maybe twenty-five people there, total. Maybe. It was a small classroom: no podium, no stage, no microphone. A handful of ranchers, a few people who had read the book and wanted to meet the man behind it, and me, in my twenties, with a marked-up copy of Paul Martin's *Twilight of the Mammoths* in my bag. I had read Savory's book twice by that point. I was not there to be convinced. I was there because I had a question.

The room was small enough that you could see everyone's face. Savory spoke with the calm authority of someone who had been making the same argument for decades and had outlasted most of his critics, and even at his age, there was something about him that felt younger than I had expected: a quickness, an energy, a sense that the ideas were still alive in him rather than calcified into a script. The talk he gave was genuinely good, substantive, grounded in decades of observation. He showed photographs of degraded rangeland in Zimbabwe and the same rangeland after years of planned grazing. He showed soil profiles. He showed vegetation transects. The evidence was real, and he presented it without overselling, which I respected. A lot of people in the regenerative space oversell. Savory did not need to. Only a small number of people can say they were in that room to witness such a great man on the way to becoming who he is today. I photobombed him afterward.

I asked him whether holistic management worked everywhere, or whether it worked better in some landscapes than others.

He paused. Then he said it was not working in the arid Southwest.

The room was small enough that you could hear the shift — the way twenty people adjusted in their chairs when the answer was not the one they expected. Savory did not hedge. He did not qualify it into nothing. He said the results had not tracked in the Southwest the way they had tracked in southern Africa or the Great Plains. He said it with the particular kind of candor that serious people use when they are being honest about something they have not yet resolved — not defensive, not dismissive, just honest.

I spent the rest of the session thinking about it. The talks continued. I took notes on a few of them, but my attention kept circling back to what Savory had said. The arid Southwest. The results had not tracked. I thought I knew why.

The food truck was parked at the edge of the conference grounds, one of those converted Airstream trailers with a sliding window and a short menu of things that could plausibly be called lunch. I got a plate and a bottle of water. Savory had also gotten a plate, and instead of eating outside we both took our food back into the classroom, and that is where the conversation happened. A few others wandered in and listened. I monopolized him during the entire lunch period. The food was good. Neither of us ate it until much later.

I should say what I had with me, because it matters. I had my conference bag, my phone, and a copy of Paul Martin's *Twilight of the Mammoths*. It was not a new copy. I had read it three times by that point, and it looked like it. The spine was cracked, the pages were dog-eared in places I had found worth returning to, and I had marked passages in the margins with the kind of shorthand that means nothing to anyone else: page numbers cross-referencing other pages, small arrows connecting ideas across chapters, question marks next to claims I had wanted to verify. It was a working copy of a book I had been thinking with for several years. I did not bring it to impress him. I brought it because I thought I might need to show him specific passages, and I did.

The conversation started where the Q&A had ended. I told him I thought I understood why the Southwest had been difficult, and that the answer was not in his framework but in the fossil record beneath the landscape his framework was being applied to. He looked at the book on the table between us. I opened it.

I introduced myself. I told him I ran a certification organization and that I had been working on food-system evaluation for years, which was true and also not the point. The point was the book. I told him I had been reading Paul Martin, the paleoecologist at the University of Arizona who had spent his career on the late Pleistocene extinctions and their ecological consequences. I thought Martin's work explained something about the Southwest that Savory's framework, on its own, could not.

He looked at the book on the table between us. He said he actually did know who Paul Martin was. And something in me shifted — I remember holding my hand out toward the book, a little wide-eyed, thinking: *Why has this work not been integrated with your work yet?* The fossil record and the management framework were sitting in the same intellectual space, pointed at the same landscapes, and no one had connected them. I told him the version I had been turning over in my head for months: that the Pleistocene fossil record preserves, in extraordinary detail, the specific animals that co-evolved with specific landscapes across the American continent, and that the record for the Southwest tells a story that does not involve cattle.

What I walked him through, and what I want to walk the reader through now because the argument is the hinge of everything that follows in this book, is a specific claim about the relationship between animal species and landscape. The claim is this: the fossil record tells us which animals co-evolved with which landscapes, and the American Southwest's fossil record does not say cattle.

Martin had spent decades building the evidentiary case. His chapter on the Grand Canyon, titled "Grand Canyon Suite," is the most concentrated version of it, and it is the chapter I had marked most heavily. The cave deposits along the Colorado River and its tributaries preserve one of the most detailed records of late Pleistocene fauna in the American West. Rampart Cave, a limestone overhang above the river in western Grand Canyon, contained stratified deposits of Shasta ground sloth dung, five feet of it in places, layered over thousands of years, preserved so well that the plant material in it could be identified to genus and sometimes species.<sup>1</sup> Stanton's Cave, downstream in Marble Canyon, held the bones and dung pellets of Harrington's mountain goat, an extinct species whose remains turned up alongside those of bighorn sheep, small equids, and a scattering of bison. Condor remains appeared at multiple cave sites. The assemblage that emerged from the deposits was specific: ground sloths, mountain goats, equids, camelids, condors. Not one of those animals is a bovid.

I explained this to Savory as plainly as I could. The ground sloths at Rampart Cave had been eating globe mallow, Mormon tea, saltbush, catclaw acacia, yucca, and cactus. Dick Hansen's composition analysis of the dung showed that desert globe mallow leaves and stem fragments made up roughly half the plant parts in the deposit, with Mormon tea pollen accounting for eighteen percent and the remainder distributed among saltbush, acacia, common reed, and various succulents.<sup>2</sup> What Hansen found almost none of was grass. The Shasta ground sloth, a thousand-pound browser that had occupied the Southwest for tens of thousands of years, was not a grazer. Neither were the camelids whose bones turn up across the basin-and-range country. The equids grazed, and so did the mammoths, but the overall assemblage was weighted heavily toward browsing, toward animals whose digestive systems and foraging behavior were calibrated to the woody, thorny, armed vegetation that still dominates the arid Southwest today.

Martin made the connection explicit in his later chapters. The thorny, oil-rich, tannin-laden shrubs of the Southwest — creosote, mesquite, catclaw acacia, the entire armored understory of the Sonoran and Chihuahuan deserts — carry the structural signatures of vegetation that evolved under heavy browsing pressure from large-bodied animals.<sup>3</sup> The thorns are not decorative. They are anti-herbivore defenses, and the herbivores they evolved against are the ones the fossil record preserves: ground sloths, camelids, and to a lesser extent the equids and proboscideans that ranged through the region. Martin put the principle as directly as anyone has: "the grasses, forbs, shrubs, and trees of the Americas coevolved with a much greater variety of large herbivores than exists today."<sup>4</sup> The vegetation is still shaped by the animals. The animals are no longer there.

> **FIELD NOTE: The Contract**
>
> For the record, when I eat fruits, I eat the seeds. I have ever since it occurred to me that there was a contract that we made with these fruits.
>
> The contract is simple. The plant grows a sweet, fragrant, nutritious package around a small hard seed. Something eats the package, carries the seed some distance from the parent tree, and deposits it in its own fertilizer. The plant invests in the package because the seed gets dispersal and a fertilized start. The animal invests in finding the fruit because the package is calorie-dense, and its appearance is, literally, an advertisement.
>
> Most of the animals the fruit trees of this continent made their contract with are gone. Mastodons, gomphotheres, ground sloths. The mouths that Osage orange and honey locust and pawpaw grew their fruit for are not coming. The tree drops its crop every autumn and most of what is down there will rot where it fell.
>
> I am not a mastodon. But I can keep one small piece of the contract. I eat the seeds when I eat the fruit. Dispersal in fertilizer is not guaranteed the way it once was. But there are few things in this world that actually want to be eaten. Fruits want to be eaten. Milk wants to be drunk. Grass wants to be grazed down. It seems to me that if a thing is asking, the polite response is to answer.

Savory listened. He did not interrupt, which I noticed. People who are performing attention interrupt. People who are actually paying attention do not. He asked a question about the camelids: where specifically the fossil evidence placed them, whether the Arizona deposits had been dated reliably. I turned to the pages I had marked and showed him the relevant passages. He read them. He asked about the equids, and I walked him through the Grand Canyon deposits and the broader Western record that Chapter 3 of this book has already covered. He asked about the dating methods, and I explained that the radiocarbon dates on the Rampart Cave deposits were among the most robust in the North American Pleistocene record: tens of thousands of years of stratified organic material, independently dated by multiple laboratories, cross-referenced against the geological stratigraphy of the cave itself.

He sat back in his chair. His plate was mostly untouched. He knew Martin's work. He said so. But he had not read it from this angle, had not connected the proxy question to the management question. He said something I will paraphrase rather than quote, because I did not record the conversation and I do not trust my memory to preserve his exact words across the years since: he said that in southern Africa, where the framework had been developed, the large herbivores were still present. The wildebeest, the zebra, the buffalo, the elephant — the animals the African grasslands had evolved under were still there, and the management methodology had been designed around their behavior. He had always known that the American situation was different in some respects. He had not thought about it from the direction I was pointing.

This was the moment the conversation pivoted, and it is the moment I want the reader to sit with, because the pivot matters. Savory was not admitting a flaw in his framework. He was recognizing a variable his framework had not needed to specify in the context where it was developed. In southern Africa, the proxy question does not arise, because the original animals are still there. You do not need to ask "which herbivore?" when the wildebeest and the zebra and the buffalo are standing in front of you. The question only becomes visible when the original animals are gone and you are choosing a substitute. And in the Americas, the original animals have been gone for twelve thousand years, and every herbivore currently on the landscape is a substitute, some closer to what the fossil record prescribes and some further away.

What I was suggesting was simple enough to say at a folding table over lunch, and difficult enough that almost no one had said it yet. The arid Southwest was calling for equid species: donkeys, horses, the animals whose lineage actually belonged to that landscape. It made sense to take a look at the fossils to get a better idea of the match with the flora of the area. Not just the animal, but the relationship between the animal and the vegetation: the specific co-evolutionary partnership that the fossil record preserves and the current management regime ignores.

I want to state the broader version of this argument with the same care I stated the lunch version at that table.

Allan Savory's holistic management framework is a genuine intellectual achievement. I do not say this as a courtesy before a critique, because what follows is not a critique. It is a refinement, one that I believe makes the framework significantly stronger.

The core of holistic management, as Savory and Jody Butterfield laid it out in *Holistic Management: A New Framework for Decision Making*, is the proposition that the relationship between large herbivores and grassland is not incidental but constitutive.<sup>5</sup> The grassland needs the animal. Not the animal standing in place, overgrazing a single patch until the roots give out, but the animal moving —concentrated by predation pressure or management pressure into dense herds that graze intensively, trample standing dead material into contact with the soil surface, deposit urine and dung, and then move on, leaving the grazed area to recover for an extended period before the herd returns. The cycle of impact and recovery is what the grassland evolved under, and in its absence the grassland degrades: the standing dead material oxidizes in place rather than being incorporated into the soil, the root systems weaken without the stimulus of defoliation, and the bare ground between grass plants widens until the system tips into desertification.

The framework is powerful, and I do not use that word casually. It explains a great deal about the dynamics of grassland degradation that rest-based conservation management has struggled to address, and the empirical results from southern Africa, from the Great Plains, and from managed operations around the world are real. The ranchers and land managers who have adopted holistic management and seen their landscapes recover are not imagining it. The grass is coming back. The soil biology is rebuilding. The water-infiltration rates are improving. The framework works.

The honest thing to add is that the empirical record on holistic management's specific claims is mixed at best and damning at worst. Briske and colleagues' 2008 systematic review of the rangeland literature found no support for the overstocking-reverses-desertification claim that has been the loudest version of Savory's argument in popular circulation. Hawkins's 2017 follow-up review reached similar conclusions. Replicated trials including those run by the Carbon Drawdown Initiative have found that holistic management does not produce, at the scales it has been tested at, the outcomes Savory's TED talk described.

I do not write that as a takedown. I write it because the chapter you are reading is taking Savory seriously, and taking him seriously means taking the criticism seriously too. The specific claims that have failed peer review are claims about cattle overstocking on degraded rangeland in arid zones reversing desertification. Those claims have not held up. What I want from this chapter is the underlying observation — that the missing-proxy problem is real and that some animal disturbance, on some land, in some configuration, is part of how the system kept itself running before we removed the disturbance. The argument is not that Savory was right about cattle. The argument is that the diagnostic question Savory was asking — what is the missing animal disturbance — is the right question, and the work of figuring out which proxies actually work in which places is still in progress.

I am not on team Savory. I am on the team of figuring things out, and that team needs the people who have run the systematic reviews more than it needs another defense of any single practitioner. The refinement that follows in this chapter is offered in that spirit.

What the framework does not do — and this is the refinement, not the critique —is specify which animal. Holistic management tells you that the landscape needs a large herbivore, managed in a way that mimics the evolutionary pressure the landscape evolved under. It tells you how to manage the animal: the timing, the density, the recovery period, the monitoring. What it does not tell you is which animal belongs in which landscape. It treats the herbivore slot as generic: fill it with whatever large ruminant is available, manage it correctly, and the system responds.

For the Great Plains, this works. The shortgrass prairie evolved under bison, and cattle, which are bovids like bison, occupy a similar enough ecological niche that the substitution holds. The grass species, the root architectures, the soil microbiology, the grazing-and-recovery dynamics are all calibrated to a large-bodied bovid grazer, and cattle are a large-bodied bovid grazer. The proxy is close enough that the framework's management methodology can do the rest of the work. Holistic management on the Great Plains is, in a meaningful sense, restoring a relationship that the landscape recognizes.

The Southwest is different. And the reason it is different is the reason Savory's results had not tracked there, and the reason I had come to the conference with Martin's book.

The Southwest did not evolve under bovids. The fossil record is unambiguous on this point. The dominant large herbivores of the late Pleistocene Southwest were ground sloths, camelids, and equids: browsers and mixed feeders whose digestive systems and foraging behaviors were adapted to the woody, thorny, browse-heavy vegetation of the arid basins and canyons. Bison were present in the Southwest in the late Pleistocene, but they were a minor component of the fauna, not the dominant one. The bones in Stanton's Cave included some bison, but the assemblage was overwhelmingly Harrington's mountain goat and equids.<sup>6</sup> The Rampart Cave deposits were ground sloth from top to bottom. The landscape's primary large-herbivore relationships were with animals that no longer exist, and those relationships were not with grazers.

When you put cattle on that landscape, even well-managed cattle under the best holistic management protocol available, you are asking a bovid grazer to fill an ecological role that was held by browsers and mixed feeders. The grass species of the arid Southwest, to the extent that they exist, did not evolve under the kind of intensive grazing pressure that the Great Plains grasses did. The shortgrass species of the Plains — the blue grama, the buffalo grass, the sideoats grama — co-evolved with bison over millions of years; their growth points sit at or below the soil surface, their root systems respond to defoliation with renewed vigor, their entire architecture is an adaptation to heavy, periodic grazing. The browse species of the Southwest — the acacias, the mesquite, the saltbush, the globe mallow, the yucca and prickly pear —evolved under a fundamentally different kind of pressure. They evolved to be eaten by animals with the body mass and the gut capacity to process woody, thorny, chemically defended plant tissue. Cattle do not browse acacia. Cattle do not eat globe mallow. Cattle do not strip mesquite pods. Cattle graze, and when the grass in the arid Southwest gives out under grazing pressure, the system does not recover the way a Great Plains grassland recovers, because the system was never organized around that kind of pressure in the first place. The browse thickens. The mesquite encroaches. The bare ground between the surviving grasses widens. And the conventional diagnosis — overgrazing, poor management, insufficient rest — misses the deeper cause. The animal is wrong for the landscape.

This is what I told Savory in the classroom over plates neither of us was eating. The holistic management framework provides the management methodology — the how. Martin's fossil record provides the species guide — the which. Together, they are stronger than either one alone. Separately, the framework has a blind spot in exactly the landscapes where the proxy mismatch is largest.

He did not reject it. He sat with it. He asked me whether anyone had tried running camelids in the Southwest under managed grazing protocols, and I told him the honest answer, which was that almost no one had, because the conversation about proxy selection had barely begun. Josh Donlan and his colleagues had published their provocative essay in *Nature* in 2005 proposing Pleistocene rewilding as a conservation strategy, arguing that the ecological histories of the Americas make the case for restoring large vertebrates, including proxies for extinct species, to landscapes that had co-evolved with them.<sup>7</sup> Donlan's longer treatment in *The American Naturalist* the following year had laid out the agenda more carefully, with specific proposals and specific risk assessments.<sup>8</sup> But the rewilding conversation had remained largely in the conservation-biology lane. Almost no one had connected it to the food-systems conversation. Almost no one had asked the question that seemed, to me, obvious: if the fossil record tells us which animals belong in which landscapes, and if those animals can be managed for food production, then the rewilding agenda and the food-systems agenda are the same agenda.

Savory, to his credit, saw the connection immediately. The man had spent his career arguing that the landscape needs the animal. He did not need to be persuaded of the principle. What the fossil record offered was a specification of the principle that his framework had left unspecified. The landscape needs *this* animal, in *this* place, because *this* is the animal the landscape's vegetation and soil biology evolved under. The proxy is not interchangeable. The proxy is chosen by the fossil record.

The principle extends far beyond the Southwest, and it extends far beyond the conversation I had with Savory in that classroom. Let me widen it.

> **CASE: Proxies Already in Use**
>
> The proxy concept this chapter has been developing — large herbivores selected to match the co-evolutionary record of a landscape — is not theoretical. Versions of it are running, on the ground, in several places. The catch is that in almost every case, the proxy chosen has been the proxy that was *available,* not the proxy the landscape co-evolved with. California: goats. Cal Fire and various municipal fire-prevention contractors deploy domesticated goat herds across thousands of acres of chaparral each year, driven there to reduce the fuel load that drives California's increasingly catastrophic fire seasons. The goats are effective at brush reduction. They are also Old World animals introduced to a continent whose chaparral co-evolved under browsing pressure from large American camelids, ground sloths, and a now-extinct mountain goat (*Oreamnos harringtoni*), none of which were goats in the Eurasian sense.
>
> Australia: cattle. Crown-land grazing experiments in the savanna woodlands of the Northern Territory have used cattle to reduce native grass fuel loads ahead of fire seasons, with measurable effects on fire intensity. The cattle are doing useful work. They are also doing it in a landscape whose original large grazers were marsupial — *Diprotodon* and the now-extinct megafauna of the Pleistocene Australian interior — and the proxy match between cattle and what was there is even looser than the cattle-for-bison match in North America.
>
> The pattern repeats. The proxy in use is the proxy at hand: cattle and goats are domesticated, available, regulatorily uncomplicated, and economically embedded in the food system. The proxy the fossil record points at — for the American Southwest, large camelids; for southern Africa, the still-living elephant; for the central Asian steppe, the species Pleistocene Park has been working with — is, in most cases, harder to procure, harder to permit, and easier to skip.
>
> Nobody has, to my knowledge, tried camels at any scale in the American Southwest specifically as a fire-and-desertification management proxy. The biology is in their favor: dromedaries do thrive on the thorny browse the chaparral and basin-and-range produce; the historical record places *Camelops* in those landscapes for most of their geological history; the regulatory framework Chapter Thirteen describes does not yet exist for proxies of any kind, but if it did, this would be a candidate. The experiment has, for the southwestern American case, never been run.

If the fossil record provides the species guide, and I believe the evidence from multiple continents and multiple research programs shows that it does, then the question of which animals belong in which landscapes is answerable on a continental scale. The answer is not uniform. It varies by region, by biome, by the specific evolutionary history of the specific landscape in question. And the variation is exactly what makes the principle useful, because the variation tells you something the current food system does not know it needs to know.

The Great Plains, as I have said, want bovids. The shortgrass prairie is a bovid landscape, and the best available proxy for the extinct *Bison antiquus* is its living descendant, *Bison bison*. Bison restoration on the Plains, the work American Prairie is doing in Montana and tribal nations are doing across the northern Plains, is proxy selection that the fossil record endorses. The management methodology that Savory provides is appropriate here, because the proxy is appropriate here. The framework and the fossil record agree.

The Southwest wants something different. The arid basins and canyons of Arizona, New Mexico, and the northern Sonoran and Chihuahuan deserts evolved under camelids, equids, and ground sloths. The ground sloths have no living proxy; no extant species closely replicates a thousand-pound herbivorous xenarthran. But the camelids and equids do. Camels are already in the Americas: feral in parts of the West, stabled on hobby ranches, used in tourism and sport. Dromedaries thrive in arid heat, browse thorny shrubs that cattle will not touch, and produce milk with higher fat and protein content than cow's milk, a fact that matters if the question is not just which animal belongs here but what the animal provides. Horses and donkeys are already in the Americas: feral in vast herds across the basin and range, managed on public and private land, embedded in the cultural and economic fabric of the West. Donkeys in particular are superbly adapted to the arid Southwest: hardy in heat, efficient on sparse forage, and as willing to browse as to graze. The small equids whose bones turned up in Stanton's Cave were closer in size to a donkey than to a modern ranch horse. The species are here. What is missing is the ecological framing that would put them in the role the fossil record identifies as theirs. A camel on Southwest rangeland is not an exotic introduction. It is a proxy for *Camelops*, returning to a landscape its lineage shaped for millions of years before a twelve-thousand-year interruption. A donkey or a horse on that same rangeland is not a feral pest. It is the closest living relative of the Pleistocene equids whose bones lie in the canyon deposits, returning to an ecological role that the vegetation still remembers. The Southwest does not need more cattle. It needs the animals it lost.

The eastern hardwood forests present yet another case, and one that connects directly to the ecological-anachronism argument the Prologue introduced. The Pleistocene fauna of the eastern woodlands included mastodons, the dominant large-bodied browser of the deciduous forests, whose range extended from the Great Lakes to the Gulf Coast, along with woodland-adapted ground sloths, elk, deer, and a suite of smaller herbivores. The Osage orange, the pawpaw, the honey locust, the Kentucky coffee tree. These megafaunal-dispersed fruit trees, which Janzen and Martin identified as ecological anachronisms, are eastern species, and the mouths they evolved for were mastodon and ground-sloth mouths.<sup>9</sup> There is no living proxy for an American mastodon. But the principle still operates: the eastern hardwood food system should be oriented around the animals that the eastern hardwood fossil record identifies, which are browsers and mixed feeders, not open-grassland grazers. Silvopastoral systems in the eastern woodlands — integrating ruminant browse, mast, and tree crops on the same acreage — are closer to the fossil record's prescription than any open-pasture cattle operation, even a well-managed one.

The Gulf Coast and the subtropical Southeast present their own puzzle. The Pleistocene fauna of Florida and the coastal plain included tapirs, capybara, llamas, ground sloths, glyptodonts, and a suite of animals whose nearest living relatives now occupy Central and South America. The fossil presence of vampire bats and tapirs in Arizona and California, animals whose modern ranges are exclusively tropical, tells us that the ecological communities of the southern United States were connected to Neotropical faunas in ways that the current landscape does not reflect. Martin noted that tapirs ranged from the Sonoran lowlands to over six thousand feet on the Colorado Plateau, and that their late-Quaternary disappearance is more reasonably modeled as a retreat to tropical refugia under predation pressure than as a response to climate change.<sup>10</sup> The proxy implications for the subtropical Southeast are different from those for the Southwest or the Plains: the animal assemblage the landscape evolved under was more diverse, more Neotropical in character, and farther from anything the current food system provides.

The boreal and subarctic tell a different story again. The Pleistocene fauna of the far north included woolly mammoths, steppe bison, horses, muskoxen, and caribou. Caribou and muskoxen survive. The mammoth and the steppe bison do not. The proxy question here is whether elephants, African or Asian, could serve as mammoth proxies in the boreal, a question Donlan and his colleagues raised explicitly and that the Pleistocene Park experiment in Siberia has begun to test with horses, bison, muskoxen, and yak.<sup>11</sup> The point is not that elephants are about to be introduced to Alaska. The point is that the principle, the fossil record tells you which animal, applies to every landscape on the continent, and the answers it gives are not the same from one landscape to the next.

The map that emerges from this exercise is nothing like the map the food system currently operates from. The current map is simple: cattle everywhere, with regional adjustments for breed and forage type. The fossil-record map is complex: bovids on the Plains, camelids and equids in the arid West, browsers and mixed feeders in the eastern hardwoods, Neotropical assemblages in the subtropical South, Arctic-adapted species in the far north. The current map treats the continent as a single production surface. The fossil-record map treats it as what it actually is — a mosaic of distinct evolutionary histories, each with its own set of animal relationships, most of which have been unreciprocated for a hundred and twenty centuries.

This is what the current food system misses entirely. The industrial system treats animals as interchangeable production units and landscapes as interchangeable production surfaces. The regenerative-agriculture movement, for all its real achievements, has treated the proxy question as settled — cattle for grasslands, chickens and pigs for everything else — without consulting the record that would tell it whether those proxies are correct. Savory's framework gets the management right and leaves the species unspecified. Martin's fossil record specifies the species and does not provide the management. The synthesis, holistic management guided by paleontological proxy selection, is what neither framework achieves alone.

I am not proposing that the synthesis is easy to implement. I am proposing that it is the correct framework, and that the difficulty of implementation is not an argument against its correctness. The permits for running camels on Southwest rangeland do not currently exist in most jurisdictions. The infrastructure for processing camel or horse meat for the US market does not currently exist at any meaningful scale. The cultural resistance to eating horses in the United States is real and historically deep, and I am not dismissing it. That resistance has its own history, its own logic, and its own emotional weight, and a framework that requires people to change what they eat had better take seriously the reasons they eat what they eat now. The ecological risk assessments for introducing proxy species to landscapes they have not occupied in twelve thousand years are genuinely complex, and the critics of Pleistocene rewilding, Rubenstein and colleagues' careful objections in particular, deserve the engagement they will receive in Chapter 9 of this book.<sup>12</sup> None of these obstacles are trivial. All of them are navigable.

What I am saying is that the question "which animal belongs here" has an answer, and the answer is in the ground beneath our feet. The fossil record is not a metaphor. It is a dataset. It contains the bones and the dung and the pollen and the diet profiles of the animals that co-evolved with every landscape on this continent, and those profiles tell us — specifically, empirically, verifiably — which species the landscape's vegetation, soil biology, and ecological dynamics evolved under. When we ignore that record and put cattle on a landscape that evolved under camelids, we are making a proxy-selection error. When we compound that error by building an entire food system around the wrong proxy, we are making the error structural.

The chapter before this one ended with a question: what would actually escape the substrate? What would an operation look like whose animals are selected for the landscape rather than for the commodity market?

The answer begins with the fossil record and a worn copy of Martin's book on a folding table between two people who care about the land. Savory spent his career arguing that the landscape needs the animal. Martin spent his career identifying which animal the landscape needs. The food-truck conversation was the moment I understood that the two frameworks are halves of the same argument, and that the argument, stated whole, reorganizes everything the food system thinks it knows about which species belong where.

I think about that conversation often. I think about Savory sitting with the information, not arguing, not deflecting, just absorbing it and asking the right questions. I think about Martin's book on the table between us — marked up, worn out, full of someone else's lifetime of work distilled into a set of observations so straightforward that the only mystery is why they have not yet reorganized the conversation they belong to. I think about the folding table, the food truck, the conference going on inside without us, and two people outside eating lunch and talking about which animals belong in which places on a continent that has been making do without most of its animals for longer than there has been agriculture.

The synthesis is not mine. The management framework belongs to Savory. The species framework belongs to Martin. What I am doing, what this book is doing, is putting the two together and asking what follows. What follows is the subject of the next chapter: from the principle to the plate. What does it actually look like to eat from landscapes managed under proxy assemblages the fossil record endorses? The question is not abstract. The animals are real, some of them are already here, and the landscapes are waiting.

# Chapter Eight: Rewilding the Plate

The previous chapter ended with a principle: the fossil record tells you which animal belongs in which landscape, and the management framework tells you how to manage the animal once it is there. The principle is clean. What I want to do now is show what the principle looks like when it touches actual ground: real landscapes, real animals, real food being produced and consumed by real people, today, not in some aspirational future that requires a revolution before breakfast.

The vision this chapter describes is not hypothetical. Parts of it are already operating. Other parts are within reach of deliberate effort. The honest assessment of scale, what rewilded food systems can and cannot do at present, comes later in the chapter, because the honest assessment matters and the book's credibility depends on not overselling. But the starting point is what exists, because what exists is more substantial than most people in the food-systems conversation realize.

## WHAT ALREADY EXISTS

Start with the feral hog.

Texas is home to an estimated 2.6 million feral hogs (*Sus scrofa*), descendants of domestic pigs escaped or released over the centuries since Spanish colonization, now established across every one of the state's 254 counties.<sup>1</sup> The animals are ecological demolition crews. They root through native grassland, destroy ground-nesting bird habitat, contaminate waterways, damage agricultural crops to the tune of an estimated $52 million per year in direct agricultural losses in Texas alone, and compete with native wildlife for mast and forage.<sup>2</sup> They are, by any ecological measure, an invasive species causing measurable harm. They are also, by any nutritional measure, a substantial source of animal protein living on land that did not ask for them.

The dual value is underappreciated in the food-systems conversation. Texas Parks and Wildlife Department reports that hunters harvest approximately 750,000 feral hogs per year in the state, a figure that does not keep pace with the animals' reproductive rate. Feral sows can produce two litters per year, averaging five to six piglets per litter, and populations can double in four months under favorable conditions.<sup>3</sup> The harvest, even at three-quarters of a million animals annually, is not reducing the population. It is converting a fraction of the ecological damage into usable food. The meat is lean, free-range in the most literal sense of the term, and produced without any of the inputs that industrial pork requires: no commodity feed, no confinement infrastructure, no antibiotics, no manure lagoons, no federal subsidies for corn. The animal feeds itself on the landscape, damages the landscape in the process, and the harvest simultaneously produces food and reduces the damage.

I am not romanticizing feral hogs. They are genuinely destructive, and their management is a serious and expensive problem. What I am pointing out is that the food-system value of their harvest is almost never counted in the food-systems literature, because the animals do not fit any of the categories the literature recognizes. They are not livestock. They are not game in the managed sense. They are not rewilded proxy species. They are an accident of colonial history that happens to produce millions of pounds of protein per year as a byproduct of pest management. The protein is real. The ecological benefit of the harvest is real. The fact that neither appears in any sustainability assessment I have encountered tells us something about the narrowness of what the assessments are measuring.

Bison are a different case: not an accident but a deliberate restoration, and one already well under way.

The American bison, *Bison bison*, is the only surviving North American megaherbivore from the Pleistocene assemblage this book has described. It survived the terminal extinction pulse, persisted through the Holocene in herds that European observers described in the millions, was reduced by the commercial hide hunt of the 1870s and 1880s to fewer than a thousand individuals, and has since been rebuilt to a continental population of roughly 500,000, most of them in commercial herds managed as livestock, roughly 20,000 in conservation herds managed primarily for genetic diversity and ecological function.<sup>4</sup>

The conservation herds are the ones that matter for this chapter's argument. American Prairie, a private land conservation initiative in northeastern Montana, is assembling the largest contiguous tract of rewilded grassland in the temperate United States: currently over 450,000 acres of deeded and leased land along the Missouri River breaks, with a long-term acquisition target that would create a continuous habitat block adjoining the Charles M. Russell National Wildlife Refuge.<sup>5</sup> Bison are central to the project. American Prairie's herd, managed for genetic diversity and free-ranging behavior rather than for commercial production metrics, is intended to restore the grazing dynamics the northern Great Plains evolved under: concentrated impact, long recovery, nutrient redistribution across a landscape large enough that the animals' movement patterns can express themselves without fencing constraints.

The food-system dimension is real and deliberate. American Prairie's bison produce meat, marketed under the Wild Sky Beef brand, that is sold commercially as a revenue stream supporting the conservation mission. The animals are not supplemented with grain. They eat what the prairie grows. They are harvested by skilled marksmen in the field rather than transported to a processing facility, which eliminates both the transport stress and the infrastructure dependency. The product that reaches the consumer is, in a meaningful sense, food from a rewilded landscape: an animal that belongs on that landscape by any baseline the fossil record supports, managed under conditions that approximate the ecological dynamics the landscape evolved under, producing food as a function of its ecological role rather than in spite of it.

Tribal nations across the northern Plains are doing related work on a different institutional and cultural foundation. The InterTribal Buffalo Council, a cooperative of more than seventy-six tribal nations, has facilitated the restoration of bison to tribal lands across nineteen states, with a collective herd now numbering in the tens of thousands.<sup>6</sup> The restoration is simultaneously cultural and ecological: bison are central to the spiritual and subsistence traditions of many Plains nations, and their return to tribal land is an act of cultural restoration as much as an ecological one. The food produced from these herds feeds tribal communities directly, in some cases through programs that distribute bison meat as a healthier alternative to the commodity foods that federal nutrition programs have historically provided to reservation communities. The survivability profile of these operations is notable: the animals require no purchased feed, no confinement infrastructure, and no industrial inputs beyond the fencing and water infrastructure that any large-herbivore operation requires. The dependency profile is thin. The system functions because the animal and the landscape have a relationship that predates agriculture itself.

Wild-caught fish from intact fisheries represent another existing component that is already producing food from systems the industrial model has not organized. The Alaska salmon fisheries are the clearest case. Alaska's constitution mandates sustainable management of its fisheries, and the state's management regime, built on the stock-assessment methodology that Ray Hilborn and Carl Walters systematized in their foundational *Quantitative Fisheries Stock Assessment*, is among the most rigorous in the world.<sup>7</sup> The system works by monitoring escapement: the number of spawning adults that pass upstream past counting stations to reproduce. Harvest is permitted only when escapement targets are met. In years when returns are low, fisheries are restricted or closed entirely. The result is a fishery that has sustained commercial-scale harvest for decades without the stock collapses that have characterized poorly managed fisheries elsewhere.

Hilborn and colleagues, in a 2020 analysis in *PNAS*, demonstrated that this kind of science-based management works at a global scale: fisheries under intensive management have, on the whole, been improving in stock status, while unmanaged or poorly managed fisheries continue to decline.<sup>8</sup> The finding matters for the food-systems argument because it establishes that wild-harvest food production from intact ecosystems is not inherently extractive or unsustainable. It is extractive when it is unmanaged. It is sustainable when the management matches the biology. Alaska's salmon fisheries produce hundreds of millions of pounds of protein per year from a system that requires no feed inputs, no freshwater diversion, no antibiotic administration, and no arable land. The system's primary requirement is that the ecosystem remain intact and that the harvest be governed by the stock's reproductive capacity. The dependency profile is thin in the same way the bison operation's is thin: what the system requires is the ecosystem itself, and management disciplined enough to not take more than the ecosystem can give.

Shellfish restoration on both coasts extends the marine case. Oyster reef restoration in the Chesapeake Bay, driven by federal and state partnership programs, has rebuilt reef acreage that was functionally destroyed by a century of overharvest and disease pressure. A 2011 synthesis by Michael Beck and colleagues in *BioScience* documented that approximately eighty-five percent of the world's oyster reef habitat had been lost, and called for restoration as both an ecological and an economic priority: oyster reefs provide water filtration, shoreline stabilization, and habitat for commercially important fish and invertebrate species, in addition to producing a harvestable food product.<sup>9</sup> The restored reefs in the Chesapeake, on the Gulf Coast, and in Puget Sound are early-stage examples of what marine rewilding looks like when it produces food: a system in which the harvested organism is simultaneously performing ecological work — filtering water, building reef structure, providing habitat — and producing a food product for human consumption. The dependency profile is, again, thin. The oyster needs the water column. It does not need feed, antibiotics, or industrial infrastructure.

Managed elk and deer populations across much of the American West round out the existing picture. State fish and wildlife agencies manage ungulate populations through regulated hunting seasons calibrated to population surveys, habitat carrying capacity, and predator-prey dynamics. The system is imperfect (chronic wasting disease is a serious and growing problem in cervid populations, and the politics of predator management complicate the ecological picture), but the food produced is substantial. The US Fish and Wildlife Service estimates that hunters harvest approximately six million white-tailed deer per year nationally, producing roughly 600 million pounds of venison.<sup>10</sup> Elk harvests in the western states add to the total. This is food from landscapes that have been managed, at least in part, for the ecological relationships the animals participate in. It is not food from a rewilded system in the full sense this book describes — most state wildlife management operates under the 1492 baseline the book has critiqued — but it is food from animals on landscapes they belong to, managed for population health rather than for maximum commodity throughput, and it represents an existing infrastructure of harvest, processing, and distribution that a more deliberately rewilded food system could build upon.

## WHAT COULD EXIST

The distance between what already exists and what could exist with deliberate effort is shorter than most people assume. It is a distance of policy, of infrastructure, and of imagination, not of technology or of ecological feasibility.

Knepp Estate in West Sussex, England, is the most thoroughly documented case of what happens when a conventional farm is released into a rewilding process, and Isabella Tree's 2018 account of the project in *Wilding* is the single best available narrative of rewilding as a food-producing enterprise.<sup>11</sup>

The Knepp story is worth telling in some detail, because the details are what make it instructive. The estate had been farmed conventionally for generations: arable crops, dairy cattle, the full infrastructure of a productive English lowland farm. By the late 1990s, the operation was losing money. The heavy Weald clay soils were marginal for arable production, and the costs of maintaining the fertility and drainage that intensive cropping required on those soils exceeded what the crops returned. Charlie Burrell and Isabella Tree made a decision that was, at the time, seen by neighboring farmers as somewhere between eccentric and irresponsible: they stopped farming the land conventionally and allowed it to begin reverting.

What happened over the subsequent two decades is one of the most closely observed ecological transitions in the European literature. The key decision — and the decision that makes Knepp relevant to this book rather than merely to the conservation literature — was to stock the land with proxy herbivores chosen for their ecological role rather than for their production characteristics. Old English longhorn cattle, Exmoor ponies, Tamworth pigs, and fallow deer were introduced, not managed in paddocks or rotations, but allowed to range freely across the estate, creating their own grazing and browsing patterns in response to the vegetation as it developed. The ponies browsed scrub and maintained open rides. The cattle grazed the grassland areas and created the dung-based invertebrate habitat that supports insectivorous birds. The pigs rooted through the woodland floor, creating the soil disturbance that germination of many native plant species requires. The deer browsed the woodland edge. Each species was performing a different ecological function, and the combination of functions produced a landscape that no single species could have produced alone. The results, documented by ecological surveys conducted by Imperial College London and other research institutions, were striking. Species that had been absent from the region for decades reappeared: turtle doves, nightingales, peregrine falcons, purple emperor butterflies, and all five British species of owl now breed on the estate.<sup>12</sup> Soil biological activity increased. Water retention improved. The landscape shifted from a simplified agricultural surface into a structurally complex mosaic of scrub, wood pasture, grassland, and wetland, a mosaic that ecologists recognized as closer to the natural vegetation structure of lowland Britain than either the arable farmland it had replaced or the dense closed-canopy woodland that "natural" often implies.

The food produced from Knepp is not incidental. The estate sells pasture-fed organic meat from its free-roaming herds — beef, venison, pork — and the revenue from the meat operation, combined with income from ecotourism and conservation stewardship payments, has made the estate financially viable in a way the conventional farming operation had not been for years. The animals are not supplemented with purchased feed. They eat what the rewilding landscape produces. The meat is marketed as a premium product, and the market absorbs it. The estate demonstrates, at a scale large enough to be taken seriously (roughly 3,500 acres), that a rewilded landscape can produce food, support ecological recovery, and generate revenue simultaneously.

Tree's documentation of the Knepp process is careful about what the project does and does not prove. It proves that a specific piece of marginal English farmland, released from conventional management and stocked with proxy herbivores, produced measurable ecological recovery and a financially viable food product over a twenty-year period. It does not prove that every piece of farmland would respond the same way, or that rewilding is universally more profitable than conventional agriculture, or that the Knepp model scales to feed a nation. What it proves is that the model works: that rewilding and food production are not in opposition, that the landscape can do both, and that the animals doing the ecological work are also the animals producing the food.

> **CASE: The Knepp Experiment**
>
> Knepp Estate in West Sussex, England — 3,500 acres of former Weald-clay farmland released from conventional management beginning in 2001 — is the most thoroughly documented ecological rewilding experiment in Europe.

Charlie Burrell and Isabella Tree stocked the land with proxy herbivores chosen for ecological function rather than production characteristics: Old English longhorn cattle, Exmoor ponies, Tamworth pigs, fallow deer. No paddocks, no rotations, no purchased feed. Within two decades, turtle doves, nightingales, peregrine falcons, purple emperor butterflies, and all five British owl species were breeding on the estate.

The meat operation (beef, venison, pork), combined with ecotourism and conservation stewardship payments, made the estate financially viable where the preceding conventional operation had failed. Tree's 2018 *Wilding* is the narrative record, and the ecological monitoring literature around Knepp continues to grow.

American Prairie, which I described above in its current form, represents the most ambitious version of this model currently under development in the United States. The long-term vision — a continuous habitat block of several million acres along the Missouri River — would create a landscape large enough for bison herds to express genuinely free-ranging behavior, for predator-prey dynamics to operate without intensive human management, and for the prairie ecosystem to function at something approaching its pre-industrial ecological capacity. The food-system implications are direct: bison managed at that scale, on that landscape, under those conditions, would produce food as a function of their ecological role. The harvest would be governed by population dynamics rather than by production targets. The product would be genuinely wild-harvested meat from a genuinely rewilded landscape.

Smaller-scale rewilding efforts on private and public land across the United States represent a distributed version of the same trajectory. Ranchers in the southern Plains who have replaced cattle with bison on shortgrass prairie are running operations that the fossil record endorses and that produce food with dependency profiles substantially thinner than conventional beef. Landowners in the Southwest who are experimenting with managed burro and horse populations on degraded rangeland are testing, in practice, the proxy-selection hypothesis Chapter 7 described. Coastal restoration programs that combine oyster reef rebuilding with regulated harvest are producing food from ecosystems they are simultaneously restoring.

The distributed model deserves attention on its own terms, because the distributed model is how most food-system transitions actually proceed. The shift from draft horses to tractors did not happen by decree. It happened farm by farm, across decades, as individual operators made individual decisions based on what worked for their land and their economics. A shift toward rewilded food production would likely follow the same pattern: individual ranchers choosing bison over cattle on land that responds better to bison, individual landowners leasing acreage for conservation grazing rather than for commodity production, individual coastal communities investing in reef restoration that produces both ecological benefit and harvestable shellfish. Each individual decision is small. The aggregate is a trajectory, and trajectories compound. A hundred small operations producing food from rewilded landscapes, spread across a dozen bioregions, constitute a proof-of-concept network that no single demonstration site can provide. They test the model across soils, climates, regulatory environments, and market conditions. They generate the data that policy needs to scale what works. And they produce food while they do it: food that people eat, food that enters local and regional markets, food that gradually makes the idea of eating from rewilded systems less exotic and more ordinary.

None of these operations is large enough, individually, to register in the national food-supply statistics. Collectively, they represent a direction the food system could move in, with deliberate support, at a pace that is honest about what is currently possible and ambitious about what could become possible.

## THE HONEST SCALING ASSESSMENT

The trajectory matters, and stating it honestly matters more.

Rewilded food systems cannot, today, feed every person currently eating from industrial systems. The numbers do not support that claim and I am not making it. The roughly 500,000 bison in the United States, even if all of them were managed as conservation herds on rewilded grassland, would produce a small fraction of the roughly 27 billion pounds of beef the US market consumes annually.<sup>13</sup> The Alaska salmon fisheries, impressive as they are, produce roughly 700 million to 1 billion pounds of fish per year, a substantial total, but a fraction of the roughly 6.3 billion pounds of seafood Americans consume annually.<sup>14</sup> Feral hog harvest, managed elk and deer harvest, restored shellfish, and the scattered rewilding operations on private land add real volume without changing the fundamental arithmetic. Rewilded food systems today occupy a margin. The margin is real and productive, but it is a margin.

The argument this book is making is not that the margin should replace the center overnight. The argument is about trajectory: about which direction the food system should be moving, about which investments in land, infrastructure, and policy would produce the most durable food systems over the next century, and about what the framework developed in this book reveals when it is applied honestly to the options available.

Three things are true simultaneously, and the honest position requires holding all three.

First, rewilded food systems score well across all four axes of the framework this book has developed. They score well on the economic axis where they have been given the chance to operate: Knepp is financially viable, American Prairie's bison operation generates revenue, Alaska's salmon fisheries support a multi-billion-dollar industry. They score well on the social axis: tribal bison restoration has direct community-health and cultural-restoration dimensions, managed hunting supports rural economies and wildlife management infrastructure, coastal shellfish restoration employs restoration workers and supports fishing communities. They score well on the environmental axis: by definition, a system that produces food as a function of ecological restoration is not trading ecological health against food production but producing both together. And they score well on the survivability axis, which is the axis that matters most for the argument this book has been building.

The survivability score is where the distinction sharpens. Recall the axis as Chapter 4 defined it: survivability asks what a system requires to keep functioning, and what happens when those requirements are not met. An industrial feedlot requires continuous commodity grain, continuous diesel, continuous antibiotics, continuous federal subsidies, and continuous stable climate. A regenerative pasture operation requires most of what the industrial system requires for its monogastric enterprises, as Chapter 6 documented. A bison herd on rewilded prairie requires grass, water, and management. The dependency profile is not zero — the management requires human skill, the fencing requires materials, the harvest requires processing infrastructure — but the profile is structurally thinner than anything the industrial or regenerative alternatives can offer. A system whose primary input is an intact ecosystem is a system that does not collapse when the commodity supply chain is disrupted, because it was never on the commodity supply chain. It does not require diesel for feed delivery, because the feed is growing under the animals' feet. It does not require antibiotics for disease management, because the stocking densities and movement patterns that produce disease pressure in confined systems do not exist. It does not require federal subsidies to make the economics work, because the economics are not built on the gap between input costs and commodity prices that the subsidies exist to bridge.

This is not a theoretical advantage. It is the practical consequence of a food system organized around ecological relationships rather than around industrial inputs. Consider what this means concretely. When the 2022 disruption of Ukrainian grain exports drove US feed costs to levels that Chapter 4 described as existential for some pastured-poultry operations, American Prairie's bison herd was unaffected. The animals did not eat commodity grain. The price of corn was irrelevant to their diet, to their management, and to the economics of their harvest. When the Texas winter storm of 2021 knocked out grid power across the state and killed livestock in operations dependent on electrically pumped water and heated shelters, free-ranging elk and deer in the same state continued to drink from streams and shelter in draws, as they had always done. The resilience was not engineered. It was inherent in the relationship between the animal and the landscape. That relationship predates, and does not depend on, any of the industrial infrastructure that the conventional system treats as a permanent condition. The poultry operation in the Piedmont that opened Chapter 4, the one that scored well on three axes and collapsed when the corn supply was disrupted, could not have collapsed on the fourth axis if it had never been on the commodity corn supply chain in the first place. Rewilded food systems are not on that supply chain. Their vulnerability surfaces are different: they are vulnerable to ecosystem degradation, to climate shifts that alter the vegetation the animals depend on, to disease events in wild or semi-wild populations, to political decisions about land use and access. Those vulnerabilities are real and should not be minimized. They are also vulnerabilities of a fundamentally different kind from the ones the industrial system carries, because they are vulnerabilities of the ecosystem itself rather than vulnerabilities of the industrial substrate layered on top of the ecosystem.

Second, the margin is expandable. The amount of land currently managed for rewilded food production is a small fraction of the land that could be managed that way. American Prairie's eventual footprint, if the acquisition and leasing program reaches its long-term target, would be measured in millions of acres, a single project on a single section of the northern Great Plains. Equivalent projects on equivalent landscapes across the continent — the shortgrass prairies of the central Plains, the basin-and-range country of the Southwest, the Gulf Coast marshlands, the eastern hardwood silvopastoral opportunities — would multiply the acreage by an order of magnitude. The constraint is not ecological feasibility. It is capital, policy, and the cultural willingness to manage landscapes for ecological function and food production simultaneously rather than treating the two as competing uses.

Third, the question is one of trajectory, not of overnight replacement. No serious person proposes dismantling the industrial food system on Monday and replacing it with bison and salmon on Tuesday. The industrial system feeds people. It feeds people badly, in the sense that the system's externalities are substantial and its survivability is poor, but it feeds them, and any transition away from it has to account for what people eat during the transition. The trajectory this book proposes is the deliberate expansion of rewilded food systems alongside — not instead of —the existing infrastructure, with the understanding that every acre moved from industrial production to rewilded production is an acre that scores better on all four axes and is more likely to still be producing food in fifty years under conditions that may look nothing like today's.

The trajectory framing is not a concession. It is the honest version of the argument. A food system that scores well on all four axes, that produces food as a function of ecological restoration rather than in spite of ecological degradation, and that does not depend on the persistence of an industrial substrate whose vulnerabilities the last decade has made starkly visible — that system deserves expansion. It deserves investment. It deserves the policy support and the cultural attention that have historically been directed at the systems it would gradually, partially, honestly replace. The argument is not that rewilded systems are the only answer. The argument is that they are the most underexplored answer, and that the framework this book has developed reveals them as the direction most consistent with the conditions the next century is likely to impose.

## FROM THE FRAMEWORK TO THE FORK

The systems described in this chapter are not abstractions. They produce food that people eat. The bison from American Prairie and from tribal herds becomes steaks and roasts and ground meat that appears in kitchens and restaurants. The salmon from Alaska's managed fisheries fills freezers and feeds families. The venison from managed elk and deer harvests is processed and consumed by millions of American hunters and their communities every year. The pork from feral hog harvest in Texas, still underutilized relative to the volume available, feeds the hunters who take the animals and, increasingly, supplies processors who sell it into regional markets. The beef and pork and venison from Knepp feeds people in West Sussex and beyond.

What eating from rewilded systems looks like, in practice, is not exotic. It looks like a bison burger. It looks like grilled salmon. It looks like venison stew. It looks like oysters from a restored reef. It looks like food: recognizable, preparable, delicious in the ways that animal protein from well-managed systems is always delicious, because the animals ate what they were supposed to eat and lived on land that supported them. The plate is not strange. The system behind the plate is different, and the difference is what the framework measures.

There is a deeper point here about the relationship between the food and the landscape it comes from, and the point is worth stating because it connects to the book's larger argument about what sustainability means. When a bison grazes the northern Plains, it is not merely producing meat. It is performing the ecological work the prairie requires — the trampling, the grazing, the nutrient deposition, the seed-coat scarification — and the meat is a consequence of that work. When an oyster filters water in a restored Chesapeake reef, it is not merely producing a shellfish product. It is cleaning the estuary, building reef structure, creating habitat for juvenile fish and crustaceans, and the harvestable oyster is a consequence of that function. When a managed deer herd is held at carrying capacity by regulated hunting, the hunting is performing the predator function that wolves and cougars once performed at broader scale, and the venison is a consequence of that ecological substitution. In each case, the food is not the purpose of the system: the ecological function is the purpose, and the food is what the function produces.
