This inverts the relationship that industrial agriculture assumes. Industrial agriculture organizes landscapes around food production and treats ecological consequences as externalities to be managed. Rewilded food systems organize landscapes around ecological function and treat food production as an output of that function. The inversion is not merely philosophical. It produces systems that score differently on the framework this book has developed, because the framework asks what a system requires to keep functioning, and a system whose purpose is ecological function requires the ecosystem — not the industrial substrate — to keep functioning.

The previous chapter established the principle that the fossil record provides the species guide for which animals belong in which landscapes. This chapter has shown that the principle is not merely a principle. It is an operational reality in the places where it has been given the chance to operate. Bison on the northern Plains are the fossil record's recommendation in practice. Salmon in Alaska's rivers are an intact fishery managed for persistence rather than for maximum extraction. Elk across the western mountains are a managed population of animals that belong where they stand. Even the feral hog — the accidental case, the invasive species converted into food — demonstrates that the landscape produces animal protein whether we plan for it or not, and that the question is whether we harvest it intelligently.

I want to pause on that inversion, because it is the thing this chapter has been building toward. The food systems described in Chapters 5 and 6 (industrial production and regenerative agriculture, respectively) both organize the landscape around the production of food. They differ enormously in how they do it, and the regenerative version is substantially better by every measure this book has applied. But both start from the same premise: the landscape is a production surface, and the question is how to manage the production surface responsibly. Rewilded food systems start from a different premise: the landscape is an ecosystem, the ecosystem has a set of relationships it evolved to maintain, and the food is what those relationships produce when they are allowed to operate. The distinction is not semantic. It produces food systems with fundamentally different dependency profiles, fundamentally different vulnerability surfaces, and fundamentally different relationships to the conditions the next century is likely to impose.

The next chapter takes the serious critics of Pleistocene rewilding head-on. The vision this chapter has described is not uncontested. Biologists have raised legitimate concerns about proxy-species equivalence, about ecological risk, about the cultural and political feasibility of the proposals the rewilding literature has advanced. Those concerns deserve the engagement Chapter 9 will give them. What this chapter has tried to establish, before the critics are heard, is that the vision is not speculative. Parts of it are already here. The animals are real. The landscapes are real. The food is real. The question is not whether rewilded food systems can work: they already do, at the scales where they have been tried. The question is whether the scales can grow, and what it would take to grow them honestly. The framework says the direction is right. The next chapter asks whether the objections are answerable.

# Chapter Nine: Proxies and Critics

In 2005, Josh Donlan and twelve co-authors published a one-page essay in *Nature* titled "Re-wilding North America."<sup>1</sup> The essay proposed, in compressed form, what the previous three chapters of this book have been working toward at length: that the ecological histories of the Americas make the case for restoring large vertebrates, including Old World proxies for extinct Pleistocene species, to landscapes that had co-evolved with them. Donlan and his co-signatories included Paul Martin, Michael Soulé, Dave Foreman, and several prominent conservation biologists. The essay was short, ambitious, and deliberately provocative. It ended with a call for an "optimistic agenda for twenty-first century conservation" centered on the phased introduction of large mammals to carefully selected North American sites.

The response was substantial and, in large part, skeptical.

Within a year, multiple peer-reviewed rebuttals had appeared. The critiques ranged from the procedural to the ecological to the political, made by serious scholars whose concerns were grounded in real knowledge of conservation biology, invasion ecology, and the practical realities of land management. The critiques deserve to be engaged, not summarized, not caricatured, not dismissed. This chapter's job is to present the strongest version of each major critique, engage it on its merits, and arrive at a position the reader can evaluate for themselves.

I want to say at the outset what this chapter does not do. It does not decry the critics. The scholars who questioned the Donlan proposal were doing what scholars are supposed to do: subjecting an ambitious claim to rigorous scrutiny and identifying where the claim outran its evidence. Some objections I accept substantially. Some I think are answerable on empirical grounds. Some miss the larger point, for reasons I will try to articulate. In no case do I think the objections are trivial, and in no case do I think the person making them was wrong. The rewilding proposal asks for much. The scrutiny is proportionate to the ask.

## THE PROXY-EQUIVALENCE PROBLEM

The most ecologically grounded critique of Pleistocene rewilding was articulated by Tim Caro in a 2007 commentary in *Trends in Ecology & Evolution* titled "The Pleistocene Re-wilding Gambit."<sup>2</sup> Caro is a behavioral ecologist whose career has been built on careful empirical work — mostly on African mammalian ecology, where the large-bodied fauna that the rewilding proposal seeks to restore or proxy is still intact. His critique cuts to the heart of the proxy-selection argument this book has been making.

Caro's central concern is what might be called the proxy-equivalence assumption: that a closely related living species will perform the same ecological function as its extinct relative when placed in the same landscape. The rewilding proposal depends on this assumption. Donlan and colleagues proposed that African or Asian elephants could serve as proxies for mammoths, African lions or cheetahs as proxies for the extinct American lion and cheetah, and Bactrian camels or dromedaries as proxies for *Camelops*. The assumption underlying each proposal is that phylogenetic proximity — the degree of relatedness between the proxy and the extinct species — predicts ecological equivalence. A close relative, the assumption holds, will occupy a similar niche, exert similar effects on vegetation and soil, and restore something like the ecological function the extinct species performed.

Caro argued the assumption is unsupported by available evidence. Ecological function is not a simple derivative of phylogenetic relatedness. Two closely related species can occupy very different niches if they have diverged in diet, behavior, habitat preference, or body size. An African elephant is not a mammoth. The lineages diverged millions of years ago and adapted to different climatic regimes, vegetation communities, and predator assemblages. Placing an African elephant on the Great Plains and expecting it to perform the ecological role of *Mammuthus columbi* is an extrapolation from phylogeny to ecology that available evidence does not license. The same concern applies to every proxy pairing the Donlan proposal advanced. A modern dromedary is not *Camelops*. A modern African cheetah is not *Miracinonyx*. The degree to which the proxy's actual behavior in the actual landscape would replicate the extinct species' ecological function is an empirical question that cannot be answered from phylogeny alone.<sup>2</sup>

Caro went further. He pointed out that introductions of large-bodied species into novel ecosystems have, historically, produced ecological consequences that introducing parties did not anticipate and could not control. The history of species introductions is not reassuring. Cane toads in Australia, rabbits in Australia, Nile perch in Lake Victoria—each was introduced with some expectation of ecological benefit, and each produced cascading damage still being managed decades or centuries later. The proxy-species proposal, Caro argued, was asking conservationists to introduce large-bodied animals with enormous individual ecological footprints, capable of transforming vegetation structure and soil dynamics across large areas, into ecosystems that have not experienced those animals for twelve thousand years. The ecosystems have reorganized in the interim. The vegetation communities have shifted. The predator-prey dynamics have stabilized around a different cast of species. Introducing a proxy megaherbivore into a system operating without megaherbivores for a hundred and twenty centuries is not a restoration in any straightforward sense. It is an experiment with uncertain outcome, and the scale of potential consequences, both positive and negative, is proportional to the size of animals being introduced.

I find this critique serious and, in its strongest form, largely correct. The proxy-equivalence assumption *is* unsupported as a blanket proposition—phylogenetic proximity does not guarantee ecological equivalence. The history of species introductions warrants caution. Caro is right that the burden of evidence lies with proponents, not skeptics, and that evidence as of 2007 was not sufficient to justify continental-scale proxy introductions on phylogenetic reasoning alone.

Where the critique reaches its limit is in what follows from it. Caro's argument establishes that the proxy-equivalence question is empirical—that it cannot be assumed but must be tested. It does not establish that the question is unanswerable, or that the answer will necessarily be negative—it establishes only that the empirical work remains undone. The proposition that a dromedary camel would browse the thorny vegetation of the American Southwest in a manner ecologically similar to *Camelops* is not a proposition that can be confirmed or denied from phylogeny. It is a proposition that can be tested by putting dromedaries on Southwest rangeland and measuring what happens to vegetation, soil, the animal, and the surrounding ecological community. The test would need to be designed carefully, monitored rigorously, and conducted at a scale small enough that negative outcomes could be managed without ecological catastrophe. It would need control sites and long time horizons, the kind of sustained empirical attention that conservation biology gives to reintroduction programs for endangered species. What it would not need is certainty in advance. It would need a hypothesis worth testing and a methodology capable of testing it. And people with the nerve to actually do it.

> **FIELD NOTE: Why Not Goats?**
>
> I have been asked, more than once, why I do not just propose goats.
>
> The question is reasonable on its face. Goats are easy to permit. The regulatory pathway exists. The market for goat meat exists, modestly. They eat thorny browse that cattle will not touch. California already deploys them, by the thousands of acres, for fuel-load reduction in chaparral. Goats are right there. Why bring in something exotic?
>
> My honest first answer to that question, given to a person whose work I respect over an exchange that escalated faster than I had planned, was a tit-for-tat: “Why not camels?” That is not a real answer. The ludicrous question deserved an equally ludicrous non-response, and that is what it got from me at the time. The better answer, the one this Field Note exists to make, is body size, and there is a literature behind it.
>
> The 2023 Pringle review in *Current Biology* synthesizes a decade of large-herbivore impact studies and lands on a finding that has been clean across exclosure experiments and observational comparisons: small herbivores do not functionally compensate for large ones. Body size governs ecological impact in ways that scale across multiple variables, including biomass consumption, seed dispersal, depth of trampling, structure of browse pressure, and generation of ground disturbance. Above roughly one hundred kilograms, the regulation of the population shifts from top-down predation to bottom-up food limitation. Above roughly one thousand kilograms, the animal becomes essentially invulnerable to non-human predators and exerts ecological influence that no smaller animal can replicate.<sup>10</sup> The review’s framing is direct: livestock are poor surrogates for wild large herbivores precisely because the body-size profile of common livestock species is concentrated in the smaller end of the range that the wild species occupied.
>
> The numbers locate the argument. Goats run thirty to fifty kilograms. Cattle run five to seven hundred. The dromedary is in the cattle range. The Bactrian goes higher, six hundred to a thousand. *Camelops*, the animal the proposal is actually about, sat above the cow and probably above the modern Bactrian. Goats and camels are not interchangeable proxies for the same lost role. They occupy different positions on the body-size scale, and the ecological impact of the larger animal is not something the smaller animal can produce by being more numerous.
>
> It also deserves saying that cattle themselves are proxies for the wild aurochs the European landscape lost. The fact that nobody calls them out as substituted does not make them less so. Cattle have monopolized the regenerative-agriculture conversation, but they are not the only large herbivore, and they are not the largest available. Camels, particularly Bactrians, are larger. Their ecological footprint, on a per-animal basis, is correspondingly greater. The argument for proposing them is not that goats are bad. It is that goats cannot do what *Camelops*-sized animals did, and the landscape was calibrated to *Camelops*-sized animals for most of its evolutionary history.
>
> The eye-rolling I have received on this is real. It does not, by itself, change what the fossil record says or what the body-size literature documents.

> **CASE: Pleistocene Park**
>
> The proxy hypothesis the previous section discussed has, for thirty years, been undergoing a sustained empirical test. Sergey Zimov, a Russian geophysicist, started Pleistocene Park in 1996 in Yakutia, in the eastern Siberian Arctic. The premise is the proxy proposal carried to its conclusion: reassemble, at landscape scale, the large-herbivore community that occupied the mammoth steppe before the terminal Pleistocene losses, and watch what the land does.
>
> The experimental enclosure now covers roughly one hundred fifty square kilometers and holds Yakutian horses, plains bison transferred from Alaska, musk-oxen, reindeer, yak, and a small number of Bactrian camels. The mammoth itself is, of course, absent; the project's framing has always been that the mammoth's *function* in the system — heavy-bodied browsing, snow disruption, dung-driven nutrient cycling — could be reconstructed from a community of available large herbivores even without the original keystone species. What has been measured. Soil temperatures under grazed plots run five to ten degrees Celsius colder than under ungrazed adjacent ground, because the herbivores compact and disturb the winter snow that otherwise insulates the soil and accelerates permafrost thaw. Vegetation has shifted in grazed plots from moss-and-shrub tundra toward a grass-dominated cover that more closely resembles pollen records of the Pleistocene mammoth steppe. Carbon dynamics — the project's larger claim, that grazer-driven cooling could buffer permafrost methane release at climatically meaningful scales — remain under measurement and dispute.
>
> What it is not. Pleistocene Park is small relative to the landscape its hypothesis is about; it is not a controlled experiment in the strict statistical sense; and it is operating in a political environment that has constrained both the science and the international collaboration the project relies on. Critics including Caro 2007 would note that a single uncontrolled site does not settle the proxy-equivalence question even after thirty years. They are right that it does not settle it. They are also wrong, in the form the critique typically takes, that nothing has been learned: thirty years of measurements at a real site running a real version of the hypothesis is more empirical attention than the proxy debate had received from any other quarter when the project began.
>
> The point of naming Pleistocene Park here is not to argue that it has succeeded or failed. It is to point out that, when the previous section said the experiment had not been run, that statement was true for the American case and not true for the Eurasian one. Somebody has been running it for thirty years. The question is whether anyone will run an analogous experiment on the continent the book is actually about.

The proxy-equivalence question is a question about experimental design rather than about ecological principle. The principle that an ecosystem missing its keystone herbivores functions differently than one in which they are present is well established in the ecological literature and has been demonstrated empirically in multiple contexts. The question is whether a specific proxy species, in a specific landscape, restores a specific ecological function to a measurable degree. That question is answerable by experiment, not by argument. The fact that the answer is not yet known is an argument for conducting the experiment, not for declining to conduct it.

Donlan and colleagues, in their fuller 2006 treatment in *The American Naturalist*, anticipated this line of response.<sup>3</sup> They proposed a phased approach: beginning with species that are already present in North America in managed or feral populations (horses, burros, camels in small numbers), moving to species with closer ecological parallels to extinct fauna (Bolson tortoises, which have since been the subject of a small-scale reintroduction program), and proceeding to larger introductions only as evidence accumulated from earlier phases. The phased approach directly responds to the proxy-equivalence concern: rather than assuming equivalence and introducing at scale, it proposes testing equivalence at small scale and scaling only what the evidence supports.

The phased approach is the right framework. It also requires honesty about what it can and cannot deliver. A small-scale experiment with dromedaries on a single Arizona ranch would tell us something about dromedary browsing behavior on Sonoran vegetation. It would not tell us what a self-sustaining population of dromedaries would do to the landscape over decades. The experimental evidence accumulates incrementally, and the increments may not resolve the proxy-equivalence question as cleanly as either side would like. What the experiments would produce is data: specific, empirical, measurable data about the interaction between proxy species and the landscapes they are being considered for. The current state of the conversation, in which advocates assert equivalence and critics deny it on the basis of argument rather than evidence, is less useful than a conversation in which both sides work from data generated by carefully designed field experiments.

## THE POLITICAL AND CULTURAL OBSTACLE

The second major critique came from Daniel Rubenstein and colleagues, most forcefully in a 2006 paper in *Biological Conservation* titled "Pleistocene Park: Does Re-wilding North America Represent Sound Conservation for the 21st Century?"<sup>4</sup> Rubenstein, a behavioral ecologist at Princeton whose work on equid ecology is among the most cited in the field, brought different concerns than Caro had raised. Where Caro focused on the ecological risks of proxy introductions, Rubenstein focused on the practical, political, and cultural barriers.

The Rubenstein critique is, in essence, a feasibility argument. Suppose the ecology works. Suppose the proxy-equivalence question, when tested, yields favorable results for at least some species-landscape pairings. The proposal still requires land, regulatory approval, and the consent of communities living adjacent to the introduced animals, or at least their tolerance. It requires a political and cultural environment in which the introduction of large, potentially dangerous animals onto working landscapes is regarded as an acceptable use of public and private land. Rubenstein and colleagues argued that none of these conditions obtained in the United States of the early twenty-first century, and that the distance between the ecological vision and the political reality was large enough to make the proposal practically unworkable.

The argument was specific. Americans do not, on the whole, want large predators near their homes, livestock, or children. The political conflict over wolf reintroduction in the Northern Rockies, which had been ongoing for a decade at the time of the Rubenstein paper, illustrated the scale of resistance that even a single native predator species could generate. Extending the reintroduction concept to African lions, elephants, or cheetahs, the authors suggested, was to move from a politically difficult proposal to a politically impossible one. The same reasoning applied to megaherbivores. A free-ranging elephant on the Great Plains would be an extraordinary management challenge: property damage, liability exposure, risk to human safety, conflict with existing agricultural land use. None of these concerns were ecological. All were practical, and all were, in the judgment of the critics, sufficient to render the proposal dead on arrival in any real political context.

Rubenstein and Rubenstein returned to this line of argument a decade later, in a 2016 letter to *PNAS* prompted by Svenning and colleagues' synthesis on trophic rewilding.<sup>5</sup> The updated critique noted that the intervening decade had not produced the political or cultural conditions necessary for the kind of introductions the Donlan proposal envisioned, and that the conversation about rewilding had largely shifted from Pleistocene-proxy introductions toward trophic rewilding, a framework that emphasized the restoration of ecological processes (top-down regulation, herbivory, seed dispersal) rather than the reintroduction of specific Pleistocene analogs. The shift, they suggested, itself acknowledged that the original proposal's ambitions exceeded its political reach.

I accept this critique in significant part. The cultural and political obstacles to large-scale megafaunal proxy introductions in the United States are real, substantial, and not likely to be resolved by ecological argument alone. A proposal that requires Americans to accept free-ranging elephants on the Great Plains is not a proposal that will be enacted in any political environment I can foresee. The obstacles are not irrational—they are the reasonable concerns of people who live on working landscapes and have legitimate interests in what lives on those landscapes alongside them. The rewilding conversation cannot proceed as though those interests do not exist or as though they will yield to the force of ecological reasoning.

Where the critique warrants a response, and here the response is more modest than the original Donlan proposal, is in the scope of what is being proposed. The political obstacles that Rubenstein identifies are real for the proposal as Donlan et al. stated it in 2005: a continental-scale reintroduction program involving African megafauna. The political obstacles are substantially different for a more modest version: small-scale proof-of-concept experiments, conducted on private or willing public land, using species that are already present in the Americas, at scales small enough that the management challenges are manageable and the risks are bounded.

Consider what a modest proof-of-concept program would actually look like. A rancher in southern Arizona, one of many whose land is degraded enough and whose relationship to the conventional cattle economy is marginal enough to be open to alternatives, places twenty dromedary camels on a thousand acres of Sonoran desert rangeland under a research protocol developed in partnership with a university. The camels are fenced. The vegetation response is monitored. The soil biology is sampled. The animal behavior is documented. The experiment runs for five years. At the end of five years, the data either support the hypothesis that dromedary browsing produces measurable ecological benefit on that landscape, or they do not. If they do, the experiment is expanded to additional sites, species, and scales. If they do not, the experiment is ended and the hypothesis is revised.

The political barriers to this version of the proposal are not zero. They include permitting, liability, neighbor relations, and the cultural unfamiliarity of camels on Arizona rangeland. But they are barriers of a different order than those to placing elephants on the Great Plains. They are navigable, and in fact are already being navigated in analogous contexts: Bolson tortoise reintroduction in New Mexico, experimental bison management on tribal and private land across the northern Plains, and the ongoing management of feral horse and burro populations on public land in the West. Each of these involves the placement of large-bodied animals on working landscapes under regulatory frameworks that already exist.

The political critique is strongest when aimed at the most ambitious version of the proposal. It is weakest when aimed at the most modest version. The distance between the two versions is not small. One asks a nation to accept a fundamentally different relationship to its landscapes. The other asks a handful of willing landowners and researchers to run a bounded experiment and publish the results. The book's ask is the modest version: not a continental program but a set of experiments, not certainty but data, not political transformation but the kind of incremental, evidence-based expansion that conservation biology already practices with endangered-species reintroduction programs.

## THE BASELINE-AS-FANTASY CRITIQUE

The third critique cuts deeper because it challenges not a specific proposal but the frame within which all rewilding proposals are made.

The argument, advanced by various scholars with different emphases, is that the Pleistocene baseline is itself a kind of conservation fantasy, a longing for a world that existed under conditions so different from the present that its restoration is not merely difficult but conceptually incoherent. Conservation, under this view, should focus on protecting what currently exists: the species, ecosystems, and ecological processes that are present now, under current conditions, and that face measurable threats from current human activity. The late-Pleistocene assemblage is interesting as paleontology, not a useful reference point for conservation in the Anthropocene. The world has moved on. The ecosystems have reorganized. The question is not how to get back to a Pleistocene state that no living person has experienced but how to protect and improve the ecological communities we actually have.

This is not a fringe position. Versions of it appear in thoughtful conservation writing across the last two decades, and the version embedded in the trophic-rewilding framework that Svenning and colleagues articulated in their 2016 synthesis in *PNAS* is, in part, a response to the force of the objection.<sup>6</sup> Svenning and colleagues shifted emphasis from "restore the Pleistocene assemblage" to "restore the ecological processes that large-bodied animals perform," a shift that sidesteps the historical-baseline question by making the argument functional rather than historical. If a landscape needs top-down regulation by large predators, the relevant question is whether large predators can provide that function now, not whether the specific predators being considered are phylogenetically correct proxies for Pleistocene species.

I respect the pragmatism of the trophic-rewilding reframe. It is a useful bridge between the Donlan proposal's ambitions and the political and ecological realities the proposal confronted. Where the baseline-as-fantasy critique ultimately falls short is in what it asks us to accept.

The argument that conservation should focus on protecting what currently exists takes the current ecological state as its reference point. But the current ecological state of North America, as Chapter 2 of this book argued at length, is not a stable equilibrium that happens to have arrived at its present form through natural processes—it is a depleted state, one that resulted from the loss of more than forty species of large mammals in near time, a loss that reshaped every terrestrial ecosystem on the continent. The vegetation communities are different. The soil dynamics are different. The predator-prey relationships are truncated. The seed dispersal networks are broken. The grasslands that evolved under proboscidean herbivory operate differently without it. The canyons that evolved under camelid browsing are being colonized by thorny vegetation the camelids used to suppress. The pronghorn still runs at speeds calibrated to a predator that no longer exists.

To argue that conservation should protect "what currently exists" is to argue that we should protect the depleted state, that we should defend the post-extinction baseline against further degradation without asking whether the baseline itself represents a condition worth defending on ecological grounds. It is the argument from Chapter 2 returning in a different register: the Columbian Curtain, extended. Except that here the curtain is not drawn at 1492. It is drawn at the present moment, and what it hides is the same thing the Columbian Curtain hides: the depth of the loss and the possibility that addressing the loss would produce ecological outcomes that the current baseline cannot.

Granted, the comparison has limits. The Pleistocene is gone in a way that 1491 is not. The ecosystems have had twelve thousand years to reorganize, and the reorganization is not nothing. New ecological relationships have formed, new equilibria have been reached, and disrupting those equilibria carries its own risks. Caro's concern about the consequences of introducing megafauna into systems that have stabilized without them is a version of this point, and it is a legitimate one. The twelve-thousand-year gap is real. The ecosystems on the other side of it are not frozen in time, waiting for the return of the animals they lost. They have moved, and the moving is itself an ecological fact.

But the moving has not been unconstrained. The ecosystems have reorganized within the limits of what was available to them—and what was available was a diminished cast of species. A grassland that loses its proboscideans does not replace them with an ecological equivalent. It reorganizes around the absence: vegetation structure shifts, nutrient cycling changes, fire regime adjusts, soil biology adapts to a world with less large-mammal disturbance. The reorganization is real, but it is a reorganization around a loss, not a recovery from one. The new equilibrium is a depleted equilibrium. To take the depleted equilibrium as the baseline and to argue that conservation should protect it against disturbance is to naturalize the depletion, to treat the consequence of a mass extinction as the reference condition for all subsequent ecological management.

I do not think this is a defensible position when stated plainly. The fact that it is the de facto position of most conservation practice in the United States does not make it defensible—it makes it familiar.

The response is not to demand that every conservation agency immediately adopt a Pleistocene baseline—that would be as politically unrealistic as the most ambitious version of the Donlan proposal, and for similar reasons. The response is to recognize that the current baseline is contingent, that it results from a specific historical event (the late-Pleistocene megafaunal extinction), that it represents a depleted state rather than a natural one, and that proposals to address the depletion deserve evaluation on their ecological merits rather than dismissal on the grounds that the baseline is the baseline.

## WHAT THE WOLF TAUGHT US

There is one case in which the rewilding conversation moved from argument to evidence, and the evidence is worth pausing on, because it is the closest thing the field has to a completed experiment.

In 1995 and 1996, gray wolves were reintroduced to Yellowstone National Park after an absence of approximately seventy years. The reintroduction was politically contentious: ranching communities adjacent to the park opposed it vigorously, and the legal and regulatory battles surrounding it lasted years before and after the animals arrived. The wolves were not Pleistocene proxies. They were a species reintroduced to a landscape it had occupied within living human memory, under a regulatory framework that already existed for endangered-species recovery. It matters here not as a Pleistocene-rewilding experiment but as something simpler: a large-bodied animal returned to a landscape that had reorganized in its absence, with the reorganization measured before, during, and after the return.

What happened was substantial. William Ripple and Robert Beschta, in a 2012 synthesis covering the first fifteen years of wolf presence in the park, documented a series of measurable ecological changes that cascaded from the reintroduction through the park's trophic structure.<sup>7</sup> Elk, which had been the park's dominant herbivore in the wolves' absence, altered their behavior and distribution in response to predation risk. Riparian areas that had been heavily browsed by elk—willows, aspens, and cottonwoods along streams and rivers—began to recover as elk browsing pressure declined. Luke Painter and colleagues, in a 2015 study in *Ecology*, documented that the proportion of young aspen browsed by elk in northern Yellowstone dropped from ninety percent in 1998 to thirty-seven to sixty-three percent by 2012, with corresponding increases in aspen recruitment and height growth in areas of high wolf activity.<sup>8</sup> The vegetation recovery had downstream effects: stream-bank stabilization improved as willow and cottonwood root systems reestablished, channel morphology began to shift, and beaver — absent from much of the park's northern range for decades — returned to streams where the recovering riparian vegetation provided both food and building material.

The trophic cascade was not simple. Creel and Christianson, in a 2009 study also in *Ecology*, found that individual elk willow consumption actually increased in the presence of wolves, suggesting that vegetation recovery was driven primarily by elk population reduction rather than by behavioral avoidance alone.<sup>9</sup> The mechanism was more complex than the initial "landscape of fear" narrative suggested. What was not in dispute was the outcome: the reintroduction of a top predator to a landscape that had reorganized in its absence produced measurable, cascading, and largely beneficial ecological effects within two decades.

The Yellowstone wolf case is not a perfect analogy for the proxy-species proposals this book has discussed. Wolves are a native species reintroduced to a landscape from which they had been absent for decades, not centuries or millennia. The ecological continuity between the pre-removal and post-reintroduction periods is much tighter than the continuity between the late Pleistocene and the present. The political and regulatory framework for wolf reintroduction, while contested, was grounded in the Endangered Species Act, a legal infrastructure that does not exist for Pleistocene-proxy introductions.

What the wolf case does demonstrate—and this is what I want us to pause on before moving to the chapter's close—is that the principle works. An ecosystem missing a keystone species reorganizes around the absence. When the species is returned, the ecosystem begins to reorganize around the presence. The reorganization is measurable, cascading, and ecologically significant. The fact that the wolf was absent for seventy years rather than twelve thousand does not change the principle; it changes the degree of uncertainty about the outcome. A twelve-thousand-year absence produces more reorganization, more ecological distance between the pre-removal and the present state, and more uncertainty about what reintroduction would produce. The uncertainty is real. It is not a reason to decline the experiment but a reason to design the experiment carefully.

## THE ASK

The book has now made its case across eight chapters. It has argued that the dominant framework for evaluating food systems is incomplete. It has proposed a fourth axis — survivability — that captures what the existing framework misses. It has applied the framework to industrial systems, to regenerative agriculture, and to the rewilded food systems that score best across all four axes. It has taken the fossil record seriously as a guide to which animals belong in which landscapes. It has described what rewilded food systems look like where they already exist, and what they could look like with deliberate effort.

This chapter has taken the critics seriously. Caro's concern about proxy equivalence is valid: the assumption that phylogenetic proximity equals ecological equivalence is unsupported as a blanket claim and should be tested rather than assumed. Rubenstein's concern about political and cultural feasibility is valid: the most ambitious version of the rewilding proposal exceeds what the current political environment can absorb, and the conversation has to meet that reality where it is. The baseline-as-fantasy critique has force: the Pleistocene is gone, the ecosystems have reorganized, and any intervention carries risks that must be weighed against the potential benefits.

What the book asks for, in light of all three critiques, is not certainty—not a continental program, not the introduction of elephants to the Great Plains or lions to the Rockies, not a demand that the conservation establishment abandon its existing frameworks or that ranching communities accept megafauna on their land without consent.

What the book asks for is proof of concept. Rewilding hypotheses can be tested at small scales: camels on a single ranch in Arizona, managed burro populations in the Grand Canyon watershed, bison on tribal and private land across the northern Plains (where this is already happening, with measurable results), wolves in specific Northern Rocky Mountain ecosystems (where this has already been done, with the trophic cascade data to show for it). Each of these is a bounded experiment that produces data and can be expanded if the data are favorable and ended if they are not.

The proof-of-concept approach does not require resolving the proxy-equivalence debate in advance; it generates the data the debate needs. It does not require overcoming the political obstacles to continental-scale megafaunal introduction; it works within the political space that already exists for small-scale research and land-management experimentation. It does not require accepting the Pleistocene baseline as the one true reference point for conservation; it requires only the recognition that the current baseline is contingent, that the depletion it represents is real, and that proposals to address the depletion deserve to be evaluated rather than dismissed.

The experiments are not hypothetical. Some of them have already been run. Wolves in Yellowstone. Bison on tribal and conservation land across the northern Plains. Bolson tortoises in New Mexico. Feral horse and burro management—contested and imperfect, but generating ecological data in real time about what happens when equids occupy landscapes their lineage shaped. Each of these is a data point that says something about the interaction between large-bodied animals and the landscapes they are placed on. The data are not yet sufficient to answer the proxy-equivalence question for every species-landscape pairing the rewilding literature has proposed. They are sufficient to show that the question is answerable, that it yields to empirical investigation rather than resolving only to argument.

The critics have earned the book the obligation to state its ask carefully. The ask is this: try the experiments honestly, at small scales, with rigorous monitoring and genuine willingness to accept negative results. Try them on landscapes where the fossil record and the current ecological evidence both point to an animal that is not there. Measure what happens. Publish the results. Let the data accumulate. And then decide not from argument, not from longing, not from the fossil record alone, but from the evidence of what actually happens when the animals return to the land that shaped them.

The critics say the proposal is uncertain. They are right. The response is not to retreat from the uncertainty but to design experiments that reduce it. The response is not certainty.

The response is science.

# Chapter Ten: Water and Indigenous Systems

The argument this book has been making rests on a particular frame. The Pleistocene baseline, the proposal that restoration should be guided by what the fossil record tells us about the ecological assemblages that preceded the near-time extinctions, is the frame the previous nine chapters have developed, applied, and defended against its serious critics. The frame is, I believe, defensible. It is not the only one.

This chapter exists because intellectual honesty requires saying so. The Pleistocene baseline is one way of answering the question "what should this landscape look like?" It is not the only way the question has been answered, and it is not the only way the question should be answered. Indigenous peoples across the Americas managed their landscapes for millennia between the terminal Pleistocene extinctions and the arrival of Europeans, and the management traditions they developed constitute a parallel intellectual framework for thinking about restoration, one that the Pleistocene frame should be in dialogue with rather than in competition with, and one that the rewilding conversation has been slower to engage than it should have been.

The chapter also exists because the rewilding conversation, as conducted in the conservation biology literature and in this book through Chapter 9, has been almost entirely terrestrial. The oceans are missing. Kelp forests, oyster reefs, pelagic fisheries, the entire marine domain: the water column has been largely absent from popular rewilding discourse, and the absence is a gap the book needs to address before it moves into practical application. The marine systems are as depleted as the terrestrial ones, and the case for their restoration is, if anything, more empirically advanced.

Two expansions, then. The first is a framework expansion: from the Pleistocene baseline alone to the Pleistocene baseline in dialogue with Indigenous management traditions. The second is a domain expansion: from terrestrial rewilding alone to terrestrial rewilding alongside marine restoration. Both expansions strengthen the book's argument by making it less parochial. Neither contradicts what has come before.

## A PARALLEL TRADITION

Robin Wall Kimmerer opens *Braiding Sweetgrass* with a creation story, the Skywoman story of the Potawatomi, and then spends the next three hundred pages demonstrating what it looks like when Indigenous ecological knowledge and Western scientific training are held in the same mind simultaneously.<sup>1</sup> The book is not an argument against Western science. It is an argument that Western science, when practiced in isolation from the traditions of people who have lived with and managed specific landscapes for thousands of years, is working with an unnecessarily thin dataset. The observation has force. It has force because Kimmerer is not making it from outside the scientific tradition. She is a plant ecologist at the SUNY College of Environmental Science and Forestry, a researcher who publishes in peer-reviewed journals, and she is making the argument from inside both traditions at once.

What Kimmerer describes, and what a substantial body of scholarship by Indigenous researchers has documented across multiple ecosystems, is a form of landscape management that does not fit cleanly into either the conservation or the agriculture categories that Western land-use thinking provides. The management is neither preservation (leaving the landscape alone) nor cultivation (reorganizing the landscape around a production target). It is something in between, and the in-between space is where the framework this book has been building meets a tradition it needs to reckon with.

M. Kat Anderson's *Tending the Wild*, a study of Native American management of California's ecosystems, provides the most thoroughly documented case for the scale and sophistication of this management.<sup>2</sup> Anderson spent years working with Indigenous elders and with the ethnobotanical and fire-ecology literatures to reconstruct what California's landscapes looked like under active Indigenous management before European contact. The picture that emerges is not wilderness in the sense that the word is usually used. It is a managed landscape: managed through fire, through selective harvesting, through the cultivation of specific plant communities, through the deliberate maintenance of habitat for specific animal species. The management was not incidental. It was systematic, it was transmitted across generations, and it produced landscapes that early European observers described as paradisiacal — open, productive, teeming with game — without understanding that the condition they were admiring was the product of human management rather than the absence of it.

Anderson documented that California's Indigenous peoples used fire as a primary management tool across virtually every vegetation type in the state. Grasslands were burned to maintain open habitat and to promote the growth of seed-bearing grasses and bulb-producing plants. Oak woodlands were burned to reduce competition from conifers, to promote acorn production, and to clear the understory for ease of harvest. Chaparral was burned on rotation to produce a mosaic of successional stages, each of which provided different resources: early successional stages for basket materials and herbaceous food plants, later stages for browse and mast.<sup>3</sup> The burning was not random. It was timed, targeted, and calibrated to the specific ecology of each vegetation type. The resulting landscapes were not natural in the sense of being unmanaged, and they were not agricultural in the sense of being reorganized around a single crop. They were something the Western vocabulary does not have a clean word for: managed ecosystems that produced food, fiber, and habitat as functions of ecological management rather than as alternatives to it.

The scale of this management was not marginal. Anderson estimates that Indigenous burning in California shaped millions of acres annually, a figure that challenges the assumption, embedded in the word "wilderness," that the landscapes Europeans encountered were unmanaged. The ecological consequences of the management's cessation after European contact were rapid and, in many cases, still unfolding. The dense conifer forests that now dominate much of California's Sierra Nevada, forests that burn catastrophically in modern wildfires because they have accumulated fuel loads that historical fire regimes would have prevented, are not the forests that existed under Indigenous management. They are the forests that grew in the absence of Indigenous management, the same way the grasslands that Chapter 7 described are the grasslands that exist in the absence of their Pleistocene grazers. The parallel is structural. In both cases, the current state of the landscape is a consequence of a relationship that has been interrupted, and the interruption is legible in the landscape's present condition.

The parallels to the rewilding argument are striking, and they are also incomplete. The rewilding framework this book has developed asks: what did this landscape look like before the near-time losses, and how do we restore the ecological relationships the losses disrupted? The Indigenous management framework asks a different question: how did the people who lived on this landscape between the Pleistocene and 1492 manage it, and what can that management tradition teach us about the landscape's productive capacity under human stewardship? Both questions are valid. Both produce different answers. The difference matters, and collapsing the two frameworks together would be a mistake that serves neither well.

## TWO BASELINES, NOT ONE

The Pleistocene baseline and the Indigenous management baseline are not the same thing, and I want to be precise about why.

The Pleistocene baseline looks backward past human presence on the continent: past the near-time extinctions, past the arrival of the first peoples, to the ecological assemblages that existed before the megafaunal losses. The question it asks is ecological in the deep sense: what was the full complement of species and relationships this landscape evolved under, and what has been lost? The answer involves animals — mammoths, ground sloths, camelids, equids — that no living human on this continent has ever seen in the wild. The restoration it envisions is a restoration of ecological function that predates human management of any kind.

The Indigenous management baseline looks at a different window: the millennia between the Pleistocene losses and European colonization. During that window, Indigenous peoples across the Americas were not passive inhabitants of a depleted landscape. They were active managers of it. The management traditions they developed were responses to the landscape as it existed after the megafaunal extinctions, a landscape already missing its mammoths and ground sloths, already reorganized around a reduced fauna, and managed within those constraints by people whose knowledge of the land's ecology was deep, specific, and accumulated over hundreds of generations. The restoration this framework envisions is a restoration of human-landscape relationships that were disrupted not by the Pleistocene extinctions but by European colonization, forced removal, and the suppression of Indigenous land-management practices.

The two baselines produce different prescriptions. A Pleistocene baseline applied to the American Southwest asks about camelids and equids, the animals the fossil record identifies as co-evolutionary partners of the landscape's vegetation, as Chapter 7 described. An Indigenous management baseline applied to the same landscape asks about fire regimes, about the cultivation of agave and mesquite, about the management of riparian corridors, about the specific practices of the O'odham, the Diné, the Apache, and other peoples whose management shaped the region's ecology for thousands of years before Spanish contact. Both sets of questions are worth asking. They are not the same questions, and they lead to different places.

Lyla June Johnston, a scholar of Diné and Tsétsêhéstâhese descent whose work on Indigenous regenerative land management has drawn attention across disciplinary boundaries, has articulated a version of this distinction with particular clarity. Johnston's research documents Indigenous land management practices across North America that produced what she describes as food forests: complex, multi-story, species-diverse managed ecosystems that provided food, medicine, fiber, and habitat simultaneously.<sup>4</sup> The management was not the management of wild landscapes by people who happened to live in them. It was the management of landscapes by people who understood themselves as participants in the ecological relationships the landscape maintained, people who saw their role not as external managers imposing order on nature but as keystone species whose management was itself a form of ecological function.

The keystone-species framing is the point at which the two traditions come closest to each other, and it is also the point at which the distinction between them is most important. The Pleistocene rewilding framework asks: which keystone species are missing from this landscape, and how do we restore them? The Indigenous management framework asks: what happens when humans function as keystone species, when human management is itself the ecological process the landscape depends on? The first question imagines restoration as the return of non-human animals to landscapes they have been absent from. The second imagines restoration as the return of human management practices to landscapes they have been absent from. Both involve restoring something the landscape has lost. What they propose to restore is different.

Notice that the two frameworks produce different models of what restoration looks like in practice. The Pleistocene frame, in its strongest version, imagines large tracts of land managed for ecological function with relatively minimal human intervention: proxy species reintroduced and then allowed to interact with the landscape in something approaching the self-organizing dynamics the Pleistocene assemblage would have produced. The Indigenous management frame imagines something closer to what Kimmerer calls the "honorable harvest": a landscape under continuous, attentive, knowledgeable human management, in which the management itself is the ecological process and the food is what the management yields. The two models are not opposites. They exist on a continuum, and any real landscape restoration would likely draw from both. But the emphases are different, and the difference in emphasis reflects a difference in what each tradition identifies as the primary loss to be restored.

I do not think one framework is correct and the other mistaken. I think they are complementary in a way that neither is sufficient to capture alone. A landscape like the American Southwest has lost both its Pleistocene megafauna and its Indigenous management traditions. A complete restoration framework for that landscape would need to engage both losses: the twelve-thousand-year absence of the camelids and equids, and the five-hundred-year suppression of the fire regimes, the managed harvesting, and the cultivation practices that Indigenous peoples maintained for the intervening millennia. The two losses are different in kind, different in timescale, and different in what their restoration would require. They are both real.

## WHAT THIS CHAPTER DOES NOT DO

I want to be explicit about the limits of this engagement, because the limits matter.

This chapter does not speak for Indigenous peoples. It engages the published work of Indigenous scholars (Kimmerer, Johnston, Anderson) on the terms those scholars have set. Their work represents a parallel intellectual tradition with its own epistemology, its own standards of evidence, and its own relationship to the landscapes it describes. The Pleistocene framework this book has developed is not a superior framework into which Indigenous knowledge can be slotted as supporting evidence. It is a different framework, developed from a different disciplinary tradition, asking a different set of questions. The dialogue between the two is valuable precisely because it is a dialogue between genuinely different perspectives, not because one subsumes the other.

This chapter does not romanticize Indigenous land management as a monolith. The practices Anderson documents in California are specific to California's ecosystems and to the peoples who managed them. The practices Johnston describes across the continent vary enormously by region, by nation, by ecosystem, and by the specific cultural and ecological knowledge of the specific peoples involved. There is no single "Indigenous land management practice" any more than there is a single "Western agricultural practice." The diversity is the point. The scholarship this chapter engages is valuable because it is specific: specific peoples, specific landscapes, specific practices, specific ecological outcomes.

This chapter does not instrumentalize Indigenous knowledge as support for the book's argument. The Pleistocene rewilding case stands or falls on its own evidence, as the previous chapters have presented it. What the Indigenous management literature adds is not evidence for the Pleistocene case but a parallel framework that the Pleistocene case should be in conversation with, a framework that reveals dimensions of the restoration question the Pleistocene frame alone cannot see. The conversation is additive. It is not appropriative.

## THE WATER COLUMN

The Indigenous management framework just described establishes something the Pleistocene frame alone cannot: a model of restoration in which the restored relationship, not just the restored species, is the unit of ecological function. Fire regimes, managed harvests, food forests maintained across centuries: these are relationships between people and landscapes that produced measurable ecological outcomes and that were lost not in the Pleistocene but within the last five hundred years. The question the framework raises is whether restoration of this kind can be demonstrated at scales and on timescales that make the case legible to policymakers and skeptics, not just to ecologists and Indigenous scholars who already understand it.

The marine domain is where that demonstration is furthest along. The rewilding conversation, in both the popular and the peer-reviewed literature, has been overwhelmingly terrestrial. The animals that dominate the discussion — mammoths, bison, wolves, camels, horses — are land animals. The landscapes that dominate the discussion — grasslands, forests, deserts, savannas — are land surfaces. The oceans have been present in the book only as a source of analogy (Daniel Pauly's shifting baseline syndrome in Chapter 2, the Alaska salmon fisheries in Chapter 8) rather than as a domain with its own restoration framework and its own set of losses to reckon with. That absence is a gap, and the gap matters for a specific reason: marine restoration operates on a faster clock than terrestrial restoration, and the results are correspondingly more concrete. If the argument this book has been making — that restoring ecological relationships produces food systems that score well on all four axes — needs a proof of concept, the water column is where the proof is most advanced.

The marine losses are enormous. They are also, unlike the Pleistocene megafaunal extinctions, primarily historical rather than prehistoric. They occurred within the window of human record-keeping, often within the last two centuries, and the documentation of what was lost is correspondingly detailed.

Kelp forests are the marine equivalent of old-growth terrestrial forests: structurally complex, biologically diverse, and foundational to the ecosystems they anchor. Kira Krumhansl and colleagues, in a 2016 analysis in *PNAS*, assembled the most comprehensive global dataset on kelp forest change to date, drawing on more than a hundred time series spanning multiple decades across the world's temperate and subpolar coastlines.<sup>5</sup> The synthesis found that kelp forests had declined in thirty-eight percent of the ecoregions studied, with particularly severe losses in parts of the North Atlantic, the southern coast of Australia, and several Pacific regions. The declines were driven by a combination of ocean warming, sea urchin population explosions following the removal of urchin predators — otters, large predatory fish — and coastal water-quality degradation from nutrient runoff and sedimentation. The pattern echoes the terrestrial trophic cascades this book has described: remove the predator, and the herbivore population expands, and the foundation species collapses, and the entire community reorganizes around the loss.

> **DEFINITION: Trophic Cascade**
>
> A trophic cascade is the amplification of ecological change through predator-prey relationships.

When a top predator is removed, herbivore populations expand without control, consuming the foundation species (kelp, grass, coral) those herbivores depend on. The herbivores eventually starve, but not before the foundation species has been destroyed. The system reorganizes around the loss: kelp forests become urchin barrens, grasslands become shrublands, coral reefs become algal deserts.

The cascade demonstrates that ecosystems are held in shape by relationships rather than by species individually. The predator does not merely eat the herbivore. It holds the entire structure in place by eating the herbivore. Remove the predator and the structure inverts. The pattern runs both directions, and the restoration record (sea otters, wolves, large sharks) shows the inversion can often be reversed if the predator returns in time.

The difference between the kelp story and the mammoth story is that the kelp story is reversible on timescales that matter for policy. Where sea otter populations have recovered, through legal protection under the Marine Mammal Protection Act and through active reintroduction programs along the Pacific coast, kelp forests have recovered with them. James Estes and colleagues documented this relationship in foundational work beginning in the 1970s, showing that the presence or absence of sea otters was the primary determinant of whether a given stretch of coastline supported a kelp forest or an urchin barren.<sup>6</sup> The mechanism is straightforward trophic regulation: otters eat urchins, urchins eat kelp, and the presence of the predator controls the herbivore, which allows the foundation species to persist. Remove the otters and the system collapses to an urchin barren. Return the otters and the system recovers to a kelp forest. The time required for recovery, once the predator is restored, is measured in years rather than in millennia. The marine trophic cascade operates on a faster clock than the terrestrial one, and the restoration results are correspondingly more visible within a human career.

Oyster reefs present a parallel case with an additional dimension: the harvested organism is simultaneously performing ecological work. Michael Beck and colleagues, in their 2011 synthesis in *BioScience*, documented that approximately eighty-five percent of the world's oyster reef habitat had been lost, primarily through overharvest, disease, and habitat degradation.<sup>7</sup> The loss is not merely a fisheries loss. Oyster reefs are ecosystem engineers, the marine equivalent of the keystone herbivores this book has described on land. A healthy oyster reef filters water: a single adult oyster can filter approximately fifty gallons of water per day, removing suspended sediment, excess nitrogen, and algal biomass from the water column.<sup>8</sup> Oyster reefs provide structural habitat for hundreds of species of fish, crustaceans, and invertebrates. They stabilize shorelines against wave erosion. They sequester carbon and nitrogen in their shell matrix. The loss of eighty-five percent of the world's oyster reefs is not a loss of one species. It is the loss of an ecosystem function that maintained water quality, supported fisheries, and protected coastlines across every temperate and subtropical estuary where oysters once grew.

The restoration case is as strong for oyster reefs as for any terrestrial system the book has described. Federal and state partnership programs in the Chesapeake Bay, the Gulf of Mexico, and Puget Sound have rebuilt reef acreage on substrates of recycled shell and limestone, and the restored reefs have shown rapid colonization by native oyster populations, with measurable improvements in water clarity, fisheries productivity, and shoreline stability within years of construction.<sup>9</sup> The food-system dimension is direct: restored oyster reefs produce a harvestable food product while simultaneously performing the ecological work the estuary depends on. The dependency profile is thin in exactly the way the survivability framework measures: the oyster needs the water column, and the water column needs the oyster, and neither requires commodity feed, antibiotics, diesel, or federal subsidies to keep the relationship functioning.

The food-system dimension of kelp forest restoration is also worth noting. Kelp forests are not merely habitat — they are productive ecosystems that support fisheries directly. The rockfish, lingcod, sea urchin, and abalone populations that kelp forests sustain are commercially harvested species. A coastline that supports a kelp forest supports a fishery. A coastline that has collapsed to an urchin barren supports neither. The restoration calculus is not an abstraction: it is a calculation about whether a specific stretch of coast produces food or does not, and the variable that determines the answer is whether the trophic structure is intact.

The broader marine picture extends the argument. The distinction between farmed salmon and wild Alaskan salmon is not merely a culinary preference or a marketing category. It is a distinction between two fundamentally different food systems with fundamentally different dependency profiles. Farmed Atlantic salmon — produced in net-pen aquaculture operations predominantly in Norway, Chile, Scotland, and British Columbia — requires continuous inputs of processed fish meal and fish oil derived from wild forage-fish harvests, continuous antibiotic and antiparasitic administration to manage the disease pressure inherent to high-density aquaculture, and continuous management of the waste stream the operations produce.<sup>10</sup> A major farmed-salmon operation is, in structural terms, the marine equivalent of a terrestrial CAFO: a confined animal feeding operation in the water column, with many of the same dependency vulnerabilities the terrestrial version carries. Wild Alaskan salmon, managed under the stock-assessment methodology Chapter 8 described, requires the river system to remain intact, the ocean ecosystem to remain productive, and the harvest to remain within the bounds the stock's reproductive capacity sets. The dependency profile is the ecosystem itself. The difference between the two systems, scored on the survivability axis, is the same difference the book has documented on land: a system organized around industrial inputs versus a system organized around ecological function.

The salmon case connects, moreover, to the Indigenous framework this chapter has been developing. Pacific Northwest salmon fisheries were managed by Indigenous peoples for thousands of years before European contact, under systems that sustained both the harvest and the ecological function the salmon perform: nutrient transport from ocean to riparian forest, bear and eagle feeding, marine-derived nitrogen deposition in inland soils. The industrial replacement of those management systems with commodity extraction is a marine parallel to the terrestrial replacement of Indigenous fire management with fire suppression. In both cases, a management tradition that sustained the ecosystem's productive capacity across millennia was replaced by an extractive regime that degraded it within decades. In both cases, the restoration question is not merely ecological but cultural: whose knowledge of the system is being restored alongside the system itself?

What marine ecosystems would look like restored to historical baselines is a question the fisheries-history literature has begun to answer in remarkable detail. Jeremy Jackson and colleagues, in a landmark 2001 paper in *Science*, assembled historical, archaeological, and paleontological evidence to reconstruct the ecological state of coastal marine ecosystems before the onset of industrial exploitation.<sup>11</sup> The reconstruction was sobering. Coastal ecosystems that are now managed as though their current depleted state were normal had, within the last few centuries, supported populations of marine vertebrates — whales, sea turtles, sharks, large predatory fish, seabirds — at densities that no living marine scientist has observed in the wild. The Chesapeake Bay, before industrial oyster dredging, contained oyster reefs sufficient to filter the bay's entire water volume in approximately a week; the current remnant population takes approximately a year to filter the same volume.<sup>12</sup> Caribbean coral reefs, before the sequential removal of green turtles, manatees, and parrotfish, maintained algal communities in states that no modern reef ecologist has studied in the absence of the animals whose grazing controlled them. The marine shifting baseline, in Jackson's reconstruction, is at least as severe as the terrestrial one this book has documented.

The convergence of the marine and Indigenous frameworks is worth noting here, because it is not incidental. Many coastal Indigenous peoples managed marine systems with the same sophistication Anderson documented for terrestrial ones. The clam gardens of the Pacific Northwest, intertidal rock-wall terraces constructed by First Nations peoples to increase the productivity of clam beds, illustrate this pattern precisely. Research by Dana Lepofsky, Nicole Smith, and colleagues has documented that clam gardens increased clam productivity by factors of two to four compared with unmodified beaches, through deliberate modification of substrate, tidal exposure, and sediment dynamics.<sup>13</sup> The management was not fishing in the extractive sense. It was mariculture: the active management of a marine ecosystem to increase its food-producing capacity while maintaining the ecological relationships the system depended on. The clam gardens were, in structural terms, the marine equivalent of the managed fire regimes Anderson documented on land: human management that enhanced rather than degraded the ecosystem's productive capacity, transmitted across generations, and disrupted by colonization.

> **CASE: The Clam Gardens of the Pacific Northwest**
>
> Between what is now British Columbia and Southeast Alaska, First Nations peoples engineered the intertidal zone for millennia through the construction of rock-wall terraces that modified substrate angle, sediment dynamics, and tidal exposure.

These clam gardens increased clam biomass by factors of two to four compared to unmodified beaches, not through extraction but through deliberate ecological manipulation that enhanced the beach's productive capacity. Research by Dana Lepofsky, Nicole Smith, and colleagues has documented systematic construction, transmission across generations, and rapid recovery of productivity under restoration.

Clam gardens represent the marine equivalent of California's Indigenous fire management: human-engineered ecosystems that produced food while maintaining, and often increasing, the ecological health they depended on. The archaeological record of the practice extends back thousands of years.

The marine domain, in short, has its own version of every argument this book has made about land. It has its own missing keystone species. It has its own depleted baselines mistaken for natural states. It has its own trophic cascades demonstrating what happens when the missing species return. It has its own food systems — wild fisheries, restored shellfish, intact pelagic ecosystems — that produce food as functions of ecological health rather than as alternatives to it. And it has been almost entirely absent from the popular rewilding conversation, which has focused on charismatic terrestrial megafauna to the exclusion of the water column that covers seventy percent of the planet's surface.

## INTEGRATION

The Indigenous management framework adds something the Pleistocene frame alone cannot provide: a model of restoration that includes people. The Pleistocene rewilding conversation, as it has developed in the conservation biology literature, has tended to imagine restoration as the return of non-human animals to landscapes from which humans are largely absent, or at least from which human management is minimized. Indigenous management traditions offer a different vision: restoration as the return of human-landscape relationships in which human management is itself an ecological function. The two visions are not incompatible. A landscape could support both the return of proxy megafauna and the restoration of Indigenous fire and harvesting practices. The proxy species and the management practices would interact (as they always have, throughout the history of human habitation of every landscape on Earth), and the interaction would be the ecology of a managed, inhabited, restored system rather than the ecology of a wilderness emptied of people.

The marine framework adds something the terrestrial frame cannot: completed experiments. The terrestrial rewilding proposals this book has described operate on timescales of decades to centuries. The marine domain has already demonstrated what restoration looks like when it works: kelp forests recovering in years, oyster reefs becoming functional habitat within seasons, fish populations rebounding within a few reproductive generations under calibrated harvest. The clam gardens of the Pacific Northwest sit at the intersection of both expansions: Indigenous mariculture that enhanced marine productivity for centuries before colonization, disrupted by contact, and now being documented in ways that make its restoration empirically tractable.

Both expansions operate within the four-axis framework the book has developed. Indigenous management traditions, where they have been practiced and documented, produce food systems that score well on all four axes — economically viable within their scale, socially equitable by design, environmentally generative rather than extractive, and highly survivable because their dependency profiles are thin and their relationship to the landscape is constitutive rather than instrumental. Marine restoration programs produce food systems with the same profile: the ecosystem remains intact, the management remains disciplined, and the food is what the intact ecosystem produces when the management holds.

The book's argument is not weaker for admitting that the Pleistocene frame is one frame among several. It is stronger. A single-frame argument is brittle: it stands or falls on the validity of one analytical lens. An argument that holds the Pleistocene frame in dialogue with Indigenous management traditions and with marine restoration is an argument with multiple foundations. The frames support each other not by agreeing on everything but by covering each other's gaps. If the Pleistocene proxy-selection hypothesis proves more difficult to test than its advocates hope, the Indigenous management framework and the marine restoration framework remain. If the political obstacles to terrestrial rewilding prove as formidable as Rubenstein has warned, marine restoration offers a domain where the ecological case is already demonstrated and the political barriers are lower.

Part IV of this book, which this chapter opens, is about doing. The preceding chapters made the case. The remaining chapters ask what follows from it: how the framework adapts to where readers actually live, what practical guidance it offers, what must change in policy and culture for the trajectory to shift. The Pleistocene frame asks what the land was before the losses. The Indigenous frame asks what the land was under the management of the people who knew it best. The marine frame asks what the water was before the baselines shifted. Each question produces a different restoration target. Together, they produce a picture of what this continent could be — on land and at sea, in managed landscapes and in rewilded ones — that is richer, more honest, and more defensible than any single frame can draw alone.

# Chapter Eleven: Bioregional and Urban

The framework this book has developed does not produce a single answer. It produces different answers for different places, and the difference is not a weakness in the framework. It is the framework working correctly.

The previous chapters built the argument in general terms: a four-axis assessment, a proxy-selection principle guided by the fossil record, a set of restoration frameworks (Pleistocene, Indigenous, marine) that converge on the proposition that food systems organized around ecological function score better across every axis than food systems organized around industrial inputs. The argument, as stated, is continental in scope. It applies, in principle, to every landscape on the continent. What it does not do, stated at that level, is tell anyone what the argument means for the specific piece of ground they are standing on. A rancher in southern Arizona, a fisherman on the Gulf Coast, a family in the hardwood hills of New England, and a software engineer in a Chicago apartment all inhabit different ecological realities, and the framework, if it is worth anything, must be able to speak to each of them in terms that are specific rather than abstract.

This chapter does two things. First, it walks through specific bioregional examples to show what the four-axis framework looks like when it is applied to particular ground: specific landscapes, specific species assemblages, specific food-system possibilities. Second, it addresses the readers who do not have ground to apply it to: the urban readers, the apartment dwellers, the people whose relationship to the food system runs through a grocery store rather than through a pasture. Most readers of this book will be those people. The chapter takes them seriously.

## THE ARID SOUTHWEST

Chapter 7 established the proxy-selection argument for the Southwest through the Savory food-truck conversation and the Grand Canyon fossil record. I will not repeat that material here. What I want to do is take the argument one step further: from the proxy-selection principle to a worked picture of what a food system guided by that principle would look like across the region.

The arid Southwest — the Sonoran and Chihuahuan basins, the Colorado Plateau, the sky-island ranges of southeastern Arizona and southwestern New Mexico — is a landscape whose vegetation is still calibrated to animals that have been absent for twelve thousand years. The thorny, browse-heavy plant communities that dominate the region — creosote, mesquite, catclaw acacia, palo verde, saltbush, yucca, prickly pear — evolved under browsing pressure from camelids and ground sloths whose foraging kept the woody vegetation in check and whose dung cycled nutrients through soils that receive less than twelve inches of rainfall per year.<sup>1</sup> The current management regime puts cattle on this landscape. The cattle graze what grass exists, avoid the browse, and leave the woody species to expand unchecked.
