The result, documented across decades of range-science literature, is mesquite encroachment: the steady conversion of open grassland to dense mesquite thicket, driven not by climate change but by the absence of the browsers that historically suppressed the mesquite.<sup>2</sup> The range is not degrading because it is being used. It is degrading because it is being used by the wrong animal.

A food system guided by the fossil record would look different. Dromedary camels browsing mesquite and palo verde on Sonoran rangeland; Bactrian camels in the cold-winter and saline basins to the north and west. Horses and burros (already present in the region by the hundreds of thousands, currently managed as nuisance populations on BLM land) reframed as proxy species occupying an ecological role their lineage held for millions of years before a twelve-thousand-year interruption.<sup>3</sup> The browse species that cattle refuse are the species the fossil record's animals consumed. The mesquite pods that accumulate uneaten under the current regime are a calorie-dense feed source for camelids whose digestive systems evolved to process exactly that kind of material. The prickly pear fruits that drop and rot are fruits the ground sloths once dispersed. The landscape is not short of food. It is short of the mouths the food was made for.

The products of such a system would be unfamiliar to the American consumer but not to the global one. Camel milk (higher in fat and protein than cow's milk, lower in lactose, and already a significant commercial product in the Middle East, East Africa, and parts of Central Asia) is produced by an animal that thrives on arid-adapted vegetation without irrigation or supplemental feed.<sup>4</sup> Horse and burro meat, while culturally unfamiliar in the United States, is consumed widely in Europe, Central Asia, and South America, and the protein is lean, low in intramuscular fat, and nutritionally comparable to venison. The cultural barriers are real, and I do not dismiss them. The ecological logic is also real. The framework does not insist that everyone eat camel. It observes that a food system organized around the animals the Southwest's landscape evolved under would score better on every axis (environmental integrity, survivability, and the ecological dimension of social equity) than the cattle-based system currently imposed on that landscape.

> **DEFINITION: Bactrian and Dromedary**
>
> The proxy literature names “camels” as the candidate replacement for *Camelops*, and the book has followed that convention. The proxy decision is, in fact, two decisions. There are two living Old World camel species, they occupy distinct ecological niches, they tolerate different climates, and the difference matters for which American landscape gets which animal.
>
> The dromedary, *Camelus dromedarius*, is the single-humped camel of the Sahara, the Arabian Peninsula, and the Horn of Africa. It is heat-adapted: a sparse coat, body mass typically four to seven hundred kilograms, and a metabolism optimized for high-temperature, low-humidity environments. Dromedaries thrive on hot-desert browse, including acacia, mesquite, palo verde, and the thorny succulents, and tolerate water deprivation that would kill cattle or sheep within days. They are the species the Saudi-Gulf trade is built around, and the species the US Camel Corps experimented with in the 1850s.
>
> The Bactrian, *Camelus bactrianus*, is the two-humped camel of the Central Asian steppe, the Gobi, and the Mongolian and northern Chinese deserts. It is the wide-temperature-tolerance specialist of the camelid lineage. A dense winter coat, body mass commonly six hundred to a thousand kilograms, and a tolerance range that runs from forty degrees below zero in steppe winters to over a hundred degrees Fahrenheit in summer rangeland use. Bactrians eat plants other camels treat as marginal: saxaul, salt-stressed *Atriplex*, and the halophytic shrubs of the Amaranthaceae and Chenopodiaceae families that store sodium at concentrations toxic to most herbivores. They drink saline water that other livestock will not, with documented tolerance above two percent salinity. The Bactrian's foot pad, broader and heavier than the dromedary's, also performs a service the dromedary does less of: it fractures the biological soil crusts and physical caps that form on overgrazed and compacted desert ground, opening the surface to water infiltration and seedling establishment.
>
> The halophyte-eating ability is the unsung edge. Halophytes — *Atriplex*, *Salicornia*, *Halocnemum*, *Salsola* — accumulate salt in their tissues at concentrations that exclude most herbivores from using them. The plants are dominant on salt-affected soils across the western United States: irrigated farmland where a century of input water has accumulated sodium and chloride beyond what conventional crops can tolerate, and natural saline playas where the geology has produced the same outcome. The Bactrian, alone among large livestock species, can convert halophyte biomass into meat, milk, and other commodities while removing salt from the system through the animal's tissues and excreting it in urine. This is phytoremediation with a productive output, and the working land it could be applied to is not abstract: several million acres of the western US carry soil salinity sufficient to limit conventional agriculture, and most of that acreage is not currently producing food.
>
> The utility extends well beyond restoration. Camel meat is lean, high in protein, and an established staple across much of the Old World. Camel milk is higher in protein and several minerals than cow's milk, lower in lactose, and increasingly used in the United States by the autism community for behavioral and gastrointestinal symptoms — documented in small randomized clinical trials by Al-Ayadhi (2013) and Bashir (2014), and rising in the marketplace. A 2026 systematic review by Alshebrami and colleagues further documents the nutritional and immunological case for camel milk as a candidate infant formula, particularly for infants with cow’s milk protein allergy or lactose intolerance. Lactoferrin, a milk protein with antimicrobial, antiviral, and immunomodulatory activity, occurs in camel milk at high concentration; recent work has identified that camel tears carry it as well, and a small extraction industry has begun to form around it. Bactrian wool, comparable in fineness to cashmere, supports the substantial commercial fiber industry of Mongolia and northern China. The animal is not a single-product animal. It is a multi-product animal in a regulatory environment that does not yet recognize multi-product animals as a category.
>
> The bioregional decision follows. The hot southern deserts (Sonoran, Chihuahuan, lower Mojave) are dromedary country: the climate matches the species. The cold-winter Great Basin, the Colorado Plateau, the Wyoming Red Desert, and the saline-affected playas across the basin-and-range are Bactrian country: the cold tolerance, the halophyte browse, the soil-crust breaking, and the salt-water tolerance all line up. *Camelops*, the extinct American camel the proposal is actually about, was probably closer to the Bactrian in body mass and in temperature tolerance, and the paleogenomic work of Heintzman and colleagues now places it as sister to the Old World *Camelus* lineage rather than to *Lama* and *Vicugna*. The right answer to “which camel” is therefore both, allocated by landscape.

> **CASE: A Million Camels Looking for a Continent**
>
> The supply question for the Southwest's camelid scenario has a counterintuitive answer: there are too many camels in the world already, and one continent is killing them rather than using them.
>
> Australia carries the world's largest population of feral dromedaries — descended from camels imported in the nineteenth century to support exploration and freight across the interior, released into the bush after the rail and motor lines came in, and reproducing across a continent whose vegetation suits them well. By the late 2000s, federal and state surveys placed the population at approaching one million animals, expanding at roughly ten percent per year, and producing the kind of damage to fences, water infrastructure, and native vegetation that puts a species into the legal category of pest.
>
> The federal response was the Australian Feral Camel Management Project, a four-year program (2009–2013) funded at roughly nineteen million Australian dollars, which culled more than one hundred forty thousand camels, primarily through aerial gunning from helicopters across the central deserts. The program reduced the population to an estimated three hundred thousand by program end. By 2020, with reproduction outpacing residual culling effort, the population had recovered enough that the Anangu Pitjantjatjara Yankunytjatjara Lands authorized an emergency cull during drought, killing approximately ten thousand additional camels over five days when the animals began damaging community water infrastructure searching for moisture. The current population is again estimated in the high hundreds of thousands and rising.
>
> The Gulf states are running the inverse experiment. Saudi Arabia is the world's largest importer of camel meat and live camels, anchored in a regional camel-meat economy that runs through the United Arab Emirates, Qatar, Oman, Kuwait, and Bahrain at multi-billion-dollar scale. The kingdom's interest is not nostalgic. Camel meat is a staple protein, camel milk a premium commodity, and camel racing a substantial industry. The trade has scaled to the point that the regional shipping infrastructure now includes dedicated multi-deck livestock-carrier vessels — purpose-built ships, not chartered aircraft — moving live animals across the Indian Ocean at volumes that air freight cannot economically support.
>
> The contrast is the same contrast the feral-hog discussion will draw at smaller scale and inside one country. Australia treats the surplus animal as a damage vector and pays its taxpayers to kill it. Saudi Arabia treats the same animal as a food source and invests in infrastructure to import it. The United States, which has the landscape that wants the animal and a federal regulatory architecture that does not yet have a category for it, treats the question as not yet asked. Three continents, three outcomes, one species.
>
> The animal-welfare reading writes itself. If the choice for a particular cohort of feral Australian camels is between aerial gunning and live transport across an ocean, the second outcome is the better outcome for the animal. If the choice is between aerial gunning and a transport pathway that ends with the camels released onto American Pleistocene-functional rangeland under a research protocol of the kind Chapter Thirteen describes, the gap between actual and possible widens further.
>
> The supply problem the Southwest scenario faces is not, in the end, a supply problem. It is a regulatory and logistical problem: which animals are permitted to cross which oceans on which paperwork. The animals exist. The market for them exists. The transport infrastructure exists. What does not yet exist is the American regulatory category that would permit the third option.

The survivability advantage is particularly sharp. A camel on Sonoran rangeland requires no irrigation, no commodity feed, no antibiotics, and no subsidized grain. It drinks infrequently, tolerates heat that would kill cattle, and feeds itself on vegetation the landscape produces without human input. The dependency profile is as thin as anything the framework can measure. When the corn supply is disrupted, when the diesel price spikes, when the aquifer drops another foot, the camel is still eating mesquite. The landscape is still producing. The system is still functioning, because the system's primary input is the landscape itself, and the landscape has been producing browse for twelve thousand years without asking anyone for help.

## THE NORTHERN GREAT PLAINS

The Great Plains are the bioregion where the framework's prescription comes closest to what is already being practiced. The shortgrass prairie evolved under bison, and bison are still here: roughly 500,000 of them, most in commercial herds, roughly 20,000 in conservation herds managed for genetic diversity and ecological function.<sup>5</sup> The proxy-selection question, for this landscape, is largely answered. The animal the prairie evolved under is the animal that should be on the prairie. The question is not whether bison belong on the Plains. It is what happens when you take the relationship seriously.

American Prairie, in northeastern Montana, is the most ambitious attempt to answer that question on the ground. The project, which began acquiring private ranchland and grazing leases in Phillips County in 2004, is assembling a contiguous landscape of more than three million acres — private land, BLM grazing allotments, and the Charles M. Russell National Wildlife Refuge — and managing it for a functioning prairie ecosystem with bison as the keystone grazer. The herd, as of 2025, numbers roughly 800 animals on approximately 90,000 acres of deeded and leased land, managed under a protocol that allows the bison to graze freely across the landscape without the rotational confinement that cattle operations impose. The results are visible from satellite imagery: the bison-grazed sections show a mosaic of close-cropped and tall-grass patches (the heterogeneous structure the prairie's plant diversity depends on) that the adjacent cattle-grazed sections, managed under standard rotational grazing, do not produce. The difference is not management philosophy. It is the behavior of the animal. Bison graze selectively, return to preferred patches repeatedly, wallow in depressions that become ephemeral wetlands used by breeding shorebirds and amphibians, and move across the landscape at scales that create the spatial variability the prairie's full community of grasses, forbs, insects, and ground-nesting birds requires. Cattle, even under the best rotational management, do not produce this mosaic because they are not bison and do not behave like bison on grass.

The food-system dimension of American Prairie is still developing, but the economics of bison production on native grassland are already legible from the commercial herds that have been operating across the northern Plains for decades. A bison cow on native prairie feeds herself year-round. She requires no commodity grain, no supplemental hay in most winters if the range is adequate, no antibiotics (bison are notably resistant to the respiratory diseases that plague cattle in confined conditions), and no subsidized inputs of any kind. The animal's maintenance cost is the grass, and the grass is what the prairie produces without being asked. The calves grow on their mothers' milk and on the prairie's forage, and the harvested animals (processed through the small but growing network of bison-certified processing facilities in Montana, South Dakota, and Colorado) produce a lean, nutrient-dense meat that commands a retail premium of roughly two to three times the price of commodity beef. The premium reflects consumer demand for a product perceived as healthier and more environmentally responsible, and it is large enough to sustain the operations at their current scale.

The framework adds specificity to what this arrangement means in survivability terms. The cattle ranching economy of the northern Plains operates under the dependency structure the book has documented: commodity markets set the price, federal subsidies smooth the volatility, and the rancher's margin depends on conditions the rancher does not control. A bison operation on native prairie operates under a different structure. The animal feeds itself on the prairie's own production. The harvest is governed by the herd's population dynamics and the range's carrying capacity rather than by the commodity cycle. The ecological services the bison provide while alive — the grazing that maintains grass vigor, the wallowing, the selective foraging — are not incidental. They are the ecological process that keeps the prairie functioning as prairie, and the food the operation produces is a byproduct of that maintenance rather than an extraction from it. The survivability score is high not because bison are exotic or novel but because the relationship between bison and prairie is the oldest working partnership on the landscape, and it requires the least from outside itself to keep functioning.

The tribal dimension is equally important and should not be treated as secondary. The InterTribal Buffalo Council, a consortium of more than seventy tribal nations, manages bison herds across reservations from Montana to Oklahoma, and the herds serve cultural, ecological, and food-sovereignty purposes simultaneously. For the Blackfeet, the Fort Peck Assiniboine and Sioux, the Rosebud Sioux, and other northern Plains nations, the bison's return to tribal land is not merely an ecological restoration. It is the restoration of a relationship that was deliberately severed as an instrument of colonial policy. The nineteenth-century slaughter of the bison herds was, among other things, a military strategy to deprive Plains nations of their primary food source and force dependence on federal commodity rations. The return of the bison to tribal management is, in this context, simultaneously a food-system intervention, an ecological restoration, and a reassertion of sovereignty over a relationship the federal government spent a century trying to destroy. The framework scores the tribal bison operations well because the operations score well. The historical weight behind the scores is something the framework does not capture but the reader should know.

The scaling question for the Plains is not ecological but institutional. The land exists. The northern Great Plains contain millions of acres of native grassland, much of it under cattle grazing leases that could, in principle, support bison instead. The animal exists. The ecological relationship is intact. What does not yet exist at scale is the market infrastructure, the processing capacity, and the policy framework that would allow bison production on native grassland to move from a conservation-niche market to a meaningful fraction of the region's protein supply. The USDA's inspection requirements, the geographic concentration of bison-certified processing facilities, and the absence of bison from the commodity-support programs that underwrite cattle production all create friction that is institutional rather than ecological. The constraint is not the landscape. The constraint is the institutional architecture built around cattle, and the question is whether that architecture can be expanded to accommodate the animal the landscape actually wants.

## TWO FURTHER BIOREGIONS, BRIEFLY

The Gulf Coast and the Pacific Northwest each deserve extended treatment, and each illustrates a different dimension of the framework's application. I will sketch them here rather than develop them fully, because the principles the Southwest, Plains, and New England sections have established apply to these regions with modifications of detail rather than of structure.

The Gulf Coast (from the Texas barrier islands through the Louisiana marshes to the Florida panhandle) is where the marine and terrestrial frameworks converge most visibly. On land, the region's Pleistocene fauna included tapirs, capybaras, llamas, and ground sloths, a Neotropical assemblage whose proxy-selection question is more complex than for the arid Southwest or the grassland Plains.<sup>6</sup> On water, the Gulf is where the oyster-reef restoration arguments from Chapter 10 come into sharpest focus: reef restoration programs across four states are rebuilding habitat that simultaneously filters water, stabilizes shoreline, and produces a harvestable food product whose dependency profile is the estuarine ecosystem itself.<sup>7</sup> The wild-caught Gulf shrimp fishery, battered by competition from farmed imports whose dependency profile runs through the industrial substrate, is a food system the framework can distinguish from its competitor on the fourth axis alone.<sup>8</sup>

The Pacific Northwest (western Oregon and Washington, coastal British Columbia) is the bioregion where all three frameworks converge on a single watershed. The salmon is the organizing species: Pacific salmon connect the ocean to the interior through their spawning migrations, transporting marine-derived nitrogen upstream into riparian forests where it is measurable in the growth rings of old-growth trees miles from the coast.<sup>12</sup> The terrestrial side runs through the region's extraordinary soil productivity and the Indigenous management traditions Chapter 10 described: the fire-maintained prairies of the Willamette Valley, the camas cultivation, the oak savannas that Europeans mistook for natural meadows.<sup>13</sup> The shellfish dimension (Puget Sound's geoduck, Dungeness crab, and Pacific oyster industries) adds marine food systems whose dependency profiles run through ecosystem health rather than the industrial supply chain. The Northwest's food-system potential, viewed through the framework, is a network of interlocking marine and terrestrial systems whose collective survivability depends on the health of the watershed that connects them. Restore the salmon, and the riparian forests receive their nitrogen. Restore the otters, and the kelp forests recover, and the nearshore fisheries follow. The systems are coupled, and restoring one restores the conditions the others depend on.

## THE NEW ENGLAND HARDWOODS

New England is a landscape that has been farmed, logged, abandoned, and reforested within the span of written American history. The stone walls that run through second-growth forest across Connecticut, Massachusetts, Vermont, and New Hampshire mark the boundaries of fields that were cleared in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries and released back to forest as New England agriculture moved west or collapsed under competition from the Midwest's deeper soils.<sup>9</sup> The forest that has regrown is young by ecological standards (most of it is less than a hundred and fifty years old), and it is missing most of the large-bodied fauna that the region's Pleistocene and early-Holocene forests supported.

The mastodon was the eastern hardwoods' dominant large browser. Its range extended from the Great Lakes to the Gulf Coast, and its feeding behavior (browsing the understory, stripping bark, creating canopy gaps) shaped the structure of the deciduous forest in ways the current forest does not reflect.<sup>10</sup> There is no living proxy for a mastodon. The framework does not pretend otherwise. What the framework does suggest is that the New England food system should lean into the ecological character of the landscape it sits on — a forest landscape, not a grassland — and that the food-system possibilities of managed forest are more extensive than the region's current agricultural economy recognizes.

The food-system potential of the New England forest is not hypothetical. It is already being demonstrated at small scale across the region, though the formal economy barely registers it. Consider what the forest produces without being asked: mast crops — acorns, beechnuts, hickory nuts, black walnuts — that fall by the ton across millions of acres every autumn and are consumed primarily by deer, turkeys, and squirrels because no commercial infrastructure exists to harvest them at scale for human use. Maple syrup, already a $150-million-per-year industry in the Northeast, produced from trees that require no planting, no irrigation, no fertilizer, and no annual disturbance: a food product whose dependency profile is the forest itself and a late-winter freeze-thaw cycle. Wild mushrooms — chanterelles, hen-of-the-woods, black trumpets, morels — that fruit reliably in the same forests, sought by restaurant buyers who pay $15 to $30 per pound for a product that grows on dead wood without human intervention. Ramps, fiddleheads, sumac, elderberry, and the broader category of wild edibles that the foraging economy has begun to rediscover but that the formal food system still treats as marginal curiosities rather than as products of an ecosystem's inherent productivity.

What distinguishes these products from niche delicacies is that the ecosystem produces them as a function of its own ecological processes. The maple sap flows because the tree is cycling nutrients between root and crown in late winter. The mushrooms fruit because the mycelial network is decomposing wood and cycling carbon. The mast falls because the oaks and hickories are reproducing. None of these require the forest to be anything other than a forest. The food is what the forest does. The economic question is not whether the forest produces value (it does, continuously) but whether the institutional and market infrastructure exists to capture that value as food rather than leaving it on the ground.

Silvopastoral systems in the New England hardwoods take the argument one step further: not merely harvesting what the forest produces passively but managing the forest as a food-producing ecosystem through deliberate integration of animals under the canopy. Pigs on the forest floor, processing mast and rooting through the understory in a pattern that mimics the soil disturbance the extinct peccaries once produced. The heritage breeds suited to this work — Berkshires, Large Blacks, breeds whose foraging behavior and fat-deposition patterns were selected over centuries for woodland production rather than confinement — produce a pork whose flavor and fat profile reflect the mast-based diet in ways that commodity pork, raised on corn and soy in climate-controlled buildings, does not. A pig finished on acorns and beechnuts under a New England hardwood canopy is producing Ibérico-grade pork (the same mast-finishing principle that makes Spanish *jamón ibérico* one of the most valued cured meats in the world) from an ecosystem that is reproducing the mast without human input. The economics of mast-finished pork are not commodity economics. The product commands a premium because it is a different product, and the premium is supportable because the input costs are low: the feed is what falls from the trees.

Sheep and goats browsing the understory in managed rotation add a second layer. The Janzen-Martin anachronism fruits — pawpaws, honey locust, Kentucky coffee tree — are eastern species whose oversized fruits, thick pods, and large seeds are calibrated to dispersal by animals that have been absent for twelve thousand years.<sup>11</sup> Cultivating these species in silvopastoral systems would begin to restore a plant-animal relationship that has been one-sided since the mastodons disappeared. The pawpaw, in particular, is a fruit whose commercial potential has been recognized only recently: a custard-textured, tropical-flavored native fruit that grows in the understory of eastern hardwood forests and requires almost no management once established. The tree is shade-tolerant, pest-resistant, and productive for decades. It is also almost entirely absent from the commercial food system because the fruit is soft, perishable, and does not ship well in the cold-chain infrastructure built for apples and oranges. The market limitation is logistical, not ecological. The fruit exists. The demand exists. What does not exist is the supply-chain infrastructure calibrated to a forest product rather than an orchard product.

The New England case illustrates the framework's sensitivity to history in a way the other bioregions do not. The region's agricultural landscape was created by clearing forest. Its ecological trajectory, in the absence of active management, is reforestation. A food-system framework that asks New England to maintain open pasture at scale is asking the landscape to maintain a condition it is actively trying to leave. A framework that asks New England to produce food from its forest (the ecological state the landscape is returning to on its own) is working with the trajectory rather than against it. The survivability implications are direct: a food system aligned with the landscape's own ecological direction requires less energy to maintain than one that fights that direction.

The stone walls in the woods are the evidence. They mark the boundaries of a food system that required constant labor to hold the forest back (clearing, plowing, fencing, draining) on soils that were thinner and rockier than the Midwest bottomland that ultimately outcompeted them. The forest won that contest. It reclaimed the fields within decades of abandonment, and the walls now run through maple and oak and birch as monuments to a wager the landscape refused to honor. The next food system for this landscape should not make the same wager against the same opponent. It should ask, instead, what the forest is willing to produce. And the answer, if you look at what the forest is already producing without being asked, is more than the formal economy has begun to measure.

> **CASE: Two Future-Food Categories Through the Four-Axis Test**
>
> The framework has to hold up against food systems we are still building, not just food systems we have inherited. Two of the most-discussed categories in 2026 are cultivated meat (sometimes called cellular agriculture or lab-grown meat) and closed-loop aquaponics. Below is how each scores when the framework is applied at the level of present-day implementation, with the caveat that both categories are evolving and each score moves with the technology.
>
> *Cultivated meat.* Economic viability is the axis that has, as of this writing, been the hardest. Production costs have fallen by orders of magnitude since the 2013 Mosa Meat demonstration but remain well above grocery-shelf parity for unsubsidized retail. Social fit is mixed: regulatory approval has come in Singapore, the United States, and Israel, but consumer acceptance varies regionally and the supply chain (bioreactors, growth media, regulatory infrastructure) does not employ the agricultural workforce the displaced production did. Environmental fit depends entirely on where the energy comes from; with low-carbon electricity, life-cycle greenhouse-gas emissions can be substantially below conventional beef, but with grid-average electricity, the advantage shrinks. Survivability is where the framework gets least friendly: the system depends on continuous high-purity inputs (growth media, sterile bioreactor environments, reliable cold chain) that are themselves outputs of the industrial system. A cultivated-meat plant cannot operate during a sustained electricity or supply-chain disruption. The system is, on the survivability axis, more brittle than conventional pasture and roughly comparable to industrial broilers.
>
> *Closed-loop aquaponics.* Economic viability depends heavily on scale and on energy cost; small operations carry high per-unit cost but high product premium. Social fit is high in urban and peri-urban contexts where the production is visible and integrates with restaurant or institutional buyers. There is a closed-loop aquaponics system running underneath a restaurant in downtown Kansas City as I write this; the produce reaches the kitchen of the restaurant above without leaving the building. Environmental fit is generally favorable: water reuse approaches ninety percent and inputs do not cycle through external soils. Survivability is the interesting axis. The system carries some of the same input-fragility as cultivated meat — pumps, filters, water heaters, climate control all depend on continuous power — but the system’s smaller scale and shorter supply chain mean a more graceful failure mode. A neighborhood aquaponics system loses its fish before its bok choy. A cultivated-meat plant loses everything when the bioreactor’s temperature drifts.
>
> The four-axis test does not declare either system a failure or a success. What it does is force the conversation to happen on more than one axis. Cultivated meat may pass the environmental axis spectacularly while failing the survivability axis just as spectacularly. Aquaponics may pass survivability at small scale while losing economic viability at the scales it would need to feed a city. Both observations are true. Neither is a takedown. The framework’s job is to surface the trade-offs, not to deliver a verdict.

## THE URBAN READER

I have been writing, so far in this chapter, as though the reader has land. Most readers of this book do not. Most readers live in cities, in apartments or houses without acreage, and their relationship to the food system runs through a grocery store, a farmers' market, a restaurant menu, or a delivery app. If the book's argument ends at the pasture gate, it loses its audience. The argument does not end there.

The framework this book has developed is, at its core, an evaluative tool. It tells you how to assess a food system (any food system, at any scale) by asking four questions: is it economically viable, is it socially equitable, is it environmentally sound, and will it survive when conditions change? Those questions are as useful to a consumer in a grocery aisle as they are to a rancher on a thousand acres. The consumer cannot raise bison or manage a kelp forest. The consumer can ask, of the food they are buying, what system it comes from, what that system depends on, and whether what it depends on is likely to persist. The framework does not require land. It requires attention.

The urban reader has three forms of agency that the book's argument speaks to directly, and none of them require moving to the country.

The first is purchasing. Every food purchase is a vote for the system that produced it. This is not a novel observation, but the framework gives it specificity that the familiar formulation lacks. The question is not merely "is this organic" or "is this local" (labels whose limitations Chapter 1 documented) but "what does the system behind this product depend on, and how does it score on the fourth axis?" Wild-caught salmon from a managed fishery and farmed salmon from a net-pen operation sit side by side in the same case. The framework distinguishes them. Bison from a conservation herd on native prairie and beef from a feedlot finished on commodity corn both produce a burger. The framework distinguishes them. The distinction is not a matter of taste or preference. It is a structural difference in the systems' relationships to the conditions the next century will impose, and the consumer who understands the distinction is making a different kind of choice than the consumer who does not.

I do not want to overstate this. Individual purchasing decisions, even aggregated across millions of consumers, will not by themselves restructure the food system. The structural constraints — subsidy architecture, commodity markets, processing infrastructure, regulatory frameworks — are larger than any individual's grocery cart. The consumer who buys bison instead of feedlot beef has made a choice that the framework supports. That choice, multiplied, shifts demand. Shifted demand creates market signals. Market signals, over time, influence what gets produced and how. The mechanism is real but slow, and it operates within a larger system that individual choices alone cannot redirect. The purchasing power of urban consumers is a lever. It is not the only lever.

The second form of agency is political. The policy architecture that sustains the current food system — the farm bill, the commodity support programs, the regulatory definitions that Chapter 1 described, the land-management frameworks that determine what happens on public land — is built and rebuilt by political decisions that urban voters participate in. The farm bill is reauthorized roughly every five years. Its provisions determine which crops are subsidized, which conservation programs are funded, which land-management practices are incentivized. An urban voter who understands the survivability framework understands that the subsidy architecture is propping up systems that score poorly on the fourth axis while systems that score well receive little or no support. That voter can advocate for policy that redirects support toward the systems the framework identifies as more durable.

Conservation policy is equally relevant. The management of public lands — BLM rangeland, national forest, national wildlife refuge — is governed by plans that are subject to public comment and political influence. The question of whether feral horses on BLM land are managed as nuisance animals or reframed as proxy species occupying an ecological role is a policy question, not an ecological one. The ecology is what it is regardless of the policy. The policy determines whether the ecology is managed in a way that produces food, restores function, and scores well on the framework, or whether it continues to be managed under assumptions the book has spent ten chapters questioning. Urban voters participate in these decisions. Their participation matters, not least because urban voters constitute the majority of the electorate and the majority of the political pressure on the representatives who write the farm bill and confirm the heads of the agencies that manage public land.

The third form of agency is advocacy. The experiments this book has called for (proof-of-concept trials with proxy species on willing private land, expanded bison restoration, marine-restoration programs that produce food alongside ecological recovery) require funding, institutional support, and public attention. Urban readers are the primary audience for the public-attention component. They are the readers of the journalism, the supporters of the nonprofits, the donors to the research programs, the consumers whose demand signals tell producers what is worth producing. The book's argument reaches the landscape through the people who care about the landscape, and most of those people live in cities.

I want to be direct about this because the alternative is a book that talks past its own readers. A food-systems book that speaks only to people with acreage is a book written for a small and shrinking fraction of the American population. The framework this book has developed is relevant to everyone who eats, which is everyone. The urban reader is not a bystander to the food-system conversation. The urban reader is the majority of the food-system conversation, and the conversation will go nowhere without them.

## SPECIFICITY AS FEATURE

The chapter has walked through five bioregions, and the five have produced five different pictures. The Southwest wants camelids and equids on browse. The northern Plains want bison on grass. The Gulf Coast wants silvopastoral systems and restored marine ecosystems. New England wants food from its forests. The Pacific Northwest wants its salmon and its managed lowland agriculture integrated across a single watershed.

The variation is the point. The four-axis framework does not produce a single prescription for every landscape. It produces a method for arriving at the prescription the landscape calls for, and the prescriptions differ because the landscapes differ. The arid basins of Arizona and the rain-soaked valleys of Oregon have different evolutionary histories, different vegetation communities, different fossil records, different Indigenous management traditions, and different food-system possibilities. A framework that produced the same answer for both would be ignoring everything that makes each landscape what it is. The framework's value lies precisely in its sensitivity to the specific. It asks: what did this landscape evolve under? What does this landscape need? What does this landscape produce when its ecological relationships are allowed to function? The answers are local. They have to be.

This specificity is also, I think, what distinguishes the framework from the approach it is meant to improve upon. The industrial food system treats the continent as a single production surface: corn from Iowa to feed cattle in Texas to produce beef for consumers in New York. The commodity system is designed to erase local difference, to make a bushel of corn from Nebraska interchangeable with a bushel from Indiana, to make a pound of beef from a Kansas feedlot indistinguishable from a pound from a Colorado one. The erasure of local difference is the system's efficiency, and the efficiency is real. It is also the mechanism by which the system's survivability deficit is produced, because a system that treats every landscape the same cannot respond to the ways in which landscapes are not the same.

The framework proposed in this book asks the opposite question. It asks what each landscape is, specifically, and what each landscape can do, specifically, when its ecological relationships are allowed to operate. The answers are diverse. The diversity is the feature. A food system that produces camel milk in Arizona, bison on the Montana plains, oysters in the Chesapeake, forest-raised pork in Vermont, and salmon in the Columbia River watershed is a food system that is not vulnerable to a single point of failure, because it is not organized around a single commodity, a single animal, a single supply chain, or a single set of assumptions about what the future will look like. It is a food system that survives because it is many food systems, each adapted to its own place, each drawing on its own ecological relationships, each capable of continuing to function when the conditions that any one of the others depends on change.

The four-axis framework does not produce a universal answer. It produces the right question — what does this place need, what does this place produce, what will this place still be doing in a hundred years? — and the right question, asked of every landscape on the continent, produces a set of answers that are collectively more durable, more diverse, and more honestly sustainable than any single answer could be.

The next chapter asks what these answers look like at the scale of an individual meal, an individual household, an individual set of economic constraints. The framework has been applied to landscapes. It is time to apply it to the plate.

# Chapter Twelve: Eating Toward Vision

The framework is built. Over eleven chapters, this book has assembled a way of seeing food systems that most sustainability conversations leave incomplete — four axes rather than three, survivability alongside economic viability, social equity, and environmental integrity. The question now is what to do with it.

This chapter is not a meal plan. It does not tell anyone what to eat for breakfast. It offers no prescriptions, no ranked lists of approved foods, no weekly menus calibrated to a particular body weight or health outcome. What it offers instead is a set of thinking tools: ways to evaluate what is in front of you at the point of purchase, ways to ask better questions of the people who produce your food, and ways to think about the real cost of what you are buying without either ignoring that cost or pretending it does not matter.

The distinction matters. A meal plan is a finished answer. A framework is a way of generating answers that fit circumstances the author cannot predict: your bioregion, your household budget, your access to producers, your cooking capacity, your values. The framework respects the distance between the person who wrote this book and the person reading it. That distance is real, and any guidance that pretends it does not exist is guidance that will fail the moment circumstances diverge from the author's assumptions.

This is also, it should be said, the chapter of this book most likely to slip into a register the rest of the book has worked to avoid. Practical guidance chapters in food-systems writing have a well-established failure mode: they begin by acknowledging that food choices are complex and structurally constrained, and then they proceed to tell the reader what to buy as if those constraints do not exist. The chapter you are reading was written with that failure mode in mind. Where it succeeds in avoiding it, the framework is doing its job. Where it does not — and the author invites the reader to notice — the draft needs revision.

## SEEING THROUGH LABELS

The reader who has stayed with this book since Chapter 1 entered it at a meat counter in Overland Park, Kansas, standing in front of grass-fed beef that turned out to be grain-finished. The labels said one thing; the practice was another. The observation that opened the book was that this gap between label and practice is not a failure of the labeling system — it is what the labeling system is designed to do. Labels communicate less, not more, because communicating more would make visible the practices that the current food system depends on remaining invisible.

Eleven chapters later, the reader has the framework to see through this. The four-axis assessment does what labels cannot: it asks not just whether an animal was raised on pasture or whether a crop was grown without synthetic pesticides, but whether the system that produced the food can survive the removal of its key inputs. A label tells you what a product *is*, within a narrow and sometimes misleading set of categories. The framework asks what a product *requires* — and what happens when those requirements are not met.

*I have written certification standards for sixteen years. The irony is not lost on me. The framework is still better than the label.*

Consider a carton of eggs labeled "cage-free." The term certifies a specific thing: the hens were not kept in battery cages.<sup>1</sup> It does not certify that they went outdoors. It does not certify what they were fed. It does not certify the stocking density of the barn they were housed in. It does not address survivability at all. The operation may depend entirely on commodity soy feed shipped from a thousand miles away, and the label will not tell you this.<sup>2</sup> The consumer who reads "cage-free" and imagines hens on grass is not being stupid. They are being managed. The label is doing exactly what it was designed to do: communicating enough to justify a price premium while leaving the structural dependencies of the operation invisible.

The framework changes this. When you stand in front of that carton and ask the four-axis questions — what does this system require economically, what are its social conditions, what is its environmental footprint, and what happens to it under stress — the label becomes what it actually is: a single data point in a much larger assessment. It is not worthless. Cage-free is better than battery-caged, for the hens and for the person eating the eggs. But it is radically incomplete, and treating it as a sufficient guide to a food system's sustainability is the mistake the labeling architecture is designed to encourage.

The same analysis applies across the label landscape. "Organic" certifies the absence of specific synthetic inputs but does not certify biodiversity, does not address survivability, and does not distinguish between a diversified small farm and a ten-thousand-acre monoculture that has simply substituted approved inputs for prohibited ones.<sup>3</sup> "Grass-fed" has been diluted by the USDA's 2016 withdrawal of the federal standard, leaving the term's meaning dependent on which certifying body (if any) stands behind it.<sup>4</sup> "Pasture-raised" means something different depending on whether it appears on a chicken label (where meaningful outdoor access standards exist through some third-party certifiers) or a beef label (where the term has no federal definition at all).<sup>5</sup> "Humane" is perhaps the most variable of all: the gap between the American Humane Certified standard and the Animal Welfare Approved standard is enormous, and the consumer who sees "humane" on both products has no way to know this from the label alone.<sup>6</sup>

The pattern extends beyond animal products. "Natural" on a processed-food label has no federal definition beyond the prohibition of artificial flavors, added colors, and synthetic substances — a bar so low that high-fructose corn syrup from an industrial monoculture system qualifies.<sup>17</sup> "Sustainable" appears on seafood labels backed by certifiers whose standards range from rigorous stock-assessment science to industry self-reporting.<sup>18</sup> "Local" has no regulated definition at all; a product grown three hundred miles away in a system identical to the industrial commodity system it purports to oppose may be labeled "local" in any jurisdiction where the seller chooses to do so.

Each of these terms occupies the same structural position. Each certifies something real but narrow. Each communicates less than the consumer assumes. And each leaves the survivability axis, the question the framework adds to the standard three, entirely unaddressed. No label currently in widespread use tells the consumer what happens to the system that produced this food when its key inputs are disrupted.

None of this means labels are useless. It means they are insufficient. The framework does not replace labels; it contextualizes them. A label is a claim about one dimension of a food system. The framework asks about all four dimensions, and it asks about the dependencies that no label is designed to reveal.

## WHAT TO ASK A PRODUCER

Labels mediate between producers and consumers at scale. They exist because most people buying food will never meet the person who produced it. But where direct purchasing is possible (at a farmers' market, through a CSA, from a ranch that sells direct, from a fishing cooperative), the framework generates a different set of questions than the ones most consumers have been taught to ask.

The conventional questions are about outcomes: Is this grass-fed? Is this organic? Were these hens on pasture? These are reasonable questions, and the answers matter. But they are questions about the product. The framework generates questions about the system.

What does your operation require to function? This is the survivability question, translated into a conversation. A producer who raises cattle on pasture and finishes them on grain purchased from a regional feed mill has a different dependency profile than a producer who raises cattle entirely on managed grassland with no purchased inputs. Both may produce excellent beef. Both may be doing good work. But their operations have different vulnerabilities, and the consumer who understands this is making a more informed choice than the consumer who knows only that both products are "grass-fed." The question is not accusatory. It is genuinely curious, in the way that Chapter 4 framed the survivability axis, not as a judgment but as a description of what a system needs to persist.

How many species does your operation depend on? This is a question most producers have not been asked before, and the answer is often revealing. A cattle ranch running a single breed on monoculture pasture has a different ecological profile than a diversified operation running cattle, sheep, and poultry across managed multi-species grassland. Ecological diversity on the production side correlates with resilience, a principle the book has traced from Pleistocene ecosystems through to the present. The producer whose operation depends on a single species, a single breed, a single feed source, and a single market channel has concentrated risk in the same way a monoculture concentrates risk in crop agriculture. The producer who has diversified across species, markets, and input sources has distributed it.

Where does your feed come from? For any operation that purchases feed (and as Chapter 6 established, even the best pasture-based poultry operations purchase substantial quantities), this question reveals the structural tether to the commodity system. The answer is not disqualifying. A producer who buys feed from a regional mill sourcing from regional farms has a shorter, more resilient supply chain than a producer whose feed originates in the industrial commodity belt. Neither is fully independent of the larger system, but the degree of dependence differs, and that difference matters on the survivability axis.<sup>7</sup>

What happens to your operation if diesel doubles in price? If your primary feed supplier goes under? If the county changes its zoning? These are stress-test questions, and they sound unusual in the context of buying a dozen eggs. But they are the questions the framework generates, because survivability is about what happens under stress, not what happens under current conditions. A producer who has thought about these questions — who has contingency plans, who has diversified their inputs, who has reduced their dependence on any single external system — is running an operation that scores differently on the fourth axis than a producer who has not.

What does your land look like in twenty years if you keep doing what you are doing? This is the environmental-integrity question extended through time. Soil health, water quality, biodiversity, carbon sequestration: these are trajectory questions, not snapshot questions. A producer who is building soil organic matter year over year is on a different trajectory than a producer who is mining it, even if both operations look similar today.<sup>8</sup>

These questions are not tests. They are conversations. A producer who answers them openly is telling you something valuable about their operation. A producer who is uncomfortable with them is also telling you something, though the discomfort may simply reflect the fact that most customers do not ask questions like these and the producer has not had occasion to articulate what they know.

The questions work differently in different purchasing contexts. At a farmers' market, they are face-to-face conversations. Through a CSA, they might be questions posed in a newsletter or at a farm visit. For direct-purchase meat or seafood, they might be part of the research a buyer does before committing to a quarter-beef or a fishing share. The form adapts; the substance is the same. The framework generates questions about systems, not just about products.

A note on the limits of this approach: direct purchasing is not available to everyone. The farmers' market with the knowledgeable rancher selling quarter-beefs is not equally distributed across geography, income levels, or time availability. The questions above are tools for contexts where they apply. In contexts where they do not (where the grocery store is the only option, where the nearest farmers' market is forty miles away, where the household's schedule does not permit a Saturday morning at a farm stand), the framework still operates, but it operates on the information available rather than on the information ideal. A reader who applies the four-axis assessment to the options actually in front of them at a conventional grocery store is using the framework correctly. The framework does not require access to a particular kind of retail environment. It requires a way of thinking about whatever retail environment the reader has.

## THINKING BIOREGIONALLY

Chapter 10 introduced the principle that restoration looks different in different landscapes: that the Pleistocene baseline generates different answers in the arid Southwest than in the Pacific Northwest, and that Indigenous management traditions provide a parallel framework adapted to specific places over millennia. The same principle applies to food choices. What scores well on the four axes in one bioregion may score differently in another, because the ecological and economic conditions that determine survivability are local.

In the Pacific Northwest, wild-caught salmon from well-managed fisheries represents one of the highest-scoring protein sources available on all four axes. The fishery is ecologically adapted, the management science is among the most rigorous in the world, the economic structure supports coastal communities, and the system's survivability depends on intact river ecosystems rather than industrial inputs.<sup>19</sup> In the landlocked interior of the country, that same salmon arrives by truck or plane, its survivability profile degraded by the transportation infrastructure it now depends on. The fish is the same; the system that delivers it is not.

In the Southern Plains, the feral hog population represents an ecological problem (invasive, destructive, and growing) that is simultaneously a food-system opportunity. Wild-harvested feral hog scores at the top of the survivability axis: the protein source requires no feed, no housing, no veterinary inputs, and no supply chain beyond the harvest itself. It also addresses an ecological damage vector. The framework scores this differently than a Pacific Northwest reader's access to managed salmon, but both represent the same principle: the highest-scoring food choices are often the ones most adapted to the specific ecological conditions of the place where the reader lives.

> **CASE: The Feral Hog Paradox**
>
> Feral hogs descended from nineteenth-century escaped domestic stock and twentieth-century imported Eurasian wild boar now number roughly 2.6 million in Texas alone, with rapidly expanding populations across the Southeast and lower Midwest. USDA estimates the national herd at six to nine million animals and growing.

They cause roughly $1.5 billion in annual agricultural damage in the United States, destroy ground-nesting bird habitat, and accelerate soil erosion on the landscapes they colonize. They are, by any standard ecological accounting, an invasive species problem.

They are also a high-scoring protein source: wild-harvested feral hog requires zero feed, zero housing, zero veterinary care, and no supply chain beyond harvest. The harvest addresses the ecological damage while producing food. The contradiction is not a contradiction. It is what it looks like when a damage vector is also a food system.

> **CASE: Broken Arrow Ranch**
>
> One operation worth naming in this chapter is Broken Arrow Ranch, a Texas game-meat processor that handles wild-harvested feral hog, axis deer, blackbuck, and other free-ranging species across the Texas Hill Country and surrounding regions. The model is straightforward: trained mobile-harvest crews work directly on the ranches where the animals are, field-dress the animals there, and bring them back to the Broken Arrow facility for processing.
>
> The animals were never in a feedlot. They were never in a confined-feeding pipeline. They never required transportation to a centralized slaughterhouse, never received veterinary inputs, never ate purchased grain. They lived on the land and were harvested from it, and the operation that processes them is one of the few that has built a regulatory pathway through the existing USDA architecture for wild-harvested protein at scale.
>
> Paleo Approved certified Broken Arrow under its animal standards, and the operation more than met the standards as written. The standards, in some sense, were written with an operation like this in mind — an operation working with, rather than against, the animals already on the land.
>
> The contrast is worth holding alongside the survivability scoring of Chapter Five. The federal government, through the USDA and various state agencies, currently funds aerial-gunning programs in which contracted shooters in helicopters cull feral hogs across Texas rangeland, treating the animals as an invasive nuisance to be reduced as cheaply as possible. The same animals, harvested rather than gunned and routed through an operation like Broken Arrow's, become protein for human food, revenue for the landowner, and processing work for the local economy. Both are management responses to the same population. One costs the taxpayer money and produces nothing. The other costs the taxpayer nothing and produces protein. The difference is structural rather than ecological — a function of which regulatory pathway exists and which does not.
>
> Broken Arrow does not solve the broader feral-hog problem, and it is not at the scale to feed a region. What it demonstrates is that a regulatory pathway for wild-harvested protein at any scale can exist. If it can exist for feral hog and axis deer in Texas, it can in principle exist for whatever the next animal is.

This is not an argument for localism as ideology. It is an argument for localism as ecological logic. The framework does not score "local" as inherently superior. A greenhouse tomato grown a mile away using natural gas heating and hydroponic nutrient solution may score worse on survivability than a field tomato shipped from a region where tomatoes grow without climate-controlled infrastructure. What the framework scores is the fit between a food system and the ecological, economic, and social conditions of the place where it operates. That fit is often local, because ecological conditions are local. But "local" is a geographic descriptor, not a sustainability credential, and the framework treats it as such.

The bioregional principle also connects to Chapter 10's discussion of Indigenous management traditions. Where those traditions are accessible and active — through tribal food sovereignty projects, through Indigenous-managed fisheries and wild-harvest cooperatives, through the growing body of work that Robin Wall Kimmerer and M. Kat Anderson have documented — they represent a knowledge base calibrated to specific places over centuries. A reader in the Great Lakes region who has access to manoomin (wild rice) harvested under Anishinaabe management traditions is encountering a food system that has been adapted to its specific ecological context for longer than the agricultural systems surrounding it have existed. The framework scores this favorably not because it is Indigenous — identity is not a sustainability metric — but because the system's ecological fit, survivability profile, and intergenerational knowledge base are measurable strengths on the axes the framework evaluates.

## THINKING ABOUT COST

This is the section of this chapter most likely to go wrong, so it is worth being direct about what it will and will not do.

It will not pretend that food from well-managed, ecologically sound, survivable systems costs the same as food from industrial systems. It does not. A dozen eggs from a pasture-based operation with diversified feed sources will cost more at the point of purchase than a dozen eggs from an industrial cage-free barn. A pound of grass-finished beef from a ranch that purchases no commodity feed will cost more than a pound of conventional ground beef. Wild-caught Alaskan salmon will cost more than farmed Atlantic salmon. This is true, and pretending otherwise would insult the reader's intelligence and their lived experience.

It will not lecture anyone about priorities. The financial constraints people operate under are real, they are various, and they are not the business of this book to judge. A household spending thirty percent of its income on housing in a city where wages have not kept pace with costs is not making a lifestyle choice when it buys the cheaper eggs. It is navigating a set of structural conditions that no individual purchasing decision can resolve. The framework is a tool for thinking, and it is only useful if it can be applied honestly within the actual circumstances of the person using it.

What the cost discussion can do honestly is make visible what the price difference represents.

Industrial food is cheap at the point of purchase because a substantial share of its actual costs are externalized: borne by someone other than the buyer.<sup>9</sup> The environmental remediation costs of CAFO waste lagoons are borne by municipalities and by the communities living near them. The healthcare costs associated with antibiotic resistance, which the FDA's own reporting links to agricultural antibiotic use, are borne by the healthcare system and by the individuals who develop resistant infections.<sup>10</sup> The soil-depletion costs of continuous monoculture are borne by future farmers who will inherit degraded land. The subsidy costs that keep commodity corn and soy artificially cheap are borne by taxpayers. A 2021 analysis by the Rockefeller Foundation estimated that the total externalized cost of the U.S. food system (including environmental, health, and social impacts) exceeds $2.1 trillion annually, roughly three times the direct consumer expenditure on food.<sup>11</sup>

> **DEFINITION: Externalized Costs**
>
> Externalized costs are expenses borne by parties who are not party to the transaction that produced them.

When an industrial hog operation exports waste to groundwater, the cost of remediation falls to municipalities and affected residents rather than to the operator. When agricultural antibiotic use produces resistant bacteria, the cost appears as healthcare spending rather than as feed expense. When federal commodity subsidies hold corn below production cost, the cost is transferred from producer to taxpayer. When monoculture depletes topsoil, the cost is transferred to future farmers.

The price at checkout reflects only the portion of total cost the producer has internalized. The 2021 Rockefeller Foundation analysis this chapter cites estimated the externalized cost of the U.S. food system at roughly $2.1 trillion annually, approximately three times the direct consumer expenditure on food.

This does not mean industrial food is secretly more expensive than pastured food, if you do the math correctly. It means the cost comparison at the register is structurally misleading. The cheaper product is cheaper in part because some of its costs have been removed from the price and distributed elsewhere in the economy. The more expensive product is more expensive in part because it has internalized costs that the cheaper product has externalized. When a consumer pays $8 for a dozen pastured eggs instead of $3 for a dozen industrial eggs, some portion of that $5 difference represents costs the industrial operation has offloaded onto the public.<sup>12</sup>

Understanding this does not change anyone's grocery budget. It changes the frame within which the budget operates. The question shifts from "why is the good food so expensive?" to "why is the cheap food so cheap, and who is paying the difference?" This is not a rhetorical question. It has answers, and the answers are specific: taxpayers, through commodity subsidies; rural communities, through environmental degradation and depressed wages; future generations, through topsoil depletion and aquifer drawdown; the healthcare system, through the downstream costs of antibiotic resistance and diet-related disease.

Within that frame, the practical question becomes: given my actual budget and my actual constraints, where do the highest-return choices lie? The framework suggests a way of thinking about this that respects financial reality.

Not all four-axis improvements cost more. Some of the highest-survivability food choices are among the cheapest. Dried beans and lentils — which require minimal processing, store indefinitely, grow in diverse climates, fix their own nitrogen, and depend on almost no external inputs — score remarkably well across all four axes and cost less per serving than almost any animal protein.<sup>13</sup> Whole grains from regional mills, where available, offer better survivability profiles than processed grain products at comparable or lower cost. Frozen vegetables, which retain most of their nutritional value and eliminate the waste associated with fresh produce that spoils before it is eaten, are an economically efficient choice that the fresh-is-always-better orthodoxy tends to obscure.<sup>14</sup>

Where animal products are concerned, the cost gradient correlates with the degree to which the operation has internalized its costs. The choices that represent the largest per-dollar improvement over industrial systems are often not the most expensive options on the shelf. Choosing conventional eggs from a regional producer over conventional eggs shipped from a distant industrial operation may represent a meaningful survivability improvement at minimal additional cost. Choosing whole chickens over boneless breasts (and learning to use the entire animal) reduces the per-serving cost of higher-quality poultry substantially. Buying less meat of better provenance, rather than the same quantity at a higher price point, is a strategy that the framework supports and that household budgets can often accommodate.<sup>15</sup>

The wild-harvest and restoration-based protein sources that Chapter 8 described — feral hog in Texas, managed deer and elk across much of the West, wild-caught fish from well-managed fisheries — represent a different cost structure entirely. For households with access to hunting or fishing, these are among the most affordable protein sources available, and they score at the top of the survivability axis because they depend on intact ecosystems rather than industrial inputs. The barriers here are access and knowledge rather than price, and they are barriers that communities, not individuals, are best positioned to address.<sup>16</sup>

There is also the question of time, which functions as a cost even when it does not appear on a receipt. Cooking from whole ingredients — whole chickens rather than boneless breasts, dried beans rather than canned, whole grains rather than processed — costs less money and more time. For households where time is the binding constraint, this trade-off is not trivially navigable, and the framework does not pretend otherwise. What the framework can do is make visible that the processed-food economy has externalized time costs onto the consumer in the same way the industrial food system has externalized environmental costs onto the public: by creating a default in which the time-saving option is also the option most deeply embedded in the industrial supply chain. Understanding this structure does not add hours to anyone's day. It does clarify why the choices available at the intersection of limited time and limited budget tend to cluster at the low end of the survivability axis — and why changing this requires structural intervention, not individual willpower. <sup>20</sup>

The honest position on cost is this: the framework cannot make sustainably produced food cheaper than it is. What it can do is help a reader identify which choices, within their actual budget, represent the highest improvement across the four axes. Some of those choices cost more. Some cost the same. Some cost less. The framework makes the differences visible without pretending the reader's constraints are not real.

## WHAT THE FRAMEWORK CANNOT DO

The framework is a tool for individual decisions. It helps a reader evaluate what is in front of them, ask better questions, and make choices that are more fully informed than the ones the labeling system is designed to support. This is worth doing. Individual choices aggregate, and the direction they aggregate in matters.

But individual choices, even fully informed ones, cannot produce the systemic changes the framework reveals as necessary. The reader who buys pastured eggs instead of industrial eggs is making a better choice on all four axes. They are not, by that choice, reforming the subsidy structure that makes industrial eggs artificially cheap. They are not changing the zoning laws that make small-scale pastured poultry operations difficult to establish near population centers. They are not funding the research that would test proxy-species reintroductions at meaningful scale. They are not restructuring the commodity feed system that tethers even the best regenerative operations to industrial inputs.

Consider the structural ceiling that Chapter 6 described. The best pasture-based poultry operation in the country still purchases tens of thousands of pounds of commodity feed annually, most of it produced under industrial monoculture conditions. No amount of consumer preference for pastured chicken, expressed at any price point, changes this structural dependency. The dependency exists because the alternative (a poultry operation that grows its own feed on-site, at the scale needed to support commercial production) requires land, labor, and capital configurations that the current agricultural economy does not reward and current zoning law often prohibits. The consumer's choice is constrained by a structure the consumer's choice cannot modify.

The gap between what individual choices can accomplish and what systemic change requires is the subject of the next chapter. It is worth naming here because the alternative (the suggestion that conscious consumerism is sufficient to transform a food system) is a form of dishonesty the framework does not permit. The framework reveals structural dependencies. Structural dependencies require structural responses.

This is not a reason for despair, and it is not a reason to conclude that individual choices are meaningless. It is a reason to hold both things at once: the choices matter, and they are not enough. The reader who has followed this book through eleven chapters of framework, evidence, and vision is equipped to make better choices at the point of purchase. They are also equipped to understand why better choices at the point of purchase, alone, will not produce the food systems this book has described as possible.

What those systems require (at the level of policy, culture, economics, and institutional will) is the question Chapter 13 takes up. The framework has done what a framework can do. It has made visible what was hidden, connected what was fragmented, and given the reader a way of seeing that the labeling architecture was designed to prevent. What remains is whether the seeing produces not just better shopping but better systems.

The reader who stood at the meat counter in Chapter 1, confused by labels that communicated less than they appeared to, now has something the labels were never going to provide: a way of asking what a food system actually requires, and what happens when those requirements are not met. That is not a meal plan. It is something more durable — a way of thinking that survives the next relabeling, the next marketing cycle, the next round of standards dilution. The framework is portable. It travels with the reader who has internalized it, into whatever purchasing context they encounter next.

The framework, applied honestly, produces this double awareness. It equips the reader to make better individual choices. It simultaneously reveals why individual choices are insufficient. Both of these are real. The temptation to collapse one in favor of the other, to conclude either that individual action is everything or that individual action is nothing, is a temptation the framework resists, because the framework is descriptive. It describes what is actually happening in the systems that produce food. What is actually happening is that individual choices matter and structural conditions matter more.

That is what this chapter can offer. Not a prescription, but a practice of seeing.
