# Chapter Thirteen: What Must Change

Sixteen years from the meat counter, the question I asked there has not gotten smaller. It has gotten more concrete. The framework Chapter 4 named was the answer to the question that started in front of a label that did not say what I thought it said, and the chapters since have been the test of whether the answer holds up against every food system the framework can be pointed at. Most of what follows is what is left when the test is done.

This book has been, for the most part, an argument directed at readers, at people who buy food, think about food, and have some capacity to choose what food they buy. The framework it has proposed, the four-axis assessment of food systems, is a tool for individual evaluation: a way for a person standing in a grocery store or a farmers' market or a restaurant to think more clearly about what the food in front of them depends on, what it costs in ways the price does not capture, and whether the system that produced it could survive conditions different from the ones it was designed for. The practical guidance the previous chapter offered was guidance for individuals making decisions within the food system as it currently exists. That guidance is real, and I stand behind it.

It is also not sufficient.

The limits of consumer choice as an engine of systemic change are well documented in the food-systems literature and are worth stating directly, because the temptation to end a book like this with an appeal to individual action is strong and the appeal is, on its own, inadequate to the scale of the problem the book has described. A 2012 analysis by Daniel Welch in the *Journal of Consumer Culture* examined the structural assumptions behind the "vote with your fork" framing that has dominated the sustainable-food conversation since Michael Pollan's *The Omnivore's Dilemma* helped popularize it, and concluded that consumer choice operates within a system whose structure is determined by forces the consumer does not control: commodity policy, land-use regulation, subsidy allocation, infrastructure investment, and the path dependencies of an industrial food system built over seventy years of policy and capital accumulation.<sup>1</sup> The consumer can choose among the options the system offers. The consumer cannot, by choosing, change what options the system offers.

This is not a criticism of the readers who have followed this book's argument. It is a structural observation about the relationship between individual action and systemic architecture. A reader who adopts the four-axis framework, shifts purchasing toward rewilded and sustainably managed food sources, supports local producers operating outside the industrial commodity system, and advocates for the experiments the book has called for is doing everything the individual can do. If a hundred thousand readers did the same, the effect on the food system would be measurable but bounded. If a million readers did the same, the effect would be larger but still bounded by the same structural constraints. The industrial commodity system, the subsidy architecture, the land-use regulatory framework, the conservation baselines, and the institutional infrastructure that governs what kind of food production is permitted, incentivized, and scaled in the United States are not determined by consumer purchasing decisions. They are determined by policy, by regulation, by the allocation of public resources, and by the institutional inertia of a system that has been building in its current direction since the middle of the twentieth century.

The book's argument, if it is correct, has implications that extend past the individual reader. If the four-axis framework reveals structural vulnerabilities in the dominant food system that three-axis assessment cannot see, then the response to those vulnerabilities cannot be purely individual. If the fossil record provides a guide to which animals belong in which landscapes, and if the proxy-species experiments the book has called for are worth conducting, then the institutional and regulatory conditions under which those experiments could occur need to exist. If the rewilding vision the book has described, in which food is produced from landscapes managed under ecologically appropriate species assemblages, represents a direction the food system should move toward, then the policy environment needs to make that direction possible rather than impossible.

This chapter walks through what would have to change at the structural level for the book's vision to move from argument to practice. I want to be direct about the difficulty. None of what follows is easy. None of it is politically assured. I do not have a theory of how these changes get enacted, because I do not think such a theory exists in honest form. What I have is a description of the structural conditions that would need to be different, offered in the hope that naming them clearly is a precondition for working toward them, even when the path from naming to enactment is not visible.

## SUBSIDY REFORM

The federal subsidy structure is the floor on which the current food system stands. The commodity support system, administered through successive iterations of the farm bill since the Nixon-era restructuring of agricultural policy in the early 1970s, has shaped what the United States grows, how it grows it, where the economic incentives in agriculture point, and what kinds of food production are financially viable at scale. The structural effects are not subtle. Between 1995 and 2021, the federal government disbursed approximately $478 billion in agricultural subsidies, with the majority flowing to commodity crop producers — corn, soybeans, wheat, cotton, and rice — and to crop insurance premium subsidies that further underwrite commodity production risk.<sup>2</sup> The distribution is concentrated: Environmental Working Group analyses of USDA subsidy data have consistently shown that the top ten percent of subsidy recipients receive the majority of payments, that the payments flow disproportionately to the largest operations, and that the crops subsidized are overwhelmingly the commodity inputs to the industrial food system the previous chapters have scored.<sup>3</sup>

The consequence of this structure for the food system the book has described is direct. When commodity corn is subsidized below the cost of production, the feedlot system that depends on cheap corn is effectively subsidized as well. The Tufts analysis documented roughly $35 billion in implicit savings to the industrial livestock sector from below-cost feed between 1997 and 2005, and the structural dynamic has not fundamentally changed since.<sup>4</sup> When crop insurance subsidies reduce the financial risk of planting commodity crops on marginal land, the incentive to convert that land to other uses (perennial pasture, agroforestry, rewilding) is suppressed. When conservation payments through the Conservation Reserve Program and its successors pay landowners to take land out of production but do not create pathways for that land to be managed under the kinds of ecological restoration the book has described, the policy creates a binary — produce commodities or produce nothing —that forecloses the middle option of producing food from ecologically managed landscapes.

> **DEFINITION: Subsidy Incidence**
>
> Subsidy incidence is the economic principle that the party receiving a subsidy check is not necessarily the party capturing its economic benefit.

A federal payment supporting corn production below cost is technically paid to the corn farmer, but the real beneficiary is whoever purchases the subsidized corn downstream. Between 1997 and 2005, the industrial livestock sector captured an estimated $35 billion in implicit savings through below-cost feed enabled by commodity subsidies, the Tufts figure this chapter cites. The incidence of the subsidy determined what kind of livestock production became economically viable at scale.

Restructuring agricultural support toward ecosystem-service payments rather than commodity price supports would shift that incidence, and with it the kinds of land use the subsidy system incentivizes. Where the money ends up determines what gets built on the land.

Restructuring the subsidy system toward ecological outcomes would change the economics of food production in the United States in ways that the current system's beneficiaries have understandable reasons to resist. The restructuring would mean, concretely, that federal agricultural spending would be redirected, partially or substantially, from commodity price supports and crop insurance premium subsidies toward payments for ecosystem services: soil carbon accumulation, biodiversity outcomes, water quality improvements, and the maintenance of ecological function on working landscapes. The concept is not novel. The Conservation Stewardship Program already makes payments for conservation practices on working land, and the 2018 farm bill expanded some of these provisions. What does not yet exist is a subsidy architecture in which the ecological outcomes the book's framework measures, including survivability and the restoration of species-landscape relationships, are weighted as heavily in the allocation of federal agricultural spending as commodity production volumes currently are.

Julie Witcover and colleagues, in a 2019 analysis for the UC Davis Policy Institute for Energy, Environment, and the Economy, examined the potential effects of redirecting a fraction of federal commodity subsidies toward ecosystem-service payments and found that even modest redirections could produce measurable shifts in land-use patterns, particularly on marginal cropland where the return to commodity production is lowest and the ecological return to alternative management is highest.<sup>5</sup> The finding is intuitive: if the subsidy system pays you to grow corn on land that is marginal for corn, you grow corn; if the subsidy system pays you to manage that land for ecological outcomes, you manage for ecological outcomes. The incentive structure determines the land use. Changing the incentive structure changes the land use. The policy question is not whether this mechanism works. It is whether the political conditions exist to redirect the incentive.

I am not confident they do. Farm bill politics in the United States are among the most entrenched in the federal legislative process. The commodity support programs have institutional constituencies in the agricultural committees of Congress, in the commodity organizations, in the agribusiness firms that supply inputs to commodity producers, and in the rural communities whose economies have been organized around commodity production for generations. Those constituencies have successfully defended the basic architecture of the subsidy system through more than a dozen farm bill reauthorizations since 1973. Structural reform of the subsidy system is not impossible. It has happened before: the 1996 farm bill's elimination of deficiency payments and the subsequent reinstatement of support mechanisms under different names in the 2002 and subsequent bills illustrate both that the system can be restructured and that restructured systems tend to reconverge on the patterns they replaced.<sup>6</sup> I do not think the political difficulty is a reason to stop talking about the structural need. I do think it is a reason to be honest about the distance between the need and the enactment.

## CONSERVATION BASELINES

Chapter 2 of this book argued that the Columbian Curtain, the conservation paradigm that treats 1492 as the ecological baseline, shapes what counts as native, what counts as damage, and what counts as restoration. The argument was analytical. The policy implications are concrete.

Conservation policy in the United States operates, in the main, under a post-Columbian baseline. The Endangered Species Act protects species that are threatened or endangered relative to their post-Columbian range and abundance. The National Park Service manages park ecosystems toward reference conditions derived from the early historical record. The Fish and Wildlife Service's management of wildlife refuges is oriented toward the maintenance of habitat for species present in the post-Columbian fauna. The regulatory architecture, in short, is built on the baseline the book has argued should be reexamined.

Shifting the conservation baseline to include near-time frames would not require abandoning the work that has been done under the post-Columbian paradigm. The bald eagle recovery, the alligator recovery, the wolf reintroduction in the Northern Rockies: all of these are genuine conservation achievements accomplished under existing baselines, and none of them would be undermined by an expanded baseline. What the shift would do is create regulatory space for a category of restoration that the current baseline forecloses: the reintroduction of species, or proxies for species, that were present in the near-time fauna but absent from the post-Columbian baseline. Under the current regulatory architecture, a proposal to introduce camels to the American Southwest as proxies for *Camelops* is not merely unusual. It is categorically outside the frame of what conservation policy is designed to evaluate. The camel is not listed under the Endangered Species Act, because it is not a native species under the Act's baseline. It is not a candidate for reintroduction under any existing wildlife management program, because no existing program operates under a baseline that recognizes its lineage's presence on the continent. The regulatory system does not say "no" to the proposal because the proposal has been evaluated and found wanting. The regulatory system does not have a category in which the proposal can be evaluated at all.

Creating that category, a regulatory pathway for the evaluation of near-time proxy reintroductions, would be a structural change of considerable significance. It would not require the approval of any specific reintroduction. It would require only that the regulatory system acknowledge the existence of the question and create a process for answering it: a framework for evaluating proposals, assessing ecological risks, setting monitoring requirements, and establishing the conditions under which a proof-of-concept experiment could proceed. The framework would need to be rigorous. The ecological risk assessments would need to meet or exceed the standards applied to endangered-species reintroductions. The monitoring requirements would need to be sustained and independently evaluated. The bar should be high, because the stakes of getting it wrong (introducing an animal that causes unforeseen ecological damage) are real, as Caro and Rubenstein have argued and as this book has acknowledged.

The institutional model closest to what would be needed is the experimental-population designation under Section 10(j) of the Endangered Species Act, which allows the Fish and Wildlife Service to designate reintroduced populations of listed species as "experimental" and manage them under more flexible regulatory provisions than fully protected populations receive.<sup>7</sup> The gray wolf reintroductions in Yellowstone and central Idaho were conducted under 10(j) designations, which allowed the managing agencies to address the legitimate concerns of livestock operators and adjacent landowners through management flexibility that full ESA protection would not have permitted. The mechanism is not directly applicable to proxy-species introductions, since a camel is not a listed species, but the conceptual architecture is relevant: a regulatory pathway that acknowledges the experimental nature of the introduction, provides for rigorous monitoring, establishes conditions for expansion or termination based on empirical results, and creates a legal framework within which the experiment can proceed without being foreclosed by categorical exclusion.

Jens-Christian Svenning and colleagues, in their 2016 synthesis on trophic rewilding in *PNAS*, argued that the scientific case for expanding conservation baselines to include Pleistocene-era ecological assemblages has strengthened substantially over the last decade, and that the institutional frameworks governing conservation practice have not kept pace with the science.<sup>8</sup> The gap between the scientific literature and the regulatory architecture is not unique to this issue. Regulatory frameworks routinely lag behind the science they are meant to implement. But the gap is particularly consequential here, because it forecloses an entire category of restoration work that the science has identified as worth pursuing.

## FURTHER STRUCTURAL CHANGES

Three additional areas of structural change bear noting, each briefer than the subsidy and baseline questions but each a real barrier to the vision the book has described.

The first is land-use regulation. Current agricultural zoning and conservation-easement structures assume a binary: commodity production or conservation set-aside. Ecologically managed food production — the Knepp model, the bison operations, the proxy-browsing experiments — is a third thing, and the regulatory apparatus does not have a well-developed framework for it. The Conservation Reserve Program pays landowners to take land out of production and maintain vegetative cover, but CRP contracts generally restrict the commercial use of enrolled acreage.<sup>9</sup> The result is a policy binary that forecloses the option of producing food from an ecologically managed landscape. What would need to change is the creation of a third land-use classification with its own ecological-outcome standards, its own monitoring requirements, and its own eligibility for public support. The classification would need to be rigorous enough to prevent greenwashing and flexible enough to accommodate the diversity of landscapes the book has described. State-level habitat exchange programs for greater sage-grouse offer institutional models that could be adapted.<sup>10</sup>

The second is regulatory space for proxy-species experiments. The proof-of-concept work the book has called for faces a permitting landscape that was not built with it in mind. A dromedary camel on Arizona rangeland under a university research protocol might need permits from the state department of agriculture, the state game and fish department, and the county — each operating under a different statutory framework, none designed for ecologically motivated species introductions. The friction is not deliberate. It is a side effect of regulatory architecture built for different purposes. What is needed is a streamlined pathway that integrates federal, state, and local authority into a single review process with clear criteria and defined timelines, modeled on the ESA's Section 10(j) experimental-population designation, which enabled the Yellowstone wolf reintroductions and the California condor recovery.<sup>7</sup> The institutional capacity to manage large-bodied animal introductions on working landscapes exists. It needs to be adapted to a new category of question.

> **FIELD NOTE: The Standard Already Exists**
>
> I should say what is true about my own relationship to the regulatory architecture this chapter has been describing.
>
> I wrote a camel standard. By 2013, or 2014 at the latest, under the Paleo Approved program, more than a decade before the book in your hands began to argue that the American Southwest could use the animal. The standard ran the categories a third-party certification standard runs: grass-fed and grass-finished, humanely treated, free range, antibiotic-free, hormone-free. It carried a banned-feedstuffs list — corn, soy, cereal grains except oats, milk replacers containing antibiotics or growth hormones, non-protein nitrogen, antibiotics, hormones. It carried a traceability requirement of one hundred percent from birth to harvest, with forty-eight-hour trace-back. It carried a welfare-indicator inventory the auditor uses to flag environmental issues: calluses, swelling, injuries, bruising, infections, laminitis, hematomas, abscesses, broken bones and tails, scars. The definitions section ran more than thirty terms, from Baleage to Vitamin, because a standard that cannot be audited is not a standard.
>
> I used it. We certified Desert Farms, the US camel-milk operation, by visiting their producer outside Springfield, Missouri, walking the operation, and applying the standard against what we saw. We saw Walid Abdul-Wahab, the founder, again at the Ancestral Health Symposium in 2014. I could not have certified without writing the standard. The standard existed because I had written it.
>
> I am telling you this in Chapter Thirteen because the chapter has been describing a regulatory architecture that does not yet have a federal category for keystone-proxy species. That description is correct as far as it goes. What it is not is complete. The federal pathway does not exist. The third-party certification pathway, for the specific animal this book is most directly about, does. It existed by 2014. The Paleo Approved program no longer runs, and the standard is no longer certifying anyone. But the document was written, the categories were defined, and the framework for what an audited American camel operation would have to demonstrate was laid out, by an author who is now asking the country to consider operations like the ones the standard was designed to certify.
>
> If a federal pathway is built, it will not be built from scratch. There is already a draft. There is already an example of using it to certify a real producer in a real state. The harder work, what a federal architecture would have to add to a third-party standard to make it generally applicable, is the work the rest of this chapter has been naming. The pathway has been waiting on someone with federal-rulemaking authority to take the categories seriously. The categories have been there.

The third is fisheries management. Here the institutional infrastructure is, in some respects, further along than the terrestrial equivalent. The Magnuson-Stevens Act provides the statutory framework, the regional fishery management councils provide governance, and Hilborn's stock-assessment methodology provides the scientific tools. The Alaska salmon fisheries are a working example of sustainable wild-harvest food production at commercial scale.<sup>11</sup> What would need to change is the extension of this model to marine restoration — kelp forests, oyster reefs, coastal wetlands — under regulatory frameworks that treat restored marine ecosystems as productive systems rather than merely as conservation amenities, and that treat the food-production value of restoration as part of the justification for public investment.<sup>12</sup>

## WHAT WOULD HAVE TO BE TRUE

I have described five areas of structural change: subsidy reform, conservation-baseline expansion, land-use regulatory modernization, regulatory pathways for proxy-species experiments, and the extension and strengthening of science-based fisheries management. Each is individually difficult. Together they constitute a reorientation of the policy framework governing food production and land management in the United States, a reorientation that is, by any honest assessment, politically formidable.

I want to name what would have to be true for these changes to become possible, because naming the preconditions honestly is more useful than pretending the changes are around the corner.

There would have to be a political constituency for ecological food-system reform that is large enough and organized enough to influence farm bill deliberations. Farm bill politics are currently dominated by the commodity agriculture constituency, the nutrition-assistance constituency (SNAP and related programs account for the majority of farm bill spending), and the conservation constituency, which has achieved meaningful but incremental gains over successive reauthorizations. A constituency for the kind of structural reform this chapter describes — one that includes producers operating outside the commodity system, conservation organizations working on near-time baselines, Indigenous nations managing land under traditional and restored practices, and consumers who have adopted some version of the framework this book proposes — does not currently exist as a coordinated political force. It exists in fragments. The fragments have not yet found each other.

There would have to be a sustained research program generating the empirical evidence that policy change requires. The proof-of-concept experiments the book has called for are not just interesting ecological questions. They are the evidentiary foundation without which no policy advocate can credibly argue for regulatory change. A policymaker being asked to create a permitting pathway for proxy-species experiments will ask what the evidence shows. If the evidence does not yet exist because the experiments have not been conducted, the argument stalls. The research comes first. The policy follows the research.

There would have to be an honest reckoning with the distributional consequences of subsidy reform. Redirecting federal agricultural spending from commodity support to ecosystem-service payments would produce winners and losers, and the losers would include producers and communities whose livelihoods are organized around the current commodity system. A reform that does not account for the transition costs — that does not provide pathways for commodity producers to shift toward ecological management, that does not invest in the rural infrastructure and retraining that the transition would require — is a reform that imposes its costs on the people least able to absorb them. The people most affected by subsidy reform are not the agribusiness corporations that capture the largest share of the payments. They are the family-scale commodity producers and the rural communities whose economies have been organized around commodity production for generations. A just transition would need to address their interests, and addressing their interests would need to be part of the reform's design rather than an afterthought.

There would have to be, finally, a cultural shift in how Americans think about the land they live on: a shift from treating the post-Columbian landscape as the default ecological condition to recognizing it as one state in a longer ecological history. This is the shift the Columbian Curtain argument was designed to support. It is not a shift that a book or a policy proposal can produce on its own. It is a shift that happens, if it happens, through the accumulation of evidence, education, and experience: through people visiting rewilded landscapes and seeing what they look like, through students learning near-time ecology alongside contemporary ecology, through the slow accretion of a different ecological imagination. I do not know how long this takes. I know it has not happened yet.

> **CASE: When the Test Stops Being a Thought Experiment**
>
> This chapter has been arguing that the survivability axis matters because the conditions under which our food systems were optimized may not hold. The argument is not abstract.
>
> The most-discussed grain-export chokepoint in the world as I am writing this is the Odesa region of the Black Sea, through which Ukraine has historically shipped roughly four-fifths of its grain and oilseed output. The export corridor has been under episodic military pressure since 2022, has operated at reduced capacity for stretches of every year since, and remains among the most concentrated routes by which carbohydrate calories reach the food-insecure populations of Africa and the Middle East. When that route falters, the World Food Programme reports an immediate caloric cascade thousands of miles away. The chokepoint is not theoretical.
>
> The most-discussed nitrogen-fertilizer chokepoint is the Strait of Hormuz, through which a substantial fraction of the world’s seaborne natural-gas trade transits, and which therefore sets the marginal global price of the natural gas that feeds the Haber-Bosch process. Roughly half of the nitrogen currently in living human bodies traces back to that industrial synthesis. Vaclav Smil has been making the point in print for thirty years: four billion people are alive who would not be alive without it. The dependency is not a hypothetical future risk. The dependency is the current architecture, and the pricing of its key input cycles through a strait that has been militarily contested repeatedly in this century.
>
> These are not the only chokepoints. Phosphate runs through North Africa. Potash runs through Belarus and Russia. Seed stock concentrates in a handful of multinational firms. The cold chain runs on refrigerants whose supply is its own chokepoint. Each of these is a single point of failure for a different essential input to industrial food production — and each of them compounds with the climate-driven failure modes (drought, heatwave, flood) that act on the same production base from a different angle. The list is not new and it is not partisan. It is the structural reality of a global food system that has been optimized for cheap energy and stable trade lanes for the better part of a century.
>
> The four-axis framework was built to make exactly this kind of dependency visible. An industrial food system that scores well on economic viability, on social fit, and even on present-day environmental footprint can score catastrophically on survivability if its essential inputs cycle through a few politically contested geographies. The reader does not have to take any particular position on the geopolitics to take the survivability score seriously. The dependency exists regardless of who holds office anywhere.
>
> This is no longer a question only the Pentagon planners are asking. Singapore’s *30 by 30* program is an explicit state-level commitment to producing thirty percent of the country’s nutritional needs domestically by the decade’s end — built around exactly the survivability axis this chapter has been describing. Israel’s national food-security planning treats the dependency profile as an input on equal footing with conventional defense planning. China’s strategic grain reserves, held at a scale that has been publicly estimated at roughly half of the world’s wheat stockpiles, are the same calculation in a different vocabulary. The Pentagon, the war colleges, and the food-systems-resilience desks of the major think tanks are running the same math. The countries doing this work are not all on the same political team, and the math does not care which team they are on.
>
> The United States enters this conversation carrying a structural disadvantage of its own, and a specific opportunity. The disadvantage is that the continent it operates on is, by the ecological measure that matters most for the fourth axis, already depleted — running its food systems on land that lost most of its large-bodied animals twelve and a half thousand years ago and has been operating below ecological capacity ever since. The Americas paid that survivability cost more acutely and on a more recent timescale than most other inhabited continents, and the cost is still on the books. The opportunity is that the disadvantage is one we can get ahead of. The framework this book has built points at what getting ahead of it would actually look like. The chapter that ends this book makes the case.
>
> The Walking Dead question that opened this work was a thought experiment when I asked it fifteen years ago. It is no longer.

> **FIELD NOTE: The Minimum Viable Flip**
>
> The structural changes this chapter has named are individually difficult and collectively formidable. None of them is a precondition for running the smallest version of the experiment. The MVP, to borrow a frame from outside ecology, is what Eric Ries, writing about startups rather than ecosystems, called a minimum viable product: not the cheapest version of the project, but the smallest version that can be falsified.
>
> A hundred camels in an Osage orange orchard is the long-tail outcome, not the MVP. The MVP is something more like: forty acres of distressed basin-and-range ground, six to ten camels (or feral burros, or whatever combination of available proxies for what the fossil record says belongs there can actually be permitted), a small planting of a co-evolved tree species, and a monitoring protocol that captures soil carbon, water infiltration, plant diversity, and animal body condition at fixed intervals over three to five years. Bounded. Instrumented. Designed to fail informatively if it fails. The point is not to prove the orchard hypothesis — the MVP cannot do that. The point is to generate the first real signal where signal currently does not exist, at a cost low enough that the permitting pathway and the funding architecture can be tested against a concrete proposal rather than a theoretical one.
>
> The case for running the MVP rests on a separate argument this book has been making in different vocabulary throughout. The current food system is fragile in the sense Chapter Four named: optimized for stable conditions and brittle when those conditions change. The dominant agricultural modality is engineered toward an equilibrium of stable rainfall, stable energy prices, stable trade lanes, stable phosphate, stable nitrogen, stable insect ecology, stable microbial ecology. The engineering succeeds only as long as the equilibrium holds. The Dust Bowl was a fragility cascade. The Irish Potato Famine was a fragility cascade. Panama disease in bananas, late blight, foot-and-mouth, BSE, the broiler flock culls of the last decade — all fragility cascades. Each one was solved by reaching for more of the input the system already depended on, which deepened the specialization, which set up the next cascade.
>
> A keystone-driven, ecologically diverse system would not be immune to disturbance. It would be the opposite of fragile to it. Fire, drought, herd movement, mass mortality — these are not anomalies in a Pleistocene-functional landscape. They are the conditions the landscape is calibrated to. Take the disturbance away and the system degrades. Put it back and the system reorganizes.
>
> Intellectual honesty asks the policy framework to plan for both worlds. The one in which the dominant agricultural modality keeps refining itself, throws ever-better solutions at its dependencies, and reaches the better version of what it already is. That world is worth planning for. So is the other one. If the input chains tighten, if the climate signal shifts past what the cultivars can tolerate, if a microbial event takes out a single-genome crop the way late blight took out the lumper potato, the contingency is not “find a cleverer monoculture.” The contingency is “have the alternative system already running, somewhere, at any scale, with enough data that it can be expanded.”
>
> That is the case for funding the MVP even if the dominant system is fine. Diversity of crops is what survivability looks like at the field level. Diversity of approaches is what survivability looks like at the system level. We did not learn nothing from the Dust Bowl. We learned, at minimum, that running every farm by the same playbook is what makes a Dust Bowl possible. The same logic applies one level up. The MVP is a way of buying optionality before the option needs to be exercised.

A note on the limits of the MVP frame, because the metaphor will do work I do not want it to do unless I name the limit. In a software MVP, *fail* means the product did not find market fit and *discard* means you stop running it. The cost of the failure is the cost of the time and capital spent. There are no other consequences. You shut the server down, you pay the people, you move on.

In an ecological MVP, *fail* and *discard* do not work the same way. If a hundred camels are introduced into an Osage orange orchard in the Southwest and the experiment fails, the camels are still there. They are large mammals with reproductive capacity and a shared continent. Discarding them means a euthanasia program, a re-export program, or a containment program — every one of which is a moral, financial, and political event. The Australian feral camel population, now around a million animals, exists because the original introduction’s failure mode was not to remove the camels. Failure was a direction, not a stop button.

I am not retreating from the MVP frame. I think it remains the right metaphor for how to design the experiment — small scale, defined success criteria, willingness to revise. What I am doing is naming what the metaphor’s edges are, because the edges are where most importing of one field’s logic into another field goes badly.

The honest version of the camel-hypothesis MVP is: small initial population, on enclosed land, with explicit conditions under which the experiment is judged a failure, and an explicit, pre-committed plan for what happens to the animals if the failure conditions are met. The plan has to exist before the camels arrive. Without that plan, the framework I have built does not authorize the experiment. With it, the experiment is a real test of the survivability axis at a real scale. The camels are not a metaphor.

## CLOSING

This book has made its argument. It has proposed a framework, applied it, followed it into territory the food-systems conversation has mostly not visited, and arrived at a set of conclusions I believe the evidence supports. The framework identifies structural vulnerabilities in the dominant food system. The fossil record identifies a direction for restoration that most of the sustainability literature has not considered. The existing proof-of-concept work — the bison operations, the feral hog harvests, the intact fisheries, the Knepp restoration — demonstrates that rewilded food production is not an abstraction. The serious critics have been engaged, and the responses to their critiques are, I think, defensible.

What the book cannot do is enact what it proposes. The distance between an argument and its realization is not a distance the argument itself can cross. The structural changes this chapter has described are preconditions for the book's vision to scale beyond the readers who adopt the framework individually. Those changes are politically difficult, institutionally complex, and dependent on conditions — a political constituency, an evidentiary base, a cultural shift — that do not yet fully exist.

I am not ending with certainty about outcomes, because certainty about political outcomes is not something the evidence supports, and the book's commitment to stating what it can defend requires applying that standard here as well. I am ending with a description of the door. The door is the set of structural conditions under which the experiments could be tried, the baselines could be expanded, the subsidy system could be restructured, and the food system could begin to move in the direction the framework points. The door exists. It is not open. Whether it opens depends on work that this book can contribute to but cannot complete.

The epilogue returns to the orchard. What remains after the argument, after the framework, after the policy, is the question the prologue already asked: is the experiment worth trying?

The book has laid out what it would take. The answer to whether or not the experiment is worth trying is not the book's to give.

It is the reader's.

# Epilogue: One Hundred Camels

The orchard does not exist yet. I want to return to it anyway.

I opened this book with a camel: *Camelops hesternus*, yesterday's camel, native to North America for most of the history of its genus, gone for thirteen thousand years, remembered mostly in bones and the occasional museum mount. I opened it with an Osage orange tree, producing its softball-sized fruit for a mouth that is no longer there. I opened it with a wish: to see one hundred camels in an Osage orange orchard, just to see if it could work. The wish was not a program. It was the thing the program would be for.

The book between the prologue and this page has been the program, or at least the argument for one. Thirteen chapters of framework, evidence, application, and honest reckoning with what the framework cannot do. The reader who has come this far knows what the four-axis assessment measures and what it reveals about the dominant food system. The reader knows what the Columbian Curtain hides and what the near-time window makes visible. The reader has walked through the Polyface math, the Savory food-truck conversation, the fossil record of the Grand Canyon, the critics' strongest arguments, the marine restorations, the Indigenous management traditions that operate under a parallel frame. The reader has sat with Chapter 13's description of the structural changes that would need to happen for any of this to move from argument to practice, and the reader has noticed — I hope —that the chapter was honest about the distance between the argument and the enactment.

I do not want to summarize any of that. The reader has just read it. What I want to do, here at the end, is return to the image that started it, because the image has changed, not in its content but in what it carries.

When I described the orchard in the prologue, it carried one thing: a longing. A specific want, plainly stated, for a specific arrangement of animals and trees on a specific kind of ground. The longing was personal. It was mine. I did not try to dress it as policy or as science, because it was not those things. It was the reason I had spent years thinking about the questions the book asks, and it seemed honest to say so at the beginning, before the analytical apparatus had been set up and before the reader had any reason to take the analysis seriously.

The longing has not gone away. I still want to see those camels. I still want to know what the fruit looks like when it has a mouth. But the image is no longer sitting in the space it sat in at the prologue's close — a private wish, carried by a person the reader had just met, before the argument had been made. It is sitting, now, on the other side of the argument. The orchard has accumulated meaning it did not have sixty thousand words ago.

The camels in the orchard are not just camels anymore. They are the proxy-selection principle from Chapter 7 — the fossil record consulted, the co-evolutionary relationship identified, the right animal placed in the right landscape for reasons the paleontology supports. They are the survivability argument from Chapter 4 —animals that feed themselves on what the landscape produces, with a dependency profile thinner than anything the industrial system can offer. They are the proof-of-concept experiment from Chapter 9 — bounded, monitored, designed to generate data rather than to prove a point. They are the structural change from Chapter 13 —the permitting pathway that does not yet exist, the regulatory category that has not been created, the political constituency that has not yet found itself. The hundred camels carry all of that now. They carried none of it before.

The Osage orange carries something different than it did at the beginning, too. In the prologue, it was an anachronism: a tree stranded in a present that had forgotten what it was for, producing fruit for a disperser that had been gone for a hundred and twenty centuries. That much has not changed. What has changed is the frame around it. The tree is no longer an isolated curiosity, a bit of natural-history trivia about the deep past. It is an instance of a pattern the book has traced across the entire continent: the grasslands still calibrated to grazers that are gone, the thorny shrubs still armed against browsers that are absent, the pronghorn still running from nothing, the kelp forests and oyster reefs stripped to fractions of their historical extent, the Indigenous fire regimes suppressed, the soil communities waiting for trampling patterns that have not been produced in twelve thousand years. The Osage orange, in the prologue, was one tree with one absent partner. In the epilogue, it is a representative of a continent whose ecological vocabulary has been spoken at half-volume for longer than there has been agriculture, and whose restoration (if it is attempted, if it is permitted, if the experiments are funded and the baselines are expanded and the political will materializes) would be the most ambitious act of ecological repair in the history of the species attempting it.

That is a large thing to rest on one tree and a hundred hypothetical camels. I am aware of the disproportion. The book has been aware of it from the beginning. The analytical argument is not proportional to the longing. The longing is outsized, personal, and not fully defensible on the evidence the book has assembled. The evidence supports a framework. The framework supports a set of experiments. The experiments might support a direction. The direction might, over decades, produce a food system organized around ecological function rather than industrial inputs. None of that is the same as a hundred camels in an orchard. The orchard is where the argument started, and it is further from realization than any of the intermediate steps the book has described.

I think this disproportion is honest rather than embarrassing. The book began with a wish because the wish is what generated the work. The work is what the book contains. The work does not fulfill the wish, and it would be dishonest to pretend otherwise. What the work does is take the wish seriously enough to ask what it would actually require (what framework, what evidence, what engagement with critics, what policy changes, what institutional capacity, what cultural shift) and to lay that out as clearly as I can, knowing that I do not control most of the conditions the answer depends on.

The book has not solved the problem it describes. It has not demonstrated that Pleistocene rewilding works at scale, because the experiments have not been conducted. It has not demonstrated that the four-axis framework produces better policy outcomes than the three-axis model, because the framework has not been adopted by any policymaking body. It has not demonstrated that Americans will eat camel, or that the permitting pathway will be created, or that the farm bill will be restructured, or that the cultural imagination will expand to include a continent whose memory is older than its paperwork. The book has made an argument. The argument is partial, contested, and dependent on conditions I do not control. I have tried to make it carefully and to be honest about where the evidence is strong, where it is provisional, and where I am asking for something the evidence does not yet support.

What I am asking for, in the end, is not certainty. It is not even, really, that the experiment be mine. I read Stephen Hawking when I was young, the way you do, with a flashlight, understanding half of it, and ever since I have wanted to see a black hole and throw popcorn at its event horizon. The popcorn was mine. The want was mine. The physics were going to go on doing what they do without my participation, and the participation was never the point. The point was to watch the popcorn go in and know, finally, what happens at the edge. Or a refrigerator. Just to watch. I will most likely die before I get to throw popcorn at an event horizon, or a refrigerator. What will not die is that I really, truly, just want to see. The wanting does not go away. It hasn't now for thirty years.

> **FIELD NOTE: The Popcorn**
>
> The popcorn thing is real. I was twelve, maybe thirteen, and I had read Stephen Hawking the way you read Stephen Hawking when you are that age, grasping the shape of the thing without grasping the math.
>
> It has stirred me deeply, ever since. And like camels, it has taken up residence in my mind. The point of the popcorn, I understood only much later, was not the popcorn. It was that some wants are worth carrying even when they exceed what the carrier can do about them. Throwing popcorn at the event horizon, what does that achieve, meaningfully? Seeing whether a hundred camels can reverse desertification, seeing whether we can learn something useful from running an experiment like that — that is a wish worth having.
>
> I am not embarrassed to end a book of analytical argument talking about popcorn and refrigerators and event horizons and camels in an Osage orange orchard. Some wishes are the reason for the analysis. At the very least, I have full faith that at least one of these wishes could plausibly be granted within my lifetime. And if I had to choose one — and that would be a difficult choice — I would choose camels. There is no utility in finding out what happens to popcorn, or refrigerators, tossed into an event horizon. There is utility in finding out what camels can do for land-flipping and reversing desertification, while producing meat, milk that the autism community has been turning to in growing numbers, camel baby formula powder, hump fat, leather, and “camel cashmere,” to name a few of the commodities I am aware of. And this does not even begin to include the money from potential safaris and tourism drawn by the attraction of large megafauna in a space where they seem foreign and exotic.

What I am describing at the end of this book is the same kind of wanting. I want to know whether a parcel of desertified ground in the basin and range country of California or Nevada — real ground, not hypothetical, ground that is sick and losing what little it has left to lose — can be flipped. Not flipped the way a house gets flipped, exactly, though that is the register I mean. Bought distressed. Returned to function by putting the right animals back on it and the right trees in it, running as a closed-loop system for a decade with honest monitoring. Sold, or kept, or left to the commons, whichever way the experiment wants to go once the experiment has been run. What I want to know is whether the ground can be healed. I see desertification as a disease. I do not see it as a settled condition. I do not see it as something the ground has chosen, or earned, or is stuck with. I see it as something nobody has yet been permitted to treat.

I have said as much at Allan Savory conferences and been eye-rolled by people in that audience whom I love and respect and whose work I admire. I do not care. I am not trying to win the argument at the conference. I am trying to find out whether the answer exists. Those are not the same project, and the second one is the one I am interested in. The experiment is the point.

What I am asking for, when I say the experiment is the point, is the smallest version of the experiment that can give a real answer — what Chapter Thirteen named the minimum viable flip. Forty acres of distressed ground. Six to ten camels. A monitoring protocol that can detect signal in three to five years. That is the test the question deserves.

I think it is going to work. I cannot see how it does not. The animals are calibrated to the landscape. The landscape is calibrated to the animals. Take the disturbance away and the system degrades. Put it back and the system reorganizes. The hypothesis is the most plausible thing I have ever proposed in print, and the evidence the book has assembled supports it.

I am also aware that I have been wrong before. The Revision sidebars are in the book for a reason. The first manuscript I turned in was answering a smaller question than the one Martin was asking, and I did not know it until I read him. I have changed my mind on monocultures, on the overkill hypothesis, on the right tone for an argument like this one. The change-of-mind track record is part of why I am unwilling to oversell this. The same uneasiness that has me unable to oversell it is also why I think we should run it. The way you find out whether an idea works is to test it. Dumber experiments have been done.

If it works — if the ground responds, if the camels feed themselves on what the landscape produces, if the soil carbon and the water infiltration and the plant diversity move in the directions the framework predicts — then the hundred camels in an Osage orange orchard is not a wish. It is the next acreage.

The experiment does not need to be mine. It needs to be somebody's. The ground is there. The camels are there: feral in parts of the American West, stabled on hobby ranches, eating mesquite because that is what camels eat when you put them on ground that grows it. The Osage orange is there, falling and rotting and asking for a mouth nobody has yet put in front of it. The regulatory pathway does not exist, but that is what Chapter 13 was for. The patron, the university, the protocol, the acreage: none of these exist yet in the configuration the experiment requires, and I do not control whether they come together. What I can do is name what I am asking for, and hope the person who can do it reads this far.

I do not know what the data would show. I think the hypothesis is worth testing, or I would not have written the book. I think the ecological logic is sound: that a continent missing its keystone herbivores for twelve thousand years is functioning below its capacity, that the fossil record provides a guide to restoration that the current conservation baseline does not see, and that food systems organized around ecological function are more survivable than food systems organized around industrial inputs. I think these things because the evidence the book has assembled supports them. I do not think them with the certainty I once brought to simpler versions of this argument, because the revisions the book has documented have taught me something about the relationship between confidence and evidence that the earlier version of me had not yet learned.

The orchard is still hypothetical. The camels are not standing in it. The Osage oranges are still falling, still rotting in place, still producing for no one. Somewhere in the arid basin country of the Southwest, a dromedary on a hobby ranch is eating mesquite, because mesquite is what camels eat when you put them on ground that grows it, and neither the camel nor the mesquite requires an argument to know what they are for.

I would still like to see those camels. I would still like to know what happens when the fruit has a mouth. I would like, more than either of those things, to know whether the ground can be flipped — whether a parcel that has been desertifying for a century can, under the right animals and the right trees and enough time, come back. I will probably not be the person who finds out. I want to be alive when somebody does.

# Afterword: The Rabbit Holes Adjacent

The book you have just read began with a question at a meat counter in 2009. The question, as the Field Note in Chapter One described, was about the gap between what a label said and what the package contained. The book that came out of that question is the one in your hands. It is about agricultural systems, about the survivability axis, about an experiment with one hundred camels in an Osage orange orchard.

What the book does not say — and what I want to say briefly here, because it is part of the same story even though it did not fit inside the four corners of the argument — is that the question, having opened, did not close. Other rabbit holes opened alongside the agricultural one. They are still opening.

The first thread to pull free from the agricultural one was the human gut microbiome. The two questions braided in my head almost from the start. What was being fed to the animals; what was being fed to the soil; what those feedings did to the microbial communities the soil and the gut both depend on. As the work has continued, the braid has tightened: the dysbiotic gut microbiomes that show up across so many modern conditions look, on close inspection, like downstream effects of heavy-metal exposure and mismetallation rather than independent etiologies. The work I have done on that braid is now organized under two reference resources I built for my own continuing study: **Wikibiome** (wikibiome.com), focused on the connection between microbes, disease, and heavy metals; and **Microbiome Medicine** (microbiomemedicine.com), focused on microbiome and metallomic signatures and on how to reverse disease through interventions that reduce heavy-metal exposure, including dietary protocols like the low-nickel diet.

The next thread, by then no longer separable from the first, was heavy metals as a system-level contamination question. The same agricultural systems that had simplified the soil microbial communities had also been concentrating heavy metals in those soils for decades — cadmium in phosphate-fertilized cropland, arsenic legacies from organoarsenical poultry litter, atmospheric lead, nickel from manure and fertilizer applications. Once I started looking, the metals were everywhere in the food supply, almost always invisible at the label and almost always unmonitored at the producer. The standards work I had been doing through the Paleo Foundation began turning toward metals testing. That work is now organized under a label called **HMTc — Heavy Metal Tested & Certified** (heavymetaltested.com), and around a public reference called the **Heavy Metal Index** (heavymetalindex.com), which catalogs heavy-metal contamination across food categories and supply chains alongside published remediation strategies.

I name these here for two reasons. The first is honest disclosure: these are my projects, and the book you are reading was written alongside them. The second is more practical: if anything in this book made you want to look further, the four sites name the threads I have already pulled. Whatever you find on them is more current than what is in the book, because the work is still moving.

Two specific lines of that work are documented in preprints already in circulation. *Heavy Metals in Fertilizers: A Historical Analysis of Contamination Trends (1960–2025)* traces the rising heavy-metal load in agricultural fertilizers across six and a half decades, reconstructing the four-phase historical arc from the unregulated industrial expansion of the 1960s through the 1990s peak in cadmium and nickel concentrations to the partial regulatory recovery of the present (Zenodo, https://doi.org/ 10.5281/zenodo.18439158). *Heavy Metals, Microbial Metallomics, and the US Obesity Epidemic: A Mechanistic Examination of a Population-Level Metabolic Disruption* advances what I call the *heavy metal dysbiosis hypothesis* — the proposition that the dysbiotic microbiome signatures observed across the modern US population, and the metabolic conditions associated with them, are mechanistically downstream of chronic dietary heavy-metal exposure and microbial mismetallation rather than independent etiologies. The paper draws on US population data, including an NHANES analysis in which insulin resistance was found to mediate roughly seventy-four percent of the association between urinary nickel and metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (Zenodo, https://doi.org/ 10.5281/zenodo.18434951). The two papers together carry the analytical case the book gestures at: that the agricultural thread, the microbiome thread, and the heavy-metal thread are one thread.

All four began with the same question I was asking at the meat counter in 2009. None of them are finished. Sixteen years in, I am still standing where the camel and the chicken and the man behind the counter put me: convinced that the systems I had trusted were not telling the truth, and committed to figuring out what was actually true, by whatever route the question opens next.

*The book is the agricultural thread. The other threads are at:*

- WIKIBIOME.COM — microbes, disease, and the heavy-metals connection
- MICROBIOMEMEDICINE.COM — microbiome and metallomic signatures, dietary interventions
- HEAVYMETALTESTED.COM — HMTc certification (Heavy Metal Tested & Certified)
- HEAVYMETALINDEX.COM — heavy-metal contamination in food, plus remediation strategies

# Notes

## PROLOGUE · ONE HUNDRED CAMELS

1. Martin, P. S. (2005). *Twilight of the Mammoths: Ice Age Extinctions and the Rewilding of America.* University of California Press. Martin synthesizes the Late Pleistocene loss of more than forty North American large-mammal species, including *Camelops hesternus* and *Hemiauchenia*, dated to approximately 13,000 years before present in the North American fossil record.

2. Stuart, A. J. (2015). "Late Quaternary megafaunal extinctions on the continents: a short review." *Geological Journal,* 50(3), 338–363, https://doi.org/10.1002/gj.2633. Stuart’s synthesis documents that of the large-bodied terrestrial mammals (genera with body mass typically ≥ 44 kg) present at the Last Glacial Maximum, North America lost approximately 72 percent of its megafaunal genera during the Late Pleistocene–early Holocene window, with comparable losses in South America and somewhat smaller losses in Eurasia. See also MacPhee, R. D. E. (2018). *End of the Megafauna: The Fate of the World’s Hugest, Fiercest, and Strangest Animals.* W. W. Norton, for the broader synthesis of cause-of-extinction debates and the comparative continental record.

3. Martin (2005). The "Columbian Curtain" is Martin's own framing for the conservation paradigm that uses 1492 as an ecological baseline, treating European contact as the effective starting line for questions of native and non-native status.

4. Janzen, D. H., & Martin, P. S. (1982). Neotropical anachronisms: the fruits the gomphotheres ate. *Science*, 215(4528), 19–27. The foundational paper on megafaunal fruit syndrome and ecological anachronism.

## CHAPTER ONE · THE MEAT COUNTER

1. United States Department of Agriculture, Agricultural Marketing Service. (2007). "United States Standards for Livestock and Meat Marketing Claims, Grass (Forage) Fed Claim for Ruminant Livestock and the Meat Products Derived from Such Livestock." *Federal Register,* 72(199), 58631–58637. The standard defined grass-fed as meaning the animal's diet was derived solely from forage after weaning; grain and grain byproducts were excluded from the permitted diet.

2. The voluntary nature of the USDA grass-fed marketing claim standard meant that producers who did not apply for certification could still use the term "grass-fed" on their packaging, subject only to the general USDA-FSIS labeling approval process for meat products. See USDA Food Safety and Inspection Service, "Labeling Policies" guidance documents, particularly the label approval process for special claims under 9 CFR 317.4.

3. United States Department of Agriculture, Agricultural Marketing Service. (2016). "Withdrawal of United States Standards for Livestock and Meat Marketing Claims, Grass (Forage) Fed Claim for Ruminant Livestock and the Meat Products Derived from Such Livestock." *Federal Register,* 81(12), 3073–3074. Effective January 12, 2016.

4. United States Department of Agriculture, Food Safety and Inspection Service. "Meat and Poultry Labeling Terms." The FSIS defines "free-range" or "free-roaming" for poultry as meaning "producers must demonstrate to the Agency that the poultry has been allowed access to the outside." No specifications for duration, frequency, or size of outdoor area are included in the operative definition. See FSIS Directive 7120.1, "Safe and Suitable Ingredients Used in the Production of Meat, Poultry, and Egg Products," Appendix A, and the FSIS labeling guidance for animal-raising claims.

5. Organic Foods Production Act of 1990, 7 U.S.C. §§ 6501–6523; implementing regulations at 7 CFR Part 205. The National Organic Program standards specify allowed and prohibited substances, require annual inspection by accredited certifying agents, and define organic production as "a production system that is managed in accordance with the Act and regulations in this part to respond to site-specific conditions by integrating cultural, biological, and mechanical practices that foster cycling of resources, promote ecological balance, and conserve biodiversity."

6. The term "natural" as applied to meat and poultry is defined by USDA-FSIS as meaning "a product containing no artificial ingredient or added color and is only minimally processed." The definition addresses processing, not production practices; it says nothing about how the animal was raised, what it was fed, or what inputs were used in its production. See 9 CFR 317.16 and FSIS Policy Memo 055B. For non-meat food products regulated by the FDA, "natural" has no formal regulatory definition and has been the subject of repeated citizen petitions and comment periods without resolution. See FDA, "Use of the Term 'Natural' on Food Labeling," Docket FDA-2014-N-1207.

## CHAPTER TWO · THE COLUMBIAN CURTAIN

1. MacFadden, B. J. (1992). *Fossil Horses: Systematics, Paleobiology, and Evolution of the Family Equidae.* Cambridge University Press, pp. 1–14 and chapter 5. MacFadden's synthesis of Equidae evolution is the standard reference for the North American origin of the genus.

2. Scott, E., & Cox, S. M. (2008). "Late Pleistocene distribution of *Equus* (Mammalia: Equidae) in western North America." In *Geology and Vertebrate Paleontology of Western and Southern North America,* Natural History Museum of Los Angeles County Science Series 41, pp. 189–208. For the specific North American late-Pleistocene equid taxa and their chronology.

3. Orlando, L., et al. (2013). "Recalibrating *Equus* evolution using the genome sequence of an early Middle Pleistocene horse." *Nature,* 499(7456), 74–78. The Thistle Creek genome.

4. Weinstock, J., et al. (2005). "Evolution, systematics, and phylogeography of Pleistocene horses in the New World: a molecular perspective." *PLoS Biology,* 3(8), e241.

5. Martin, P. S. (2005). *Twilight of the Mammoths: Ice Age Extinctions and the Rewilding of America.* University of California Press, chapter 3 and chapter 9. Martin's synthesis of the North American near-time losses and the date range of the terminal Pleistocene pulse.

6. Bureau of Land Management. "Wild Horse and Burro Program: Program Data." Current public population estimates are published annually by the BLM's National Wild Horse and Burro Program office; the AML (appropriate management level) framework and the discrepancy between AML and current population numbers are detailed in the Program Data documentation. See also the 2020 National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine review of BLM wild horse and burro management, which describes the same discrepancy.

7. MacPhee, R. D. E., ed. (1999). *Extinctions in Near Time: Causes, Contexts, and Consequences.* Kluwer Academic / Plenum. The introductory chapter and the volume's synthesis chapters are the primary references for the near-time frame as used here.

8. Koch, P. L., & Barnosky, A. D. (2006). "Late Quaternary extinctions: state of the debate." *Annual Review of Ecology, Evolution, and Systematics,* 37, 215–250. Koch and Barnosky's review assembles the continent-by-continent loss figures used here.

9. Smith, F. A., et al. (2016). "Body size downgrading of mammals over the late Quaternary." *Science,* 351(6280), 310–313. Smith and colleagues quantify the loss of large-mammal body mass across the late Quaternary, with regional breakdowns that include the North American figures referenced here.

10. Graham, R. W., et al. (2016). "Timing and causes of mid-Holocene mammoth extinction on St. Paul Island, Alaska." *PNAS,* 113(33), 9310–9314. For the latest securely dated North American mammoth populations, on a Bering Sea refugium, dating to roughly 5,600 years before present. Mainland North American mammoths are generally gone by roughly 10,500–11,000 BP; the refugial populations extend the range somewhat.

11. Martin, P. S., Sabels, B. E., & Shutler, D., Jr. (1961). "Rampart Cave coprolite and ecology of the Shasta ground sloth." *American Journal of Science,* 259, 102–127. For the classic description of preserved Shasta ground sloth dung and the reconstruction of late-Pleistocene browse based on it.

12. See the Executive Order 13112 definition of "invasive species" as operationalized by the US National Invasive Species Council: a non-native species whose introduction does or is likely to cause economic or environmental harm, or harm to human health. The "non-native" qualifier in the operative federal and most state-level definitions is assessed against the pre-Columbian biota. See also Richardson, D. M., et al. (2000). "Naturalization and invasion of alien plants: concepts and definitions." *Diversity and Distributions,* 6(2), 93–107, for the parallel academic formulation in invasion biology.

13. Pauly, D. (1995). "Anecdotes and the shifting baseline syndrome of fisheries." *Trends in Ecology & Evolution,* 10(10), 430. A short note that has since anchored a substantial literature; see also Jackson, J. B. C., et al. (2001). "Historical overfishing and the recent collapse of coastal ecosystems." *Science,* 293(5530), 629–638, for a major application of the shifting-baseline concept to marine historical ecology.

14. Hansen, R. M. (1978). "Shasta ground sloth food habits, Rampart Cave, Arizona." *Paleobiology,* 4(3), 302–319. For the systematic reconstruction of *Nothrotheriops shastensis* diet from preserved coprolites, which remains one of the most detailed diet reconstructions for any extinct megaherbivore.

15. Stock, C., & Harris, J. M. (1992). *Rancho La Brea: A Record of Pleistocene Life in California* (7th ed.). Natural History Museum of Los Angeles County Science Series 37. For the systematic description of the La Brea faunal record, including *Panthera atrox*, *Smilodon fatalis*, and the dire wolf *Aenocyon dirus*, with chronological framework.

16. Heintzman, P. D., Zazula, G. D., Cahill, J. A., Reyes, A. V., MacPhee, R. D. E., & Shapiro, B. (2015). “Genomic data from extinct North American *Camelops* revise camel evolutionary history.” *Molecular Biology and Evolution,* 32(9), 2433–2440. https://doi.org/10.1093/ molbev/msv128. The paleogenomic analysis of three Hunker Creek (Yukon) *Camelops* specimens recovered both mitochondrial and partial nuclear genomes, placing the genus as sister to the Old World *Camelus* clade and dating the Camelops–Camelus divergence to the Late Miocene.

2. Martin, P. S. (2005). *Twilight of the Mammoths: Ice Age Extinctions and the Rewilding of America.* University of California Press, chapter 1 and chapter 9, and table 1 (pp. 10–11). 4. Scott, E., & Cox, S. M. (2008). "Late Pleistocene distribution of *Equus* (Mammalia: Equidae) in western North America." For the terminal Pleistocene chronology of the North American equids.

For isotopic reconstruction of *Arctodus* diet and ecological role; see also Figueirido, B., Pérez-Claros, J. A., Torregrosa, V., Martín-Serra, A., & Palmqvist, P. (2010). "Demythologizing *Arctodus simus,* the 'short-faced' long-legged and predaceous bear that never was." *Journal of Vertebrate Paleontology,* 30(1), 262–275, for a contrary view of the animal's ecology; the loss of *Arctodus* is uncontested, whatever its exact trophic role.

6. Martin (2005), chapter 1 and chapter 2, on the residual post-extinction fauna; and Koch, P. L., & Barnosky, A. D. (2006). "Late Quaternary extinctions: state of the debate." *Annual Review of Ecology, Evolution, and Systematics,* 37, 215–250, for the continent-by-continent loss figures.

7. Barnosky, A. D., Koch, P. L., Feranec, R. S., Wing, S. L., & Shabel, A. B. (2004). "Assessing the causes of Late Pleistocene extinctions on the continents." *Science,* 306(5693), 70–75, for the intercontinental pattern of selective loss of large terrestrial mammals and its implications.

8. Mosimann, J. E., & Martin, P. S. (1975). "Simulating overkill by Paleoindians." *American Scientist,* 63(3), 304–313, for the simulation work demonstrating that a small human population advancing through naive megafaunal prey could plausibly produce the observed extinction pattern in a few centuries. Also Martin, P. S., & Klein, R. G., eds. (1984). *Quaternary Extinctions: A Prehistoric Revolution.* University of Arizona Press, for the field's mid-1980s synthesis; Martin, P. S., & Wright, H. E., eds. (1967). *Pleistocene Extinctions: The Search for a Cause.* Yale University Press, for the earlier collected volume that established the debate.

10. Alley, R. B. (2000). "The Younger Dryas cold interval as viewed from central Greenland." *Quaternary Science Reviews,* 19(1–5), 213–226, for the Greenland ice-core record and the place of the Younger Dryas within the broader pattern of late-Quaternary climate variability; also Dansgaard, W., et al. (1993). "Evidence for general instability of past climate from a 250-kyr ice-core record." *Nature,* 364(6434), 218–220, for the earlier comparable oscillations.

11. Martin (2005), chapter 9, p. 165, quoting E. O. Wilson's 1992 characterization as epigraph and offering the subsequent "I will lay aside impartiality" declaration.

12. For a representative version of this refined climate-centric account, see Grayson, D. K., & Meltzer, D. J. (2003). "A requiem for North American overkill." *Journal of Archaeological Science,* 30(5), 585–593; and Grayson, D. K. (2007). "Deciphering North American Pleistocene extinctions." *Journal of Anthropological Research,* 63(2), 185–213. Grayson and Meltzer are the most persistent recent critics of the strong overkill formulation; their counter-model is best read as an example of what a refined climate-centric account looks like.

13. Broecker, W. S. (2006). "Was the Younger Dryas triggered by a flood?" *Science,* 312(5777), 1146–1148, for the canonical statement of the Lake Agassiz meltwater mechanism; and Carlson, A. "Geochemical proxies of North American freshwater routing during the Younger Dryas cold event." *PNAS,* 104(16), 6556–6561, for geochemical evidence constraining the routing. ## CHAPTER FOUR · THE FOURTH AXIS

1. United States Department of Agriculture, Economic Research Service. (2023). *Feed Outlook.* USDA-ERS, multiple issues, 2012 and 2022. Corn futures exceeded $8.00/bushel during the 2012 drought, the highest nominal prices in the history of the US corn market at that time. The 2022 spike, driven by the combination of drought stress, post-pandemic supply-chain disruption, and the loss of Ukrainian grain exports, pushed feed costs to levels not seen since the 2012 event in inflation-adjusted terms. For pastured-poultry-sector impacts specifically, see trade-press accounts in *Acres U.S.A.*, *Growing for Market*, and *Stockman Grass Farmer* covering the 2012 and 2022 seasons.

2. The three-pillar model of sustainability — economic, social, environmental — evolved through several institutional formulations in the late twentieth century. For the institutional genealogy, see Purvis, B., Mao, Y., & Robinson, D. (2019). "Three pillars of sustainability: in search of conceptual origins." *Sustainability Science,* 14, 681–695, which traces the model's emergence through the Brundtland Report, the 1992 Rio Declaration, and the subsequent corporate and institutional sustainability literature.

3. World Commission on Environment and Development. (1987). *Our Common Future.* Oxford University Press. The Brundtland definition of sustainable development appears in Chapter 2 of the report.

4. For the ISO 14000 family of environmental management standards as applied to food-system assessment, see International Organization for Standardization. (2015). *ISO 14001:2015 —Environmental management systems — Requirements with guidance for use.* For life-cycle assessment methodology applied to food systems specifically, see Notarnicola, B., Sala, S., Anton, A., McLaren, S. J., Saouter, E., & Sonesson, U. (2017). "The role of life cycle assessment in supporting sustainable agri-food systems: A review of the challenges." *Journal of Cleaner Production,* 140, 399–409.

5. Global Reporting Initiative. (2021). *GRI Standards: Consolidated Set of GRI Sustainability Reporting Standards.* Global Sustainability Standards Board. The GRI Standards are the most widely adopted sustainability reporting framework globally, organizing disclosure around economic, social, and environmental performance categories, and have been applied to food and agriculture cooperatives through the GRI 13 sector standard for Agriculture, Aquaculture and Fishing Sectors (2022).

6. McBride, W. D., & Greene, C. (2009). *Characteristics, Costs, and Issues for Organic Dairy Farming.* Economic Research Report No. 82, United States Department of Agriculture, Economic Research Service. See also Su, Y., Cook, M. L., & Park, T. (2019). "The impact of the farmgate milk price on the survival of U.S. organic dairy farms." *Renewable Agriculture and Food Systems,* 34(6), 558–568, documenting the role of the organic price premium in organic dairy farm survival, and the sector's repeated contraction episodes when premium compression has reduced the gap between organic and conventional milk prices.

7. Waltenburg, M. A., Victoroff, T., Rose, C. E., et al. (2020). "Update: COVID-19 among workers in meat and poultry processing facilities — United States, April–May 2020." *Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report,* 69(27), 887–892. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. The report documents infection clusters at 115 meat and poultry processing facilities across 19 states in the first two months of the pandemic, resulting in facility closures and throughput reductions that produced simultaneous live-animal surpluses on farms and retail meat shortages.

8. Henson, C. B., Luedke, A., Allured, D. J., & Hoerling, M. P. (2022). "The February 2021 cold event in Texas and the south-central US: Historical context and attribution." *Journal of Climate,* 35(18), 5927–5943. For the agricultural impacts specifically, see Texas A&M AgriLife Extension Service. (2021). *Preliminary Texas Agricultural Losses from Winter Storm Uri.* The estimated crop and livestock losses across the Texas agricultural sector exceeded $600 million, concentrated in Rio Grande Valley citrus, livestock mortality in operations without backup power, and cold-chain disruption for produce exports.

9. Glauber, J., & Laborde, D. (2022). "How will Russia's invasion of Ukraine affect global food security?" *International Food Policy Research Institute Blog,* February 24, 2022. See also Ben Hassen, T., & El Bilali, H. (2022). "Impacts of the Russia-Ukraine war on global food security: towards more sustainable and resilient food systems?" *Foods,* 11(15), 2301, for a systematic assessment of the trade disruption's effects on global grain markets and food security in import-dependent regions.

10. Boyer, J. S., Byrne, P., Cassman, K. G., Cooper, M., Delmer, D., et al. (2013). "The U.S. drought of 2012 in perspective: a call to action." *Global Food Security,* 2(3), 139–143. The 2012 drought affected approximately eighty percent of US agricultural land and reduced the national corn yield by roughly twenty-seven percent relative to trend, driving commodity corn prices above $8/bushel.

11. Holling, C. S. (1973). "Resilience and stability of ecological systems." *Annual Review of Ecology and Systematics,* 4, 1–23.
